Preface

Lao She (1899-1966), whose original name was Shu Qingchun and style name Sheyu, was a native of Beijing. He lost his father at an early age and grew up in poverty, deeply influenced by the lifestyle, thinking, and aesthetic preferences of the lower-class people in old Beijing. In 1924, he went to the UK to teach and began his literary career, attracting attention with his humorous style. After returning to China in 1930, he wrote works such as "Camel Xiangzi" and "Divorce", establishing himself as a leading figure in urban folk literature in the history of modern literature. During the War of Resistance, he headed the Association of National Literary and Art Circles Against the Enemy and wrote the multi-volume novel "Four Generations Under One Roof". After the founding of New China, he produced hits like the dramas "Dragon Beard Ditch" and "Teahouse", earning the title "People's Artist".

The novel tells the tragic story of a rickshaw puller named Xiangzi in old Beiping.

Xiangzi, having come from the countryside, ended up in the city and became a rickshaw puller. He was young, strong, had no bad habits, and was full of confidence for the future. His only wish was to buy a rickshaw and become an “independent”, “free” rickshaw puller. But three ups and downs in trying to buy a rickshaw dashed his dreams, trapping him in despair.

Emotionally, Xiangzi also lost his autonomy. Tiger Girl, the daughter of the rickshaw garage owner Liu Siye, liked Xiangzi. Although Xiangzi disliked her for being old and ugly, he didn’t resist her enticements and ended up marrying her. After marriage, Xiangzi used Tiger Girl’s private savings to buy an old rickshaw from Er Qiangzi. But not long afterward, Tiger Girl died of dystocia, and Xiangzi had to sell the rickshaw to arrange her burial. This loss of home and loved one brought Xiangzi deep grief. After Tiger Girl died, Xiaofuzi’s thoughtful care made him truly fall in love with the ill-fated girl. It seemed as if he found the courage to keep going, but fate continued to play tricks on him—driven by livelihood, Xiaofuzi was forced into prostitution, and before Xiangzi could “make it”, she hanged herself. This turned Xiangzi’s last glimmer of hope into a bubble.

From then on, Xiangzi completely broke down and degenerated. He indulged in food, drink, gambling and prostitutes, being cunning and lazy, even selling people’s lives, until he ultimately became the scum of society, a walking corpse, silently awaiting death.

"Camel Xiangzi" uses Beijing vernacular, dialect, and describes some local customs of old Beijing, making it feel intimate and natural, easy to read aloud. It is a classic work of modern vernacular (baihua) fiction.

One

We are going to talk about Xiangzi, not camels; “Camel” is just Xiangzi’s nickname. So, let’s first talk about Xiangzi. As for the relationship between “camels” and Xiangzi, we’ll just mention it here in passing and leave it at that.

There are many types of rickshaw pullers in Beiping: Young and strong, nimble-footed ones prefer to rent nice rickshaws, work “all day”, and decide for themselves when to go out and when to turn in their rickshaw; when they pull a ride, they park at an established “rickshaw station”[1]

 or mansion gate, waiting especially for those in a hurry. If things go well, they may make one or two yuan in a day; if unlucky, they may waste the whole day and not even make their “rickshaw fee”, but they don’t care much. These guys have two basic hopes: either to land a long-term client, or to buy their own rickshaw. Once they own a rickshaw, doing long or short-term fares doesn’t matter much—anyway, it’s their own rickshaw.

Slightly older than this group, or weaker runners due to their health, or those with family obligations who can’t afford to waste a whole day, most of them pull an eight-tenths-new rickshaw; both the person and the rickshaw are rather decent, so their dignity is mostly intact when bargaining for fares. These rickshaw pullers might work “all day” or just “half a day”. In the latter case, since they still have energy, regardless of winter or summer, they always go for the “night shift”[2]

. At night, you need to be more careful and skillful than during the day; naturally, you also make a bit more money.

Those over forty or under twenty probably can’t hold their own among the previous two groups. Their rickshaws are battered, and they don’t dare work the “night shift”, so they can only start early, hoping to work from dawn till three or four in the afternoon, just making their “rickshaw fee” and a bite to eat. Their rickshaws are beat up, and they run slow, so they have to walk more and charge less. They’re the ones hauling goods at melon, fruit, and vegetable markets; the pay is low, but at least no need to run fast.

Here, for those under twenty—some start this line of work as young as eleven or twelve—very few can become high-class rickshaw pullers after twenty, because injuries in childhood make it hard to become robust. They might pull rickshaws their whole life, yet never get their moment in the limelight. The over-forty ones might have been pulling rickshaws for eight or ten years, and muscular decline makes them content to fall behind. Gradually, they realize sooner or later they’ll die on the street. Their posture, bargaining tricks, shortcuts, and detours evoke memories of their former glory, and they use their flared nostrils to mock the younger crowd. But that bit of pride does nothing to offset the looming darkness, and so they often sigh as they wipe their sweat. However, compared to some other rickshaw pullers around forty, it seems they haven’t suffered all the way yet. These others never expected they’d ever be involved with rickshaws; only when life and death blurred would they finally grab the rickshaw handles. Laid-off police or school runners, peddlers who ate through their capital, or unemployed craftsmen, when they had nothing left to sell, nothing left to pawn, would bite their lips, with tears in their eyes, and start down this road to ruin. They had already sold the prime years of their life, and now their blood and sweat, earned from eating millet cakes, was left on the street. No strength, no experience, no friends, and even among their peers, they got no kindness. They pulled the shabbiest rickshaws, belts maybe springing leaks several times a day; even as they hauled people, they had to beg forgiveness, even though fifteen copper coins was already a good deal.

In addition, differences in background and knowledge create further divisions among rickshaw pullers. Those born in the Xiyuan or Haidian naturally prefer runs to Xishan, Yanjing, Tsinghua; likewise, those outside Andingmen favor Qinghe, Beiyuan; and those beyond Yongdingmen head to Nanyuan… These guys go for longer routes and don’t care for short fares; a long trip is worth it, and they disdain wasting time on trifling coins. But they can’t match the stamina of the Dongjiaominxiang rickshaw pullers, who specialize in foreign clients, running directly from Dongjiaominxiang all the way to Yuquan Mountain, the Summer Palace, or the Western Hills. Stamina aside, the main reason ordinary rickshaw pullers can’t compete for this business is that these men who “eat foreign rice” know a special kind of knowledge: they can speak foreign languages. English soldiers, French soldiers, the so-called Wanshou Mountain, Yonghegong, “Eight Great Hutongs”, they all know. They have their own set of foreign words, never sharing with others. Their running style is special too—keeping a steady trot, head down, eyes straight ahead, sticking to the curb, with an air of detachment but self-confidence. Because they pull foreigners, they don’t have to wear a rickshaw number vest, instead always in long-sleeved white shirts, white or black trousers, with especially roomy pants, ankle ties; on their feet, broad, double-soled blue cloth shoes; clean, neat, commanding. At the sight of such attire, other rickshaw pullers wouldn’t even bother to compete for these fares or races—they seemed to belong to another profession altogether.

Given this simple analysis, we can now pinpoint Xiangzi’s status, hopefully as accurately as identifying a certain screw in a machine. Before he got the nickname “Camel”, Xiangzi was a relatively independent rickshaw puller. That is, he belonged to the group of young, strong men who owned their own rickshaw: his own rickshaw, his own life, all in his own hands—a high-class rickshaw puller. This was by no means an easy achievement. One year, two years, at least three or four; sweat drop by sweat drop, who knows how many drops, before finally earning that rickshaw. Through grit in wind and rain, through thrift at every meal, he earned that rickshaw. That rickshaw was the sum and reward of all his struggles and hardships, like a warrior’s medal earned through a hundred battles. When he rented rickshaws, he spun like a top all day and night, east to west, south to north, whipped along by others; he had no self. Even in such dizzying toil, his eyes stayed clear, his mind calm, always thinking of owning a distant rickshaw, one that would make him free, independent—a rickshaw as much his own as his hands and feet. With his own rickshaw, he could avoid the humiliation from rickshaw owners and didn’t have to curry favor with others; with his own strength and rickshaw, he just had to open his eyes to put food on the table.

He wasn’t afraid of hardship, nor did he have the easily forgiven but inadvisable bad habits of most rickshaw pullers. His intelligence and diligence should have made his dream a reality. If he had come from better circumstances or had more education, he certainly wouldn’t have ended up “in the rubber crowd”; whatever he did, he wouldn’t have wasted his chances. Unfortunately, he had to pull rickshaws; but even in this trade, he proved his talent and wit. He seemed the type who could even be a good ghost in hell. Born in the countryside, orphaned and losing his few acres of land, he came to the city at eighteen. With the robust build and honesty of a rural youth, he’d done just about every kind of manual job he could find to put food on the table. But soon he realized that pulling rickshaws was more lucrative; other hard labor had meager pay, while rickshaw pulling offered more options and opportunities—never knowing when or where a windfall would appear. Of course, he also knew not all opportunities were by chance; both person and rickshaw had to be presentable in order to attract good clients. After thinking it over, he believed he had what it takes: he had strength, he was young; all he lacked was some experience and the courage to start out pulling a good rickshaw. But this was an obstacle he could definitely overcome—with his physique and strength, he just needed to try it out for ten days or half a month, and he’d surely acquire the right look, then rent a new rickshaw, maybe soon haul for a long-term client, then through frugality for a year or two, or at most three or four, he’d be able to buy his own rickshaw—the very best! Looking at his powerful muscles, he felt it was just a matter of time—a goal he was bound to achieve, not just a dream!

His build and muscles matured ahead of his years; in his early twenties, he was already big and tall. Though time had not yet fully shaped his figure, he already looked like an adult—a grown man still sporting the innocent, mischievous look of a kid. Watching the high-class rickshaw pullers, he plotted how to cinch his waist tighter to better show off his broad chest and straight back; turning to look at his shoulders—how wide, how imposing! Cinching his waist, slipping on wide-legged white trousers tied with a thin cord at the ankle, exposing his “numbered” big feet! Yes, he could undoubtedly become the most outstanding rickshaw puller; he giggled like a fool at the thought. He wasn’t much to look at, but what made him likable was the spirit on his face. His head was not large, his eyes round, his nose fleshy, his eyebrows short and thick, and his scalp always freshly shaved and shiny. There was no excess flesh on his cheeks, but his neck was almost as thick as his head; his face was always ruddy, with a prominent scar between his cheekbone and right ear—a gift from being bitten by a donkey while napping under a tree as a child. He didn’t care much about his looks. He loved his face just as he loved his body—solid and tough; he considered his face a part of his limbs, as long as it was sturdy. Yes, even after coming to the city, he could still stand on his head for ages. Upside-down like that, he felt like a tree—every part straight and upright.

He really was a bit like a tree—tough, silent, yet full of life. He had his ambitions, some careful thoughts, but didn’t like to talk about them with others. Among rickshaw pullers, individual grievances and troubles were public conversation. At the “rickshaw stand”, in little tea houses, in big courtyards, everyone reported, described, even argued over their stories, which then became common property, just like folk songs circulating everywhere. Xiangzi was a country boy,His speech wasn't as quick as city folks'; if quick speech was a gift of genius, he was naturally quiet, so he wasn't interested in learning the sharp tongues of city people. He knew his own business and didn't like discussing things with others. Since his mouth was often idle, he had plenty of time to think, and his eyes seemed to always look inward, at his own heart. Once his mind was made up, he'd follow the path he'd set in his heart; if the path was blocked, he could go a day or two without making a sound, biting his teeth as if biting into his heart! When he decided to pull a rickshaw, he just went and did it. Renting a broken-down rickshaw, his first step was to train his legs. He didn't earn much the first day. Business was better on the second, but after two days, his ankles were so swollen they looked like two gourds, and he couldn't lift his feet anymore. He endured it, no matter how much pain there was. He knew this was inevitable, something every rickshaw puller must go through. If he didn't pass this hurdle, he'd never dare to run wholeheartedly.

Once his legs recovered, he had the courage to run. This brought him real joy, because there was nothing else to fear: he knew the streets well, and even if he sometimes took a longer route, it didn’t matter much; after all, he had plenty of strength. As for rickshaw-pulling technique, with all the pushing, pulling, carrying, and hauling he'd done in the past, he caught on quickly enough. Besides, he had his own ideas: pay attention, don’t compete needlessly, and you generally won’t make mistakes. When it came to bargaining for fares, though, he wasn't quick enough or slick enough to go up against the seasoned old-timers. Knowing this weakness, he simply avoided the "rickshaw stand"; wherever there were no rickshaws, that's where he'd park his own. In these quieter spots, he could bargain at his ease, and sometimes he wouldn’t even name a price—just say, “Hop on, pay what you think is right!” He looked so honest, his face so simple and endearing, that people just couldn’t help but trust him. They couldn't believe this big, simple guy would overcharge them. Even if they were suspicious, they’d just assume he was a country bumpkin new to the city who probably didn’t know the way around and that's why he couldn't quote a price. And whenever they asked, “You know the way?” he would smile, half acting dumb and half playing coy, leaving people unsure of what to think.

Within two or three weeks, his legs were broken in. He knew his own way of running looked good. The way one ran was proof of a rickshaw puller's skill and qualifications. The ones who ran with their feet splayed out, like a pair of fans sweeping across the ground, were clearly country novices. The ones who hunched way over, their feet dragging along the ground at barely more than a walking pace, but still looking like they were running, were mostly the old-timers in their fifties. The very experienced but not strong had another style: chest pulled in deeply, lifting their legs high, sticking their heads out with each step; doing so, they looked like they were running hard, but really weren’t any faster than anyone else; they relied on “posturing” to hold onto their dignity. Xiangzi certainly never adopted these styles. His strides were long, his waist firm, and when he ran there wasn’t much noise—each step stretched and contracted a bit, the handlebars stayed steady, and the passenger felt safe and comfortable. No matter how fast he was running, if he had to stop, a couple of light scuffs of his big feet and he’d come to a halt; it seemed his strength reached every part of the rickshaw. His back was slightly bent, hands loosely gripping the handles; he was agile, neat, accurate—moving rapidly but never looking flustered, fast but never dangerous. Even among rickshaw pullers, this counted as quite rare and precious.

He upgraded to a new rickshaw. From the very day he switched, he found out exactly what one like the one he rented—a flexible yoke, genuine brass fittings, large waterproof curtain, double lamps, slender-necked big brass horn—cost a bit over a hundred. If the paintwork and fittings used a little more iron, a hundred yuan would cover it. Basically, if he had a hundred yuan, he could get a rickshaw. Suddenly, he thought, if he could save one dime a day, a hundred yuan would take a thousand days—a thousand days! He could barely figure how long that really was. But he made a firm decision: whether a thousand days or ten thousand, he had to own a rickshaw! The first step he should, he figured, was to rent for monthly hire. If he picked up a regular client with ten-odd business dinners a month, he could snag two or three yuan extra for car fare. Plus, if he saved another one or two yuan—or even three to five—each month, that was fifty or sixty yuan a year! That brought his hope much closer. He didn’t smoke, drink, gamble, or have any hobbies, nor did he have family to weigh him down, so as long as he grit his teeth, nothing was impossible. He made a vow to himself: within a year and a half, he—Xiangzi—would get his own rickshaw! Brand new, not a secondhand one ever touched by another.

He really did get a monthly client. But reality didn’t fully support his hopes. True, he gritted his teeth, but after a year and a half, he still hadn’t achieved his goal. He did get some regular clients, and handled things with care; but, unfortunately, things in the world aren’t one-sided. He was careful, but the employers didn’t always keep him; sometimes after two or three months, sometimes after ten days or a week, he’d be let go! He had to look elsewhere for work. Of course, while job hunting, he also took odd gigs; looking for a horse while still riding one, he couldn’t just be idle. During such times, he made frequent mistakes. He forced himself to keep going, not just to make enough to get by, but to keep saving for his rickshaw. But forcing yourself is never a good idea: when rickshaw pulling, he couldn’t run with total concentration—he seemed preoccupied, and the more he worried, the more upset he became. If things went on this way, when would he ever get his rickshaw? Why was this happening? Wasn't he strong-willed anymore? When his thoughts were in disorder, he forgot his usual caution. The tire hit a bit of junk metal, blew out—and he had to bring the rickshaw in. Worse, sometimes he’d hit a pedestrian, or even once, in a hurry, lost a wheel cap squeezing past someone. If he was with a monthly client, these mistakes would never happen; but when he was between jobs and feeling off, he'd get clumsy. If he damaged the rickshaw, of course he had to pay for repairs; that made him even more anxious, like fuel on a fire; and afraid he'd cause even bigger trouble, sometimes he’d sleep through an entire day out of laziness. When he woke up, a whole day wasted, he’d regret and blame himself. Also, in these times, the more anxious he got, the more he punished himself, his eating and drinking became irregular; he thought he was made of iron, but in truth, he too could get sick. When he fell ill, he hated to spend money on medicine, toughing it out on his own. The result was, his illness got worse and worse: not only did he have to buy medicine, he’d end up having to rest for several days at a stretch. All these hardships just made him grit his teeth harder, but still didn’t speed up the money he set aside for his rickshaw. A full three years, and he finally scraped together a hundred yuan!

He couldn’t wait any longer. His original plan was to buy the best, newest, most ideal rickshaw, but now he had to work with just a hundred yuan. He couldn’t wait anymore; what if something happened and he lost a few more yuan? Just then, there happened to be a brand-new rickshaw (custom made, client couldn’t pay for it) that was almost just what he wanted; normally worth over a hundred, but because the deposit was forfeited, the shop was willing to let it go for less. Xiangzi’s face turned bright red, his hands shaking, as he slapped down ninety-six yuan: “I want this rickshaw!” The shopkeeper tried to bump it up to an even number, talked and talked, rolled his rickshaw outside and back in, opened the canopy, closed it, honked the horn—each action accompanied by a string of lavish adjectives; finally, he kicked the steel spokes twice and said, “Listen to that sound, like a bell! Take it, even if you break it piece by piece, or if a spoke goes soft, just bring it back, slam it in my face! One hundred bucks, not a cent less!” Xiangzi counted out the money again: “I want this rickshaw, ninety-six!” The owner realized he had a man of principle in front of him, looked at the money, looked at Xiangzi, sighed: “Let’s be friends. The rickshaw’s yours; six-months’ warranty: as long as you don’t smash the main body, I’ll repair everything for free; here’s your warranty slip—take it!”

Xiangzi’s hands shook even more as he pocketed the warranty, pulled his rickshaw away, and nearly burst into tears. Once he got to a quiet spot, he carefully inspected his own rickshaw, using the lacquered side to catch his own reflection! The more he looked, the more he loved it—he could forgive even the small ways it didn’t match his ideal, for it was now his. When he felt he could rest for a bit, he sat down on the new straw mat on the footboard, looking at the gleaming brass horn on the handlebar. Suddenly, he remembered, this year he was twenty-two. Since his parents died young, he’d forgotten which day was his birthday. Since coming to the city, he’d never celebrated a birthday. Well then, buying a new rickshaw today could count as his birthday, too; both his and the rickshaw’s—it would be easy to remember, and since the rickshaw was the product of his own sweat and toil, what reason was there not to count man and rickshaw together?

How to celebrate this “double birthday”? Xiangzi had it figured out: the first ride must be for a well-dressed person, definitely not a woman. Ideally, he’d get a fare to Qianmen, second best to Dong'an Market. Once there, he deserved a meal at the best food stall, something like piping hot sesame flatbread stuffed with spicy lamb. After eating, if he got more good jobs, he’d do another one or two; if not, then he’d call it a day. That was a birthday!

Since he got the rickshaw, Xiangzi’s life grew more energetic by the day. Whether he drove for a monthly client or just picked up random fares, he no longer had to fret about “rickshaw rental.” Everything he earned was his own. In a good mood, he was even more pleasant to others, and business went all the more smoothly. After half a year, his hope grew even greater: at this rate, in two years, at most two, he could buy another rickshaw. One, two… maybe open his own rickshaw company!

But, hope more often than not goes unrealized—Xiangzi was no exception.

Rickshaw stand: i.e., rickshaw stop.

Night hire: refers to taking a rickshaw out after 4 pm and working until before sunrise.

Making a living.

2

Ever since he bought the rickshaw, Xiangzi ran even faster. He was handier, and bolder, too. But he was always more careful with his rickshaw. Still, he’d look at himself and then at his rickshaw and feel something wasn’t right if he didn’t run fast.

Since coming to the city, Xiangzi had grown another inch or more. He could tell himself, and felt as if he might still grow taller yet. Indeed, his skin and features had grown tougher and more defined, and a small mustache had sprouted on his upper lip; but he still thought he ought to grow a little more. When he walked up to a small hut door or a street gate and had to duck deeply to get inside, though he said nothing, he felt a secret pride: he was already so tall, and still growing. He seemed both an adult and a child at the same time. It was really quite amusing.

Such a big man pulling such a beautiful rickshaw—his own rickshaw—with such a soft, springy yoke that even the handles trembled slightly; the body so shiny, the seat so white, the horn so loud—how could he not run fast? How could he treat himself and his rickshaw right if he didn’t? It wasn’t vanity; it was a sense of responsibility. If he didn’t run fast—fly, really—he couldn’t fully show off his own strength or the beauty of the rickshaw. That rickshaw really was lovely: after being used for half a year, it seemed to have a sense and feeling in every part; any twist of Xiangzi’s waist, bend of the knee, or straightening of the back, it would respond instantly, giving just the right help. There was never any awkwardness between him and it. When he came across a flat stretch of road with few people, Xiangzi could grip the handles with one hand, the tire making a soft, swift sound like a breeze driving him on, fast and steady. After a run, his clothes would be soaking wet—he could wring them out, like they’d just come out of a washbasin. He felt tired but very content, a prideful weariness—just like after riding a fine horse for dozens of miles. Boldness for him didn’t mean carelessness: when he dared to run flat out, he was still never careless. Failing to run fast was letting others down; but if he damaged the rickshaw by running wild, he’d be failing himself. The rickshaw was his life—he knew how to take care. By matching caution with boldness, his confidence grew by the day—he firmly believed both he and the rickshaw were made of iron.

As a result, not only did he dare to race flat out, he barely even worried about when it was safe to work. He thought earning a living by the force of his own arms was the most honorable thing in the world; he was eager to go out—no one could stop him. He didn’t pay much mind to rumors: soldiers coming again to Xiyuan, fighting in Changxindian, new cases outside Xizhimen, Qihuamen locked up half the day—he paid little heed. Of course, if all the shops had shuttered their doors and the road was lined with armed police, he wouldn’t go looking for trouble and, like everyone else, would hurry to pack up. But rumors—he didn’t believe them. He knew how to be careful, especially now that the rickshaw was his own, but after all he was a countryman, not like city people who panicked at the slightest rumor. Besides, his build made him confident: even if he were unlucky enough to run into disaster, he figured he’d get by; he wasn’t someone to be bullied—such a big guy, such broad shoulders!

News and rumors of war sprang up almost every year, along with the spring wheat; the ears of wheat and bayonets could be seen as symbols of the hopes and fears of northerners. When Xiangzi’s new rickshaw was just six months old, it was the season when wheat most needed spring rain.Spring rain may not always fall according to people's wishes, but war will always arrive, whether anyone wants it or not. Rumor or fact, Xiangzi seems to have forgotten that he once worked in the fields; he doesn’t care much about how war destroys farmland, nor does he pay much attention to whether there is spring rain or not. He only cares about his rickshaw; his rickshaw produces pancakes and all kinds of food, it is like a piece of land that grows everything, following him obediently wherever he goes, a living field, a treasure. Because of the lack of rain and the news of war, the price of grain has gone up; that much, Xiangzi knows. But like everyone else in the city, he only complains about how expensive food is, without having any idea what to do about it; food is expensive—well then, it’s expensive, who can make it cheap? This attitude makes him focus only on his own life, putting all disasters and dangers to the back of his mind.

If city people are powerless about everything, they can always start a rumor—sometimes completely making something up out of nothing, sometimes turning a bit of truth into a huge story—just to show that they’re neither stupid nor idle. They are like little fish; when there’s nothing going on, they poke their mouths above the water and blow out a few useless bubbles, feeling quite pleased with themselves. Among rumors, the most interesting are always about war. Other rumors often remain just that, like stories of ghosts and fox spirits, you’re not going to suddenly see a ghost just because folks keep talking about them. But when it comes to war, precisely because there’s never any accurate news, rumors actually produce instant results. The details might differ considerably from reality, but about whether war will happen or not, they’re right eight or nine times out of ten. “There’s going to be a war!” Once these words are spoken, sooner or later there really will be a war; as for who is fighting whom, or how the fighting will go, everyone has their own version. Xiangzi was well aware of this. Still, the laborers—the rickshaw pullers included—might not welcome war, but they don't necessarily suffer most from it when it comes. Whenever war breaks out, those who panic the most are the rich folks. At the first sign of trouble, they’re desperate to escape; money helps them come quickly, and leave just as quickly. But they themselves can't escape easily, weighed down by the burden of their wealth. They need to hire many people to act as their legs, someone to carry their trunks, old and young, men and women all need rickshaws to carry them away; in such times, the hands and feet of those who make a living selling their strength suddenly become highly valuable: “Qianmen, East Station!” “Where?” “East—Station!” “Fine, just give me one yuan forty cents! No need to haggle, it’s chaos out here!”

It was in this situation that Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw out of the city. The rumors had been circulating for more than ten days, prices had already gone up, but the fighting still seemed far off, and before long North Peking wouldn't see war. Xiangzi continued working as usual, not slacking off because of the rumors. One day, while pulling in the western city, he sensed something fishy. At the west end of Huguosi Street and at Xinjiekou, there wasn’t a single person calling for a ride to “Xiyuan? Tsinghua?” He wandered for a while near Xinjiekou. He heard that rickshaws didn’t dare leave the city anymore, that rickshaws were being grabbed outside Xizhimen—carts, carriages, mule carts, foreign-style rickshaws, all being confiscated. He wanted to have a cup of tea, so he headed south; the calm rickshaw stand made the danger real, and though he was fairly courageous, he didn’t want to deliberately walk into certain danger. At that very moment, two rickshaws came from the south, occupied by what looked like students. The rickshaw pullers shouted as they walked: “Anyone going to Tsinghua? Hey, Tsinghua!”

None of the rickshaw pullers at the stand answered; some looked at the two rickshaws with relaxed, patient smiles, some sat there puffing their small pipes, not even lifting their heads. The two rickshaws kept calling out: “Everyone mute? Tsinghua!”

"Two yuan, I’ll go!” piped up a young, bald, short fellow, as if making a joke, since no one else responded. “Bring it over! Find another one!” The two rickshaws stopped.

The young bald guy hesitated for a moment, as if unsure what to do. The others still didn’t move. Xiangzi realized that leaving the city must truly be dangerous—otherwise, why did nobody race for a two-yuan Tsinghua run, something that usually went for just 20-30 cents? He didn’t want to go either. But the bald fellow seemed determined—if someone would go with him, he was ready to risk it; his eyes landed on Xiangzi: “Hey, tall guy, how about you?”

The phrase “tall guy” made Xiangzi laugh; it was a compliment. His heart warmed: with such praise, maybe he should back up this bold little bald guy; besides, two yuan is two yuan—not something you come across every day. Danger? Would things happen to be that unlucky? Besides, someone had claimed a few days back that the Temple of Heaven was full of soldiers—he saw it with his own eyes, there wasn’t even a single soldier’s hair there. Thinking this over, he pulled his rickshaw over.

At Xizhimen, the city tunnel was almost deserted. Xiangzi grew uneasy. The bald fellow also sensed the danger, but he kept smiling, “Let’s just do it, buddy! Fortune or disaster, today is the day!” Xiangzi knew things were going south, but after all these years hustling on the street, you can’t go back on your word, you can’t throw a tantrum like a child!

After leaving Xizhimen, they didn’t see a single rickshaw; Xiangzi lowered his head, not daring to glance at either side of the road. He felt his heart pounding against his ribs. At Gaoliang Bridge, he glanced around—no soldiers in sight; he relaxed a bit. Two yuan was still two yuan, and he figured, without courage, how could you land such a sweet deal? Normally, he was a man of few words, but right now he felt like chatting with the bald man—the streets were eerily quiet. “Want to take the dirt path? The main road—” “Of course,” the short guy guessed what he meant, “once we’re on the side road, we’ll feel a bit more secure!”

Before they even made it to the side road, Xiangzi and the bald guy, rickshaws and all, were seized by a gang of about ten soldiers!

Even though it was already the season when people went to Miaofengshan for temple fairs and incense, the nighttime chill was still too much for just a thin shirt. Xiangzi had nothing extra on him except for a gray single-layer military shirt, and blue cotton military pants, both of which reeked terribly from sweat—just as stinky before he ever put them on. Wearing this ragged army uniform, he thought of his former clean white undershirt and the set of indanthrene blue cotton-padded jacket and pants; how clean and respectable they were! Sure, there are fancier things in the world than indanthrene blue, but Xiangzi knew how hard he’d worked just to get that clean and tidy. Smelling the reek of sweat on himself now, he thought of his past struggles and successes with extra pride, ten times more than before. The more he thought about the past, the more he hated those soldiers. His clothes, shoes, hat, rickshaw, even the cloth belt at his waist, had all been taken by them, leaving him only with a body black and blue and covered in blisters! Still, clothes didn’t mean much; the injuries would soon heal. But his rickshaw, the rickshaw he’d sweated for years to get, was gone! Ever since reaching the military camp, it had disappeared! All the past troubles and hardships could be forgotten in the blink of an eye, but he couldn’t forget that rickshaw!

He wasn’t afraid of hardship; but getting another rickshaw wasn’t something that could be done just by talking; at the very least, it would take years of work! All his previous success counted for nothing, and now he had to start over again! Tears came to Xiangzi’s eyes! He hated not only those soldiers, but the whole world. On what grounds do people get treated this way? On what grounds? “On what grounds?” he yelled out. That shout—though it made him feel a bit better—instantly reminded him of the danger. Never mind everything else, escaping was most important!

Where was he? He couldn’t really say for sure himself. These past days, he’d been running along with the soldiers, sweat running from his head to his heels. When moving, he had to carry, drag or push the soldiers’ belongings; when stopped, he had to fetch water, cook, and feed the livestock. All day long, his only thought was about putting every last bit of strength into his hands and feet, his mind a blank. At night, as soon as his head touched the ground, he’d pass out as if dead, and to never wake up again didn’t seem like such a bad thing.

At first, he seemed to remember the soldiers retreating toward Miaofengshan. Once they reached the back of the mountain, he was too busy climbing to notice anything else, thinking every now and then that he might tumble into a ravine, where wild eagles would pick his bones clean, and he couldn’t worry about anything more. After winding through the mountains for many days, one day the mountain paths dwindled, and with the sun at his back, he caught sight of the plains ahead. The bugle sounded for dinner, calling the soldiers back to camp, and a few armed men led in some camels.

Camels! Xiangzi was suddenly jolted into thought, like a lost traveler spotting a familiar signpost, and everything came rushing back. Camels can’t cross mountains; he must have reached the edge of the plain. From what he knew, the western suburbs of Beijing—places like Balizhuang, Huangcun, Beixinan, Moshikou, Wulitun, Sanjiadian—all had camels. Could it be that after all this wandering, he’d come to Moshikou? What kind of strategy is this—if these soldiers who only know how to run and loot could even have a strategy—he didn’t know. But he knew that if this really was Moshikou, then the soldiers were stuck in the mountains and had come down looking for a way out. Moshikou was a good spot: to the northeast you could get back to the Western Hills; to the south you could rush for Changxindian or Fengtai; head due west and there’s another way out. As he considered the routes available to the soldiers, he mapped out his own: this was his chance to escape. If the troops retreated into the wild hills again, even if he broke free from them, he’d still risk starving. If he was going to run, he had to take this chance. From here, he believed, with one dash he could get back to Haidian! Though there were lots of places in between, he knew them all—close his eyes, and he had a map in his head: this was Moshikou—heaven help him, it must be Moshikou!—he’d head northeast, cross Jinding Mountain and Liwangfen, and there was Badachu; from Sitai Ping he’d head east toward Xingzikou, and arrive at Nanxinzhuang. For cover, it was best to stick to the hills, go from Beixinzhuang north to Weijiacun; keep north, pass Nanhetan; keep going, reach Hongshantou, Jiewangfu, then Jingyiyuan! Once he got to Jingyiyuan, even blindfolded he could find his way to Haidian! His heart was about to burst out of his chest! All his blood seemed to have flowed into his limbs these past days; now it rushed back to his heart, making him hot inside while his arms and legs went cold; burning hope made him tremble all over!

He couldn’t sleep all the way till midnight. Hope made him elated, terror made him anxious; he wanted to sleep but couldn’t, his limbs sprawled on the hay as if they’d fallen apart. All was silent, only the stars above beating in rhythm with his heart. The camels suddenly wailed twice, not far from him. He liked the sound—like hearing a rooster crow late at night, it was both mournful and somehow comforting.

Far off, there were cannon shots—very distant, but distinctly cannon shots. He didn’t dare move, but immediately the camp was in turmoil. He held his breath—the chance was here! He was sure that the soldiers would have to retreat, and definitely head back into the mountains. Experience had taught him that these troops fought like bees trapped in a room, crashing around aimlessly. With the cannon fire, the soldiers were bound to flee; so he’d better be ready too. Slowly, holding his breath, he crawled along the ground, heading toward the camels. He knew the camels couldn’t really help him, but as fellow captives, he had to feel some sympathy for them. The camp was getting more chaotic, he found the camels—dark shapes like dirt mounds, lying motionless in the darkness except for deep breathing, as if the world were completely peaceful. This gave him a little courage. He lay beside the camels, much like a soldier hiding behind a sandbag. He quickly reasoned it out: the cannon fire was coming from the south, even if it wasn’t a real battle, it was at least a warning that “this route is blocked.” The soldiers would have to flee into the mountains again. If they really did, they couldn’t bring the camels. So, the fate of the camels would be his fate. If the soldiers gave up the animals, he’d have a chance to escape; if they didn’t, he’d be finished too. Pressing his ear to the ground, he listened for footsteps, his heart pounding fiercely.

He didn’t know how long he waited, but no one came to fetch the camels. He plucked up his courage and sat up; peering between the camels’ humps, he saw nothing, and all around it was pitch dark. Run! Whether for better or for worse, run!

[1]

 “Yibian’er” means “at the same time.”

[2]

 “Zhaohu ba” means “do it, go for it.”

[3]

 “Jin’er ge jiu shi jin’er ge” means “today is the critical moment, success or failure is decided today.”

Three

Xiangzi had barely run a few dozen steps when he stopped. It turned out Xiangzi was reluctant to leave behind those camels, because now all he had left in the world was his life. Even a piece of hemp rope lying on the ground, he would gladly pick up. Even if it was useless, at least it brought some comfort—at least he’d be holding a piece of rope, not completely empty-handed. Sure, escape was most important, but what good was a naked life? He had to lead the camels away—even if he had no idea what use they might serve, at least they counted as belongings, and not small ones.

He got the camels up. Handling camels wasn’t something he knew much about, but he wasn’t afraid—they were livestock, he came from the countryside, and dared to get close to them.The camels stood up very, very slowly. He didn’t bother to check carefully if they were all tied together; as soon as he felt he could pull them along, he started to walk, whether he was pulling along just one or the whole “batch.”

As soon as he started walking, he regretted it. Camels—so used to carrying loads in their mouths—just cannot walk fast. Not only do they have to move slowly, but extremely carefully as well. Camels are afraid of slipping; a puddle of water, a patch of mud, and they might splay their legs or twist a knee. The entire value of a camel lies in its four legs; if a leg is lost, it’s all over! But Xiangzi wanted to escape with his life!

Still, he couldn’t bring himself to let them go. He left everything to fate—camels that came to him for free couldn’t be abandoned!

Having pulled rickshaws for so long, Xiangzi had a pretty good sense of direction. Even so, his mind was a bit muddled now. When he first found the camels, his whole heart had focused on them; once he’d started leading them, he lost track of everything—where was what, where he was headed—the night was so dark, his heart so frantic, that even if he knew how to navigate by the stars, he didn’t dare to leisurely take his bearings. The stars—in his eyes—seemed even more anxious than he was, bumping and jostling each other around the black sky. Xiangzi didn’t dare look at the sky anymore. He kept his head lowered, moving forward in a hurry but not daring to quicken his steps. He remembered this: as long as he was leading the camels, he needed to stick to the main road; he couldn’t take the mountain path anymore. From Moshi Kou—if this really was Moshi Kou—to Huangcun was a straight road. This was not only the main camel road, but also didn’t take him any further out of the way. “Not taking the long route” meant a lot to a rickshaw puller. But, there was no cover on this road! What if he ran into soldiers again? Even if he didn’t run into a big army, with his tattered army uniform, the mud on his face, and his bushy long hair, who would believe he was a camel driver? No, he didn’t look like a camel driver at all—he looked very much like a deserter! If the authorities caught a deserter, it could turn out to be a small matter; but if caught by the villagers, at the very least he might be buried alive! Thinking about this, he started to tremble, and the light, rhythmic thud of camel hooves behind him made him jump in fright. If he wanted to save his own life, he still needed to abandon these burdens. Yet, he just couldn’t let go of that rope tied to the camels’ noses. Just walk, keep going—wherever he ends up, so be it. Whatever he comes across, he’ll deal with it as it comes. If he survives, he’ll have earned some livestock; if he dies, he accepts his fate!

But then, he took off his army coat, yanked off the collar in one go; even the pair of loyal brass buttons were pulled off and tossed into the darkness, without even a sound. Then, he draped the now collarless, buttonless shirt diagonally over his body, tying the two sleeves together across his chest like a bundle. This, he thought, might reduce suspicion that he was a deserter; and he rolled up a section of his pants. He realized this still didn’t really make him look like a camel driver, but at the very least he didn’t look entirely like a deserter anymore. Coupled with the mud on his face and his sweat-soaked body, it would probably be enough to pass for a “coal miner.” His thoughts were slow, but thorough; and once he had an idea, he put it into action immediately. In the black of night, with no one watching, he didn’t really have to do this right away, but he couldn’t wait. He had no idea of the time—it might suddenly be daybreak. Since he wasn’t following the mountain road, he’d have nowhere to hide in daylight; if he wanted to keep moving during the day, he needed people to believe he was a “coal miner.” Having thought of this, he immediately did it, and felt a little relief in his heart—it was as if the danger had passed and Beijing was right before his eyes. He needed to make it to the city quickly and steadily, because he didn’t have a cent on him and not a morsel of food. He couldn’t afford to waste any more time. At this, he thought of riding the camel to save some energy and hold on against hunger for a bit longer. But he didn’t dare. Even if it was perfectly safe, he’d have to get the camel to kneel first in order to climb on, and time was precious—he couldn’t afford more trouble. Besides, if he climbed up so high, he’d have an even harder time seeing what was underfoot; if the camel fell, he’d go down with it. No, he’d just keep walking.

He roughly sensed he was walking along the main road; but the direction and place were all so uncertain. It was deep into the night; the exhaustion from many days and the fear of fleeing made his body and mind thoroughly uncomfortable. By the time he’d covered some distance, his steps became so even, so slow, that he gradually began to feel drowsy. The night was still deeply dark, a bit of wet, chilly mist in the air, making his heart feel all the more lost. He peered at the ground, but it always seemed to have bump after bump—yet once he stepped down, the ground proved flat. This kind of caution, and being deceived by it, made him even more uneasy, almost a bit irritable. He decided not to mind the ground anymore, tried to look straight ahead and drag his feet along. He couldn’t see anything around him; it was as if all the darkness in the world was lying in wait for him, for him to step from darkness into darkness; behind him followed the silent camels.

Gradually, he got used to the darkness outside, and his mind seemed to stop thinking. His eyes, unconsciously, slipped closed. He didn’t know if he was still walking forward or had already stopped; he only felt his heart rising, falling, churning—a wave after wave, as if he was drifting in a black sea; darkness and his heart became one, both lost, both rising and falling, both hazy. Suddenly, his heart gave a jolt, as if he remembered something—or maybe he heard some sound. He couldn’t tell; but his eyes snapped open again. He really was still walking forward, but he’d already forgotten what had come to him just now. All around, nothing stirred. His heart pounded for a while before calming down. He told himself not to close his eyes again, and not to let his thoughts run wild; the most urgent thing was to hurry to the city. But if his mind wasn’t occupied, his eyes would easily shut again; he needed to keep thinking about something, and needed to stay awake. He knew that if he collapsed, he could sleep for three days straight. What to think about? His head was woozy, his body clammy and uncomfortable, his hair itched, his feet were sore, and his mouth was dry and rough. He couldn’t think of anything else—just pitying himself. Yet even his own story he couldn’t think through in detail; his head was so vacant and swollen, as if just as he remembered himself, he forgot himself again—like a candle about to go out, unable even to illuminate its own wick. Along with all the surrounding darkness, it felt as if he were floating in a ball of black air. He knew he was still in existence, still moving forward, but without anything else to prove he was truly walking somewhere; it was like drifting alone on a desolate ocean, unable to believe in himself. He’d never experienced this kind of unsettling agony and utter loneliness. Normally, even though he wasn’t fond of making friends, he was, at least, in the sun; the sunlight shone on his limbs, things were visible before him, and he was never afraid. Now, though, he wasn’t exactly afraid, but the sheer uncertainty of things was intolerable. If the camels were as unruly as mules or horses, perhaps it would have kept him on alert, paid more attention to them—but the camels were exactly the opposite: so obedient it was annoying. At his most dazed, he’d suddenly suspect they weren’t there behind him after all, and get a fright; he was half sure these great beasts could quietly slip off into a side road in the darkness without his noticing, as if they could melt away like ice being dragged along on a rope.

He didn’t know when, but he sat down. If he died just like this, even with consciousness after death, he wouldn’t remember how or why he sat down. He sat there for five minutes or maybe an hour; he couldn’t tell. He didn’t know if he had sat down first and then fallen asleep, or fallen asleep first and then sat down. Probably, he’d dozed off first and then sat, since he was so tired he could have slept standing up.

He suddenly woke up. It wasn’t a natural waking from sleep, but a jolt—like jumping from one world to another, all in the blink of an eye. He saw nothing but darkness, but heard clearly the crow of a rooster—so clear, as if something sharp had scraped through his mind. He was now wide awake. The camels? Nothing else mattered right now. The rope was still in his hand, the camels still beside him. He felt at peace inside. Too tired to get up. His body was sore and weary; he didn’t want to stand, yet didn’t dare sleep again. He had to think, to think carefully, come up with a good idea. Right at this moment, he thought of his rickshaw, and cried out, “Why should I be treated this way?”

“Why should I?” But shouting into the void solved nothing. He reached out and touched the camels; he still didn’t know exactly how many he had. After feeling over them, he realized there were three. He didn’t think three was too many or too few; he focused his mind on these three animals. Although he hadn’t worked out exactly what to do, he vaguely felt that his future depended entirely on these three creatures.

“Why not sell them and then buy a new rickshaw?” He nearly jumped to his feet! But he didn’t move; it was as if he felt ashamed for not having thought of this simplest, most obvious solution earlier. Joy soon replaced his embarrassment, and he made up his mind: Hadn’t he just heard a rooster crow? Even if roosters sometimes crow at one or two in the morning, daybreak couldn’t be far off. Where there are roosters, there must be a village—maybe this was Beixin’an? There were camel traders there; he had to hurry, and if he could get there before dawn and sell the camels, he could buy a rickshaw the moment he reached the city! During all this chaos and turmoil, rickshaws must be cheaper; he was all focused on buying a cart, believing that selling the camels would be no problem at all.

Thinking about how the camels and the rickshaw were connected, his spirits soared, and his body suddenly felt free of discomfort. If he thought that these three camels could buy him a hundred mu of land, or exchange for a few pearls, he wouldn’t have been so happy. He quickly got up, pulled the camels, and went on. He didn’t know the current market price for camels; he’d only ever heard, back in the old days without trains, that one camel was worth a “big treasure,” because camels are strong but eat even less than mules or horses. He didn’t hope for three “big treasures”—just a hundred and eighty or so, just enough to buy a rickshaw. The further he walked, the lighter it got; yes, it was lighter ahead, and he really was heading east. Even if he took the wrong road, his sense of direction was right; the mountains were to the west, the city to the east—he knew that, at least. Bit by bit, the universal blackness gave way to shades of light and dark; while he still couldn’t make out colors, he could see outlines of fields and distant trees taking shape in the widespread gray gloom. The stars thinned; the sky was covered in a gray mist like cloud or fog, dim, but much higher than before. Xiangzi raised his head with a bit of courage. He started to smell the grass on the roadside, and hear a few birdcalls; the vague shapes he could make out made his sense of sight, hearing, even smell and taste seem to return to normal. He checked over everything on himself—though it was all so tattered and sorry-looking, at least he could see that he was still alive—just like waking from a nightmare and suddenly feeling how precious life is. After looking himself over, he glanced back at the camels—just as scruffy as he was, and just as endearing. It was their molting season—their skin already showed patches of gray-red through the shedding fur, with only a few scattered, weak clumps of long hair left hanging here and there, ready to fall off at any time, like massive beggars among beasts. Most pitiful of all were their long, bald necks, so long, so bare and bent and foolish, sticking out for a stretch like a dejected skinny dragon. But Xiangzi didn’t think any less of them, no matter how disgraceful they looked—after all, they were living beings. He had to admit, he was the luckiest person in the world: fate had given him three living treasures—enough to trade for a rickshaw. This wasn’t something that happened every day. He couldn’t help but laugh out loud.

Red tinged the gray sky, making the land and distant trees look even darker. The red gradually blended with the gray; in some places, it became gray-violet, in others, especially bright red, while most of the sky became grape-gray. After a while, gold-yellow shone through the red, and every color glowed a little; suddenly, everything became strikingly clear. Then, the morning clouds in the east turned a deep red, and the sky overhead showed blue. The red clouds scattered—beams of gold light shot forth, the clouds running horizontal, the sunbeams vertical, weaving a glorious web of light in the southeastern sky: the green fields, trees, and wild grasses all shifting from dark green to glowing emerald. The old pine trunks glowed gold-red, birds’ wings flashed with golden light, everything seemed to be smiling. Facing the glow, Xiangzi wanted to shout for joy; ever since he’d been seized by the soldiers, he felt as if he hadn’t seen the sun, always cursing inside, his head hanging, forgetting there was even sun or moon, the heavens above. Now he walked freely, the world getting brighter as he went, the sun lighting up the dewdrops on the blades of grass, shining on his brows and hair, warming his heart. He forgot all his hardships, his dangers, his pain; no matter how ragged and filthy he was, the light and warmth of the sun had not left him out—he was living in a world with light and heat; he was happy, he wanted to shout for joy!

Looking at his torn clothes, then glancing back at the three molting camels, he smiled. Just with these four so unpresentable creatures, he thought, he had managed to escape danger and now walked toward the sunrise—it really was incredible! He no longer wondered who was right and who was wrong; it was all up to fate, he felt. He let go in his heart, walked slowly on, and as long as the heavens watched over him, he had nothing to fear. Where was he now? He didn’t care to ask, even though men and women were already appearing in the fields to start the day’s work. Just keep going,It didn't really matter if he couldn't sell the camels for now; he would just head to the city first, he was eager to see the city again. Even though there were no parents or relatives there, no property at all, it was still his home—the entire city was his home, and once he got there he would figure something out. In the distance was a village, not a small one, with willow trees outside the village standing like a row of tall, green sentinels, bowing their heads over the low houses, with cooking smoke floating over the rooftops. The barking of village dogs could be heard from far away, and it sounded delightful. He kept hurrying toward the village, not expecting anything exciting to happen, as if only to show he was not afraid of anything, that he was a good person and surely had no reason to fear the honest villagers; nowadays, everyone basked in the bright and peaceful sunlight. If possible, he wanted a little water to drink; if he couldn't get any, that was fine too; he had survived the mountains, so what was a bit more thirst?

The village dogs barked at him, but he paid little attention; the women's and children's stares made him feel a bit uncomfortable. He must look like a very strange camel driver, he thought; otherwise, why would everyone stare at him so blankly? He felt very embarrassed: the soldiers didn't treat him like a human being, and now in the village, people looked at him like he was some kind of monster! He didn't know what to do with himself. His height and strength had always made him proud and self-respecting, but these past few days, for no reason at all, he had suffered endless humiliation and hardship. Looking past the roof ridge of one house, he saw the bright sun again, but now, the sun didn't seem as lovely as before!

On the village's only main road, pig urine, horse urine, and sewage merged to form several stinky little pools. To the north was a relatively well-off family, with brick houses in the back, but only a wooden fence at the big gate—no wooden door, no gatehouse. Xiangzi's heart stirred: brick houses meant the owner was wealthy; a wooden fence without a gatehouse meant: a camel owner! Alright, he’d rest here for a bit; maybe a good opportunity would come along to send off the camels!

"Tsk! Tsk! Tsk!" Xiangzi called the camels to kneel down. Of all the camel commands, he only knew that "tsk…" meant kneel. He was very proud to use it, purposely letting the villagers know he wasn't an amateur. The camels really knelt down, and he also sat down boldly under a small willow tree. Everyone watched him, and he watched everyone; he knew this was the best way to reduce the villagers' suspicion.

After a while, an old man came out from the yard, his blue cotton shirt open at the chest, his face shining, clearly a rural landowner at first glance. Xiangzi made up his mind: "Old man, have you got any water? Let me have a bowl!"

"Ah!" The old man was rubbing mud off his hands in front of his chest, gave Xiangzi a glance, and carefully looked at the three camels. "There's water! Where are you from?"

"From the west," Xiangzi dared not mention the place, as it shouldn't be disclosed.

"There are soldiers in the west, right?" The old man’s eyes fixed on Xiangzi’s army trousers.

"The big army took them away, I just escaped."

"Ah! It's not too risky to bring camels out from the west pass, is it?"

"The soldiers have all gone into the mountains, the road is very safe."

"Mm!" The old man nodded slowly. "Wait a moment, I'll get you some water."

Xiangzi followed him in. In the yard, he saw four camels. "Old man, how about you keep my three and they'll make a set with yours?"

"Hmph! A set? Go back thirty years, I had three sets! Times have changed, who can keep camels now?" The old man stopped, staring blankly at the four animals. After a while: "A few days ago I thought of teaming up with the neighbors to drive them outside the pass to graze. There's trouble with soldiers to the east, trouble in the west, who dares to go! They're just grazing at home now, it makes me anxious just to look at them, anxious just to look at them. Look at all these flies! Once it really gets hot tomorrow, add in mosquitoes, you’ll see these fine animals suffer for nothing, sigh!" The old man nodded repeatedly, seemingly full of emotion and complaints.

"Old man, keep my three, make a full set and take them to graze outside the pass. These lively beasts, if they stay here all summer, the flies and mosquitoes will torment them half to death!" Xiangzi was almost begging.

"But who has money to buy now? It's not the time to keep camels!" "Just keep them, pay whatever you can; once I've gotten rid of them, I can go to the city and make a living!"

The old man took a closer look at Xiangzi, and could tell he wasn't a criminal. Then he glanced back at the animals outside the gate, and it seemed like he truly liked those three camels—he knew there was no real benefit in buying them, but book lovers always want to buy books, horse keepers can't part with a good horse, and someone who once owned three sets of camels is the same. Besides, Xiangzi said he'd sell cheaply; people who know the business tend to forget whether acquiring a bargain is really good for them.

"Young man, if I had more money, I’d really like to keep them!" The old man spoke honestly.

"Why not just keep them, take whatever you can give!" Xiangzi was so sincere that the old man felt a bit embarrassed.

"Honestly, young man, thirty years ago, these would be worth three large ingots of silver; now, with all this chaos, I—you'd better try elsewhere!" "Pay whatever you can!" Xiangzi couldn't think of anything else to say. He knew the old man's words were true, but he didn't want to go all over the place to sell camels—if he couldn't sell them, who knew what other trouble might come.

"Look, look, it’s hard enough to even mention twenty or thirty dollars, but it's even harder to actually hand it over; these are hard times!"

Xiangzi's heart sank too; twenty or thirty dollars? Still a long way from buying a rickshaw! But, first he wanted to get it done quickly, and second, he didn't believe he'd be lucky enough to run into another buyer. "Old man, pay whatever you can!"

"What do you do, young man? I can tell this isn't your line of work!"

Xiangzi told the truth.

"Oh, so you put your life on the line to get these animals!" The old man felt real sympathy for Xiangzi, and was reassured that this wasn't stolen; although it wasn’t much better than stealing, at least there was a layer of big troops in between. After a war, nothing can be judged by normal standards.

"How about this, my friend, I’ll give you thirty-five dollars; if I say it's not a bargain, I'm a puppy; if I could give one more dollar, I'm also a puppy! I'm over sixty. Hmph, what else can I say now!"

Xiangzi was at a loss. He never left a single penny when it came to money. But after everything with the army, hearing the old man's earnest and emotional words, he was embarrassed to keep arguing. Plus, thirty-five silver dollars in hand seemed more reliable than the ten thousand he had hoped for; even though risking his life for thirty-five dollars really was too little! Just the three big live camels alone couldn't, absolutely couldn't, be worth only thirty-five silver dollars! But, what else could he do! "They're yours now, old man! I'd just ask for one more thing, help me find a little jacket, and give me something to eat!"

"Alright!"

Xiangzi gulped down some cold water, then, holding thirty-five shiny silver dollars, two coarse cornbread buns, and wearing a torn white jacket that came up to his chest, set off for the city in one stride!

[1]

 谱儿, means appearance or look. Roughly means similar.

[2]

 大宝, a large silver ingot, weighs fifty taels.

[3]

 放青, means to herd livestock to graze on fresh grass.

Four  

Xiangzi lay in a small shop in Haidian for three days, his body switching between chills and fevers, his mind foggy and confused, a row of purple blisters appearing along his gums, wanting only water and nothing to eat. After three days of hunger, his fever subsided, and his body felt as soft as gummy candy. It was probably during those three days that his dealings with the three camels were overheard by others as he muttered in dreams or delirium. When he finally regained consciousness, he was already "Camel Xiangzi."

Ever since he had arrived in the city, he was simply "Xiangzi," as if he never had a surname; now, with "Camel" placed before "Xiangzi," no one cared at all what his surname actually was. Whether or not he had a surname was of no concern to him either. However, to think that three animals had only fetched a few bucks, and yet he had ended up with a nickname—he felt it wasn’t quite worth it.

As soon as he could struggle to stand, he wanted to go out and take a look around. He hadn’t expected his legs to feel so weak; just making it to the shop door he collapsed to the ground, dazedly sitting there for more than half the day, a cold sweat breaking out on his forehead. He endured for a while longer, then opened his eyes as his stomach rumbled, feeling the stirrings of hunger. He slowly got up and found a wonton stall. Ordering a bowl of wontons, he still sat on the ground. He sipped some broth, felt nauseous, held it in his mouth for ages, then forced himself to swallow it; he didn’t want any more. However, after a while, the hot soup seemed to flow like a string right down to his belly, and he belched twice. He knew he was starting to come back to life.

With a bit of food in his stomach, he finally had the presence of mind to look at himself. He was much thinner than before; his tattered pants were filthier than ever. He was too lazy to move, but he wasn’t going to go back to the city all untidy and ghastly; to be clean and presentable he’d need to spend money—getting a haircut, changing clothes, buying shoes and socks—all of it cost. The 35 yuan in his hand couldn’t be touched, not a single yuan, and even untouched it was still a long way from the price of a rickshaw! Still, he pitied himself. Even though the time spent with the soldiers hadn’t been long, thinking back on it now, everything felt like a nightmare. That nightmare had made him age a lot, as if all at once he’d added several years to his life. Looking at his big hands and big feet—obviously his own—but they seemed as if found from somewhere else. It made him deeply sad. He dared not think about all the past grievances and dangers; even when he tried not to think, they still remained, just like an overcast day when you know it’s gray outside even without looking. He felt that his body was particularly precious, and he shouldn’t be so hard on himself anymore. He stood up, knowing well that he was still weak, but he urgently wanted to go spruce himself up, as if just a haircut and a change of clothes would make him strong right away.

All dressed up, he only spent 2 yuan and 20 cents altogether. Rough undyed cotton trousers and jacket, looking like coarse polished cloth, cost 1 yuan, blue cloth shoes 80 cents, thread-woven socks 15 cents, and a straw hat for 25 cents. The rags he took off he exchanged for two boxes of matches. Carrying the matches, he walked along the main road toward Xizhimen. He hadn’t gone far before feeling weak and tired again. But he grit his teeth. He couldn’t take the rickshaw, not from any angle: a country person could walk 5 or 10 li, right? Besides, he was a rickshaw puller. That aside, for someone with his build and strength to be brought down by such a small illness—it was laughable; unless he collapsed and couldn’t get up at all, he’d crawl and roll into the city if he had to, but he definitely wouldn’t cave in! If he failed to walk into the city today, he thought, then Xiangzi was finished. He only trusted his own body, no matter what illness!

Swaying, he quickened his pace. Shortly after leaving Haidian, golden spots danced before his eyes. Grabbing onto a willow tree, he gathered himself for a long time, dizzy and panicked, but still refused to sit down. The spinning of the world slowly calmed, his heart seemed to fall back into his own chest from far away, and wiping the sweat from his forehead, he started walking again. Now freshly shaven, in new clothes and shoes, he felt he owed himself nothing; so, his legs had to do their part—walk! All in one go he made it to Guanshang. Seeing the bustle of people and horses, hearing the complicated, piercing noises, smelling the dry, musty stench, stepping onto the fine, filthy dust, Xiangzi wanted to kneel down and kiss that gray, stinking earth—the lovable earth, earth that gave rise to foreign cash! With no parents or brothers, no relatives, his only friend was this ancient city. This city gave him everything; even going hungry here was better than the country, here at least there was stuff to see, things to listen to, everywhere was color and sound. As long as he worked, there was endless money to be made, boundless good things to eat and wear. Here, even begging could get you meat broth and brine, while in the countryside it was only cornmeal. Just west of Gaoliangqiao, he sat by the riverbank and shed a few hot tears!

The sun was already in the west, the old willows on the river leaning as their tips caught bits of golden light. There wasn’t much water in the river, but plenty of green algae grew there, like a greasy, long green ribbon, narrow and deep, giving off a faint fishy tide smell. The wheat on the north bank had already sprouted awns, short and dry, the leaves covered in a layer of dust. In the south, the lotus leaves in the ponds were small and weak, floating on the surface, with tiny air bubbles constantly surfacing around them. On the bridge to the east, people and carts went back and forth—all the more hurried and anxious in the slanting evening light, as if everyone could sense the approach of dusk. All this seemed very interesting and endearing to Xiangzi’s eyes and ears. It was only rivers like this that could be considered rivers; only these trees, wheat, lotus leaves, bridges were worthy of their names—because they belonged to Beiping.

Sitting there, he was in no hurry. Everything before his eyes was familiar and beloved, and even dying while sitting there he felt he might be happy. After resting for ages, he went to the bridgehead and had a bowl of old-style tofu: vinegar, soy sauce, chili oil, chopped chives, poured over hot, snow-white tofu, releasing the most tempting aroma, so fragrant that Xiangzi almost held his breath; holding the bowl, looking at the deep green chives, his hands trembled uncontrollably. After one bite, the tofu blazed a path of warmth through his body; he ladled two more spoons of chili oil in himself. By the time he finished a bowl, sweat had soaked through his waistband. Half-closing his eyes, he handed the bowl over: "Another bowl!"

Standing up, he felt like a person again. The sun was still at its lowest in the west, the river water with a faint red glow from the evening clouds, and he felt so wonderful he wanted to shout out loud. He touched the smooth scar on his face, patted the money in his pocket, gave one more glance at the sunlight on the corner tower; he forced himself to forget his illness, forget everything, as if he had some wish in his heart, and decided to walk back into the city.

In the city gate tunnel there was a jumble of all kinds of vehicles and people—nobody dared rush through, but everyone wanted to get through fast. The sounds of whips, shouts, curses, horns, bells, laughter were all funneled by the gate tunnel—like a giant loudspeaker—buzzing together as if everyone made a bit of noise, all buzzing. Xiangzi planted a foot to the east, then the west, his hands parting people to either side, like a long, lean fish leaping happily with the current, squeezing into the city. At once he saw Xinjiekou, the road was so wide, so straight, his eyes lit up, shining as bright as the flashes on the eastern rooftops. He nodded slightly.

His bedding was still at the Renhe Rickshaw Depot on Xianmen Avenue, so naturally he headed there. Because he had no family, he always lived at the depot, even though he didn’t always pull Renhe’s rickshaws. Renhe’s boss Liu Siye was nearly seventy years old; his age hadn’t slowed his wits. In his youth, he had served as a depot soldier, run gambling dens, trafficked in people, and even lent out money at usurious rates. All the skills and qualities needed for these trades—muscle, cunning, means, relationships, reputation—Liu Siye had them all. In the late Qing, he took part in gang fights, kidnapped women, even knelt on iron chains as punishment. While kneeling on those chains, he didn’t frown or beg for mercy. He made it through lawsuits with iron nerve—that was his reputation. After getting out of prison, he happened to enter the Republican era, with the police growing ever more powerful. Liu Siye realized that the age of street heroes was over—even if someone as legendary as Huang Tianba came back, he wouldn’t have many chances. So he opened a rickshaw depot. Coming from a street-hustler background, he knew exactly how to deal with the poor, when to be harsh, when to relax—he had a genius for managing people. No driver dared mess with him. He only needed to glare or give a hearty laugh and could have people bewildered, feeling one foot was in heaven and the other in hell, with nothing to do but let him call the shots. Today, he owned over 60 rickshaws—the worst was still 70-80% new; he didn’t keep broken ones. His rents were higher than others', but at the New Year he gave drivers two extra days compared to the competition. Renhe had accommodations, and all single drivers working his rickshaws could stay for free—as long as they paid the rental shares. If they missed payments and tried to haggle, he'd keep their bedding and kick them out like an old kettle. If anyone had an emergency or urgent sickness, they just told him—he never hesitated to help, any trouble, any risk, he was wholeheartedly supportive. That was called "reputation."

Liu Siye was tiger-like. Nearly seventy, he stood ramrod straight, could still walk ten or twenty li in one go. Huge round eyes, big nose, square mouth, two tiger fangs—he looked like a tiger when he opened his mouth. He was nearly as tall as Xiangzi, his head shaved bright, no beard. He thought of himself as the Tiger, but sadly had no son, only a tiger daughter—thirty-seven or thirty-eight—everyone who knew Liu Siye also knew Tigress. She was sturdy and broad-faced too, scaring off men, but excellent at helping run the business, though no one dared marry her. She did everything like a man, even cursing with more flair than most. Liu Siye handled the outside, Tigress the inside—the father-daughter pair ruled Renhe like an iron fortress. The Renhe depot was the authority in the rickshaw world—their methods were often quoted by drivers and owners like scholars quoting the classics.

Before buying his own rickshaw, Xiangzi used to pull for Renhe. He’d leave his savings with Liu Siye for safekeeping. When the amount added up, he’d ask for it and buy his new rickshaw.

“Siye, look at my rickshaw!” Xiangzi pulled his new rickshaw to Renhe for a look. The old man glanced at the cart and nodded, “No problem.”

“But I’m still going to live here till I get a longer-term gig, then I’ll move to a house!” Xiangzi said proudly.

“Fine!” Liu Siye nodded again.

So, whenever Xiangzi picked up a long-term gig, he’d move near his client; if that was over, and he was back on short fares, he’d return to Renhe depot.

To be allowed to live at Renhe without pulling for Liu Siye was rare, in the eyes of the other drivers. Some even guessed Xiangzi must be related to the boss; others thought Liu Siye had his eye on him, planning to marry Tigress off to him and make him a live-in son-in-law. Such suspicions carried some jealousy—but what if it turned out to be true? If Siye died, the depot would be Xiangzi’s. So they only dared guess, never saying anything unpleasant to Xiangzi’s face. But actually, Liu Siye’s favor had its own accounting. Xiangzi was such a person: able to keep old habits in a new setting. If he joined the army, he’d never become a bully just because he was in uniform. In the depot he was always moving—when done sweating, he’d find chores to do: wiping carts, pumping tires, airing rain covers, oiling things—no one had to ask, he liked doing it, and did it happily, like a favorite hobby. About 20 drivers lived at the depot year-round; after work, most just chatted or lay asleep—only Xiangzi’s hands were busy. At first they thought he was currying favor with Liu Siye, sucking up; after a few days, seeing he really wasn’t angling for anything, so genuine and natural, they had nothing to say. Liu Siye never praised him, never looked especially kindly at him, but he kept a mental note. He knew Xiangzi was a good hand, and even if he didn’t pull his own rickshaw, he wanted him at the depot. With Xiangzi around, for one, the yard and gate were always spotless. Tigress liked this big, honest guy even more; whenever she spoke, Xiangzi listened carefully and never argued; the other drivers, after rough experience, always talked back; she wasn’t afraid of them but didn’t care to waste words either, so saved her conversation for Xiangzi. When Xiangzi took a monthly gig, both father and daughter felt like they’d lost a friend. As soon as he returned, even Siye’s scolding seemed more good-natured.

Xiangzi, carrying the two boxes of matches, entered the Renhe depot. It wasn’t dark yet. Liu and his daughter were just eating dinner. Seeing him come in, Tigress set her chopsticks down, “Xiangzi! Did a wolf carry you off, or did you go digging for gold in Africa?” “Hmph!” was all Xiangzi could manage.

Siye’s big round eyes roved over Xiangzi, saying nothing. Wearing his new straw hat, Xiangzi sat across from them.
