“If you haven’t eaten yet, join us!” Tigress said, like inviting a good friend.

Xiangzi didn’t move, but suddenly a strange warmth rose in his heart. All along, he’d thought of Renhe as home: on monthly hire, the boss always changed; doing short fares,Sit for a while, then change; only here do they always let him stay, always someone saying idle words to him. Now that he just escaped with his life, has come back to familiar people, and is being fed again, he almost suspects they mean to make a fool of him, but tears nearly come to his eyes as well.

"Just had two bowls of old tofu!" He showed a little modesty.

"What were you up to?" Old Liu's big round eyes were still fixed on Xiangzi. "Where's the cart?" "Cart?" Xiangzi spat.

"Come over here and have a bowl of rice first! It won't poison you! What's two bowls of old tofu matter?!" Huniu dragged him over all at once, just like an elder sister doting on her little brother-in-law. Xiangzi didn't reach for a bowl but took out his money first: "Fourth Master, please hold this for me, thirty bucks." He put the small change back in his pocket.

Old Liu raised an eyebrow and asked, "Where'd this come from?"

Xiangzi, eating as he talked, recounted his story of being taken away by the soldiers.

"Hmph, you silly boy!" Old Liu shook his head after hearing the tale. "Dragged into the city and sold to the soup pot, even then they'd fetch more than ten bucks a head; if it were winter when the camel hair's nice and thick, three of them would sell for sixty bucks!"

Xiangzi had already been a bit regretful, and hearing this made him feel even worse. But then he thought, selling three living animals for the soup pot to meet the knife is a bit lacking in conscience; both he and the camels managed to escape, so they deserve to live. He didn't say anything, and his heart calmed down.

Miss Hu took the dishes away, and Old Liu tilted his head back as if recalling something. Suddenly he grinned, showing two tiger teeth that seemed to grow sturdier with age: "Fool, you say you were sick at Haidian? Why didn't you come back straight down Huangcun Avenue?"

"I still took the long way around Xishan to get back, afraid if I went down the main road, someone would catch up with me; if someone in the village got suspicious, they'd take me for a deserter!"

Old Liu smiled and rolled his eyes inward. He feared there might be something fishy in Xiangzi’s story—what if that thirty bucks was stolen, and he’d be holding stolen goods for someone else? Back when he was younger, he’d done all kinds of shady things himself; now, he acts like he’s reformed, so he has to be careful, and knows how to be. Xiangzi’s tale had just this one loophole, but Xiangzi explained it openly and without dodging, so the old man felt relieved.

"So what’s the plan?" He pointed at the money.

"I’ll go with your advice!"

"Buy another cart?" The old man bared his tiger teeth again, almost as if to say: "If you buy your own car, you still want to live here for free?!"

"Not enough! If I buy, I have to buy a new one!" Xiangzi wasn’t paying attention to Liu Sihye’s teeth, just looking at his own heart.

"Lend it to you? At one percent interest—a better rate than cart shops, which charge two and a half!"

Xiangzi shook his head.

"Going to pawn it, you won’t get as good a deal as I offer!"

"I’m not pawning it," Xiangzi said absent-mindedly, "I'll save up slowly, and buy it in cash once I’ve got enough!"

The old man looked at Xiangzi as if he were some strange character, irritating but impossible to be angry about. After a while, he picked up the money: “Thirty? Don’t try to pull any funny business!”

“That’s right!” Xiangzi stood up. “Off to bed now. Here’s a box of matches for you!” He put a box of matches on the table, then hesitated, “Don’t mention the camel business to anyone else!”

Five

Old Liu really didn’t spread the news for Xiangzi, but the story of the camels quickly passed from Haidian into the city. Before, though no one could find fault with Xiangzi, thanks to his stubborn spirit, they still felt he didn’t really fit in, was awkward. After the story of "Camel Xiangzi" spread, although Xiangzi still worked with his head down, not warming up to everyone, people started looking at him differently. Some people said he picked up a gold watch; some said he got his hands on a windfall of 300 silver dollars. But only the ones most sure of their facts would nod and say, he brought thirty camels back from Xishan! Though the versions differed, the conclusion was the same—Xiangzi got rich on "dirty" money! To people who’ve made "dirty" money, no matter how "un-brotherly" this guy might be, the rest always treat them with a bit of respect. Making a living though hard labor is so difficult that everyone wishes for luck. Since windfalls are so rare, the one lucky enough to get them must be unusual, blessed with remarkable fortune. So Xiangzi’s silence and lack of sociability changed to become the precious reserve of a man of status. That’s how it should be—and they figured they’d better hurry to buddy up with him. "Come on, Xiangzi! Tell us, how’d you get so rich?" Words like that reached Xiangzi every day. He didn’t utter a sound. Only when pushed to the limit, and that scar of his turned a bit red, would he say, “Get rich? Damn it, where’s my cart then?”

Yes, that was true—where had his cart gone? People started to wonder. But worrying for others is never as powerful as envy, so everyone soon forgot about Xiangzi’s cart and focused instead on his good luck. After a while, people saw he was still pulling a rickshaw, hadn’t changed jobs, bought a house, or gotten land, so they lost some interest. When talk came up of Camel Xiangzi, no one asked any more why he was called "Camel"—it was as if he was meant to be called that all along.

But Xiangzi himself couldn’t just let it go so lightly. He wished he could buy a new rickshaw immediately, and the more anxious he got, the more he thought about his old one. He worked diligently all day long, never complaining about the hardships, but as he worked, the memory of that old event would come back. When it did, his heart felt blocked and he thought, What’s the use of being strong-willed—being strong-willed doesn’t make the world any fairer. What right did they have to take his cart just like that? Even if he got a new one right away, what’s to stop something like this from happening again? The things that happened felt like a nightmare, making him almost too scared to hope for the future. Sometimes, when he saw others drinking and smoking and going to brothels, he’d even feel a twinge of envy. If being strong-willed is useless, why not enjoy life right now? They were right. Even if he didn’t start frequenting brothels, he ought to drink a couple cups at least, take it easy. Cigarettes, alcohol—now they seemed to have a special pull on him. He felt these two things didn’t cost much, but would definitely be enough to comfort him, keep him struggling on while also helping him forget past pain.

Still, he didn’t dare touch them. He had to save every possible cent; only then could he buy a rickshaw early. Even if he bought one today and lost it tomorrow, he still had to do it. This was his goal, his hope, even his religion. Without his own cart, life seemed pointless. He never thought of becoming an official, getting rich, buying property. All he could do was pull a cart; his greatest hope was to buy his own cart—without it, he couldn’t face himself. All day long, he obsessed over this matter, counting his money; if he ever forgot about it, he’d lose sense of himself, feel like he was just an animal that could run, without any prospects or human feelings. No matter how good the rickshaw was, if it was a rental, he could never get inspired pulling it—just like carrying a stone on his back, it didn’t feel right. Even with a rental, he never slacked, always kept it clean, never crashed it casually; but this was only caution and prudence, not real happiness. True, cleaning his own cart was like counting his own money—real joy. So he kept himself from smoking or drinking, even from indulging in good tea. In the teahouse, rickshaw pullers as upstanding as him, after a hard run, liked to drink tea made from leaves that cost ten copper a package, plus two packets of sugar—to refresh themselves and soothe the heat. When sweat dripped from his ears and lips after a good run, and his chest felt a bit of a burning sensation, he desperately wished to do the same; it wasn’t just a habit or style, but a real need. It was only a wish, though—in the end, he drank the tea scraps that cost a single copper. Sometimes he really wanted to curse himself, why make life so hard? But for a rickshaw man who wanted to save a little each month, how could it be done any other way? He steeled his heart. Buy the rickshaw first—just buy the rickshaw! Having his own cart would be enough to counter everything!

So tight was he with spending, and with making money he was even more relentless. If he didn’t have a monthly contract, he worked full days, left early and came home late, refusing to park until he made his quota, regardless of time or his legs; sometimes he even went an entire day and night without stopping. Before, he would never steal someone else’s fare, especially from the elderly, frail, or disabled; with his strength and his cart, if he competed with them, how could they have their fair share? But now, he didn’t care much. He only saw money—one more was one more—regardless of the hardship of the fare or who he was muscling in on; all that mattered was getting the fare, nothing else, like a hungry wild beast. As long as he had a fare, he was somewhat at ease, feeling that only by never stopping could he someday hope to buy a cart. Bit by bit, the reputation of Camel Xiangzi became much worse than when he was just plain Xiangzi. Many times, after he snatched a fare and took off, he’d hear a wave of curses behind him. He didn’t answer back, just kept his head down, running fast, saying to himself: “If it weren’t for buying this cart, I’d never be so shameless!” It was like he hoped this thought would earn the others’ forgiveness, but he wouldn’t actually say it aloud. At the rickshaw stand, or in the teahouse, whenever he saw everyone glaring at him, he wanted to explain, but once he saw how cold they were towards him—plus the fact that he never joined them in drinking, gambling, playing chess, or chatting—his words just circled in his mind, nowhere to go. Embarrassment gradually grew into a sense of resentful anger, and he started returning their stares in kind. Remembering the days just after he escaped from the mountain, when everyone treated him with so much respect, and now seeing how contemptuously they regarded him, made him feel worse. Hugging a pot of tea by himself, in the teahouse if it happened to be there, or counting his just-earned coins, if at the rickshaw stand, he did his best to press down his anger. He had no desire to fight, though he wasn’t afraid to. As for the others—they weren't afraid to fight either, but they had to think twice before getting physical with Xiangzi; none of them could take him one-on-one, and ganging up against him wouldn't look good. Forcing himself to swallow his anger, unable to think of any other solution, he could only endure for now—just wait until he had his own cart, then things would be better. Once he had his own cart and didn’t have to worry about rent day after day, he’d naturally be able to carry himself with dignity, no longer offending others by competing for business. Thinking this through cheered him up a bit, and he shot the others a look that seemed to say: We'll see about that!

Strictly speaking, he shouldn’t have been working himself so hard. After escaping back to the city, he hadn’t even waited to fully recover before taking up the rickshaw again. Though he was absolutely unyielding, he often felt tired. Even when tired, he didn’t dare rest—he always thought that sweating a little extra would get rid of the soreness and tiredness. He dared not be picky with his meals, but couldn’t eat anything rich, either. He could see he was a lot thinner, but since he was still tall and strong, he stopped worrying. He always thought that being bigger than others meant he could stand more hardship, and never considered that big guys who work harder need to eat more. Miss Hu had already warned him a few times: “If you keep going like this, you’ll be coughing up blood and it will serve you right!”

He knew it was well-intentioned advice, but, with nothing going right and poor nourishment, he was starting to burn with liver fire. He shot her a quick look, just a little fiercer: “Without working like this, when am I ever going to get my own cart?”

If anyone else looked at her like that, Huniu would have cursed them out half the street, but with Xiangzi, she couldn’t be more considerate and caring. She just pursed her lips: "You still need some downtime when buying a cart. Are you made of iron, or what! You ought to rest up for three days at least!” Seeing Xiangzi wouldn’t listen: "Fine, have it your own way, but if you die, don’t blame me!"

Old Liu also started to look down on Xiangzi a little: Xiangzi’s all-out effort, going out early and coming back late, sure did his cart no good. Although leasing a cart for a full day came with no set time limits, and you could return it whenever you liked, if everyone worked as doggedly as Xiangzi, even the toughest hardware would wear out six months early! What’s more, since Xiangzi was always hard at work, he rarely had time to help with extra chores like cleaning carts—another loss. The old man was a bit disgruntled. But, he didn’t say anything, since non-timed full-day rentals were just how things worked; lending a hand with the carts was a favor, not a duty. Given his status and reputation, he couldn’t shame himself by making an issue with Xiangzi. All he could do was throw a bit of disapproval from the corners of his eyes, and keep his mouth shut tight. Sometimes, he was awfully tempted to kick Xiangzi out. But looking at his daughter, he couldn’t do it. He never thought of Xiangzi as a future son-in-law, but if his daughter liked this stubborn kid, he couldn’t intervene. She was his only daughter, and with no hope of marrying her off, he couldn’t afford to push away her friend as well. To be honest, Huniu was so useful, that he really didn’t want her to get married; he felt a bit guilty about this secret selfishness, so he was a little afraid of her. The old man who’d never feared anything in his life was, in his old age, afraid of his own daughter. With some embarrassment, he told himself just this: as long as he was afraid of someone, it meant he wasn’t completely lawless—so maybe, with this fact in mind, he wouldn’t end up getting punished before he died. Having admitted to himself that he should be afraid of his daughter, he couldn’t bring himself to kick out Xiangzi. This of course didn’t mean he’d let his daughter do as she pleased and marry Xiangzi—not at all. He had come to suspect she might just have such intentions,But Xiangzi didn’t dare try to curry favor.

So he just stayed alert, seeing no need to bring trouble upon the daughter first. Xiangzi didn’t pay attention to the old man’s attitude; he didn’t have the energy to notice such trivial things. If he ever wanted to leave Renhe Garage, it was definitely not out of spite, but because he hoped to land a monthly contract. He was already a bit tired of picking up random fares. For one, everyone looked down on you for fighting over customers, and second, the daily income was unpredictable: more today, less tomorrow, and you’d never know when you’d finally save up enough to buy your own rickshaw. He wanted some certainty, even if the leftover was small; as long as he could reliably save a set amount every month, that gave him hope and peace of mind. He was a “one radish, one hole” kind of person.

He landed a monthly job. Hmph, it was just as unsatisfying as picking up random fares! This time it was at the Yang household. Mr. Yang was from Shanghai, Mrs. Yang was from Tianjin, and the second Mrs. Yang was from Suzhou. One man, two wives, each from a different region, and who knows how many children they had. On the very first day, Xiangzi nearly collapsed. Early in the morning, the main wife rode into the city to buy groceries. When she got back, he had to take the young masters and misses to school—some to junior high, some to elementary, some to kindergarten; different schools, different ages, different looks, but they were all equally annoying, especially when they sat in the rickshaw—the most well-behaved still had two extra hands compared to a monkey. After dropping the children off, Mr. Yang went to the office. After taking him there, Xiangzi had to rush back to take the second wife to Dong’an Market or to visit relatives. Then, bring the students home for lunch. After lunch, take them out again. When he brought the students back, Xiangzi thought he could finally eat, but the main wife, in her Tianjin accent, told him to fetch water. Sweet water was delivered to the Yangs, but the dirty laundry water was for the rickshaw puller to fetch. This job was not part of the original deal, but Xiangzi didn’t dare argue, so he quietly filled the vat with water. He set down the bucket, just about to get his food bowl, when the second wife called him to buy something else. The main wife and the second wife didn’t get along, but when it came to managing the household, they saw eye to eye: one rule was never to let the servants idle for a moment, the other was never to watch them eat. Xiangzi didn’t know that, and just thought it was a particularly busy day for the household, so he said nothing and spent his own money to buy a few sesame cakes. He loved money as much as life itself, but to keep the peace, he braced himself and spent it.

When he got back with the groceries, the main wife asked him to sweep the yard. In the Yang house, the master, the main wife, and the second wife always dressed impeccably when going out, but the inside of the house and the yard looked like a huge trash heap. Xiangzi felt sick looking at the yard, so he got right to cleaning, forgetting that a rickshaw puller wasn’t supposed to do chores. Once the yard was tidy, the second wife asked him to sweep the house as well while he was at it. Xiangzi didn’t object, but he was amazed: how could two such well-kept women tolerate a house so filthy you could hardly step inside? Once the house was clean, the second wife handed him a one-year-old “mud imp.” He was at a loss. He could do any sort of heavy work, but he’d never held a child before. He cupped the little master in both hands—if he didn’t grip hard, he was afraid the child would slip; grip too hard, and he might injure the little one. He broke into a sweat. He wanted to hand this little bundle over to Zhang Ma, a big-footed old servant woman from north of the Yangtze. But as soon as he found her, she gave him a tongue-lashing. At the Yangs’, they changed staff every three or five days—the masters always figured servants were lifelong slaves, and unless they worked them to the bone, they weren’t earning their pay. The only exception was Zhang Ma, who’d stuck around for five or six years, solely because she could curse anyone out, master or wife, whenever she was the least bit provoked. With Mr. Yang’s Shanghai-style venom, Mrs. Yang’s booming Tianjin accent, and the second wife’s smooth Suzhou lilt, they were usually invincible—but against Zhang Ma’s brashness, they felt a certain respect, like when heroes meet their match, and so they kept her on as a trusted servant.

Xiangzi was born in a rural northern village, where cursing in public was taboo. But he didn’t dare hit Zhang Ma, because respectable men don’t fight with women, nor did he want to argue back. He just glared at her. Zhang Ma fell silent, sensing a hint of danger. Just then, the main wife called Xiangzi to fetch the students. He hurriedly returned the little mud imp to the second wife. She thought he was deliberately slighting her and immediately let loose a torrent of abuse. The main wife herself wasn’t happy with Xiangzi holding the second wife’s child, so when she heard her scolding him, she joined in with her own slick Tianjin-flavored voice—scolding him as well; Xiangzi became their verbal punching bag. He hurriedly pulled his rickshaw out of there, almost forgetting to be angry, because he’d never seen such chaos before, and having it thrown at him all at once left him dizzy.

He fetched the kids back in batches, and the yard was even more chaotic than a bustling market: three women scolding, a mob of kids crying—it was as noisy as Dashilan after a theater show, and the confusion was indescribable. At least he still had to fetch Mr. Yang, so he rushed out again, thinking the shouts and horse noise in the street were actually preferable to the chaos inside.

It was nearly twelve before Xiangzi finally got a chance to sigh. He didn’t just feel physically drained—his head was buzzing; everyone at the Yangs, young and old, were already asleep, but to his ears, the squabbling of the master and the wives still played on, three phonographs spinning at once in his mind, making him uneasy. With no energy to think, he just wanted to sleep. But as soon as he stepped into his little room, his heart went cold, and he didn’t feel sleepy anymore. The gatehouse was split into two by a wooden partition with a doorway on each side. Zhang Ma lived on one side, he on the other. There was no lamp; the street-side wall had a window about two feet wide, just under a streetlight, letting in a bit of illumination. The room was damp and smelly, the dirt on the floor as thick as a copper coin, with a wooden board for a bed and nothing else. When he felt the bed, he realized that if he lay his head down, his feet would be pressed up against the wall; if he tried to stretch out, he’d almost be sitting up. He didn’t know how to sleep curled up. After thinking for a long time, he pulled the board into a slant, making both ends point toward the corners of the room, so he could stretch his legs a bit and at least get through the night.

He dragged his bedding in through the door, set it up haphazardly, and lay down. Legs dangling off, he couldn’t get comfortable, so he couldn’t sleep. He forced his eyes shut and consoled himself: sleep, you’ve got to get up early tomorrow! You’ve put up with everything else, so why not this? Food’s bad, work’s hard, but maybe sometimes you’ll get to play cards, get invited to dinner, or have a banquet. Why did you come out here, Xiangzi? Wasn’t it for money? As long as you can save more, you’ll put up with anything! With that, he felt much better, and even the stink in the room didn’t seem as bad, and he drifted off. He vaguely felt there were bedbugs, but was too tired to care.

After two days, Xiangzi was completely disillusioned. But on the fourth day, some lady guests arrived, and Zhang Ma busied herself setting up a mahjong table. Suddenly, it was as if a spring breeze swept across the frozen surface of his heart. When the wives started playing their game, they handed all the kids over to the servants; with Zhang Ma serving tea, towels, and cigarettes, the whole mob of little monkeys naturally became Xiangzi’s responsibility. He detested them, but snuck a glance into the house and saw the main wife managing the winnings very seriously. He thought: don’t be fooled by her fierceness, maybe she isn’t so dim and knows to slip the servants a little something during these games. For the sake of a tip, he was especially patient with the monkey pack, treating them as proper little lords and ladies.

Once the game was over, the main wife told him to take the guests home. Both ladies wanted to leave at the same time, so they needed another rickshaw. Xiangzi called up another one, and the main wife started rummaging for carfare to pay on behalf of her guests; the guests politely declined twice, but she insisted loudly: “Don’t be silly, sister! You come to my house and still pay for a rickshaw? Nonsense! Get on now!” Only then did she manage to pull out a dime.

Xiangzi saw clearly her hand tremble a little as she handed over the dime.

After seeing the guests off and helping Zhang Ma tidy up the game table, Xiangzi gave the main wife a look. She told Zhang Ma to get some hot water; as soon as Zhang Ma left the room, she pulled out a dime: “Here, take it, and don’t stare at me like that!”

Suddenly, Xiangzi’s face went purple. He straightened his back as if he was about to knock his head on the ceiling, grabbed the dime, and flung it onto her plump face: “Give me my wages for four days!” “What’s wrong with you?” She shot him a glance, then said nothing more and handed over four days’ pay. Bundling up his bedding, as soon as he stepped out the gate he heard cursing start up again in the yard.

[1]

 In this context, “banquet” refers to a formal meal or feast.

Six

On an early autumn evening, in a breeze rustling through starlight and shadowed leaves, Xiangzi looked up at the distant Milky Way and sighed. On such a cool night, with his broad chest, he still felt short of breath, an unbearable oppression inside. He wanted to sit and have a good cry. With his sturdy build, his endurance, his pride, being treated like a dog and unable to hold onto any job, he didn’t just resent the Yangs—he felt, distantly, a kind of despair, worried he might never have a chance to succeed in life. Hauling his bedding roll, he walked slower and slower, no longer the Xiangzi who could dash ten miles without effort.

When he reached the avenue, there were few pedestrians, but the streetlights were bright, making the emptiness more pronounced, and he felt utterly lost. Where to go? Naturally, back to the Renhe Garage. But his heart ached. For working folks, there’s always work to be found, but to have a patron and still fail at the job—it’s like a customer walking into a restaurant or barbershop, taking one look, and leaving. Xiangzi knew perfectly well that hiring and quitting was normal; if one place didn’t want him, there would always be another. Still, he’d done all he could, swallowing his pride for the sake of buying a rickshaw, and ended up lasting just three and a half days—the same as those smooth-talking pros—and he felt ashamed. He hardly felt he could face the people at Renhe Garage, fearing they’d laugh: “Look, even Camel Xiangzi only lasted three and a half days—hmph!”

If not Renhe, then where? To keep himself from dwelling on it, he headed straight down Xianmen Avenue. The front of the Renhe Garage was three shops wide; the middle one was an office—only for rickshaw pullers to settle accounts or handle business, not for hanging around, since both the east and west rooms were bedrooms for the Liu family. Next to the west room was the cart gate: two large green doors with a heavy iron bar above, and beneath a glaring, bare electric light. Hanging under the lamp was a gold-painted iron sign: “Renhe Rickshaw Garage.” All the coming and going was through this gate, and both the deep green paint and gold letters gleamed under the lamp's bright glare. The rickshaws that came and went—black-lacquered, yellow-lacquered—all gleamed as well, white cushions crisply in place, and even the pullers felt a certain pride, as if they belonged to the aristocracy of their trade. Once inside, past the west room, was a square courtyard with an old locust tree in the middle. The east and west wings were open bays where rickshaws were stored; the south rooms and a few small rooms behind them were used as the pullers’ dorms.

By now it was past eleven. Xiangzi saw the garage’s glaring but lonely lamp. There was no light in the office or east room, but the west room was still lit. He knew Miss Hu was still up. He wanted to slip in quietly so she wouldn’t notice him; she’d always looked up to him, and he didn’t want her to be the first to see him coming back in defeat.

He had just pulled his rickshaw under her window when Miss Hu came out the cart gate:

“Oh, Xiangzi? Wha—” She was about to ask more, but seeing how dejected he looked, bundled in his bedding, she swallowed her words.

Of course, what you fear is just what happens. Xiangzi’s shame and frustration pressed down on him, rooting him to the spot. He couldn’t speak, he just stared at her. But today she looked different—maybe it was the electric light, maybe it was powder, but her face was much whiter than usual; the paler face softened much of her usual harshness. There was a hint of rouge on her lips, which even made Miss Hu look a little flirtatious. This struck Xiangzi as very odd, and made him even more flustered, because he'd never thought of her as a woman before, and suddenly seeing those red lips made him feel a bit embarrassed. On top, she wore a light green silk jacket, and on the bottom, loose pleated navy crepe pants. The green jacket, under the lamp, shimmered softly with a hint of melancholy. Because it was short, a bit of the white pants waistband showed, making the green look even cleaner and more distinctive. The wide black pants fluttered in the breeze, like some eerie vapor trying to break free of the glaring lamp to fade into the night. Xiangzi dared not look any longer. He lowered his head in confusion, still thinking about that little glowing green jacket. With the Liu family’s wealth, she could afford to wear silk every day, but because she worked among rickshaw pullers, she always wore plain clothes, and even the fancier fabric wasn’t showy since it was just cotton.Xiangzi seemed to see something very novel, both familiar and strange, so his mind was a bit unsettled.

He had already been troubled in his heart, and now, under the harsh light, he encountered this strange living thing, so he was at a loss. Not willing to move himself, he actually hoped that Miss Hu would hurry inside, or order him to do something—he simply couldn't bear this kind of torment, a kind of suffering that was unlike anything else and felt truly unbearable.

"Hey!" She stepped forward and said softly, "Don't just stand there! Go put down the rickshaw and come back quickly, I have something to say to you. See you inside."

Usually, after helping her with things so many times, he could only comply. But today she was different than usual, and he wanted to ponder a bit. Standing there dazed, it felt awkward and stiff. At a loss, he pulled the rickshaw inside. Looking into the south room, there was no light, probably everyone was asleep; or maybe some hadn't put away their rickshaws. After putting the rickshaw away, he returned to her door. Suddenly, his heart began to pound.

"Come in, I have something to say to you!" She poked her head out, half-smiling and half-annoyed. He walked in slowly.

On the table were a few white pears not yet fully ripe, their skins still greenish. A wine jug, three white porcelain cups. A large No.1 plate, with half a marinated chicken, and some smoked liver and sausage and other cold dishes.

"Look," Miss Hu pointed him to a chair, waited for him to sit, and then said, "Look, I'm having a treat today, you have some too!" As she spoke, she poured him a cup of alcohol. The pungency of the baijiu mixed with the rich taste of the smoked and sauced meats made it extra intense and heavy. "Drink up, eat this chicken. I've eaten already, don't be polite! Just now I drew a fortune with dominoes, knew for sure you'd come back, isn't that accurate?"

"I don't drink!" Xiangzi stared at the wine cup, lost in thought.

"If you won't drink, get out. Trying to be nice to you, and you don't appreciate it? You silly camel! It's not going to kill you! Even I can drink four liang. If you don’t believe it, just watch!" She picked up the wine cup, downed more than half in one gulp, closed her eyes, and gave a ha! gesture. Holding up the cup, "You drink! If not, I'll pull your ear and force-feed you!"

Xiangzi was full of bottled-up resentment with nowhere to go, and being teased like this just made him want to stare her down. But he knew, Miss Hu had always been decent to him, and she was always straightforward with everyone; he shouldn't offend her. Since he didn't want to offend her, he figured he might as well pour out his grievances. He had never been much of a talker, but today it seemed there were thousands of words smothered in his heart that just had to come out to feel better. Thinking this, he realized Miss Hu was not teasing him, but caring openly for him. He took the wine cup and drained it. A wave of heat slowly, precisely, and powerfully traveled down his throat. He stretched out his neck, puffed out his chest, and belched twice, not too comfortably.

Miss Hu began to laugh. He struggled to swallow the drink, and hearing her laughter, he quickly looked over at the east room.

"No one," she stopped laughing, but her face still held a smile. "The old man went to celebrate Auntie's birthday, and will be away two or three days. Auntie lives in Nanyuan." As she spoke, she filled his cup again.

On hearing this, his thoughts took a turn—something seemed off. At the same time, he was reluctant to leave; her face was so close to his, her clothes so clean and smooth, her lips so red, all gave him a new stimulus. She was still just as old and ugly, but she now had added energy, as if she’d suddenly become someone else; still herself, but with something more. He didn't dare think deeply about this something new, nor did he dare accept it easily, but he also couldn't bear to refuse. His face reddened. Almost as if to boost his courage, he took another sip of wine. He had intended to share his grievances, and now he forgot about that. Flushing, he couldn't help but glance at her a few more times. The more he looked, the more unsettled he felt, and the more she revealed that mysterious something he couldn't understand, a growing heat and power emanating from her, turning her into an abstraction of something else. He warned himself to be cautious; but he also wanted to be bold. He drank three more cups, forgetting what caution was. Blankly looking at her, he did not know why, but he felt particularly happy, bold; extremely eager to get hold of a new experience and happiness at once. Usually, he was somewhat afraid of her; now, she wasn't frightening at all. He himself seemed to become the one with authority and power, able to treat her like a cat in his hands. The lights went out inside. The sky was very dark. Now and then, one or two stars streaked across the Milky Way, or shot into the darkness, leaving red or white glowing tails, light or stiff, plummeting straight down or sweeping across, sometimes twitching and quivering, adding movements of warmth and flashes to the night sky. Occasionally a few stars would shoot down at the same time, making the still autumn sky tremble, making all the stars swirl in confusion. Sometimes a solitary giant star would pierce the edge of the sky, its tail extremely long, radiating starry flowers; red, turning yellow; in its final thrust, suddenly with wild joy, lighting up a stripe at the corner of the heavens, as if tearing open the layered darkness and letting in some milky light to linger. When the afterglow faded, the darkness would seem to flicker then close up again, the quietly lazy stars smiling once more in the autumn wind. On the ground, autumn fireflies in search of mates played their star-like games.

The next morning, Xiangzi got up very early and went out with his rickshaw. His head and throat hurt a bit—this was his first time drinking, but he didn't pay attention to it. Sitting at the entrance of a small alley, the cool morning breeze blowing his head, he knew the headache would soon pass. But something else was weighing on his mind, and for the moment he couldn't find a way to escape it. What happened last night made him confused, ashamed, uncomfortable, and even felt a bit dangerous.

He didn’t understand what was up with Miss Hu. She hadn't been a virgin for a while—he'd just found that out a few hours before. He had always respected her, and had never heard any gossip about her; while she was always easygoing and forthright with everyone, no one talked about her behind her back; even those coachmen who spoke ill of her only called her tough, nothing more. Then how did last night's incident come about?

It was all so baffling, and Xiangzi began to doubt the events of last night. She knew he wasn’t at the rickshaw yard, so how could she be waiting just for him? If she would do that with just anyone… Xiangzi lowered his head. He came from the countryside, and although he had never given much thought to marriage, he wasn't without a plan: if he had his own rickshaw, life was a bit easier, and if he wanted to marry, he would stick to choosing a young, strong, hard-working country girl who could handle housework. Young men his age—even if under control—who among them didn't sneak off to the "White House"? Xiangzi always refused to go along. First, he saw himself as a tough, independent guy who wouldn't waste money on women; second, he’d witnessed those foolish guys—some just eighteen or nineteen—who’d wasted so much money they couldn’t even pee against the wall in the toilets anymore. In the end, he had to keep himself proper, so one day when he married, his wife would be clean and pure, and so he himself had to be the same. But now, now… Thinking about Miss Hu, as a friend, she was fine; as a woman, she was ugly, old, tough, shameless! Just the thought of the soldiers who robbed his rickshaw and nearly killed him wasn’t as detestable as thinking of her! She’d completely destroyed the bit of clarity he brought from the countryside—he'd now become a man who stole women!

Besides, if this thing got out and Liu Si found out? Did Liu Si know his daughter was spoiled goods? If he didn’t, wouldn’t Xiangzi be taking all the blame alone? If he already knew but wouldn't restrain his daughter, then what kind of people were they? If he got mixed up with them, what did that make him? Even if both father and daughter agreed, he couldn’t accept her; regardless whether old Liu had sixty rickshaws or six hundred or six thousand! He needed to leave Renhe Depot at once and cut ties with them for good. Xiangzi had his own skills—by his own merits he would buy his rickshaw, marry his wife; only that would be truly aboveboard! Thinking of this, he lifted his head, feeling he was a real man—nothing to fear, nothing to worry about. As long as he worked hard, he would surely succeed.

He turned down two rides but didn't manage to get a fare. That uncomfortable feeling suddenly flared up again. He didn't want to think anymore, but he felt stifled inside. This matter seemed different from everything else—even if there was a solution, it wouldn’t be easy to forget. Not only did it feel as if something had stuck to his body, but as if a dark stain had been left in his heart that could never be cleansed. No matter how much resentment or dislike he felt towards her, she seemed to have gripped his heart— the less he wanted to think about her, the more she’d suddenly leap into his mind: a naked version of her, giving everything, both her ugliness and beauty, to him all at once, like buying a pile of junk, with a couple of shiny, colored bits tempting you to look closer, impossible to refuse. He had never been so close with another person; though it was sudden, though it was a setup, still, this kind of relationship wasn't so easily forgotten. Even if he wanted to set it aside, it would wind and root itself in his heart. This wasn't just an experience for him—it was an indefinable disturbance, leaving him totally at a loss. As for her, for himself, for now and for the future, he had no way out, like a bug caught in a spider's web, struggling too late.

In a daze, he managed to pick up a few fares. Even while running with the rickshaw, he couldn't stop thinking about it—not clearly or in order, but with flashes of impressions, or lingering feelings, or vague emotions; all indistinct but close to the heart. He really wanted to go off and drink himself unconscious, only that might bring some relief—anything to end this torment! But he didn’t dare. He couldn't ruin himself over this. He remembered his plan to buy a rickshaw, but couldn't focus; something kept blocking his thoughts—before he could get his mind on the rickshaw, this thing would sneak out and hog his attention, like a black cloud blocking the sun, breaking up any light. At night, when closing after the day's work, he felt even worse. He had to return to Renhe Depot, but was truly afraid to go back. What if he ran into her—what then? He wandered the streets with his empty rickshaw, circling around the depot two or three times, each time getting close then deliberately turning away, like a kid skipping school afraid to go home. The strange thing was, the more he tried to avoid her, the more he wanted to run into her, and the later it got, the stronger this urge became. It was a reckless and perplexing feeling he knew was unwise, but was eager to try, like as a boy poking a wasp's nest with a stick—afraid but compelled, as if possessed. He felt as if some force even greater than himself was rolling him into a ball and tossing him into a fire; he couldn't stop himself advancing.

He circled back to Xianmen Gate, and this time didn’t want to hesitate anymore, ready to go straight in and find her. She was no longer anyone in particular—just a woman. His whole body grew hot. Just as he walked up to the entrance, a man in his forties came by under the lamplight—he seemed to recognize the face and demeanor, but didn't dare call out. Almost instinctively, he said, "Rickshaw?" The man paused, "Xiangzi?" "Yes," Xiangzi smiled. "Mr. Cao?"

Mr. Cao smiled and nodded. "I say, Xiangzi, if you’re not working at the big house anymore, will you come work for me again? The man I'm using now is too lazy, doesn’t keep the rickshaw clean, even if he is fast." "Will you come?"

"Of course, sir!" Xiangzi seemed to have even forgotten how to smile, wiping his face with a little towel the whole time. "Sir, when do I start?"

“Well,” Mr. Cao thought for a bit, “the day after tomorrow.”

"Alright, sir!" Xiangzi also considered it: "Sir, should I take you home?" "No need. I went to Shanghai for a while and since coming back I haven't been living at the old place. I’m on Beichang Street now; just out for a walk. See you the day after tomorrow." Mr. Cao told Xiangzi the house number, and added, "Still using my own rickshaw."

Xiangzi was so happy he felt like flying; all his days of suffering were suddenly swept away, like a stone road washed clean by heavy rain. Mr. Cao was his old employer, and although they hadn’t worked together for long, the relationship was excellent. Mr. Cao was a very friendly man, and his household was small, just a wife and a little boy. Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw all the way back to Renhe depot. The light was still on in Miss Hu's room. Seeing that light, Xiangzi suddenly froze there.

He stood for a long time, then decided to go in and see her, tell her he'd found a new monthly contract; pay off these last couple days of rickshaw rent; take out his savings; and finally cut things off for good—though of course he couldn't say it outright, she’d surely understand.

He went in and put away the rickshaw first, then returned, summoning all his courage, to call out, "Miss Liu." "Come in!"He pushed the door open. She was lying sideways on the bed, wearing her usual clothes, barefoot. Still lying sideways, she said, "So? You tasted some sweetness and now that's it?"

Xiangzi's face turned as red as the eggs given away when a child is born. After a long pause, he said haltingly, "I've found a new job, starting the day after tomorrow. The boss has his own rickshaw..."

She cut him off, "You just don't know what's good for you!" She sat up, half-smiling, half-annoyed, pointing at him, "There's food here for you, clothes for you; you just can't get enough unless you go out and sweat, is that it? The old man can't control me, I can't be a widow for my daughter my whole life! Even if the old man really acts stubborn, I've got a couple of private savings myself. The two of us could get two or three rickshaws and make a buck or eighty cents a day—wouldn't that be better than you running all over the streets with aching legs every day? What's wrong with me? Besides being a little older than you, but not by that much! I can look after you, love you!"

"I want to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi couldn't find any other argument.

"Typical blockhead! Sit down first, I won't bite you!" She finished, smiled, and flashed a pair of canine teeth.

Xiangzi sat down, veins bulging. "Where's my money?"

"The old man has it; it won't get lost, don't worry; just don't ask him for it. Do you know his temper? There's enough to buy a rickshaw, you'll get it, not a cent less; if you ask now, he'd curse the life out of you! He's good to you! It won't get lost, and if he shorts you one, I'll pay you two! You country bumpkin! Don't make me roast you!"

Xiangzi had nothing more to say. He kept his head down and after a while pulled out the rickshaw rental for two days, and put it on the table: "Two days' worth." Then he remembered, "I’m returning the rickshaw today, and I'm taking a break tomorrow." In his heart, he didn't want to rest for a day at all; but this way, it looked decisive; after returning the rickshaw, he would never live at Ren He Factory again.

The Tiger Girl came over, grabbed the money, and shoved it back into his pocket, "Both the rickshaw and you are free for these two days! You're in luck, kid! Just don't turn your back on those who help you, that's all!" After she finished, she spun around and locked the door behind her.

[1]

The "zhu'er" here refers to a person, specifically the rickshaw owner.

[2]

"Chui" means it's blown, it's over.

[3]

"Bai Fangzi" means the lowest-grade brothel.

[4]

"Ma li" means fast.

Seven  

Xiangzi went up to the Cao residence.  

He felt a bit ashamed about Miss Hu. But since it all started with her seduction, and since he didn’t want to take advantage of her money anyway, he thought that making a clean break with her from now on wouldn’t really make him too much in the wrong. What he was actually uneasy about was the money Master Liu still held. If he immediately went to get it back, the old man might become suspicious. If he simply never went to see the old man and his daughter again, maybe Miss Hu would get angry and say something bad about him to her father, and that money would be “wasted for nothing.” But if he kept letting the old man save money on his behalf, as soon as he arrived at the depot, he’d run into her again, which would be real awkward. He couldn’t think of a good solution; the more he couldn’t find one, the more uneasy he felt.  

He really wanted to ask Mr. Cao for advice, but how could he speak about it? That incident with Miss Hu wasn’t something he could tell anybody. Thinking about this, he really regretted it. He was starting to understand that this was something you couldn’t just cut off cleanly; something you could never really wash away, like a dark scar on the flesh. He’d lost his rickshaw for no reason, and out of nowhere there was this new entanglement—he felt like his life was probably finished like this, no matter how tough he tried to be, it seemed it was all for nothing. No matter how he turned it over in his head, he saw just one thing: in the end, he’d probably have to swallow his pride and go back to Miss Hu. If not for her, then for those rickshaws—how could he not? “Even the most wretched have to get their share!” He couldn’t stand it, but when the time came, it might be the only choice! He’d just have to keep going forward, do his best, and wait for the worst; he didn’t dare to be as self-confident as before. His height, his strength, his character—none of it counted for anything; his life belonged to himself, but it was under other people’s control; controlled by the worst kind of people.  

By rights, he ought to be very happy, because the Cao household was, among all the places he’d worked, the most pleasant. The wages weren’t higher than anywhere else, aside from a bit of extra for the three main festivals, there weren’t many little tips, but Mr. and Mrs. Cao were both very kind and always treated everyone with respect. Xiangzi wanted to earn more money, to work as hard as possible, but he also wanted a place to stay and enough to eat. The Cao house was spotlessly clean, even the servants’ quarters, and the food was good—never the kind of slop given to servants elsewhere. He had a spacious room to himself and could eat his three meals in peace—and since the masters were so courteous, even Xiangzi didn’t care only about money anymore. Besides, the living situation was comfortable, the work wasn’t hard, and keeping in good health was worthwhile. If he fed himself out of his own pocket, he’d never eat so well; now there was good food laid out for him, and since eating wouldn’t just run straight through him, why shouldn’t he eat his fill? Food cost money, after all, and he understood that clearly. And even though the Cao family didn’t gamble or entertain much and weren’t flush with extra cash, any little odds and ends could bring him a dime or two. If Mrs. Cao sent him to buy medicine for the child, she’d always give him ten cents extra and tell him to take a rickshaw, even knowing he could outrun anyone. That bit of money wasn’t much, but it let him feel a kind of connection, a kind of understanding, made him feel good inside. Xiangzi had seen quite a few employers by now; nine out of ten would delay his wages if they could, making it clear they’d rather get his work for free, and treating servants like cats or dogs—sometimes worse. The Cao family was the exception, and that’s why he liked it there. He would tidy the yard and water the flowers without waiting for orders, and whenever they saw him doing it, they’d always say kind words. And especially in a time like this, they’d find some used-up odds and ends for him to exchange for matches. Even though those things could still be used, he’d just keep them for himself. Here, he felt a touch of humanity.  

In Xiangzi’s eyes, Master Liu could rank with Huang Tianba. Though he could be tough, he cared about appearances and keeping his word, never all bad. The honorable people in his mind were just Huang Tianba and Confucius. He really had no idea what sort of person Confucius was, but it was said he knew many words and was very reasonable. In the places he’d been, there were both literary and martial types; among the martial, none could match Master Liu, and among the literary, even though there were teachers at college or clerks at the yamen who knew plenty of words, he’d never met one who was truly reasonable. Even if the gentleman was reasonable, the ladies of the house were still impossible to please. Only Mr. Cao was both literate and reasonable, and Mrs. Cao also won people over by being proper and well-behaved. So Mr. Cao must be like Confucius; if Xiangzi couldn’t remember what Confucius looked like, then he must look like Mr. Cao, regardless of whether Confucius would want it or not.  

Actually, Mr. Cao wasn’t extremely distinguished. He was a middling sort who did some teaching and various other things. He claimed to be a “socialist,” but was also an aesthete, somewhat influenced by William Morris. Politically and artistically, he didn’t have any profound or original insights; but he had one strength: whatever little he did believe in, he always managed to put into practice in the small events of his life—he seemed to realize that he didn’t have the talent to do great or earth-shaking things, so he arranged his work and home life according to his own ideals; maybe it didn’t do much for society, but at least he could strive to practice what he preached and not be a hypocrite. So, he paid attention to every little thing, almost as if saying that as long as the tiny home was kept well, the rest of society could be however it was. Sometimes this made him feel ashamed, sometimes a bit pleased, as if he saw plainly that his home was like a little oasis in the desert, supplying whoever arrived with some clear water and food, but nothing more.  

Xiangzi had just arrived at this little oasis; after so many days in the desert, it seemed a miracle. He’d never met anyone like Mr. Cao before, so he viewed him as a sage. Maybe this was because Xiangzi lacked experience, or maybe such people really were rare. When he pulled Mr. Cao outside, Mr. Cao’s clothes were always so plain and elegant, his personality lively and gracious, and Xiangzi himself was clean, neat, tall, and strong—he felt even happier pulling Mr. Cao’s rickshaw, as if he were the only one fit to do so. At home, everything was always so clean and always so peaceful, it made him feel comfortable and at ease. When he was in the country, he often saw old men sitting in silence in the winter sun or in the moonlight, holding bamboo pipes, saying nothing at all; although he was still young then and couldn’t imitate them, he loved seeing them sit so quietly, imagining—he guessed—they must have some kind of feeling from it. Now, though he was in the city, the quiet peace of the Cao home was enough to remind him of the countryside. He really wished he could smoke a pipe and reflect on things for a while.  

Unfortunately, that woman and that bit of money kept him from easing his mind; his heart was like a green leaf, wound up by the silk of a worm preparing to make a cocoon. Because of these matters, he couldn’t relax; toward others, even toward Mr. Cao, he was always distracted and answered things wrongly. This made him very upset. The Cao household went to bed early, business was done after just past nine each night, and then he’d sit alone in his room or the yard, tossing things over and over in his mind, thinking about just those two things. He even thought about getting married right away; that would surely break Hu Niu’s hold on him. But how could he support a family just by pulling a rickshaw? He knew how the poor guys lived in the crowded courtyard houses: the men pulled rickshaws, the women sewed, the kids gathered coal scraps, eating watermelon rinds off dirt piles in summer, and rushing for porridge kitchens all winter. Xiangzi couldn’t take that. Besides, if he got married, the money held by Old Liu would almost certainly be lost; how could Hu Niu be willing to let him off so easily! He couldn’t give up that bit of money—it was his lifeblood!  

He’d bought his rickshaw at the start of last autumn. It had been over a year; now, he had nothing left except the thirty yuan or so he couldn’t get back, and a load of trouble! The more he thought, the more unhappy he felt.  

It had been more than ten days since the Mid-Autumn Festival. The weather was growing cooler. He calculated that he’d need a couple more things to wear. More money! If he bought clothes, he couldn’t keep enough money to buy a rickshaw again—he hardly dared to hope for that anymore! Even if he kept working month after month, what would his life amount to?  

One evening, Mr. Cao was coming back late from the east side of the city. Xiangzi, to be careful, took the whole road past Tiananmen. The broad, smooth road was nearly empty, the cool breeze blowing, the streetlamps quietly lit; he ran at full strength. For a while, he forgot all the gloom bottled up in his heart, listening to his own footsteps, the soft creak of the rickshaw bow, everything else faded away. Unbuttoning his collar, feeling the cool wind whistling against his chest, he felt great—if only he could run like this, on and on, who knew where, running until he died, it might just be a relief. The faster he ran, the better he felt; up ahead was another rickshaw, and he “overtook” it, soon passing Tiananmen Gate. His legs felt like two springs, barely touching the ground before bouncing up again; the wheels were spinning so fast they were just a blur, the rubber seemed to have left the road altogether. The man and rickshaw felt like they were being swept along by a gale. Mr. Cao, tickled by the wind, was half dozing; otherwise, he’d surely have stopped Xiangzi from racing so fast. Xiangzi let himself go, thinking vaguely that after sweating like this, he’d sleep well tonight and wouldn’t have a care.  

They were not far from Beichang Street, the north side of the road was covered in deep shadow by the Chinese scholar trees outside the red wall. Just as Xiangzi meant to slow down, his foot struck something. Wherever his foot went, the wheels followed. Xiangzi tumbled forward. Crack! The rickshaw handle snapped. “What happened?” Mr. Cao tumbled out with the words. Xiangzi said nothing, just picked himself up off the ground. Mr. Cao sat up nimbly. “What happened?”  

It was a pile of new paving stones, but no red lantern had been placed beside them.  

“Did you get hurt?” Xiangzi asked.  

“No. I’ll just walk back, you pull the rickshaw.” Mr. Cao was still composed, feeling around the stones to see if he’d dropped anything.  

Xiangzi touched the broken rickshaw handle. “It’s not broken too badly, sir can still ride, I can pull!” he said, yanking the rickshaw from the stones. “Please get on, sir!”  

Mr. Cao didn’t really want to get back on, but hearing the edge of tears in Xiangzi’s voice, he relented and climbed up.  

When they reached the streetlamp at Beichang Street intersection, Mr. Cao saw the skin on his right hand had been scraped off in a patch. “Xiangzi, stop!”  

Xiangzi turned around; his face was covered in blood.  

Mr. Cao was frightened, momentarily at a loss for words. “Quick, you, quick—”  

Xiangzi was confused, thinking Mr. Cao meant for him to hurry home, so he bent low and rushed all the way back.  

When he put down the rickshaw, he saw blood on Mr. Cao’s hand and hurried into the courtyard to ask the mistress for medicine.  

“Never mind me, you’d better see to yourself first!” Mr. Cao ran in. Xiangzi looked himself over and finally felt the pain: both knees, his right elbow, all scraped; his face—what he’d thought was sweat was really blood. Not caring what to do next, or think, he sat down on the stone step of the entryway, staring blankly at the broken rickshaw handle. The shiny, black-painted rickshaw had a chunk broken off its handle, leaving two bare bits of wood sticking out—jarring, ugly, like a paper figurine missing its feet, two broom-straws poking clumsily out. Xiangzi stared blankly at the two bare wood bits. “Xiangzi!” The Cao family’s maid, Gao Ma, called out loudly, “Xiangzi! Where are you?”  

He kept sitting, eyes fixed on the broken handle; those two bits of wood stuck in his heart.  

“What are you hiding for! Not a sound, sitting here—you gave me a fright! Sir is calling you!” Gao Ma always mixed practical matters with emotion, making her words both complicated and touching. She was a widow of about thirty-two or three: clean, straightforward, quick and careful in her work. Elsewhere, people found her too outspoken, too opinionated, a bit superstitious. But the Caos liked bright, clean people, and weren’t picky about small faults, so she’d been with them for two or three years—even when the whole family moved, they took her along. “Sir is calling you!” she repeated. When Xiangzi stood up, she saw the blood on his face: “That scared me half to death, my word! What happened to you? Get moving, quick! You’ll get lockjaw like this! Go on! Sir has medicine!”  

Xiangzi went ahead, Gao Ma chattering behind, and they entered the study together. Mrs. Cao was there too, bandaging Mr. Cao’s hand; when she saw Xiangzi come in, she gasped.  

“Madam, he took quite a fall this time,” Gao Ma made sure the mistress saw, hurriedly pouring cool water into a basin, talking even faster, “I always knew, once he started running he’d lose his head, sooner or later something would happen. And that’s exactly what happened! Aren’t you going to wash it? Wash and get some medicine, come on!”  

Xiangzi cradled his injured elbow, motionless. The study was so neat and refined, and he, a big man with a bloodstained face, looked all wrong in it. Everyone seemed to feel something was off—even Gao Ma fell silent.  

“Sir!” Xiangzi lowered his head, spoke softly but with strength. “Sir, please find someone else. This month’s wages, you keep them for repairs: the rickshaw handle’s broken, the left lamp’s glass is smashed, though the rest is all right.”  

“Get yourself cleaned up and some medicine on first—then we can talk,” Mr. Cao said, watching his own hand as Mrs. Cao carefully wound the gauze.“Wash up first!” Gao Ma suddenly remembered to speak again. “The master didn’t say anything to you, don’t turn the blame on him first!”

Xiangzi still didn’t move. “No need to wash, it’ll be fine in a bit! A monthly rickshaw puller, who knocks someone over, crashes the cart, can’t show their face anymore…” He couldn’t finish saying what he meant, but all his feelings had drained out, and he was almost about to break down in tears. To quit and forfeit his wages was almost the same as suicide in Xiangzi’s eyes. But responsibility and reputation, at this moment, seemed even more important than life, because the person he had hurt wasn’t just anyone, but Mr. Cao. If he had knocked over that Mrs. Yang—well, so be it, she deserved it! With Mrs. Yang, he could use the same brashness as on the street, because she didn’t treat him like a person, so he had no need to be polite; money was everything, nothing to do with reputation or rules. But Mr. Cao wasn’t that kind of person—he had to give up the money to preserve his reputation. He had no one to resent but his fate, and almost thought: once he left the Cao family, he’d never pull a rickshaw again; even if his own life was worthless, he could risk it, but what about others’ lives? What if he actually killed someone, then what? He’d never thought of this before, but this time, having injured Mr. Cao, he suddenly understood. Fine, he could give up his wages, change professions, and never do this deadly business again. Pulling a rickshaw was his ideal job—giving it up meant giving up hope. He felt his life would be wretched and hopeless, and he wouldn’t even have the hope of being a good rickshaw puller, all that tall stature for nothing! When he pulled casual trips on the street, he shamelessly “snatched”[2]

 business, and got mocked, but he could forgive his shamelessness because he was ambitious and wanted to buy his own cart. But now he’d caused trouble as a monthly hire, what else was there to say? If people found out, Xiangzi had knocked someone over, damaged a cart—what kind of monthly rickshaw puller was that, what a joke! Xiangzi was finished! He couldn’t wait for Mr. Cao to fire him, he might as well go himself!

“Xiangzi,” Mr. Cao’s hand had been bandaged, “go wash up! No need to talk about quitting. It wasn’t your fault, there should’ve been a red lamp at the stone pile. Forget it, wash up and put on some ointment.”

“Yes, sir,” Gao Ma chimed in again, “Xiangzi is just feeling awkward; after all, pulling the master into this state! But since the master says it’s not your fault, you don’t need to fuss anymore! Look at him, big and strong as an ox, still like a kid, really anxious! Madam, say something to put him at ease!” Gao Ma’s words sounded like a gramophone record, going around in circles, including everyone, but with no trace of any transitions.

“Hurry and wash up, I’m scared!” Madam Cao only said this one sentence.

Xiangzi’s mind was in turmoil, but when he heard Madam Cao say she was afraid of blood, he seemed to have found a way to comfort her. He brought out the basin and washed up a few times outside the study door. Gao Ma stood inside the door with the medicine bottle, waiting for him.

“Your arm and leg too?” Gao Ma smeared his face with ointment. Xiangzi shook his head, “It’s nothing!”

The Cao couple went to rest. Gao Ma took the medicine bottle and followed Xiangzi out. In his room, she put the bottle down at the door: “Later you put it on yourself. Listen, there’s no need to be so hung up over this little thing. Back then, when my old husband was alive, I often quit my jobs too. First, I’d suffer outside, and he wasn’t ambitious, which made me angry. Second, young and impatient, if words didn’t match, I’d just leave! We work for our living, not as slaves; they've got their smelly money but I’m not a pushover; if an old lady can’t serve, she just can’t! Things are better now, since he died, I’ve got nothing to worry about, my temper has improved a bit. Here—I’ve been here almost three years now; that’s right, started work on Double Ninth Festival—the little pay is very little, but they're good people. We sell labor for money; just talking about how good things are doesn’t count for much. But then again, if you're looking at the long run, there's sense in it: with odd jobs here and there, you spend half the year out of work, no good; better to stick with a nice boss, even if the pay is low, in time you still save some money. Today’s matter, since the master didn’t say anything, let it go, let it go, why bother. Not to act big, you’re still a young brother, easy to get fired up. No need for that, hot tempers don’t feed you. Honest types like you, steadily sticking with it here is better than running all over the place. I’m not just speaking up for them, I mean it for you, we all get along!” She caught her breath. “Alright, see you tomorrow. Don't be so stubborn, I speak my mind, straight talk!”

Xiangzi’s right elbow hurt and he couldn’t sleep all night. Turning it over and over in his mind, he felt Gao Ma’s words made sense. Everything else was fake, only money was real. Save money to buy a cart; getting angry won’t feed you! Thinking this, he finally got a bit of restful sleep.

[1]

 Festivals, details, small rules.

[2]

 Grabbing someone else’s fare in progress is called “snatching.”

Study resource sharing

Mr. Cao fixed the cart, and didn’t dock Xiangzi’s wages. Mrs. Cao gave him two pills of “Three Yellow Precious Wax,” but he didn’t take them. He didn’t mention quitting again. Though for several days he still felt awkward, Gao Ma’s words ultimately prevailed. After a while, life went back to normal; he gradually forgot the incident, and hope sprouted again. Alone in his room, his eyes shone as he calculated how to save money, how to buy a cart; muttering to himself like he had a mental burden. His math skills weren’t great, but in his mind and out loud he’d repeat “six six thirty-six”; this wasn’t really related to his money, but saying it made him feel fuller inside, almost like he kept a ledger.

He admired Gao Ma quite a bit, feeling this woman had even more acumen and ability than most men, her advice was always right on target. He didn’t dare go chat idly with her, but when they met in the yard or at the entrance, and if she had the time, he loved to hear her talk. Every time she gave her spiel, it would be enough for him to ponder for half a day, so whenever he saw her he’d smile sheepishly, letting her know he admired her; she seemed to enjoy this, and even when busy, she’d find a few words for him.

However, when it came to handling money, he didn’t dare just blindly follow her ideas. Her ideas, to him, weren’t bad, but were a little risky. He liked to hear her methods, learned some tricks, and felt broad-minded; but when it came to action, he stuck to his old way—not letting go of money lightly.

Indeed, Gao Ma did have her ways: ever since being widowed, she’d lend out whatever little she could save each month, a yuan here, two there, all counted as loans to servants, second- or third-class policemen, and small vendors, at a minimum interest of three percent. These people would get red-eyed with worry over a single yuan, and even if someone lent them one and wanted two back, they’d still take the money. Otherwise, money wouldn’t come their way; the cash they could see was toxic, it would drain their blood the moment they touched it, but they had no choice but to take it. Anything that let them catch their breath, they dared to grab; life meant struggling to breathe a little longer each day, tomorrow was for tomorrow. Gao Ma, back when her husband was alive, had suffered this poison herself; he came to her drunk, refusing to leave without a yuan; if she didn’t have it, he’d make a drunken scene outside the gate. She had no choice, no matter how high the interest, she had to beg, borrow, or scrape up that yuan at once. Through such experience, she learned this way of doing things, not out of revenge, but taking it as reasonable, almost as a form of emergency charity. Whoever desperately needed money, whoever was willing to lend—it was like Zhou Yu dealing with Huang Gai, both were willing!

In principle, if she didn’t think there was anything wrong with it, then as for her methods she had to be tough, couldn’t just throw money around carelessly; business is business. It took keen eyes, some skill, caution, and nerve, so as not to let all the eagles loose[1]

 . She spent no less effort than a bank manager, maybe even more, because she had to be extra careful. Whether much or little, the idea was the same, for this was a capitalist society, like a giant sieve, slowly sifting money from above downward—the lower you go, the less there is; and at the same time, the “ideal” was being sifted downward too, but there’s always more at the bottom, since ideals don’t fear small sieve holes like money does; ideals are invisible, they slip through any tiny opening. Everyone said Gao Ma was tough, she admitted it as well; her strength came from grinding through hardship. Whenever she thought of her past suffering, and the heartlessness from her own husband, she gritted her teeth. She could be very friendly or very ruthless; she knew that’s what it took to survive in this world.

She also advised Xiangzi to lend out his money, out of real kindness, and if he’d wanted to, she would’ve helped: “Let me tell you, Xiangzi, if you keep it in your pocket, a yuan will always be a yuan! If you put it out, money grows money! That’s right, what do we have eyes for? Watch carefully before you lend, don’t lend to a lame duck. If a policeman doesn’t pay interest or return the principal, go straight to their precinct chief! Simple as that, his job won’t last, believe it! Find out exactly when they get paid and collect on the spot; if they don’t pay, just move on! Lend one for ten, whoever you lend to, know their situation; don’t just throw your money into the sea, okay? Trust me, you can’t go wrong!”

Xiangzi didn’t need to say anything; his expression alone showed how much he admired Gao Ma. But when he counted it over for himself, he felt money was safest in his own hands. Sure, this way money wouldn’t grow, but it couldn’t get lost either. Taking out his leftover few yuan from the last few months—they were all silver dollars—he’d gently flip them one by one, careful not to let them clink; the silver was so bright, solid, so eye-catching—it felt impossible to let it go, except maybe to buy a cart. Everyone had their own way, and he couldn’t just follow Gao Ma entirely.

Earlier, he’d worked for a family named Fang, where every member, even the servants, had a post office savings book. Mrs. Fang also encouraged Xiangzi: “You can open a deposit with just one yuan, why not open one? Like the old saying, when you have money think of the times when you won’t; don’t wait until you’ve got nothing and hope for something. You’re young, not saving while you can, there’ll be rainy days ahead in a year’s 360 days! It’s easy, secure, earns interest, and you can take money out when you want. What could be more convenient? Go get a form, if you can’t write, I’ll fill it for you, I mean it for your own good!”

Xiangzi knew she meant well, and that the cook, Wang Liu, and nanny Qin Ma both had savings books, he really wanted to try it. But one day Miss Fang asked him to go deposit ten yuan and he looked closely at the little booklet, saw a few characters and a red stamp; altogether, it weighed no more than a small stack of toilet paper. Handing over cash, the clerk just wrote a few marks and stamped it, that was it. He felt if that wasn’t a scam, it had to be; putting all that bright silver in just for a few marks on paper? Xiangzi wasn’t buying it. He suspected the Fang family made a deal with the post office—he always thought the post office must be a big chain, its business as old as Rui Fu Xiang or Hong Ji—so they pushed their business on him. Even if it wasn’t, cash was always better than a little booklet with a few words on it! The money in a savings book was just characters!

He knew banks and silver shops only as places you’d go for a “fare,” and if the police didn’t chase rickshaws away from there, you could surely pick up business. As for what went on inside, he couldn’t guess. Surely, there was a lot of money there; but why all the fuss about doing money business there, he didn’t get it; and since it had nothing to do with him, he didn’t bother thinking about it. There were lots of things in the city he didn’t understand, and listening to friends argue at tea houses only confused him more, because everyone said something different and nobody got it quite right. He didn’t want to keep listening, or keep thinking about it; he knew, if you were ever going to rob somewhere, hitting a bank was best; but since he didn’t want to be a bandit, just holding on to his own money was enough, no need to think about anything else. To him, that was the wisest way.

Gao Ma knew he was saving up for a cart, so she had more advice: “Xiangzi, I know you don’t want to lend, so you can buy your own cart sooner, and that’s smart! If I were a man, and if I were pulling rickshaws, I’d want my own cart too; be your own boss, do your own work, don’t rely on anyone! If I could do that, I wouldn’t swap it for being a magistrate! Pulling rickshaw is a tough job, but if I were a man, with some strength, I’d stick to pulling rickshaw rather than being a policeman; working all year round, always standing on the street,Only making that meager money a month, no side income, no freedom; either grow a beard and boast or just keep talking big, but there’s not the slightest improvement. I’m telling you, right, if you want to buy yourself a car as soon as possible, I have a great idea for you: start a shared fund, ten people, at most twenty, each person chips in two yuan a month, you take the first turn; wouldn’t you have about forty yuan right away? At the very least, you’ll have some savings—scrape it together and you could get yourself a rickshaw right away and just go into business! Once you get the car, you start another ‘black-number’ round, zero interest, plus it’s a respectable thing, I guarantee this will be the answer you’re looking for! If you really want to start a fund, I’ll join—definitely won’t let you down! How about it?”

This really got Xiangzi’s heart beating a little faster! If he could somehow put together thirty or forty yuan more, add that to the thirty-plus Liu Siye is holding for him, and what little he currently had—wouldn’t that make about eighty in total? Not quite enough for a brand new rickshaw, but surely enough for one in eighty-percent new condition! Plus, by doing this, he could ask Liu Siye to return his money, instead of leaving it hanging, which just feels off. So, eighty-percent new will have to do; as long as it works, he can always get a new one later when there’s more money.

But, where could he find twenty people for the fund? Even if he could gather them, it’s a matter of face—if he starts a fund now for his own urgent need, others may later ask him to join theirs! In these lean times, it’s common for these funds to collapse without warning! A real man relies on himself; better just to earn the money himself to buy the rickshaw—don’t ask others!

Seeing Xiangzi unresponsive, Grandma Gao wanted to tease him, but thinking of his sincere nature, she felt it was awkward: “You really are something! ‘Like herding pigs down a narrow alley—always straight ahead’; that’s fine too!”

Xiangzi didn’t say anything. After Grandma Gao left, he nodded to himself, as if to admit his own stubbornness was actually admirable, and he felt oddly happy inside.

It was already early winter, and now, besides the cries of “candied chestnuts!” and “peanuts!” in the alley at night, there was the low, mournful call of the “night pot.” The night pot seller carried a ceramic covered pot, and Xiangzi bought a big one. On his first purchase, the seller didn’t have small change. An idea popped into Xiangzi’s mind—he thought the smallest green chamber pot looked very interesting—glassy green, with a little spout, “No change needed, I’ll take this one!” Putting down the covered pot, he brought the little green night pot inside: “The young master’s not asleep yet? Here’s a little toy for you!”

Everyone was watching Xiao Wen—Cao family’s little boy—take a bath, and when they saw this, everyone couldn’t help but laugh. Mr. and Mrs. Cao didn’t say anything—perhaps thinking it was a bit silly, but Xiangzi’s good intention should be appreciated—so they smiled in thanks. Grandma Gao, of course, didn’t keep her mouth shut: “Look at that, Xiangzi! You’re a grown man and can come up with such clever ideas; how eye-catching!”

Xiao Wen liked the little pot, and immediately used his hands to scoop bathwater into the small vessel: “This little teapot has a big spout!”

Everyone laughed even harder. Xiangzi straightened up—embarrassed in his pride—then walked out. He felt really happy; this was a brand new feeling for him—seeing everyone smile just at him, as if he were someone important. Smiling, he brought out his few silver coins, and gently, one by one, dropped them into the ceramic pot, telling himself: This is more reliable than anything! When it’s finally full, I’ll smash it against the wall; crack! Real silver is worth far more than pottery bits!

He decided not to ask anyone for help anymore. Even someone as dependable as Liu Siye, sometimes things just feel off; his money wouldn’t get lost with Liu Siye, but he still just couldn’t feel totally at ease. Money is like a ring—always best kept on your own finger. This decision made him feel good, as if his belt had just been tightened another notch, his chest held a little higher and prouder.

The weather was getting colder and colder, but Xiangzi didn’t seem to feel it. With a firm goal in mind, his world seemed brighter; in brightness, there’s no room for cold. The first traces of frost appeared on the ground, even the soil on the footpath had frozen solid, everything looked drier, sturdier, the black earth beginning to faintly yellow, as if all the moisture had disappeared.
