Especially at dawn, there was a thin rim of frost where cart wheels had cut up ridges of soil, the chilly morning breeze dispersing the glow, revealing a sky so high, so blue, so crisp; Xiangzi loved to get up early and run a job, the cold wind whipping up his sleeves, making his whole body shiver as if he’d taken a cold shower—but what fun! Sometimes a wild wind would knock the breath out of him, but he’d put his head down, clench his teeth, and push forward, like a big fish swimming upstream; the harder the wind blew, the harder he resisted, as if battling the wind itself to the death. Suddenly a gust would hit so hard he couldn’t breathe, he’d clamp his mouth shut, and after a while, burst out with a belch, as if he’d just taken a deep dive underwater. After this belch, he’d just keep going, pushing forward, charging ahead, nothing could stop this giant; every muscle was taut, like a green caterpillar under attack by ants, twisting his body to fight back. What a sweat! When he finally put down the cart, straightened his back, let out a long breath and wiped the dust from his lips, he felt unbeatable; watching the wind-stirred dust sweep past, he nodded. The wind bent roadside trees, shredded shop banners, tore wall posters, darkened the sun, singing, calling, roaring, resounding! Sometimes it rushed like a wild monster, tearing through the sky and earth at breakneck speed; sometimes it reeled wildly in all directions, like a demon running amok. Sometimes it swept sideways, ambushing anything on the street, snapping branches, blowing off roof tiles, breaking power lines; but Xiangzi was just watching—he’d just come out of the wind, and it hadn’t gotten the better of him! The victory belonged to Xiangzi! And when the wind was behind him, all he had to do was hold the handle tight—no need to run, the wind would push the wheels for him, like a good friend.

Of course, he wasn’t blind; he saw those old and weak rickshaw pullers too. They wore tattered clothes—thin enough for a light breeze to cut through, a strong wind would rip apart completely; who knows what was wrapped on their feet. At the rickshaw stand, they shivered, eyes darting around like thieves, and whenever someone appeared, they’d rush to ask: “Rickshaw?!” Once they got a fare, they’d warm up, sweat soaking through their thin, ragged clothes. Once they stopped, the sweat on their backs froze solid. When the wind came, they couldn’t take a step, yet had to wrench their rickshaws forward; when the wind hit from above, they’d have to duck their heads to their chests; if from below, their feet lost the ground; if from the front, one slip and the rickshaw would fly off like a kite; if from behind, they had no way to control the cart or themselves. Still, they tried anything, used all their strength, pulled and dragged the rickshaw to the destination—risking their lives for a few coins. After a journey, sweat mixed with dust turned to mud on their faces, leaving only three frozen red circles for eyes and mouth. The days were so short, so cold, the streets nearly deserted; working this hard all day, they might not even earn enough for a full meal; yet the old had wives and children at home, the young, parents and siblings! In winter, they were living in hell—worse off than ghosts, except ghosts didn’t have to suffer so much; ghosts didn’t do this much hard work! Dying like a dog in the street was as close as they could get to peace; it’s said that people who freeze to death, sometimes wear a smile!

How could Xiangzi not see this? But he had no time to worry or ponder over them. Their fate was his too, but he was still young and strong, able to bear hardship—not scared of cold or wind; he had a clean place to sleep at night, neat clothes to wear in the day, so he felt his situation wasn’t really the same as theirs. Even though he was suffering alongside them, the degree wasn’t the same; he was suffering less, and in the future, he could escape this. He thought, when he got old, he’d never end up pulling a battered rickshaw, starving and freezing. He believed that current advantages guaranteed future success. Just like the chauffeurs who would never idle and chat with rickshaw pullers outside restaurants or mansions; chauffeurs thought it beneath them to associate with rickshaw men. The chauffeurs’ attitude toward rickshaw pullers was much like Xiangzi’s to the old and weak: all in hell together, but on different levels. They never thought to unite, everyone went their own way, personal hopes and efforts blinding each to the others, each convinced that with bare hands, they could make a living, groping alone for a future in the darkness. Xiangzi thought of no one but himself, cared about no one but himself, only thinking of his own money and future success.

The city’s beginning to show signs of the New Year. On clear, windless days, the air was dry and cold, but the streets bloomed with color: New Year pictures, silk lanterns, red wax candles, silk head ornaments, various candied snacks—all on display, which gave people a cheerfulness mixed with unease; for while everyone wanted to enjoy the holidays, everyone faced their own worries. There was a new brightness in Xiangzi’s eyes. Seeing the New Year goods along the street, he figured the Cao family would surely be preparing to give gifts; he’d at least get a few jiao for wine. The customary New Year tip was two yuan—not much, but greeting other visitors would also net him a few jiao each time. Added up, it was a tidy sum; no fear in small amounts as long as he kept earning—his ceramic pot wouldn’t betray him! On quiet evenings, he’d stare at his money-eating, never-spitting ceramic friend, muttering softly: “Eat up, eat up, pal! When you’re full at last, I’ll be set!”

The holidays were coming closer—it was already Laba in a flash. Joy or anxiety pushed people to plan and prepare; there were still twenty-four hours in a day, but these days felt different, forcing everyone to be busy with something and focus on the holiday—as if time itself had become conscious and emotional, guiding everyone’s thoughts and actions. Xiangzi stood on the happy side—the lively street, shouting vendors, hope for bonuses and tips, New Year’s day off, dreams of delicious food... all made him feel and look forward like a little kid. He decided, even if it cost eight or nine jiao, he’d buy a gift for Liu Siye. The present itself didn’t matter, but the gesture did: first, to apologize for not visiting lately—he’d been so busy at the house; second, he could use the opportunity to ask for his thirty-plus yuan back. Spending a yuan to recover that lump sum was a great deal. Pleased with the thought, he gently shook his piggy bank and imagined how much heavier and nicer it would sound with that extra thirty-plus added. Yes, as soon as he reclaimed that money, there’d be nothing left to worry about!

One evening, just as he was about to give his treasure pot another shake, Grandma Gao called to him: “Xiangzi! There’s a young lady asking for you at the door; I was just coming in from the street, and she kept on asking for you.” As Xiangzi came out, she added in a low voice: “She’s like a big black tower! Really scary!”

Xiangzi’s face instantly turned as red as if it were wrapped in fire—he knew something was wrong!

[1]

 Letting the hawk go means losing everything.

[2]

 Teasing—means to toy with over and over.

9

Xiangzi could barely bring himself to step over the threshold. His head was spinning, one foot still inside, and by the streetlamp’s glow, he could already see Miss Liu. Her face, likely dusted with powder again, took on a gray-green hue in the light, like frost on dried blackened leaves. Xiangzi didn’t dare look at her directly.

Huniu’s expression was complex: a glimmer of hope in her eyes from seeing him; her mouth was half open, with a hint of cold smile; her nose creased with lines of disdain and impatience; her brows arched, the strange powder on her face giving her a brazen, domineering air. When she saw Xiangzi come out, her lips twisted several times, and as all those expressions struggled for a place, she stood blankly staring at the ground. She swallowed, seemed to suppress her complicated feelings, then pulled out a bit of the bravado she’d inherited from Liu Siye, halfway between anger and a forced smile, pretending not to care as she let out a fake laugh: “Aren’t you something! Like meat buns fed to a dog—gone for good!” Her voice was loud, just as when she quarreled with rickshaw pullers at the depot. As she said this, the smile vanished completely from her face; suddenly she seemed to feel a wave of shame and humiliation, and she bit her lip.

“Don’t shout!” Xiangzi seemed to pour all his strength into those two words, which came out softly but powerfully.

“Hmph! I’m not afraid!” She gave a spiteful laugh, but despite herself, her voice dropped a little. “No wonder you’ve been hiding from me, looks like there’s a little goblin of an old gal here; I always knew you weren’t stupid—don’t be fooled by your big, clumsy looks, all brawn and no brains, pretending to be dumb!” Her voice rose again in pitch. “Don’t shout!” Xiangzi was afraid Grandma Gao might be listening inside. “Don’t shout! Come this way!” he said, turning toward the avenue.

“I’m not afraid to go anywhere; I’m just loud, that’s all!” she protested, but still followed him.

They crossed the street to the east side, next to the park’s red wall. Xiangzi—still remembering the habits of the countryside—squatted down. “What did you come here for?” “Me? Hmph, plenty of reasons!” With her left hand on her hip,His belly was sticking out a bit. Lowering her head, she glanced at him and thought for a moment; it seemed like she was feeling a little kind, taking pity on him: "Xiangzi! I need to talk to you, it's important!"

That gentle, low "Xiangzi" dispersed much of his anger. He lifted his head and looked at her. She still wasn’t lovable in any way, but that "Xiangzi" lingered softly in his heart, carrying tenderness and intimacy. It felt vaguely familiar, as if he'd heard it somewhere before, arousing some undeniable and inextricable feelings. His reply was still quiet, but a bit gentler: "What's the matter?"

"Xiangzi!" She moved a little closer. "I'm pregnant!"

"Pregnant with what?" He was caught off guard for a moment.

"This!" She pointed to her stomach. "You'd better make up your mind!"

Stunned and bewildered, he let out an "ah" and suddenly understood everything. A thousand things he had never considered rushed into his mind, all at once, so many, so urgent, so chaotic, that his mind suddenly became a blank, like when a film reel unexpectedly breaks. The street was very quiet. Some gray clouds covered the moon above. Occasionally, a breeze on the ground stirred the dead branches and leaves. In the distance, a few sharp cat cries. Xiangzi's mind shifted from chaos to blankness; he didn't even hear these sounds. Leaning his hands under his chin, he stared blankly at the ground, as if trying to see it move. He couldn't think of anything, nor did he want to think; he just felt himself getting smaller and smaller, yet unable to sink completely into the earth. His whole existence seemed pinned to this point of discomfort; there was nothing else at all! Only now did he feel the cold, even his lips trembled slightly.

"Don't just crouch there in silence, say something! Get up!" She seemed to feel cold too and wanted to move around a bit.

He numbly got up and followed her north, still unable to find words. His whole body felt stiff, as if he'd just woken up from being frozen.

"Don't you have any idea?" She glanced at Xiangzi, her eyes showing some pity for him.

He had nothing to say.

"On the 27th, for the old man's birthday, you have to come." "Busy, it's the end of the year!" Amidst his tangled thoughts, Xiangzi hadn't forgotten his own affairs. "I know you're the type who only responds to the hard sell, not the soft; talking nicely to you is a waste of breath!" Her voice rose again, the quietness of the street making her words seem especially clear, making Xiangzi feel particularly awkward. "What, you think I'm scared of someone? What's your plan? If you don't want to listen to me, I really don't have time to waste my spit on you! To put it bluntly, I'd just stand at your door and shout insults for three days and nights! Wherever you go, I'll find you! I'm not picking and choosing here!"

"Can you not shout?" Xiangzi stepped away from her.

"Afraid of shouting? Then you shouldn't have been greedy for a bargain in the first place! You got your way, and now you want me to take the blame all alone—you can't even open your dead eyes and look at who I am!"

"Speak slowly, I'm listening!" Xiangzi, who had been feeling very cold, suddenly felt hot from her scolding. The heat seemed to push against his frozen skin, making it itch all over, especially prickly on his scalp.

"This won't work! Don't make things difficult for everyone!" She curled her lips, revealing two canine teeth. "Honestly, I really care about you—don’t not know a good thing! If you play stubborn with me, it'll be bad for you, I'm telling you!"

"No..." Xiangzi wanted to say, "no need for a slap and a rub," but couldn't gather his thoughts; he knew plenty of Beijing-style wisecracks but just couldn't deliver them smoothly; he could understand when others said them, but couldn't say them himself.

"No what?"

"Say what you have to say!"

"I have a good idea," Tigress stopped and faced him: "Look, if you send a matchmaker, the old man definitely won't agree. He's a rickshaw storer, you're a puller—why would he want to lower his standards to marry into your family? I don't care, I like you, isn't liking enough? Who cares about anything else! No matchmaker could get it done for me—if anyone goes to propose, the old man will think you’re after his dozens of rickshaws; even people a cut above you wouldn't do. This thing, I have to handle myself, that's why I picked you—we’ll get it done and tell him after; after all, I'm already pregnant, neither of us can run away! But we can't just go straight in and tell him—the older he gets, the fuzzier his mind gets; if he gets wind of this, he'll just marry a young wife and throw me out. The old man is still strong. Don't mind that he's nearly 70—the way I see it, if he really marries a young woman, I daresay he could still have two or three more kids—believe it or not!" "Let's walk and talk." Xiangzi saw the patrolling policeman coming this way twice already and felt it wasn't right.

"Let's just talk here—who cares!" She followed Xiangzi's gaze and saw the policeman too: "You're not pulling a rickshaw, what are you afraid of? What's the cop going to do, bite off someone's ×× for no reason? That would really be wicked! Let's talk our business! See, here's what I think: On the 27th, the old man's birthday, you go and kowtow to him three times. After New Year's, you go again to wish him well and get on his good side. Once he's happy, we get him some drinks, let him enjoy it to his heart's content. Once he's tipsy, strike while the iron’s hot and ask to be his godson. Afterward, I'll slowly let him find out that I'm not in good health. He'll question me, and I'll just give him the 'Xu Shu enters Cao camp—silent as a stone.' When he gets really anxious, I'll come up with someone, say it was that newly dead Qiao No. 2—the second manager from the rickshaw house east of us. No family, already buried at the public graveyard outside Dongzhimen, where can the old man go to check? If he's out of ideas, we’ll slowly plant the seed—best case, he'll give me to you. Once you’re the godson, you’ll be his son-in-law, not much difference. Just go with the flow, saves everyone from embarrassment. How's my plan?"

Xiangzi said nothing.

Feeling she'd reached a turning point in the conversation, Tigress started walking north, head lowered, as if savoring her own words, but also seemingly giving Xiangzi time to think. At this moment, the wind parted the gray clouds, revealing moonlight, and they had reached the north end of the street. The imperial river had long since frozen solid—still, gray-bright, flat, and firm—supporting the walls of the Forbidden City. There wasn’t a sound inside the Forbidden City; its elegant corner towers, the gilded archways, red-painted city gates, and pavilions atop Jingshan Hill were all as quiet as if listening for some nearly forgotten echo. The breeze drifted by, like a sigh, softly passing through the towers and halls, as if trying to tell a bit of history. Tigress turned west, and Xiangzi followed her to Jin'ao Bridge. There were almost no pedestrians, and the faint moonlight coldly illuminated the two great sheets of ice flanking the bridge. In the distance, the shadowy pavilions cast black silhouettes, still and frozen atop the lake, only the yellow tiles atop them glimmering faintly. The trees stirred slightly, the moonlight appeared dimmer; the White Pagoda rose high upwards, shining white and bleak, casting a chill and desolation over everything. The whole Three Lakes area, in its manmade splendor, revealed the barrenness of the northern land. At the head of the bridge, cold air from both sides of the ice made Xiangzi shiver, and he didn’t want to walk further. Usually, when he pulled his rickshaw over this bridge, all his attention was on his feet for fear of making a mistake, not sparing a glance to either side. Now he was free to take a look, but he felt the scene somewhat frightening: those gray, cold ice sheets, the shifting tree shadows, the stark white pagoda, all stood lonely as if about to scream or suddenly run wild! Even the massive white stone bridge beneath his feet seemed unusually empty, particularly clean, even the streetlights carrying a sense of desolation. He didn’t want to go further, didn’t want to look again, and even less wanted to stay with her; he truly felt like jumping down headfirst, smashing the ice, sinking beneath, frozen like a dead fish.

"See you tomorrow!" he suddenly turned and walked away.

"Xiangzi! That's settled then, see you on the 27th!" she called after Xiangzi's broad, straight back. After speaking, she glanced at the White Pagoda, sighed, and walked west. Xiangzi didn't even look back, as if ghosts were chasing him; he rushed to the round city, walking so fast that he almost ran into the city wall. One hand on the wall, he couldn’t help but want to cry. He stood dazed for a while, then heard from the bridge, "Xiangzi! Xiangzi! Come here! Xiangzi!" It was Tigress’s voice!

He shuffled slowly back towards the bridge. Tigress was standing and looking down, mouth slightly open, "I'm telling you, Xiangzi, come here; for you!" He hadn't moved far before she was in front of him. "Here you go, the thirty-some yuan you saved; there was some change, I rounded it up to a full yuan. For you! Not for anything else, just to show how I feel, that I care for you, think about you, look after you! Nothing more to say—just don't be ungrateful! Take it! Hold it tight, don't blame me if you lose it!"

Xiangzi took the money—a stack of notes—stared at it in a daze, and couldn't find words.

"Alright, see you on the 27th! Not showing up is not an option!" she smiled. "The bargain is yours, you can count your luck as you like!" She turned and walked away.

Clutching the stack of bills, he stared at her until her head dipped out of sight behind the bridge. The gray clouds covered the moon again; the lights shone brighter, the bridge even whiter, emptier, and colder. He turned, quickened his steps, and walked back like someone crazed; when he reached the street gate, the cold, desolate image of the bridge was still on his mind, as if barely a blink had passed.

Inside, he first counted the bills—over and over, his sweaty palms made them sticky and hard to tally. When finished, he put them in the piggy jar. Sitting on the edge of the bed, staring blankly at the clay container, he resolved not to think of anything else; with money, there’s always a way—he trusted that his piggy bank would solve everything, and there was no need to think further. The imperial river, Jingshan, White Pagoda, the bridge, Tigress, the belly—all a dream; now he was awake and the piggy bank had thirty-odd yuan more, for real!

After looking at it enough, he hid the piggy bank and planned to sleep it all away—any problem, no matter how big, could be slept through; tomorrow he’d deal with it!

He lay down, but couldn’t close his eyes! Those things buzzed like a swarm of bees: you come out, I go in, each with a sting at its belly’s tip!

He didn’t want to think, nor could he really, because Tigress had blocked every way. There was no way to escape.

It would be best to stomp away. Xiangzi couldn’t do it. If he were assigned to guard the White Pagoda at Beihai, he’d gladly go; just not back to the village! To another city? He couldn’t think of anywhere better than Beijing. He couldn’t leave; he wanted to die here.

Since he wasn’t going, there was no point wasting more energy thinking. What Tigress could say, she could do; if he didn’t follow her way, she really would keep pestering him. As long as he was in Beijing, she’d find him! With her, he had to be sincere, no point in playing games. If he got her really angry, she’d bring in Liu Sier. If Liu Sier decided to get rid of one or two people—not to say more—he could finish Xiangzi off in any out-of-the-way spot!

Thinking through Tigress’s words from beginning to end, he felt like he’d fallen into a trap, hands and feet caught in a snare, absolutely no escape. He couldn't pick apart her plan bit by bit, so he couldn't find any weak points. He only felt she was casting an inescapable net that wouldn’t let even a tiny fish out! Since he couldn’t think it out in detail, he lumped it all together, and it pressed down on him like a thousand-pound weight. Under this irresistible pressure, he felt that the whole fate of a rickshaw puller could be summed up in two words—bad luck! A rickshaw puller, just because they're a rickshaw puller, should do nothing, not even get involved with women; get mixed up, and something big goes wrong. Liu Sier relied on his dozens of rickshaws, Tigress would rely on her stinking × to bully him! He didn’t need to think further; if he resigned himself, so be it—kowtow, become the godson, then wait to marry that damned shrew. If he didn’t submit, he’d just have to throw away his life!

With that, he set aside Tigress and all her words; no, this wasn’t just her fault, it was the fate of all rickshaw drivers—just as a dog is destined to be beaten and bullied, even little kids will hit it with a stick for no reason. What, then, is the use of such a life? If you’re going to go all out, go all out!

He stopped trying to sleep, kicked off the covers, and sat up. He decided to go buy some liquor and get completely drunk. What are problems, what are rules—damn them all! Get drunk, sleep! The twenty-seventh? He wouldn’t kowtow on the twenty-eighth either, let’s see who could do anything to Xiangzi!

Throwing on his big padded coat, grabbing the little rice bowl he used as a teacup, he rushed out. The wind was stronger, the gray clouds on the sky had dispersed, the moon was small and shining with a chilly light. Xiangzi, coming out of the warm bed, kept sucking in cold air. There were no people on the street, only one or two rickshaws parked by the roadside, the drivers warming their hands over their ears, stomping to keep warm. Xiangzi ran straight to the little shop down south; to preserve heat, the shop had already closed its door, cash and goods exchanged through a small window slot. Xiangzi bought four taels of strong liquor, and three big coins' worth of peanuts. Carrying the bowl of liquor carefully, not daring to run, but briskly—like a sedan chair carrier—he made his way back to his room. He crawled quickly under the covers, his teeth chattering a while, not wanting to sit up again. The liquor, sitting on the table, spread a hot, pungent aroma. He didn't really like the smell, and even the peanuts failed to tempt him. This wave of cold felt like a basin of cold water dumped over his head, waking him up; he didn’t want to reach out his hand, and his heart wasn’t heated anymore. Lying there for a long while,His eyes glanced once more at the wine bowl on the table by the edge of the quilt. No, he couldn't destroy himself for that bit of entanglement, couldn't break his vow to abstain from alcohol because of it. Things were indeed difficult, but there would always be a crack for him to slip through. Even if there was truly no way to escape, he still shouldn't be the first to roll himself into the mud; he had to keep his eyes open, to clearly see exactly how others pushed him down.

Turning out the lamp and covering his head completely with the quilt, he tried to fall asleep just like that. Still unable to sleep, he lifted the quilt to take a look. The paper-covered window was tinted blue by the moonlight from the courtyard, as if morning was about to break. The tip of his nose sensed the cold in the room, with a bit of wine aroma mixed into the chill. He suddenly sat up, grabbed the wine bowl, and gulped down a large mouthful!

Ten

Deal with things one by one, Xiangzi isn't that smart. Take stock of everything, he doesn't have that kind of nerve. So, he has absolutely no way out, going around all day with a belly full of grievances. Just like all other living beings, after suffering harm, there's nothing to be done but to clean up the mess on your own. That cricket with its thigh knocked off still tries to crawl with its little legs. Xiangzi doesn't have a solid plan, just wants to get by slowly, day by day, one thing at a time, just crawl wherever he can, not even thinking about leaping up anymore.

There were still more than ten days before the 27th, and all his attention was on this day—what he thought about, muttered under his breath, and even dreamed about in his sleep was all about the 27th. It was as if, once the 27th passed, he would have a way to solve everything, though he knew deep down he was just fooling himself. Sometimes he thought further out, like taking the dozens of yuan in his hand and going to Tianjin; when he got there, maybe by chance he could even switch professions and stop pulling rickshaws. Could Huniu still chase him all the way to Tianjin? In his mind, anywhere you need to take a train to is very far, no matter what she couldn’t chase him there. Nice thought, but he knew in his heart that was only a last resort; if he could stay in Beiping, he’d still stay in Beiping! So, again he thought about the 27th, still thinking of going for the simple and easy way out—as long as he got through this, maybe he could get by without making big changes; even if he couldn’t rid himself of everything, at least he’d clear one hurdle at a time.

How to get through this hurdle? He had two ideas: one was to ignore her altogether and just not go pay respects on her birthday. The other was to do as she had asked. These two ideas, though different, had the same outcome: if he didn’t go, she certainly wouldn’t let it go; if he went, she wouldn’t let him off either. He still remembered when he first started pulling rickshaws, how he’d try to cut corners like the others by diving into side alleys, only to end up lost in labyrinthine lanes; he’d go round and round, coming back to the same street. Now he had gotten into one of those alleyways again, as if whichever way he tried, he’d end up in the same place.

With no solution, he tried thinking on the bright side; what if he just accepted her, would that really be so bad? But from any angle, he still felt stifled. Think about her looks, all he could do was shake his head. Never mind the looks, then think about how she acts; hmph! With him being so proud and proper, to marry that kind of woman, he couldn’t face anyone, not even after death could he face his parents! Who could guarantee the child in her belly was his? True, she might bring him a couple of rickshaws, but could he really be sure? Liu Siye is not someone to mess with! Even if everything went smoothly, he couldn’t take it—he couldn’t outdo Huniu! With her, just a little finger and she could have him running around dizzy, not knowing which way was north. He knew how formidable she was! To start a family, he absolutely mustn’t have her, there was nothing else to say! If he accepted her, he’d lose himself, and he wasn’t the kind to look down on himself! No way out!

Being unable to deal with her, he turned his resentment inward, really wanting to give himself a couple of hard slaps. But honestly, he hadn’t done anything wrong. She had set everything up, just waiting for him to fall into her trap. The problem seemed to be with him being too honest; honest guys always get taken advantage of, there’s just no justice in it!

What was even harder on him was that he had no one to vent his grievances to. No parents, no brothers or sisters, no friends. Usually, he felt like a true hero, with his head to the sky and feet on the ground, free and untied to anyone. Now he finally realized and regretted it—humans can’t live alone. Especially when it came to his fellow rickshaw pullers, now they suddenly seemed kind of lovable. If, in normal times, he’d made some friends, he thought, even if they were strapping guys like him, with a Huniu among them, he wouldn’t be afraid. They’d help him come up with ideas, help him out. But he was alone all along; it’s not easy to find friends at the last minute! He felt a fear he’d never experienced before. Going on like this, anyone could bully him; alone, you just can't hold up the sky!

This bit of fear made him start doubting himself. In winter, if the boss was hosting a dinner or going to the theater, he’d always tuck the carbide lamp’s water tank into his coat; because if he left it on the rickshaw, it would freeze. As soon as he started running, his body would sweat, and when the icy little tank pressed to his chest, he’d shiver all over; who knows how long it would take for that tank to get warm. But he’d usually never thought there was anything wrong with this; sometimes, tucking it away, he'd even feel a kind of pride, that those pulling junk rickshaws didn’t even get to use a carbide lamp. Now, it seemed clear: earning so little in a month, suffering all the hardships, still having to make sure the little water tank didn’t freeze by keeping it pressed to his chest, as if his broad chest wasn’t even worth as much as that little tank. Before, he thought pulling a rickshaw was his dream job, that by it he could make his way and start a family. Now he was secretly shaking his head. No wonder Huniu bullied him; he turned out to be no better than a little water tank himself!

On the third night after Huniu had come looking for him, Mr. Cao went to the night movies with friends, and Xiangzi was waiting in a small teahouse, still hugging that ice-cold little tank to his chest. It was freezing, doors and windows in the teahouse tightly shut, filled with coal gas, sweat, and the sour stink of cheap cigarettes. Even so, the windows were still frosted with a layer of ice flowers. Almost all the tea drinkers were monthly-hire rickshaw pullers, some leaning their heads against the wall, eyes closed, dozing off in the cozy air. Some held bowls of baijiu, offering them around before slowly, in turn, sharing a story as they drank; after each sip, smacking their lips above and blowing cold air noisily below. Some were clutching rolled-up pancakes, taking bites big enough to fatten their necks to a thick, red column. Some wore sour faces, grumbling to everyone about how they hadn’t had a break since morning, already soaked and dried, dried and soaked, God knows how many times! Most of the rest were chatting idly, but when these complaints were voiced, everyone would fall quiet for a moment, then, like birds scattered from a nest, would all recall the day’s grievances, eager to share them with the group. Even the one with the pancake managed to clear enough space in his mouth to work his tongue, talking as he swallowed, veins on his head jumping: “You think monthly hires don’t get their asses roasted either?! I’ve been at this since two in the morning and haven’t had even a drop to eat! They talk about going from Qianmen to Pingzemen—I’ve run that route three times! In this weather, your ass freezes and cracks, just farting all day!” He glanced around the group, nodded, then took another bite of his pancake.

This sent everyone’s talk back to the weather, each telling of their own hardships. Xiangzi never said a word, but listened intently to what everyone was saying. The words varied in tone, style, detail, but all boiled down to curses and injustice. These words, touching his own grievances, felt like raindrops soaking into parched earth, every word sinking in. He didn’t have the ability—or the words—to tell his story with a beginning and end like the others; he could only absorb a bit of life’s bitterness from their talk, knowing that everyone suffered, and he was no exception; recognizing himself, and wanting to sympathize with everyone else. When the talk turned to misery, he’d frown; when it was funny, he’d twitch his lips. In this way he felt himself connected to them, all sharing the same suffering, even if he didn’t say a word, it didn’t much matter. Before, he thought they were just mouthy complainers—how could they ever get rich wasting their breath? But tonight, for the first time, he realized they weren’t complaining for nothing, but rather expressing his—and every rickshaw puller’s—hardships.

As everyone chatted lively in the middle of it all, the door suddenly opened, letting a blast of cold air in. Everyone glared at the door in anger—who on earth brought in the cold. The more impatient they were, the slower the person outside seemed to go, as if deliberately dawdling.

The teahouse waiter half-joking, half-irritated, called out: “Come on in, grandpa! Don’t let all the heat out!”

Before he finished, the person finally came in, another rickshaw puller. He looked over fifty, wearing a not-short-not-long, battered cotton-padded jacket like an upside-down basket, with cotton popping out from the collar and elbows. It seemed his face hadn’t seen soap in days—no skin color, just two ears red as apples ready to fall off. Straw-white hair stuck out messily below a battered little hat; both eyebrows and short mustache hung with ice beads. He came in, grabbed a bench and sat down, fighting to get out the words: “Brew a pot.”

This teahouse was always a haunt for rickshaw drivers on monthly hire; someone like this old driver, on a normal day, would never come in.

Everyone glanced at him, suddenly feeling something deeper than what they’d just been talking about, and no one felt like speaking. On any other day, there’d be a couple young guys cracking jokes and making fun of a tea customer like him, but tonight, not a word.

Before the tea arrived, the old rickshaw man's head slowly drooped, lower and lower, until his whole body slumped down.

Everyone immediately stood up: “What’s wrong? What’s wrong?” As they said this, they all wanted to rush over.

“Don’t move!” The teahouse manager, who was experienced, held everyone back. He went over by himself, undid the old man's collar, propped him up, wedged a chair behind him, and pressed his shoulders with his hands: “Sugar water, quick!” he ordered, then listened by the old man’s neck, muttering to himself, “It’s not phlegm!”

No one moved, but no one sat down—they all blinked through the smoky air toward the door. It was as if everyone secretly thought: “That’s us in the future! When our hair’s all white, everyone faces a fall like this!”

Just as the sugar water touched the old man’s lips, he groaned twice. Eyes still closed, he raised his right hand—black as lacquer, shiny as if painted—and wiped his mouth with the back of his hand.

“Have some water!” the manager spoke into his ear.

“Huh?” The old man opened his eyes. Seeing himself sitting on the floor, he curled his legs, trying to get up.

“Drink some water first, no rush,” said the manager, letting go. Almost everyone rushed over.

“Ah! Ah!” The old man glanced around, cradled the tea bowl in both hands, and slowly sipped the sugar water.

When he finished, he looked at everyone again: “Thanks for all your trouble!” he said warmly and kindly, totally unlike the rough mouth he had. Then he tried to stand again, with three or four rushing over to help him. His face showed a little smile, gentle as could be: “It’s alright, it’s alright, I’m fine! Just cold and hungry, got a dizzy spell! No problem, no problem! Sorry for troubling you, brothers!”

Right then, the old man’s straw-gray hair, muddy face, charcoal-black hands, and that battered hat and jacket, all seemed to radiate a kind of purity, like the sacred image of a deity in a dilapidated temple—broken, but still dignified. Everyone watched him, as if afraid for him to leave. Xiangzi still stood there silently, dazed. Hearing the old man say his belly was empty, he suddenly dashed out, then rushed back as fast as he could, a cabbage leaf holding ten mutton buns in his hand. He brought them right to the old man’s face: “Eat!” Then sat back down, hanging his head as if utterly spent. “Ah!” The old man seemed half-laughing, half-crying, nodding at everyone. “We’re all brothers here! Pulling a fare, you pull with all your might, but when it comes to asking for one more coin at the end, that’s the hardest thing!” He started to get up, getting ready to leave.

“Eat!” Everyone called out at once.

“I’ll get my little Ma’er, my grandson, he’s watching the rickshaw outside.” “I’ll go, you sit down!” the middle-aged driver said, “No one’s going to steal it here, trust me, there’s a police box right across the street.” He cracked open the door: “Ma’er! Ma’er! Grandpa's calling! Bring the rickshaw over here!”

The old man touched the buns a few times but never picked one up. The moment Ma’er came in, he took one: “Ma’er, here you go!” Ma’er was only twelve or thirteen, with a thin face but well bundled up, nose red from the cold, with two streams of white snot running down.Wearing a pair of battered earmuffs on the ears. Standing beside the old man, he took a steamed bun with his right hand, and automatically picked up another one with his left, taking a bite from one.

"Hey! Take it slow!" The old man placed one hand on his grandson's head, picked up a bun with the other, and slowly brought it to his mouth. "Grandpa will have two, that's enough, the rest are yours! Once we've finished eating, we'll pack up and head home, no more rides today. If it’s not as cold tomorrow, we’ll go out early with the cart. Right, Xiao Ma’er?"

Xiao Ma’er nodded at the bun and sniffed: "Grandpa, have three, the rest are all mine. I’ll pull grandpa home later!" "No need!" The old man smiled with pride at everyone: "We’ll walk back later just the same, it’s cold sitting in the cart."

After finishing his portion, the old man drained the wine in his cup and waited for Xiao Ma’er to finish the buns. He pulled out a tattered rag to wipe his mouth, then nodded at everyone again: "My son went off to be a soldier, never to return; my daughter-in-law—"

"Don’t talk about that!" Xiao Ma’er’s cheeks puffed up like two little peaches, stuffed with food, he tried to stop grandpa while eating and talking.

"It’s fine to say! None of us are outsiders!" Then, lowering his voice to everyone: "The child is proud, you can’t imagine how strong-willed! The daughter-in-law also left. It’s just the two of us making a living off this cart; it’s battered, but it’s ours, which means we don’t have to scramble for rental every day. Whether we earn more or less, the two of us just muddle through, no way out! No way out!"

"Grandpa," Xiao Ma’er had almost finished the buns, tugged at the old man’s sleeve, "we still have to do another run, tomorrow morning we don’t have money for coal yet! It’s all you, twenty coins just now for a backdoor run, if you’d listened to me, we’d have done it, but you insisted on not going! No coal for the morning, let’s see what you’ll do!"

"There’s a way, grandpa will get five catties of coal briquette on credit."

"And some kindling?"

"Exactly! Good boy, eat up; once you’re done, let’s be on our way!" As he spoke, the old man stood up, made a circle motion at everyone, and said: "We’ve troubled you all, brothers!" He reached out to pull Xiao Ma’er along, Xiao Ma’er stuffed the last unfinished bun completely into his mouth. Some in the room remained seated, some followed them out. Xiangzi was the first to follow, he wanted to see that cart.

It was a truly battered cart, the paint on the cart floor had already cracked, the handles were worn down enough to expose the wood grain, a rickety broken lamp clattered noisily, and the canopy supports were tied up with hemp rope. Xiao Ma’er found a match in his earmuffs, struck it on his shoe sole, and with his two small black hands, lit the lamp. The old man spat in his palm, gave a shout, grabbed the handles, "See you tomorrow, brothers!"

Xiangzi stood dazedly at the door, watching that old man and the boy with their battered cart. The old man kept talking as he walked, his voice sometimes loud, sometimes low; the streetlights and shadows alternately light and dark. Xiangzi listened and watched, feeling a kind of suffering he’d never felt before. In Xiao Ma’er he seemed to see his own past; in the old man, his own future! He had never let go of a coin easily before, but now he felt gratified, having bought ten buns for this old and young pair. He didn’t go back in until they were out of sight. People started laughing and chatting again, but he felt muddled, settled the tea bill, then stepped out, pulling his cart to wait outside the cinema for Mr. Cao.

It was bitterly cold. Dust floated in the air, the wind seemed to be racing above, the stars were not very clear, only a few big ones trembling faintly in the sky. There was no wind on the ground, but chill emanated all around, the ruts already showing long frozen cracks, the earth ashen gray, cold and hard like ice. Xiangzi stood outside the cinema for a while, feeling the chill, but was unwilling to return to the tea house. He wanted to think quietly, alone. That old and young pair seemed to have shattered his greatest hope—the old man's cart was his own! Since the first day he pulled a cart, he had decided to buy his own, and he still worked every day for this goal; he believed that owning his own cart meant having everything. Hmph, look at that old man!

He refused Hu Niu, wasn't it because he wanted to buy his own cart? Buy a cart, save up, then cleanly marry a wife; humph, look at Xiao Ma’er! If he had a son, wouldn't he be the same?

Thinking like this, he didn’t feel the urge to resist Hu Niu’s pressure anymore; in any case, he couldn't escape the circle, what kind of woman can’t do? Besides, she might even bring a few carts, why not enjoy a few easy days! Seeing through himself, he no longer needed to despise others, Hu Niu is just Hu Niu, nothing more to say!

When the movie ended, he quickly fixed the little water bottle, lit the lamp. Even took off his thin cotton jacket, leaving only a thin top, he wanted to sprint and forget everything, even if he died crashing, it didn’t matter much!

[1]

  "Mofan" means to drag things out, to waste time.

[2]

  "Hengshi" means probably.

Eleven

Whenever he thought of that old man and Xiao Ma’er, Xiangzi felt like letting go of all hopes, and just enjoying the day as it came, why spend every day gritting his teeth and tormenting himself?! He seemed to understand the fate of poor people, just like a date pit—pointed at both ends: it's lucky not to starve as a child; but very hard not to starve when old. Only the middle part—young and strong, not afraid of hunger or toil—can be somewhat like a human. In this stage, if you don’t allow yourself some happiness, you’re a real fool; you’ll miss the shop after this village! Thinking this way, he didn’t even feel troubled by the whole Hu Niu situation.

But when he saw that sealed clay jar, his thoughts shifted back again. No, can’t be careless; just a few dozen yuan away from buying a cart, he couldn't let all previous efforts go to waste; at the very least, couldn't just throw away what little he had saved in the jar—so hard to save it! He still needed to walk the right path, definitely! But, Hu Niu? Still no solution, still had to worry over that wretched twenty-seven yuan.

When he worried himself to the limit, he clutched the clay jar and muttered to himself: Do whatever, this money is mine! No one can take it! With this money, Xiangzi wasn’t afraid of anything! If I get desperate, I can stomp my feet and run, with money, my legs will move!

The street was getting busier, candies for the Kitchen God festival filled the stalls, everywhere you heard "Buy your candy, buy your candy!" Xiangzi used to look forward to the New Year, but now he felt nothing, the more chaotic the street, the tighter his heart was, that dreadful twenty-seventh loomed near! His eyes were sunken, even the scar on his face looked darker. Pulling his cart on those busy, slippery streets, he had to be extra careful. Worries and alertness attacked together, he felt his nerves overstretched, think of one thing and he’d forget another, often startled, his skin itching as if a child had gotten prickly heat in summer.

On the afternoon of Kitchen God festival, a smooth east wind brought clouds all day. The weather suddenly grew milder. Near dusk, the wind eased further, and sparse snowflakes began to fall from the sky. Candy vendors grew anxious, warm weather plus snow, everyone poured white powder over the candies for fear they’d all stick together. Not many flakes fell before it turned to tiny snow pellets, pattering lightly, whitening the ground. After seven o’clock, shops and homes began the festival prayers, incense glow and firecrackers among the steady small snow, a hint of eerie atmosphere amid the bustle. People on the street looked a bit anxious, whether walking or riding, all were eager to get home and honor the gods, but as the ground was wet and slippery, they dared not rush. Candy peddlers, eager to sell off their festival wares, shouted breathlessly, their voices jarring to the ear.

By around nine o’clock, Xiangzi was pulling Mr. Cao home from Xicheng. Passing the bustling Xidan arcade, heading east onto Chang’an Avenue, people and vehicles thinned out. The smooth asphalt was covered by a layer of thin snow, glimmering slightly under the streetlights. Occasionally a car passed, headlights sweeping far ahead, and the snow particles sparkled yellowish in the light, like ten thousand grains of gold dust. Near Xinhua Gate, the road, already broad, felt even vaster with the snow, making one’s mind feel clear and cheerful, and everything seemed a bit more solemn. The "Chang’an Arcade," Xinhua Gate's gatehouse, the red walls of Nanhai, each wore a crown of white, the vermillion pillars, red walls quietly displaying the dignity of the old capital under the lights. In that time and place, one felt as if Beiping had no residents, but was a palace of jade and gold, with only old pines silently catching snowflakes. Xiangzi had no time to admire the view; seeing the "jade road" ahead, he only wanted to run straight home in a single rush; the straight, white, calm avenue seemed to point his mind directly to his own doorstep. But he couldn't sprint, the snow on the ground though thin, clung to his soles with every step, forming a thick layer; stomp it off, and a moment later it stuck again. The snow pellets, though tiny, were heavy, sticking to his feet and stinging his eyes, making it impossible to run fast. The snow that landed on him didn’t melt easily, a thin layer already coated his shoulders; it was nothing, but the dampness made him uneasy. There were no shops in this area, but in the distance the firecrackers kept up, from time to time a double-bang or a "five ghosts troubling the judge" shot into the black sky. Sparks scattered, making the darkness seem all the heavier, almost frightening. He listened to the fireworks, saw the sparks and the darkness, and wanted to get home at once. But he dared not break into a run, feeling awkward!

What made him even more uncomfortable was that starting from Xicheng, he felt there was a bicycle trailing him. On West Chang’an Avenue, as the street grew quieter, the feeling of being followed intensified—the cart wheels crunching on the thin snow, not loud, but detectable. Xiangzi, like other rickshaw pullers, hated bicycles most. Automobiles were annoying, but at least their noise gave warning to dodge early. Bicycles squeezed through every gap, and the way they swayed back and forth made you dizzy. More than that, in case of accidents, the rickshaw puller was always blamed, the police always figured it was easier to pin it on the puller, so they'd always accuse the rickshaw-puller first. More than once, Xiangzi wanted to deliberately slam the brakes and make the guy behind fall. But he dared not, rickshaw pullers had to put up with much. Every time he wanted to stomp his shoes clear, he would have to shout: "Hold on!" At Nanhai Qianmen, the street was so wide, and the bike was still stuck right behind his cart. Xiangzi's irritation grew, so he deliberately stopped, brushed the snow off himself. As he stood still, the bicycle brushed past his cart. The rider even turned to look back at him. Xiangzi deliberately dragged things out, only grabbed the handles again when the bike was far off, cursing: "Annoying!" Mr. Cao’s "humanitarianism" meant he refused to use the wind-deflecting cotton cover, and even the canvas cover was only allowed in heavy rain—to spare the puller’s effort. This bit of snow, he felt, didn't warrant putting up the hood, besides, he wanted to see the snowy night. He too had noticed the bicycle, and once Xiangzi had finished cursing he said quietly, "If he keeps following, don't stop at your own door, go to Mr. Zuo’s at Huanghuamen; don’t panic!"

Xiangzi was a bit rattled. He only knew cyclists were annoying, but didn’t realize there could be anything truly sinister—if even Mr. Cao was afraid to go home, this guy must be tough! After a few dozen paces, he caught up with the man; the cyclist deliberately waited for him and Mr. Cao. The bicycle let Xiangzi go ahead, and Xiangzi took one look at the man on the bike. With one glance he knew: from the detective squad. He’d often seen those guys in the tea house, knew their looks and dress, even if they’d never spoken. He recognized this getup: dark blue cotton-padded robe, felt hat, hat pulled down low.

At the South Chang Street intersection, Xiangzi took advantage of turning a corner to glance back, the guy was still following. He almost forgot the snow on the ground and picked up his pace. The straight, white, gleaming road, with only a few cold streetlights, and a detective on a bike in pursuit! Xiangzi had never experienced this before, and broke out in a sweat. At the park's back gate, he looked back again, still trailing! At his own door, he dared not stop, reluctant to pass; Mr. Cao said nothing at all, so he had to keep going north. All the way to the north end, the bicycle still followed! He entered an alley, still behind him! Out the other end, still behind! Heading to Huanghuamen, he shouldn’t have taken the alley, and only realized it at the north mouth of the alley—he admitted he was a bit lost and got even angrier. Reaching the back of Jingshan, the bike turned north toward the back gate. Xiangzi wiped his brow. The snow had thinned, but there were now a few big flakes among the pellets. Xiangzi seemed to prefer the snowflakes, fluttering freely in the air, not as suffocating as the pellets. He turned to ask: "Where now, sir?"

"Back to the Zuo household. If anyone asks after me, say you don’t know me!"

"Alright!" Xiangzi felt relieved, but didn’t dare ask details.

At the Zuo home, Mr. Cao had Xiangzi bring the cart inside and shut the door quickly. Mr. Cao remained calm, but didn’t look too happy. After giving Xiangzi instructions, he went inside. Xiangzi had just parked the cart in the doorway when Mr. Cao came out again with Mr. Zuo; Xiangzi knew him, and knew Mr. Zuo was a good friend of the family. "Xiangzi," Mr. Cao talked very fast, "you take a taxi home. Tell madam I’m here. Ask them all to come, by taxi, hire another one too,No need to teach you to sit and wait in the car that's waiting. Understand? Good! Tell madam to bring the necessary things and those paintings from the study. Got it? I'll call madam right now, but let me tell you again, in case she forgets due to anxiety, you can remind her.”

“Should I go?” Mr. Zuo asked.

“No need! That person earlier might not necessarily be a detective, but I can't ignore the possibility, so we have to take precautions. Can you call a car first?” Mr. Zuo went to call for a car. Mr. Cao reminded Xiangzi again: “When the car arrives, I'll give the money here. Tell madam to pack up quickly; nothing else matters, but make sure to bring the child's things and those paintings from the study, those paintings! Once madam's ready, have Gao Ma call for a car to come here. Understand all this? After they leave, lock the front door and sleep in the study; there's a telephone there. Do you know how to use the phone?”

“I can't make outgoing calls, but I can answer.” Actually, Xiangzi didn't like even answering, but not wanting to make Mr. Cao anxious, he agreed anyway.

“That's fine!” Mr. Cao quickly continued: “If anything suspicious happens, don't open the door! Once we've all gone, you'll be left alone, and they won't let you off! If things look bad, turn off the light and jump over the back wall into the Wang family's yard. Do you know the people in the Wang family? Right! Hide there a while and then leave. Don't worry about my things or even your own, just jump the wall and leave, so you don't get taken! If you lose anything, I'll compensate you in the future. Take these five yuan first. Okay, I'm going to call madam, later remind her again. No need to mention being arrested, that cyclist earlier might be a detective, or maybe not; don't get nervous yet!”

Xiangzi was in turmoil inside, as if he had many questions to ask, but he was so focused on remembering Mr. Cao's instructions that he didn't dare ask any more.

The car came, and Xiangzi got in dazed and clumsy. The snow was falling neither too hard nor too light; things outside the car were hard to see clearly, and he sat up straight, his head almost touching the roof. He wanted to think things through, but his eyes simply kept watching the red arrow at the front of the car, which was so bright and lively and endearing. The driver's little brush in front, swaying automatically left and right, swept away the fog on the glass, which was quite interesting. Just as he started to get used to it, the car arrived at the gate, and he got out, feeling oddly uncomfortable.

As soon as he was about to press the doorbell, someone suddenly grabbed his wrist as if appearing out of the wall. Xiangzi instinctively tried to pull away, but he had already seen who it was and stopped moving—it was the detective who'd been riding the bicycle earlier.

“Xiangzi, don't you recognize me?” The detective smiled and let go of his hand.

Xiangzi swallowed and didn't know what to say.

“Don't you remember that time you took us out to Xishan? I'm Captain Sun. Remember now?”

“Ah, Captain Sun!” Xiangzi couldn't remember. When he was taken by the soldiers up the mountain, he hadn't had time to see who was captain or company commander.

“You don't remember me, but I remember you; that scar on your face is a good marker. I've been following you for a long time just now, and at first I wasn't sure myself, but after looking left and right, that scar can't be wrong!”

“Is something up?” Xiangzi tried to press the doorbell again.

“Of course there's something, and it's important! Let's talk inside, okay?” Captain Sun—now a detective—reached out and pressed the bell.

“I have something to do!” Xiangzi suddenly started sweating, desperately thinking: “Even dodging him doesn't work, how can I invite him inside?”

“Don't worry, I'm here for your own good!” The detective gave a somewhat sly smile. When Gao Ma came and opened the door, he strode right in: “Excuse me, excuse me!” Before Xiangzi or Gao Ma could say a word, he pulled Xiangzi inside and pointed to the gatehouse: “You live here?” Entering the room, he glanced around: “The little place is pretty tidy! Things aren't bad for you!”

“Is something up? I'm busy!” Xiangzi couldn't take the small talk anymore. “Didn't I tell you? It's important!” Detective Sun was still smiling, but his tone was extremely serious. “I’ll be blunt: Cao is a rebel, will be shot as soon as he's caught, and he can't get away! Since we’ve met before, you waited on me in the barracks; besides, we're both local people, so I'm risking big trouble to bring you a warning! If you're even a step late running, you’ll be caught in a raid, and no one will get away. We earn a living with hard work—what’s the point of getting ourselves tangled up with their problems? Am I right?”

“It’s not right for me to betray people!” Xiangzi was still thinking of Mr. Cao’s instructions. “Betray who?” Detective Sun smiled at the corners of his mouth, but his eyes were sharp. “They brought this on themselves, who are you betraying? They get away with things while we suffer for them, that doesn't add up! Not to mention, if you’re locked up for three months, you, being like a free wild bird, could you even take sitting in a dark cell? Plus, if they go to prison, they've got money to smooth things over and might not have to suffer; but you, my good brother, with nothing in your hands, will be chained next to a chamber pot! That’s the least of it—if they happen to spend some money and stir things up, you'll get a few years in prison; if the authorities need someone to blame, you’ll get scapegoated for sure. We didn’t mess with anyone to end up biting the bullet in the end—isn't that wrong? You're a smart guy, and smart guys don’t ask for trouble. ‘Doing right by people’? Ha! Let me tell you, brother, there’s no such thing as justice for us poor folks!”

Xiangzi was frightened. Thinking of being dragged away by soldiers, he could imagine what jail was like. “So I should run, forget about them?”

“If you worry about them, who’s worrying about you?!”

Xiangzi had nothing to say. After a moment, even his conscience nodded: “Fine, I’ll go!”

“Just leave like this?” Detective Sun sneered.

Xiangzi got confused again.

“Xiangzi, my good mate! You're too naive! Would I, as a detective, just let you go like that?”

“So—” Xiangzi was so anxious he didn't know what to say.

“Don’t play dumb!” Detective Sun stared at Xiangzi: “You probably have some savings, take it out to buy your life! I don’t make as much as you do in a month, but I have to support my family, so I live on side money—let’s talk straight! You think I'd just let you go empty-handed? We've got some history, otherwise would I be here warning you? But business is business, I can't risk my family going hungry for nothing! No need to talk more. Tell me honestly: how much do you have?”

“How much do you want?” Xiangzi sat on the bed.

“Give me all you have, there’s no set price!”

“I’d rather go to jail!”

“You said it? Don’t regret it?” Detective Sun put his hand inside his padded robe. “Look at this, Xiangzi! I can arrest you right now; if you resist, I'll shoot! If I take you away now, don’t even think about money—you’ll lose even the clothes on your back as soon as you walk into jail. You’re smart, figure it out yourself!” “If you’ve got time to squeeze me, why don’t you go squeeze Mr. Cao?” Xiangzi blurted out after hesitating a long time.

“He’s the main offender; if we catch him there’s a reward, if not, we get blamed. You, you, my dumb brother, letting you go is nothing, killing you is nothing! Give me the money and you can go; if not, fine, I’ll see you at Tianqiao! Stop messing around, let’s be direct, you’re a grown-up! Besides, I won't keep all this money, my colleagues need a cut, who knows how much I’ll get? Buying your life this cheap and you’re still not willing? Then I can't help you! How much do you have?”

Xiangzi stood up, his mind reeling, fists clenched.

“If you try anything, don’t forget, there’s a whole gang of us out there! Hurry up and give me the money! I’m giving you face, don’t push it!” Detective Sun’s gaze was unpleasant.

“What did I ever do to anybody?!” Xiangzi said, voice cracking, and sat back down on the bed. “You never hurt anyone, but it’s just bad luck! Some people are born rich, but we’re from the bottom. Nothing more to say!” Detective Sun shook his head, showing endless emotion. “Fine, I admit I'm wronging you, but let's not drag this out!”

Xiangzi thought for a while more, but there was nothing he could do. Hands trembling, he took out the clay jar of his savings from under the quilt.

“Let me see!” Detective Sun smiled, grabbed the jar, and smashed it against the wall.

Xiangzi watched the money scatter on the floor, his heart nearly breaking.

“That’s it?”

Xiangzi said nothing, just kept shaking.

“All right! I won’t push you too hard, friends are friends. But you should know, buying your life for this little money, that’s cheap!”

Xiangzi still didn’t say a word, trembling as he tried to gather his bedding.

“Leave it!”

“It’s so cold...” Xiangzi’s eyes blazed with anger.

“I told you to leave it, now leave it! Get out!”

Xiangzi swallowed hard, bit his lip, and pushed the door open to leave.

The snow was already more than an inch thick; Xiangzi walked with his head down. Everywhere was white, only the big black footprints he left behind.

Twelve

Xiangzi wanted to find a place to sit and think everything through, even if it ended in nothing but a good cry, at least he’d know what he was crying for; everything had changed too quickly, his mind couldn’t keep up. There was nowhere for him to sit, everywhere was snow. The little tea shops were all closed, it was after 10; even if they were open, he wouldn’t go in, he wanted some place quiet, he knew the tears circling in his eyes might fall at any time. With nowhere to rest, he could only walk slowly; but where should he go? In this silvery world, there was nowhere for him to sit, and nowhere for him to go; a vast whiteness, only hungry little birds and people driven to desperation knew what it meant to sigh.

Where should he go? That was the question, no need to think further! Stay in a cheap inn? Impossible! The clothes on his back could easily be stolen in the middle of the night, not to mention the bedbugs in those places were terrifying. Stay in a better inn? He couldn’t afford it, he only had five yuan left, his entire fortune. Go to a bathhouse? They closed at midnight, no sleeping overnight. Nowhere to go.

With nowhere to go, his sense of desperation only grew. After all these years scraping by in the city, all he had left was the clothes on his back and five yuan; he’d even lost his bedding! That made him think of tomorrow. What should he do tomorrow? Pull rickshaw, keep pulling rickshaw; hmph, and what does that get him? He can’t even find a place to live, any money he saves just gets stolen! Start a little business, but he only has five yuan, and even carrying poles and baskets he’d have to buy; besides, which business could really let him make a living? Rickshaw pulling could net him thirty or forty cents a day, but a little business requires capital and can’t guarantee even food for three meals. Once the capital is gone, back to rickshaw pulling—wasn’t that just wasting five yuan for nothing? He couldn’t let go of even a cent of these five yuan, it was his last hope! Be a servant? He wasn’t cut out for it: serving people, he couldn’t; washing clothes and cooking, he couldn’t either! He was useless at everything, didn’t know anything, just a big, dumb, clumsy brute!

Unconsciously, he found himself at Zhonghai. On the bridge, all was empty and open, and everywhere he looked was snow. Only then did he realize it was still snowing; touching his head, his woolen cap was already very wet. There was no one on the bridge; even the police were nowhere to be seen, and a few street lamps flickered as if blinking because of the snowflakes. Xiangzi looked at the snowy expanse, bewildered.

He stood on the bridge a long time, the world seemed dead—no sound, no movement, the gray-white snowflakes taking their chance, panicked and light, falling endlessly, silently burying the world. In this silence, Xiangzi heard the faint voice of his conscience. Forget himself for a moment, he still had to go back and check on the Cao family. Only Mrs. Cao and Gao Ma were left—no men at all! Wasn’t that last five yuan given to him by Mr. Cao? He dared not think further, but hurried back as fast as he could.

There were tracks outside the door, and two new wheel tracks on the street. Had Mrs. Cao already left? Why didn’t that Sun guy go after them?

He didn’t dare push the door, afraid he’d be caught again. Looking around, no one was there, and his heart started to race. Might as well try; he had nowhere else to go, so if he got caught, then so be it. He gently pushed the door, and it was open. Sticking close to the wall, he took a few steps and saw a light on in his own room—his own room! He almost broke down in tears. Stooping over, he crept to the window and listened; there was a cough from inside, Gao Ma’s voice! He opened the door. “Who? Oh, it’s you! You nearly scared me to death!” Gao Ma clutched her chest, calmed herself and sat on the bed. “Xiangzi, what happened?”

Xiangzi couldn’t answer, it felt as if he hadn’t seen her in years, a ball of hot air stuck in his chest.

“What happened?” Gao Ma looked like she, too, was about to cry: “You hadn’t come back, the master called to tell us to go to the Zuo house, and said you would come right away. When you arrived, wasn’t it me who opened the door? I saw you with a stranger, so I didn’t say anything and hurried in to help madam pack. You never did come inside. It was pitch black, so madam and I scrambled about, the young master was sleeping soundly, we had to pull him out of his warm bed. After packing and packing,Went back to the study to pick a painting, you never show your face, what's up with you? I'm asking you! I packed up in a hurry, came out to see you, fine, you were gone! Madam was so angry— and partly anxious— she was shaking all over. I had no choice but to call for a car. But we can't just do an 'empty city stratagem' and all leave. So fine, I argued with Madam a bit, told her to go, and I'll keep an eye. If Xiangzi comes back, I'll rush to the Zuo residence; if he doesn't, I'll just accept my fate! How could I say that!? What happened to you, speak up!”

Xiangzi had nothing to say.

“Talk! Just standing there in a daze, you think that's enough? What on earth happened?”

“You go!” Xiangzi finally managed to say something: “Just go!” “You'll watch the house?” Gao Ma's anger eased a bit.

“When you see sir, just tell him the detective caught me, but then, but then, didn't catch me!”

“What kind of talk is that?” Gao Ma was almost amused by her own anger.

“Listen!” Xiangzi snapped back: “Tell sir to run, the detective said for sure they could catch him. The Zuo residence isn’t a safe place either. Run, quick! After you leave, I’ll jump over to the Wang family and sleep there for a night. I'll lock up the big doors here. Tomorrow, I’ll go look for work. Apologies to Mr. Cao!”

“The more you say, the more confused I get!” Gao Ma sighed. “All right, I'm leaving, and the young master will freeze if I don't check on him— I'll hurry! When I see sir, I’ll say Xiangzi said to run quickly. Tonight Xiangzi will lock the main door and hop over to the Wang family to stay; tomorrow he goes job hunting. This is right, isn't it?”

Xiangzi nodded in deep shame.

After Gao Ma left, Xiangzi locked up the big gate and returned to the house. The broken clay wine jar was still on the floor; he picked up a piece of tile, looked at it, and tossed it back down as before. The bedding on the bed hadn’t been touched. Strange, what on earth was going on? Could it be that Detective Sun isn’t really a detective? Impossible! If Mr. Cao hadn’t sensed any danger, would he have gone so far as to abandon his home? Don’t understand! Don’t understand! He unconsciously sat down on the edge of the bed. The moment he sat, he sprang up as if startled. Can’t stay here long! What if that Sun guy comes back?! His mind raced: apologies to Mr. Cao, but with Gao Ma bringing a message telling him to run, that's good enough. In conscience, Xiangzi hadn’t meant to deceive anyone, and he himself was suffering. He’d lost his money first, he had no way to look after Mr. Cao anymore. Muttering to himself, he packed up his bedding as he spoke.

He shouldered the bedding and turned off the light, heading into the backyard. Putting down his bedding, he grabbed the top of the wall and whispered, “Old Cheng! Old Cheng!” Old Cheng was the Wang family’s coachman. No answer; Xiangzi made up his mind, he'd just jump over first. He tossed his bedding over, and it landed on the snow without much sound. His heart pounded for a moment. Then he climbed onto the wall and jumped over. He picked up his bedding from the snow and quietly went looking for Old Cheng. He knew where Old Cheng’s place was. Everyone seemed to be asleep, the entire yard was silent. Xiangzi suddenly realized being a thief wasn’t that hard; he got a bit braver, walked more boldly, his feet crunching a little on the firm snow. He found Old Cheng’s room and coughed. Old Cheng seemed to have just lain down: “Who is it?”

“It’s me, Xiangzi! Open up!” Xiangzi said it very naturally, gently, as if hearing Old Cheng’s voice felt like the comfort of a family member. Old Cheng turned on the light, threw on a battered sheepskin jacket and opened the door: “What’s up? Xiangzi! Showing up in the middle of the night!”

Xiangzi went in, put his bedding on the ground and sat down on it, having nothing more to say.

Old Cheng was in his thirties, with lumpy, hard muscles on his face and body. In daily life, Xiangzi and he weren’t especially close, just nodded and exchanged a few words when they met. Sometimes when Mrs. Wang and Mrs. Cao went out shopping together, the two of them had the chance to have tea and rest together. Xiangzi admired Old Cheng a lot—Old Cheng ran fast, but was always flustered, and never seemed to have a steady grip on the handles. As a person though, Old Cheng was a good guy, but with this flaw, Xiangzi could never completely respect him.

Today, Xiangzi found Old Cheng utterly lovable. Sitting there, he couldn’t say anything, but inside he felt grateful, close. Just now, he had stood on the bridge over Zhonghai Lake; now, he sat inside with an acquaintance; the sudden change left his heart empty and hot.

Old Cheng crawled back under his quilt, pointed to his battered sheepskin: “Xiangzi, have a smoke, there’s some in the pocket, not bought from a shop.” “Bieye” brand cigarettes had from the start been called “other people’s” by the rickshaw men.

Xiangzi didn’t smoke, but this time he couldn’t refuse; he took a cigarette, put it to his lips, and chewed on it.

“What happened?” Old Cheng asked: “Quit your job?”

“No,” Xiangzi still sat on his bedding, “something happened! Mr. Cao and his whole family ran away, and I didn’t dare watch the house alone!”

“What happened exactly?” Old Cheng sat up again.

“Hard to say, but it’s no small matter, even Gao Ma left!”

“All four gates wide open, nobody watching?”

“I locked the main gate!”

“Hmm!” Old Cheng thought for a while, “Should I tell Mr. Wang about this?” As he spoke, he started to put on his clothes.

“Let’s talk about it tomorrow, it's really hard to explain!” Xiangzi was afraid Mr. Wang would interrogate him.

The point that Xiangzi couldn’t explain was this: Mr. Cao taught a few classes at a university. One student there named Ruan Ming had always gotten along well with Mr. Cao, often came to chat. Mr. Cao was a socialist; Ruan Ming's views were even more radical, so they got along easily. However, their ages and positions caused some minor conflict: from Mr. Cao’s point of view as a teacher, he ought to teach diligently, while the student should hand in assignments properly—personal feelings couldn't affect grades. In Ruan Ming’s view, in this chaotic world, a young person with ideals should do revolutionary deeds, and grades didn’t matter for now. He visited Mr. Cao partly for good conversation, partly hoping that feelings would get him a passing grade, no matter how bad his test results were. Revolutionaries in troubled times often have some rascal streak; there are many such (perhaps forgivable) cases in history.

At exam time, Mr. Cao didn’t give Ruan Ming a passing mark. In fact, even if he had, Ruan Ming’s grades meant he would still be suspended. But Ruan Ming hated Mr. Cao particularly: he thought Mr. Cao didn’t understand ‘face’ at all; in China, ‘face’ is as valuable as revolution. Impatient for action, Ruan Ming looked down on study; so, over time, he got used to laziness, hoping to be admired and loved without any effort — after all, his ideas were progressive! To Ruan Ming, not giving him a passing grade meant Mr. Cao didn’t understand a young person with ambition. So, if they were friendly in daily life, why make him lose face at exam time? Since their usual friendship didn’t extend to the grades, he thought Mr. Cao was devious. There was no way to fix his grades now, or fight suspension, so he wanted to vent his anger on Mr. Cao. Since he’d lost his schooling, he would drag a teacher down with him. That way, he could make a show and demonstrate his own fierceness. Ruan Ming wasn’t one to be messed with! Plus, maybe this incident would get him into a new group — that was better than nothing.

He edited together what Mr. Cao had said in lectures and chats, all those comments on politics and society, and went to the Party office to report him — Mr. Cao was spreading radical ideas among the youth.

Mr. Cao had heard whispers of this, but found it funny. He knew exactly how half-baked his socialism was, and how his traditional love for the fine arts kept him from radical action. Ridiculous to be called a revolutionary mentor! Ludicrous, so he didn’t pay much mind, even though students and colleagues warned him to be careful. Calmness can’t — in turbulent times — guarantee safety. The winter holiday was a good time to purge universities, and detectives started investigating and making arrests. Mr. Cao had already felt several times that someone was following him. The shadow behind him put an end to his joking; he grew serious. He had to think: if it’s about making a name for himself, this was the chance; a few days in jail was easier and safer than setting off a bomb and just as valuable. Jail time is a qualification for being an important person. But he didn’t want that. He didn’t want to go along with it and create a fake reputation for himself. Conscience told him he hated not being a fighter; conscience told him he didn’t want to be a fake fighter. He went to see Mr. Zuo. Mr. Zuo had an idea: “When the time comes, just move in with me — they won’t dare search my place!” Mr. Zuo knew people; people are more powerful than the law. “Come live here for a few days, lay low. Then, once we smooth things out — might cost a bit — with the right connections and the money paid, you can go back home and it’ll be fine.”

Detective Sun knew Mr. Cao often went to the Zuo residence, and knew that in a pinch, he’d hide there. They didn't want to offend Mr. Zuo, but did want to scare Mr. Cao. If they could force him over to the Zuo residence, they could expect a payout and look good too. Squeezing Xiangzi wasn’t in the detectives’ plans, but since they saw him, quick cash was right there — why not grab ten or eight bucks while they could?

Right, Xiangzi just happened to run into it — tough luck. Everyone has their way, everywhere has loopholes, only Xiangzi can’t escape, because he’s a rickshaw puller. A rickshaw puller swallows coarse grain, bleeds like water; he must give all his strength for the lowest pay; must stand at the very bottom of society, left to be struck by everyone, everything, all hardships.

After finishing his cigarette, Xiangzi still couldn’t figure things out. He was like a chicken held by a cook, thinking only of catching his breath, with no other ideas. He wanted to talk to Old Cheng, but couldn’t, his words weren’t enough to express his feelings, he felt all the hardship but couldn’t open his mouth, like a mute. Bought a rickshaw, lost it; saved money, lost that too; all his effort only led to being cheated by others! Dared not provoke anyone, had to avoid even a stray dog, but in the end still got bullied to the limit! Best not to think about the past, but what about tomorrow? No going back to the Cao residence, so where to go? “Can I sleep here for one night?” he asked, like a stray dog finding a sheltered corner, just wanting to tough it out for a while; even so, he had to be careful, making sure he wasn’t bothering anyone.

“Stay here, then, where else are you going on a freezing night? The floor okay? Get on the bed if you want, squeeze in.”

Xiangzi didn’t want to squeeze up there; the floor was fine.

Old Cheng fell asleep, but Xiangzi tossed and turned, unable to rest. The cold from the ground soon made the mattress as hard as iron, he curled up, and his calves seemed likely to cramp. The cold wind blowing in from under the door felt like a swarm of fine needles stabbing his head. He squeezed his eyes shut, covered his head, but couldn’t sleep. Listening to Old Cheng snoring, he grew more agitated, wishing he could get up and beat Old Cheng to feel better. The cold grew and grew, his throat felt itchy from it, but he was afraid to cough for fear of waking Old Cheng.

Unable to sleep, he really wanted to sneak back to the Cao residence and take another look. Anyway, things were over, nobody was there, why not grab a few things? The money he’d scraped together so hard had been stolen, stolen because of the Cao family’s problems — so why shouldn’t he steal something in return? Lost his money over the Cao’s business, why not repay himself from the Cao family? The thought made his eyes gleam, and he forgot the cold. Go! It was so hard to earn that money, now he could get it back so easily — go!

He sat up, then hurriedly lay back down, as though Old Cheng was watching him! His heart started pounding. No, he couldn’t be a thief, he just couldn’t! He’d already let himself off the hook earlier by not following through on Mr. Cao’s request — that was bad enough; how could he go and steal, too? Can't do it! Rather die poor than steal!
