But how did he know others wouldn’t steal? If that Sun guy took some things, who would know? He sat up again. In the distance, a dog barked a few times. He lay down again. Still couldn’t do it — if others stole, so be it, his own conscience was clear. He was already this poor, no way could he add another dark mark to his heart!

Besides, Gao Ma knew he had gone to the Wang family; if something was lost from the Cao house overnight, he’d be blamed, whether or not he did it! Not only would he not steal, he now feared that someone else would break in. If anything really was stolen that night, even jumping into the Yellow River couldn’t wash him clean! He wasn’t cold now; instead, his palms were sweating a little. What to do? Jump back into the house to guard it? Didn’t dare. His life had been traded for by giving up money — he couldn’t run back to the trap. Don’t go — but what if something’s lost?

He couldn’t think of an answer. He sat up again, hugging his knees, his head nearly touching them. His head was heavy, his eyes wanted to close, but he didn’t dare sleep. The night was so long, and there wasn’t even a moment for Xiangzi to close his eyes.

He didn’t know how long he sat, how many ideas he went through. Suddenly his mind lit up — he reached out and pushed Old Cheng: “Old Cheng! Old Cheng! Wake up!”

“What?” Old Cheng really didn’t want to open his eyes: “If you’re peeing, there’s a chamber pot under the bed.”

“Wake up!”"Turn on the light!"

"Is there a thief or something?" Old Cheng sat up groggily.

"Are you fully awake?"

"Mm!"

"Old Cheng, take a look! This is my bedding, these are my clothes, this is the five yuan Mr. Cao gave me; there's nothing else, right?"

"Nothing else, why?" Old Cheng yawned.

"You fully awake? These are all my things, I haven't taken a blade of grass or a single stick from the Cao family?"

"Nothing! Us brothers, we've been eating at wealthy houses for so long, can we risk sticky fingers? If you can do the job, do it; if you can't, don't; you can't take people's things! Is that what this is about?"

"You see it clearly?"

Old Cheng laughed: "That's right! Say, aren't you cold?"

"I'm fine!"

[1]

A blow to the bridge of the nose is a guarantee.

Thirteen

Because of the reflection off the snow, it seemed like dawn arrived a bit earlier. With the year-end approaching, many families bought chickens to raise, and the sound of crowing was several times louder than usual. The constant crowing everywhere gave the feeling of a prosperous year with auspicious snow. But Xiangzi hadn't slept well all night. In the second half of the night, he forced himself to nap a few times, drifting in and out of sleep like floating on water, uneasy at heart. The more he slept, the colder he got; hearing roosters crow from all around, he couldn't hold out any longer. Not wanting to wake Old Cheng, he curled his legs up, pulling the quilt over his mouth and coughing, but didn't dare to get up. He endured it, waited, and felt extremely anxious inside. Finally, once it was light and the sound of cart wheels and the shouts of carters could be heard on the street, he sat up. Sitting wasn't enough to keep warm, so he stood up, did up his buttons, and cracked the door open for a look outside. The snow wasn't very deep, probably had stopped around midnight; the sky seemed clear, but it was gray and hard to see much, even the snow looked covered with a faint gray shadow. At a glance, he saw the big footprints he left last night; though partly covered by new snow, the depressions were still clearly visible.

Partly wanting something to do, and partly to cover up the traces, he didn't say a word, groped around a corner for a broom, and started sweeping the snow. The snow was heavy and not easy to sweep, and with no big bamboo brooms handy, he bent very low, scraping away with effort; the top layer came off, but the part near the ground still left some grains of snow, almost as if stuck to the earth. After straightening up twice, he had finished sweeping the whole outer yard, piling the snow under the two little willow trees. He broke a little sweat, felt warmer, and a bit lighter at heart. Stomping his feet, he exhaled deeply, his breath long and white.

Back in the room, he put the broom away and thought of rolling up his bedding. Old Cheng woke up, yawned before his mouth fully closed and said, "Not too early, is it?" in a voice that was complicated in tone. When he finished, he wiped his eyes, and with a casual gesture, took a cigarette out of his sheepskin coat pocket. Two puffs and he was fully awake. "Xiangzi, don't leave yet! Wait while I fetch some hot water so we can have some hot tea together. This night must've been tough for you!"

"I'll go, okay?" Xiangzi responded cheerfully. But just as he said it, he remembered last night's terror, and his heart knotted up tight.

"No; I'll go! I owe you, anyhow!" As he spoke, Old Cheng quickly got dressed, not bothering to fasten the buttons, just wrapped his tattered sheepskin robe with a sash, cigarette dangling from his mouth as he hurried out, "Hey! The yard's already all swept? You're something! Thanks for that!" Xiangzi felt a little better.

After a bit, Old Cheng returned with two big bowls of sweet millet porridge, and who knows how many chestnut cakes and little crispy fried crullers. "Didn't make tea yet, have some porridge first, come on, eat; if it's not enough, I'll get more; if we're out of cash, we can run up a tab; doing hard work, just don't pout, all right!"

The sky was fully bright; the room, coldly bright and deserted, echoed with the loud and satisfying sounds of the two eating their fill of bread and crullers. Neither said a word; the food disappeared in one go. "So how's it?" Old Cheng picked a sesame seed from between his teeth.

"Time to go!" Xiangzi looked at the bedding roll on the floor.

"So tell me— I still haven't figured out what exactly happened!" Old Cheng handed Xiangzi a cigarette, but Xiangzi shook his head.

Thinking it over, Xiangzi felt embarrassed not to tell Old Cheng everything. Stammering a bit, he repeated the events of the previous night; although he had a hard time, he didn't leave out anything important.

Old Cheng pursed his lips for a while, as if pondering over the details. "If you ask me, you should go to see Mr. Cao. You can't just drop things here, and you can't just let that money go! Didn't you say yourself that Mr. Cao told you, if anything went wrong, just run? So if you got held up by detectives the moment you got off, who's to blame? It's not that you were disloyal, it's just that things went sideways, and you had no choice but to save your own skin first! As I see it, you haven't done anyone wrong. Go, look for Mr. Cao, tell him everything, every last detail, I think he can't possibly blame you, and maybe by good luck he'll even make up your loss! Go on, leave your bedding here, go find him early. The day's short, once the sun's out it's already eight—better get going!"

Xiangzi felt revived, though still a bit guilty toward Mr. Cao; but Old Cheng's reasoning was sound— when a detective points a gun at you, how could you worry about the Cao family business? "Go on!" Old Cheng urged again. "I figure you got yourself twisted up last night; anyone would get confused in an emergency. Look, my advice is guaranteed to be right, I've seen a little more of the world than you have. Go on, the sun's out already, isn't it?"

A ray of morning sun, reflected off the snow, illuminated the whole city. Blue sky, white snow, light in the sky, light on the snow, a field of golden sparkles dancing between blue and white, so dazzling you couldn't keep your eyes open! Just as Xiangzi was about to leave, someone knocked. Old Cheng went to check, calling into the entryway, "Xiangzi! Someone's here for you!"

It was Wang Er from the Zuo residence, his nose running from the cold, stomping the snow off his feet in the entry. Seeing Xiangzi come out, Old Cheng invited, "Come inside, both of you!" and the three went into the room.

"Well," Wang Er said, rubbing his hands, "I'm here to check the house, but how do I get in, the main gate's locked. Well, it's freezing cold after the snow! Anyway, Mr. Cao, Mrs. Cao, they both left early this morning; to Tianjin, or maybe Shanghai, I don't really know. Mr. Zuo told me to check on the house. Anyway, it's really cold!"

All of a sudden, Xiangzi felt like crying! Just as he was about to follow Old Cheng's advice and look for Mr. Cao, Mr. Cao was already gone. He dazed for a long time, then asked, "Did Mr. Cao say anything about me?"

"No, well, no. Nobody said anything, it was still dark and everyone was rushing around. The train, well, it left at 7:40! Anyway, how do I get over to the yard?" Wang Er was eager to get going.

"Jump over!" Xiangzi glanced at Old Cheng, as if handing Wang Er over to him, and picked up his own bedding roll.

"Where are you headed?" Old Cheng asked.

"The Renhe rickshaw yard, there's nowhere else I can go!" That one sentence summed up all of Xiangzi's grievances, shame, and helplessness. He had no other options, he just had to surrender! All his roads were closed off; on the white snow he could only go look for that tower-like Huniu. He cared about dignity, pride, loyalty, and loyalty to friends; none of it did him any good, because at the end of the day he was just a stray dog!

Old Cheng chimed in, "Go on then. Here with Wang Er to see, you didn't touch a thing at the Cao house! Off you go. If you're ever back on this street, drop by for a chat—maybe I'll hear of a good job and recommend you. After you leave, I'll see Wang Er over there. Got any coal?"

"Coal and firewood are all in the back shed." Xiangzi lifted up his bedding.

The snow on the street didn't look so white anymore; on the road, tracks from the cart wheels exposed patches of ice. On the dirt lane, patches where horses' hooves had been were now black and white, what a pity. Xiangzi didn't think about anything, just kept moving forward, carrying his bedding. Without pause, he walked straight to the Renhe rickshaw depot. He didn't dare stop—he knew if he stopped, he wouldn't have the nerve to go in. He just walked in, his face burning hot. He'd prepared something to say to Huniu: "I'm back—do as you like! Whatever you want, I've got no other way!" But when he saw her, he ran these words through his mind a dozen times, but he still couldn't say them out loud; words just didn't come that easily.

Huniu had just gotten up, her hair disheveled, eyelids puffy, her dark face covered in tiny white goosebumps, like a frozen chicken with its feathers plucked.

"Oh! You're back!" she greeted him with unusual warmth, her eyes sparkling with mirth. "Give me a rickshaw to drive!" Xiangzi kept his head down, staring at the snow that hadn't quite melted from his shoes.

"Go talk to the old man." She spoke softly, then nodded eastward.

Old Liu Si was in the room drinking tea, a big white stove in front of him, flames leaping up more than half a foot. Seeing Xiangzi come in, he half-scolded, half-laughed: "Boy, you're still alive?! Forgot about me, huh? Count it up, how many days since you last showed up? So, how's it going? Did you buy your rickshaw yet?"

Xiangzi shook his head, pain shooting through his heart. "Gotta get a rickshaw to drive again, Boss Liu!"

"Hmph, things fell apart again, huh! Fine, go pick one yourself!" Liu Si poured himself a cup of tea, "Here, drink a bowl first."

Xiangzi picked up the bowl, stood in front of the stove, and drank in big gulps. The tea was scalding, the flames burning hot, and Xiangzi started feeling a bit sleepy. He set down the bowl and was about to head out when Liu Si called him back.

"Hold up a sec, what are you rushing for? Listen, you showed up at the perfect time. The 27th's my birthday, I'm going to set up a birthday tent, invite some guests. Lend me a hand for a few days, don't worry about pulling a rickshaw yet. Those fellows," Liu Si pointed to the yard, "can't be relied on, I don't want those slackers stirring up trouble. You help out, all right. Do what needs doing, don't wait for me to say. Go sweep the snow first, I'll treat you to hotpot for lunch." "Yes, Boss Liu!" Xiangzi made up his mind, since he was back here, he'd leave everything to Liu Si and his daughter; whatever they wanted from him was fine, he'd accept his fate! "Am I right?" Huniu came in just then, clutching a handful of something, "Still the best is Xiangzi, others just don't measure up."

Liu Si laughed. Xiangzi hanged his head even lower.

"Come, Xiangzi!" Huniu called to him from outside, "Here's some money, go buy a broom, get a bamboo one, easier for sweeping snow. Get it swept fast, the tent-builders are coming today." As they walked to her room, she counted out the money for Xiangzi and whispered, "Buck up! Get on the old man's good side! Our thing's got some hope!"

Xiangzi didn't reply, but neither was he upset. It was as if he'd given up, not thinking about anything, just muddling through another day. If there was food, eat; if there was drink, drink; if there was work, do it; keep his hands and feet busy, go round and round till nightfall, like a donkey at a mill, knowing nothing but pulling forward.

He realized, too, that no matter what he did, he couldn't be truly happy. Even though he refused to think, to talk, to lose his temper, there was always something stuck in his chest, only forgotten when he worked, but always reappearing whenever he had a spare moment—soft but always just as big; tasteless, yet choking, like a piece of sponge. With that sponge stuck in his chest, he forced himself to work, hoping to make himself so tired he couldn't move, so he could sleep in a stupor. Leaving the night’s problems to dreams, the day’s to his hands and feet, he seemed like a living dead man, able to work but nothing more. He swept snow, bought things, booked the gas lamps, cleaned rickshaws, moved tables and chairs, ate Liu Si's reward meal, slept—knowing nothing, saying nothing, thinking nothing, only vaguely aware of that sponge-like thing in his heart!

The snow in the yard was swept clean; the snow on the roof gradually melted; the tent-builders climbed up to erect the frame, calling out their directions. The agreement was for a heated tent stretching across the yard, with eaves on three sides, railings and glass windows on three sides. Inside the tent were glass partitions, decorative screens, and every bit of wood covered in red cloth. Colorful banners were hung at every entrance, and the kitchen was set up in the back yard. Since Liu Si wanted to celebrate his sixtieth in style, the first order of business was putting up a decent tent. The days were short, so by sundown the tent-builders had only managed to assemble the framework, railings, and cloth; the fancy work inside and the banners at the door would have to wait till the next morning. For this, Liu Si got impatient with the workers, yelling himself red in the face. Because of this, he sent Xiangzi to hurry the gas lamp guy and the cook, insisting nothing must go wrong. In fact, neither one would have botched their jobs, but the old man was nervous. Xiangzi had just run back from one errand when Liu Si sent him out to borrow mahjong sets, needing three or four for a proper party. Once the tiles were borrowed, he was sent to borrow a phonograph, since there had to be a bit of music for a birthday celebration. Xiangzi hadn't stopped running all day, clear through to eleven at night. Used to pulling a rickshaw, he found running errands without a load even more exhausting; on the last trip, even he himself could barely lift his feet anymore. "Good kid! You're something! If I had a son like you, I'd be glad to die a few years earlier! Go rest up, there's more to do tomorrow!"

Off to the side, Huniu shot Xiangzi a wink.

The next morning, the tent-builders came to finish the job. The decorative panels were hung up, painted with battle scenes from the Three Kingdoms: the three attacks on Lu Bu, Changban Slope, the burning of the linked camps, and so on, all sorts of warriors on horseback with swords and spears. Old Liu looked them over, head tilted up, feeling very pleased. Right on their heels, a shop arrived to deliver rentals: eight seats inside the tent, matching aprons, chair and stool covers all of bright red embroidery. The birthday altar went in the main room, incense burners and candlesticks all cloisonné enamel, with four red felt mats in front of the table. Immediately, Liu Si told Xiangzi to go buy a basket of apples; Huniu secretly gave him two yuan and told him to order longevity peaches and noodles, with one set of Eight Immortals on the peaches, making it look like Xiangzi's gift. The apples came back and were arranged at once; before long, the birthday peaches and noodles also arrived, placed behind the apples, the huge birthday peach painted red and adorned with the Eight Immortals, a magnificent sight.

"From Xiangzi, see how thoughtful he is!" Huniu boasted behind her father's back, and Liu Si smiled kindly at Xiangzi.

The only thing missing at the center of the altar was the birthday character plaque, traditionally contributed by friends, not provided by the host. None had arrived yet; Liu Si, who was impatient, was on the verge of losing his temper: "Whenever someone's throwing a red or white event, I always show up ahead of time, but when it comes to my own business, I get left high and dry,"×His mother!”

“Tomorrow’s the 26th, that’s when we seat the guests, what’s the rush?” Tiger Girl called out in reassurance. “I’d rather have everything set up at once; it’s so nerve-racking to see it all half-done and scattered! I’m telling you, Xiangzi, the gas lamp[2] has to be in place today. If they don’t come by four o’clock, I’ll skin them!”

“Xiangzi, go hurry them along again!” Tiger Girl deliberately depended on him, always ordering Xiangzi to do things in front of her father. Xiangzi didn’t say a word, understood what she wanted, and left.

“It’s not just me saying, Old Master,” she pursed her mouth, “if I’d had a son, he’d have to be like Xiangzi! Too bad I landed in the wrong body. Nothing I can do about it. Actually, having a foster son like Xiangzi isn’t bad either! Look at him, never utters a peep all day, yet he gets everything done!”

Liu the Fourth didn’t answer, thought for a moment: “Where’s the phonograph? Play a tune!”

No one knew where the beat-up gramophone was borrowed from, every sound it made was like stepping on a cat’s tail, screeching right into your nerves! Liu the Fourth didn’t care, as long as there was some noise, it was fine.

By the afternoon, everything was ready, just waiting for the cook to come and start the seating the next day. Liu the Fourth made a tour of every spot, everywhere was filled with colorful decorations, and he nodded to himself in satisfaction. That night, he went to invite the bookkeeper from Tianshun Coal Yard, Mr. Feng, a meticulous accountant from Shanxi. Mr. Feng came right over, looked around, and told Xiangzi to go buy two red account books and a piece of bright red paper. He cut the paper and wrote some “longevity” characters to stick in various places. Liu the Fourth thought Mr. Feng was really attentive and wanted to invite him for a couple of rounds of mahjong. Mr. Feng, knowing Liu the Fourth’s prowess, didn’t dare accept. Since no game was played, Liu the Fourth was a bit annoyed and called over a few rickshaw pullers, “Let’s play some bao, do you have any guts or not?”

Everyone wanted in, but no one dared join up with Liu the Fourth, everyone knew he used to run a gambling den!

“You lot, how did you even stay alive!” The old man lost his temper. “When I was your age, even without a penny I’d still play—lose first, talk later; come on!”

“For small change?” A rickshaw puller asked tentatively.

“Keep your change, Liu the Fourth doesn’t play with kids!” The old man downed a cup of tea in one gulp and rubbed his bald head. “Forget it, even if you invited me, I wouldn’t come! Here’s the thing, you go tell the others: tomorrow’s the seating, friends and relatives will arrive by midday, everyone must put away their rickshaws by four o’clock—no running in and out, crowding the place! Tomorrow’s rickshaw fees are cancelled, put away by four. Think of it as a free day’s work; but while you’re at it, recite some good wishes for me from your hearts, don’t be ungrateful! On the real big day after, no one’s allowed to pull rickshaws. At eight-thirty sharp, you get the first meal: six big bowls, two seven-inch dishes, four small side plates, one pot; good enough for you all! Dress up in long robes, anyone in scruffy clothes gets kicked out! When you’re done, get out, so I can take care of the guests. Friends and relatives get three big bowls, six cold cuts, six fried dishes, four large bowls, and a pot. Let me make it clear now, don’t get greedy. Friends and relatives are in a separate category; I don’t want anything from you. Those with some conscience can give ten copper coins as a gift, I won’t think it’s too little; or, if you’ve nothing to give, bow three times, I’ll take it too. Just behave properly, got it? If you still want to eat later, come back after six, whatever’s left is yours; but don’t come back early! Understood?” “What if we have to work late tomorrow, Fourth Master,” a middle-aged rickshaw puller asked, “why do we have to put away our rickshaws by four?”

“If you work late, come back after eleven! Just don’t crowd around when there are people in the tent! You pull rickshaws, I, Liu the Fourth, don’t pull with you, get it?”

No one had anything more to say, but they couldn’t find an excuse to walk out, just standing there awkwardly; Liu the Fourth’s words left a knot of anger and resentment in everyone’s heart. Even though missing a day’s rickshaw fees was a good deal, who wants a free meal if you still have to give at least forty copper as a gift; besides, Liu the Fourth’s words were so harsh, as if they should all scurry away and hide like rats for his birthday. What’s more, the real celebration on the 27th they weren’t allowed to work, just at the year-end when business is brisk; Liu the Fourth could afford to lose a day’s income, but a whole day was too much for the rest of them![3]

Everyone dared not complain but just stood there, not in the least giving Liu the Fourth any good wishes.

Tiger Girl pulled Xiangzi aside, and he followed her out.

Everyone’s anger suddenly found an outlet, and they all stared at Xiangzi’s back. For these past two days, everyone felt Xiangzi was now the Liu family’s lackey, desperate to curry favor, eager to do every odd job.[4] Xiangzi didn’t realize any of this, he helped the Lius as a way of dealing with his own frustration; he had nothing to say to the others in the evening because he genuinely had nothing to say. They didn’t understand his grievances and thought he was just sucking up to Liu the Fourth, so disdained talking to him. Tiger Girl’s special treatment of Xiangzi made everyone even more sour, especially thinking about the current situation, Liu the Fourth not allowing them to mingle in the celebration tent, but Xiangzi would certainly get a whole day of good food; same rickshaw pullers, yet there are still ranks and classes! Look, Miss Liu is calling Xiangzi out again! Everyone’s eyes followed Xiangzi, their legs even wanted to move, so they shuffled out. Miss Liu was talking to Xiangzi under the gas lamp, so they all nodded to one another.

[1]

 When the time is right, meaning the right moment has come.

[2]

 Water-moon lamp, meaning gas lamp.

[3]

 “Paoy” — meaning to idle away the day on purpose.

[4]

 “Sui cui” — meaning doing all the odd jobs.

Fourteen

The Liu family’s event was lively. Liu the Fourth was very satisfied to have so many people bowing their heads to wish him a happy birthday. What made him prouder was that many old friends had come especially to offer congratulations. From these old friends, he saw that not only was his event busy and crowded, but it was also “improved.” Those old friends were wearing outdated clothing, while Fourth Master’s furs and mandarin jackets were all newly made. Professionally speaking, quite a few of his friends used to be better off than he was, but now—over these two or three decades—they’d slipped further and further down; some hardly got enough to eat these days. Seeing them, then looking at his own celebration tent, the birthday hall, the Long Banpo battle scene screen, and the three-bowl banquet spread, he truly felt himself a cut above the rest, that he had “improved.” Even the gambling, he’d prepared mahjong—the very tiles themselves carried a sophistication over the old dice games. Yet, even in this crowded excitement, he also felt a tinge of sadness. Having grown used to living alone, at first he’d thought only the shopkeepers and their clerks, and his old professional acquaintances would come on his birthday. He hadn’t counted on some female guests showing up. Although Tiger Girl could help entertain, he suddenly felt his own loneliness—no wife, only a daughter, and she looked like a man. If Tiger Girl had been a son, of course, she’d have started a family by now, had kids, so even if he was an old widower he wouldn’t be so utterly alone. Yes, he lacked nothing—except a son. The older he grew, the less likely it was he’d have one. A birthday celebration should be a happy occasion, but it felt like he should be in tears. No matter how much he’d “improved,” if there was no one to continue his work, then all this busy effort was for nothing.

All morning, he was delighted. As everyone celebrated his birthday, he received it grandly, as if he were the king among the heroes. By the afternoon, his happiness wore off a bit. Watching the lady guests with their children, he felt envy, jealousy, and was afraid to get close to the kids; yet not being close felt awkward. He wanted to vent, but didn’t want to make a scene in front of the guests—he knew he was a man of the trade, and couldn’t disgrace himself in front of friends and relatives. He wished the day would end quickly, so he wouldn’t have to suffer anymore.

It was almost a perfect day, except in the morning, when the meal for the rickshaw pullers nearly turned into a fight with Xiangzi.

Dinner started a bit after eight, and the rickshaw pullers weren’t happy about it. Even though they’d been excused from paying the rickshaw fee the day before, not a single person showed up empty-handed today—whether a full dime, or just forty coins, everyone gave some money as a gift. Usually, they were the laborers, Liu the boss; today, as they saw it, they were the guests and shouldn’t be treated this way.

On top of that, as soon as they finished eating, they were told to leave—not even allowed to pull out their rickshaws, at the end of the year, no less!

Xiangzi knew perfectly well he wasn’t expected to leave with them after eating, but he wanted to dine with everyone else. First, it let him finish faster and get to work, second, it showed camaraderie. When he sat down with the group, everyone directed their dissatisfaction with Liu the Fourth onto him. As soon as they took their seats, someone said: “Hey, you’re our honored guest now, how come you’re sitting with the likes of us?” Xiangzi just gave a dumb smile, not catching the meaning in the words. These days, he hadn’t said anything at all, so his mind seemed to be running slow. No one dared get mad at Liu the Fourth, so they settled for venting their anger by eating more; they couldn’t get more food, but surely they could drink as much as they wanted—celebration wine! They all had the common idea of drinking to kill their frustration. Some drank in silence, others played drinking games; Liu the old man couldn’t stop them from playing. Xiangzi watched them drink, and not wanting to stand out, joined for two cups. As everyone drank, their eyes grew red and their mouths got loose. Someone said, “Xiangzi, Camel, you’ve got it made! All-day meals, serving the old man and young lady! Soon you won’t need to pull a rickshaw at all, best to just stay with the family!” Xiangzi caught the drift, but didn’t take it to heart; since the first day he started at the stand, he’d decided he wouldn’t pretend to be a hero anymore, just leave everything to fate. Let people say what they wanted. He kept it bottled up. Someone else then said, “Xiangzi’s got his own way, we make money with our muscle, he’s got inside connections!” The group all burst out laughing. Xiangzi sensed they were attacking him, but after all the humiliation he’d suffered, why care about a few snide remarks—he still didn’t say a word. Those at the next table caught on and one of them craned his neck and shouted: “Xiangzi, when you become the boss, remember your old pals!” Xiangzi didn’t reply, but someone at his own table said, “Say something, Camel!”

Xiangzi’s face flushed, and he mumbled, “How could I become the boss?!”

“Hmph, why not? Looks to me like you’re headed right for it, dun-dun-cha![1]” Xiangzi didn’t quite follow what “dun-dun-cha” meant, but intuitively guessed they were referring to his relationship with Tiger Girl. His face went from red to white, and all the humiliation he’d suffered before suddenly came flooding back, all blocked inside. A few days of patient silence seemed impossible to keep up any longer; he felt like a reservoir full to bursting, looking for a place to break out. Just then, one of the rickshaw pullers pointed at his face and said, “Xiangzi, I mean you— you really are ‘a mute eating dumplings— knows how it tastes on the inside’. Isn’t that right? Say for yourself, Xiangzi? Xiangzi?”

Xiangzi suddenly stood up, his face pale, and said to the man, “Let’s go outside and talk, if you dare?”

Everyone froze. They’d meant to poke fun at him, stir things up a bit, but no one had planned on a fight.

Suddenly, everything went quiet, like birds in the woods suddenly spotting a hawk. Xiangzi stood alone, much taller than the others, and he felt his isolation. But anger surged in his chest, and he was convinced that even if all of them ganged up on him, they wouldn’t be able to take him. He added, “Any of you dared, want to come outside?”

Suddenly the crowd got the point, and all at once: “Enough, Xiangzi, we’re messing with you!”

Liu the Fourth saw what was happening: “Sit down, Xiangzi!” Then to the group, “Don’t pick on those who seem quiet, if you upset me I’ll throw all of you out! Eat up!” Xiangzi left the table. Everyone darted glances at Old Liu and dug into their food. Before long, the chatter started up again, like birds twittering softly after the danger had passed.

Xiangzi crouched at the door for quite a while, waiting for them. If anyone dared mouth off again, he’d sock them—since he’d lost everything, he might as well go all out![2] But everyone just left in little groups, no one looking for him. Even though there’d been no fight, he felt he’d relieved a bit of the pressure. Thinking again, though, he’d probably made a lot of enemies today. Normally, he had no real friends, which was why he had nowhere to pour out his troubles; how could he afford to make more enemies? He felt a little regret. The food he’d just eaten sat heavily in his stomach, giving him a little pain. He stood up—so what, people who’re always fighting and making a scene somehow live pretty interesting lives! Does being honest and proper always get you anywhere? With such thoughts, he drew a new path for himself in his mind—this new Xiangzi was nothing like what he’d hoped to be before. A rickshaw puller who made friends everywhere, got by on tricks, drank other people’s tea, smoked other people’s cigarettes, borrowed money and never paid it back, didn’t dodge for automobiles, peed wherever he felt like, played cat and mouse with the cops all day and never minded spending a couple of days in the district jail. Yes, those kind of rickshaw pullers survived, even thrived— at the very least, they were happier than Xiangzi. Fine, then: honesty, rules, ambition, since they were all pointless, being a scoundrel isn’t so bad after all. Not bad at all, Xiangzi thought—even kind of heroic: no fear of heaven or earth, never bowing to take an unfair loss. That’s right! That’s how it should be! Bad apples are just good people who’ve been hacked down. He actually regretted a bit that no fight broke out. No hurry, from now on, he wouldn’t bow his head to anyone.

[1]

 “Dun-dun-cha”—the sound of celebratory drums, here implying progressing fast, or even getting married off.

[2]

 “Bu lun yangzi” — meaning “don’t care what happens.”

Liu Sanye cannot tolerate even a speck of dust in his eyes. After putting together everything he had heard and seen, he already understood most of what was happening. Over the past few days, his daughter had been especially obedient—hmph, because Xiangzi had returned! Just look at her eyes, always following him around. Keeping this matter in his heart, the old man felt even more desolate and miserable. Just think about it, he never had a son, so he couldn’t really pull together a bustling family; what if his daughter left with someone else! His whole life’s efforts would have been for nothing! Xiangzi was indeed decent, but when it came to being a son-in-law, he was still far from good enough; just a filthy rickshaw puller! After struggling all his life, fighting in brawls, kneeling on iron chains, in the end, to let some unrefined country boy take both his daughter and his property? Over his dead body! Even if it were possible, he wouldn’t let it happen under his roof! Liu Si has always been someone who farts into the toilet pit since he was a kid!

In the mid-afternoon, at around three or four o’clock, more guests arrived to celebrate his birthday, but the old man already felt bored and disinterested. The more the guests complimented him on his robust health and good fortune, the less meaning it all had for him.

After nightfall, the guests gradually departed, leaving only a dozen or so close friends and neighbors who lived nearby. Looking at the empty canopy in the courtyard, illuminated with a bluish tinge by the lanterns, and the tables now stripped of their aprons, the old man felt lonely and bored. It was as if he could see the scene when he’d died—nothing different from now, except the joy canopy would become a mourning one, and in front of the coffin, there would be no descendants kneeling in mourning, just some unrelated people playing mahjong through the night! He really wanted to chase out the remaining guests; while he still had some life left in him, he should assert his dignity! Yet, in the end, he was too embarrassed to unleash his anger on friends. So his rage took another form—the more he looked at his daughter, the more irritated he became. Xiangzi was sitting under the canopy, acting all proper, with the scar on his face glowing like a piece of jade in the lamplight. The old man just couldn’t stand to look at the pair—they made his skin crawl!

Tigress had always been boisterous and wild, but today her appearance was tidied from head to foot, and she had to put on a prim and proper act to entertain the guests. Partly to win everyone’s praise, and also to show off in front of Xiangzi. For the first half of the day, she actually found it kind of interesting, but as the day wore on and she got a bit tired, she started getting annoyed, even feeling the urge to lash out at someone. By evening, she had completely lost her patience, her eyebrows furrowed and standing upright.

After seven o’clock, Liu Sanye started to feel drowsy, but unwilling to admit his age, he refused to go to bed. When everyone invited him to join a few hands of cards, he declined—not due to fatigue, but saying that cards were boring, and that betting or Pai Gow was more his style. Since no one wanted to switch games halfway through, he had no choice but to sit aside. To perk himself up, he drank a few more cups, repeatedly claiming he hadn’t eaten enough, and complaining that the cook was making too much money for food that wasn’t substantial. From there, he began to overturn all the satisfaction he had felt earlier in the day: the canopy, the tableware, the cook, and everything else were not worth the money; they were all ripping him off—what a waste!

Mr. Feng, the accountant, had the tally ready: twenty-five scrolls with birthday couplets, three platters of longevity peaches and noodles, a jar of birthday wine, two pairs of longevity candles, and about twenty yuan in cash gifts. There were plenty of gifts, but most of them were only forty copper coins or a dime in silver.

Hearing this, Liu Sanye got even angrier. If he had known, he should have just served stir-fried noodles! People eat at a table with three large bowls and only give a dime as a gift? That’s just making a fool out of the old man! From now on, he’d never host another event; he’s not going to swallow this humiliation for nothing! Needless to say, both relatives and friends just wanted to eat for free at his expense; sixty-nine years old, sharp all his life, but muddled for a moment, letting a bunch of monkeys and bastards eat him out of house and home! The more he thought about it, the angrier he got. Even the satisfaction he’d felt during the day now seemed like foolishness; thinking this way, he started mumbling curses, using words from the streets that were already out of vogue.

His friends hadn’t all left yet, and Tigress, wanting to save face for everyone, tried to stop her father from losing his temper. But seeing that everyone seemed focused on their cards and apparently paid no attention to the old man’s mutterings, she thought it best not to speak, lest she accidentally make things worse. Let him mumble—it’s best to just let it pass.

Who would have thought, as the old man rambled on, he started turning his anger toward her. She decided not to take it lying down! He hosted this birthday, and she had worked herself ragged for several days, yet got nothing in return—she couldn’t just let that slide! Sixty-nine or seventy-nine, it didn’t matter; there had to be some reason! She immediately shot back:

“You were the one who wanted to throw this party—what’s that got to do with me?”

The old man, faced with her retort, perked up. “What’s it got to do with you? Don’t play dumb with me! You think I don’t see anything?”

“What did you see? I’ve been exhausted all day, and now you want to take it out on me—just wait! Well, then, say it. What did you see?” The fatigue in Tigress was suddenly gone, and her tongue was sharp as ever.

“Don’t pretend you don’t know! I saw everything a long time ago, hmph!”

“Why would I be jealous?!” she said, shaking her head. “What is it you think you saw?”

“There! Isn’t it obvious?” Liu Si pointed toward the canopy—Xiangzi was bending down, sweeping the floor. “Him, huh?” Tigress’s heart skipped a beat; she hadn’t realized her father’s eyes were so sharp. “Hmph! So what about him?”

“Don’t act dumb with me!” the old man stood up. “If he stays, I’m leaving; if I stay, he goes! I’m your father! I have to take charge!”

Tigress hadn’t expected things to escalate so soon; her plan was barely halfway through, and her father had already called it out! What to do now? Her face turned red—splotchy and dark with half-smeared powder under the cold gleam of the light, like a piece of overcooked liver, complex and ugly. She felt tired, but this standoff suddenly made her angrier, too flustered to come up with a plan. Still, she couldn’t just give in—better to choose an unwise move than have no move at all. She had never kowtowed to anyone before! So be it, just be straightforward for once—let the chips fall where they may! “It’s better to air it all out today—just settle this one way or another! What are you going to do? I’d like to hear it! This is a problem you created, don’t say I was the one looking for trouble!”

The card players seemed to hear the argument between father and daughter, but couldn’t bear to be distracted. To drown out their voices, they played their cards harder and shouted, “Red tiles, pong...” Xiangzi understood what was happening, but kept his head down, sweeping the floor. He made up his mind—if it came to it, he’d fight!

“You’re deliberately trying to make me angry!” the old man’s eyes were bulging. “Drive me to death, so you can get what you want? Don’t even think about it, I’ll live a few more years yet!” “Cut the crap, what are you going to do?” Tigress’s heart was pounding, but her words were tough. “What will I do? Didn’t I already say, if he stays, I go; if I stay, he goes! I’m not letting this stinking rickshaw puller have it all!”

Xiangzi threw down the broom and stood upright, staring at Liu Si. “Who are you talking about?” Liu Si burst out laughing: “Ha, you brat, are you trying to rebel? Who do you think I’m talking about? You! Get out now! I was willing to give you face, but how dare you try and take advantage here, do you even know who I am? Get lost! Never let me see you here again, coming here looking for a free ride, huh?”

The old man’s voice was so loud that it attracted a few rickshaw pullers who wanted to watch the commotion. The card players thought Liu Sanye was arguing with another rickshaw puller and still didn’t bother to look up.

Xiangzi wasn’t good at talking; he had a lot he wanted to say, but couldn’t get a single word out. He just stood there, swallowing hard. “Get out! Go! Coming here looking for a free ride? I was out on the streets before you were even born, hmph!” The old man was half bluffing—his hatred for Xiangzi wasn’t as strong as the anger toward his daughter. Even in his rage, he couldn’t help but feel that Xiangzi was actually a decent guy.

“Fine, I’ll go!” Xiangzi had nothing more to say and hurried out; in an argument, he couldn’t possibly win.

The rickshaw pullers had come to watch and, seeing Liu Sanye scolding Xiangzi, still remembered what had happened that morning, feeling quite pleased. But when they heard the old man was throwing Xiangzi out, they took Xiangzi’s side—after all he had done, to be cast away now, seemed unfair. Some came over to ask, “What happened, Xiangzi?” Xiangzi shook his head.

“Xiangzi, wait up!” Suddenly, Tigress got an idea: now that her plan wasn’t working, she’d have to move quickly and grab hold of Xiangzi—better to act fast and not lose both ways! “You and I are like two grasshoppers tied on the same string—neither of us can get away! Wait, let me be clear!” She turned to her father: “Let’s make it plain—I’m already with Xiangzi! Wherever he goes, I go too! So, are you going to give me to him, or throw us both out? Let’s hear it from you—just one word!”

Tigress hadn’t expected things to escalate so quickly or to play her trump card so early. Liu Sanye was even more shocked at how far things had gone. But given the situation, he couldn’t back down, especially with everyone watching. “You call that shamelessness? You embarrass me so much my old face is burning for you!” He slapped himself. “Bah! Absolutely shameless!”

The card players paused, sensing something was really off, but confused about what was happening and unable to chime in. Some stood up, some stared blankly at their cards.

Now that everything was out, Tigress actually felt relieved. “Shameless? Don’t make me air your dirty laundry! Have you ever done anything right? This is my first time, and it’s all your fault—boys should marry, girls too, but you’re sixty-nine, and what have you done? That’s life! Look, with everyone here,” she gestured around, “let’s clear things up now, get it all out in the open! In front of this joy canopy, why not just have another celebration!”

“Me?” Liu Sanye’s face turned from red to white, calling up all his old bravado: “I’d rather set fire to this canopy than let you have it!” “Fine!” Tigress’s lips trembled, her voice ugly, “If I pack up and leave, how much money will you give me?”

“The money is mine—I’ll give it to whoever I want!” The old man’s heart ached at the thought of his daughter leaving, but to save face, he steeled himself.

“Your money? I’ve helped you all these years. Without me, your money would have all gone to some other woman—let’s be fair!” She looked at Xiangzi, “You say something!”

Xiangzi stood there, stiff as a board, speechless.

[1]

 Drum and cymbal sounds at a wedding, symbolic of a marriage.

[2]

 It means “whoever,” no matter who.

Fifteen

When it came to fighting, Xiangzi couldn’t hit an old man, nor could he hit a woman. He had nowhere to use his strength. As for acting shamelessly, that was only in his mind; he couldn’t pull it off in real life. As for Tigress, by rights he could just stomp his foot and leave her. But seeing how she’d fallen out with her father for his sake and was willing to leave with him; as far as anyone could tell, she was sacrificing herself for Xiangzi. In front of everyone, he couldn’t help but show a bit of heroism. He couldn’t say anything, just stood there waiting for everything to be settled—at the very least, he had to do that to be a real man.

The Liu family father and daughter were left just staring at each other—nothing more to say; Xiangzi stayed silent. The rickshaw pullers, no matter whose side they were on, couldn’t really break in. The card players finally had to say something, as the silence was becoming unbearable. Still, all they could do was gloss things over, urge both sides not to be angry, to discuss things calmly, that nothing was insurmountable. That’s all they could offer; they couldn’t solve anything, nor did they wish to. Seeing that neither side would give way, it was obvious: an honest official can’t settle a family dispute. Time to slip away when an opening came.

Before everyone had gone, Tigress grabbed Mr. Feng of the Tianshun Coal Factory: “Mr. Feng, doesn’t your place have some room? Let Xiangzi stay there for a couple of days. We’ll deal with our business quickly, not take up your space for long. Xiangzi, go with Mr. Feng and I’ll see you tomorrow, we’ll talk about our future. Let me be clear, if I get married again, I’ll only go out of this house in a bridal sedan chair! Mr. Feng, I’m leaving him with you, and I’ll come for him tomorrow!”

Mr. Feng inhaled sharply, not wanting this responsibility. Xiangzi, anxious to leave, just said, “I won’t run away!”

Tigress glared at her father, went back to her room, and began wailing at the top of her lungs, locking the door from inside.

Mr. Feng and the others persuaded Liu Sanye inside, where he brought out his old confidence to ask everyone to stay for a few more drinks: “Don’t worry, everyone, from now on she’s her own person and I’m mine—I’ll never argue with her again. If she leaves, I’ll treat it as if I never had a daughter. I’ve spent my life out in the world, and now my reputation is ruined by her! Twenty years ago I’d have killed them both! Now, let her go; as for getting even a penny from me, not a chance! Not a single cent! Let’s see how she survives! She’ll learn for herself whether a father or some wild man is better! Don’t leave yet, have another drink!” Everyone played along for a few words, but hurried to escape the trouble.

Xiangzi went to Tianshun Coal Factory.

Things really did move fast. Tigress rented two small north rooms in a big shared compound in Maojiawan; she immediately hired a paperhanger to whiten all the walls; asked Mr. Feng to write a few “double happiness” signs to post indoors. Once the rooms were ready, she went to book a bridal sedan: a star-patterned sedan with sixteen musicians,No golden lantern, no attendant. Everything was arranged: she rushed to make herself a red satin wedding jacket before the end of the year, so that there would be no need to sew after the fifth day of the New Year. The chosen wedding day was the sixth day of the lunar new year, an auspicious date that didn't require avoiding any gateways. She took care of all the arrangements herself and told Xiangzi that he needed to buy all new clothes from head to toe: "You only get married once in a lifetime!"

Xiangzi only had five yuan in his hand!

Huniu glanced at him again, "What? What about the more than thirty yuan I gave to you?"

Xiangzi couldn't avoid telling the truth and confessed everything about the Cao family's house. She blinked, half-believing, half-doubting: "Alright, I don't have time to argue with you, let's just trust each other's conscience! Here's fifteen yuan for you! You'd better make sure you look like a proper groom on the big day, watch yourself!"

On the sixth day, Huniu got into the bridal sedan. She hadn't exchanged a word with her father, had no brothers to escort her, and no friends or relatives to offer congratulations. Only the gongs and drums rang cheerfully through the post-New Year streets as the bridal sedan passed steadily through Xianmen and Xisi Archway, drawing both envy and emotion from the people in new clothes, especially the shop assistants.

Xiangzi wore new clothes bought at Tianqiao, his face red, and on his head was a small satin cap that cost three jiao. He seemed to forget himself, staring blankly at everything, listening to everything, feeling as if he didn't even recognize himself. He moved from a coal shop into a new room freshly papered in snowy white. He didn't know what was happening: everything before was like the coal yard, all piles of black; now he entered the new room in a daze, so white it dazzled, decorated with a few blood-red wedding characters. He sensed a kind of mockery, a kind of pale, vague, suffocating unease. The room was furnished with Huniu's original tables, chairs, and bed; the stove and the food prep table were new; in the corner stood a feather duster with multicolored chicken feathers. He recognized those tables and chairs, but the stove, food prep table, and feather duster felt unfamiliar. The mix of old and new things made him think of the past and worry about the future. Everything was arranged for him; he felt like both something old and something new, some kind of ornament, something strange; he didn't recognize himself anymore. He couldn't even cry or laugh, his big hands and feet moving about in the small, warm room like a large rabbit in a little wooden cage, his eyes red as he looked outside and inside, legs made for running but trapped! Huniu was in a red jacket, her face painted white with powder and rouge, her eyes darting at him. He didn't dare meet her gaze. She, too, was something both old and new, some odd, bewildering thing—part girl, part woman; part female, part male; part human, part some fierce beast! This beast, clad in red, had caught him and was ready to deal with him carefully. Anyone could control him, but this beast was especially fierce, never leaving his side, glaring at him, smiling at him, and able to grab him tightly, sucking out all his strength. He had no way to escape. He took off the small satin cap and stared blankly at the red knot on it until his eyes became dizzy—when he turned, the wall was covered with dancing, spinning red dots, among them a bigger one: Huniu's grinning, ugly, red-painted face! On the wedding night, Xiangzi finally understood: Huniu wasn't actually pregnant. Like a magician, she explained to him, "If I hadn't aggrieved you a little, would you have agreed so willingly? I stuffed a pillow in my waistband! Ha ha, ha ha!" She laughed until tears streamed down, "You silly thing! Forget it, I have no regrets; after all, you know the kind of person I am and the kind you are. I even fought with my dad to come with you. Shouldn't you be grateful?" The next morning, Xiangzi went out early. Most shops had already opened for business, though some were still closed. The spring couplets on the doors were still bright red, though some of the yellow hanging money had been blown to pieces by the wind. The streets were quiet, but there were still plenty of rickshaws and the drivers seemed more spirited than usual—most wore new shoes, and there was a piece of red paper on the backs of their rickshaws. Xiangzi envied these drivers, feeling they truly had some sense of celebrating the new year, while he had been suffocating these past days as if stuck in a gourd; they were all steadily making a living, but he didn't have even a bit of work and was just drifting aimlessly down the street. He was uneasy just idling about, but planning for tomorrow meant he’d have to discuss things with Huniu—his wife; he had to beg for his living from his wife—such a wife!—and that made him feel useless, tall and strong for nothing. First of all, he had to serve his wife, that creature with the red jacket and tiger teeth; a thing that sucked the life out of people; he was no longer a person, only a lump of flesh. He had lost himself, merely struggling in her jaws like a mouse caught by a cat. He didn’t want to go discuss with her, he needed to leave; once he had a plan, he’d leave her without a goodbye. There was nothing wrong with that—after all, she was the weird woman who played tricks on him with a pillow! He felt wronged, he wanted to tear up the new clothes, and even wished to wash himself inside and out in clean water, feeling there was something unclean and disgusting clinging to him that made him sick at heart. He wished never to see her face again!

But where to go? He had nowhere to go. Usually, when pulling a rickshaw, his legs went wherever people told him, but today, his legs were free, and he felt lost. Heading south from Xisi Archway, he left Xuanwumen: the road was so straight, and his mind was just as set. Exiting the city gate, walking further south, he saw a bathhouse. He decided to have a bath.

Taking off all his clothes and looking at his body, he felt deeply ashamed. Getting into the hot pool, the water scalded him until he turned numb, and he closed his eyes, feeling his body tingling as if all the accumulated filth was being expelled. He hardly dared to touch himself, feeling empty inside, with beads of sweat rolling from his forehead. Only when his breathing grew rapid did he lazily climb out, his whole body flushed like a newborn baby. He seemed too embarrassed to come out just like that. After wrapping himself in a large towel, he still felt ugly; though sweat dripped off of him, he still felt unclean—it was as if the bit of filth in his heart would never wash away: in Old Liu’s eyes, in the eyes of everyone who knew him, he would always be the man who stole a woman!

Before the sweat even finished drying, he hurriedly put on his clothes and rushed out. He was afraid people would see his bare body! Out of the bathhouse, chilled by a gust of air, he felt a lightness. The street was much livelier than before. The clear sky added a glow to everyone’s face. Xiangzi’s heart was still tense, unsure where to go. South, then east, then south again, he made for Tianqiao. After New Year's, by a bit after nine, apprentices from the shops had finished breakfast and come here. All kinds of peddlers and performance stalls were already set up early in the day. When Xiangzi arrived, crowds were already forming in circles, and there were gongs and drums playing inside. He was in no mood to watch any of the performances—he no longer knew how to laugh.

In the past, the storytellers, bear trainers, magicians, balladeers, yangge singers, drum book narrators, and martial artists here could all bring him real happiness, make him open his mouth wide with laughter. He was reluctant to leave Beiping, and half of that was because of Tianqiao. Every time he saw the stalls and circles of people at Tianqiao, he would think of many amusing and endearing things. Now he couldn't be bothered to push forward; the laughter at Tianqiao no longer included him. He stepped away from the crowds and walked toward a quiet place, but then felt reluctant to leave! No, he couldn’t leave this lively, lovable place, couldn’t leave Tianqiao, couldn’t leave Beiping. Leave? There was nowhere to go! He would still have to go back to her—to her!—to talk things over. He couldn’t leave, nor could he idle around; he had to take a step back and reconsider, just like everyone does when there’s no other way forward. He’d already endured every grievance; why be so particular about this one point? He couldn’t undo the past, so he might as well just go with the flow.

He stopped, listening to the clamor of the crowd, the banging of drums and gongs, watching the coming and going of people, carts, and horses, when suddenly the image of those two small rooms came to mind. The sounds in his ears seemed to vanish, the people before his eyes seemed to disappear, leaving only those two white, warm rooms decorated with red wedding characters, standing squarely in front of him. Though he had slept there only one night, they felt incredibly familiar and intimate—even that woman in the red jacket seemed suddenly not so easy to abandon. Standing at Tianqiao, he had nothing, was nothing; in those two small rooms, he had everything. Go back; only by going back could there be a way forward. Everything about tomorrow was in those small rooms. Shame, fear, regret, none of it mattered; to live, he needed to look for a place with possibilities.

He hurried back in one shot, arriving home, probably just after eleven. Huniu had already finished lunch: steamed buns, cabbage soup with meatballs, a dish of "tiger skin" aspic, a dish of pickled radish. Everything else was already set, only the cabbage was still simmering on the stove, giving off a delicious aroma. She had taken off her red jacket and put on her usual cotton trousers and jacket, but was wearing a small artificial red flower on her head, with a little gold paper ingot in the middle. Xiangzi glanced at her—she didn't look like a new bride. All her movements were just like a long-married woman: quick, experienced, with a hint of smugness. Although she didn’t seem like a bride, she did give him a sense of something new; she cooked, tidied up the house; the smell, the warmth in the room, all were things he'd never experienced. Whatever she was like, he felt he now had a home. Every home has something lovable about it. He didn’t know what to think.

"Where did you go?" she asked, scooping up the cabbage. "Had a bath," he said, taking off his robe.

"Ah! Next time you go out, say something! Don’t just up and walk away like that!" He said nothing.

"Can't you at least grunt? If not, I’ll teach you!"

He grunted. There was nothing else to do! He knew he’d married a shrew, but this shrew could cook, could tidy up, could scold and help him, making him feel all kinds of mixed feelings! He started eating the buns. The food was indeed tastier and hotter than usual; yet he couldn’t enjoy it, chewing but not feeling the usual wolf-like satisfaction, not even breaking a sweat while eating.

After eating, he lay down on the kang, hands behind his head, staring at the ceiling. "Hey! Come help clean up! I’m nobody’s maid!" she shouted from the outer room.

Very lazily he got up, gave her a glance, and walked in to help. He used to be very diligent, but now he did chores holding his feelings in. At the carriage yard, he often helped her, but now he found her more and more unbearable; he’d never hated anyone as much as he hated her, though he didn’t know exactly why. He was angry but wouldn’t show it, just kept it bottled up; he couldn’t have a clean break, and arguing seemed pointless. Wandering around the little rooms, he felt his entire life was a sum of grievances.

When they’d finished tidying, she looked around, sighed, and then smiled. "So?"

"What?" Xiangzi squatted by the stove, warming his hands—not because they were cold, but because he had nowhere else to put them. These two little rooms really did feel like a home, but he didn’t know where to put his hands or feet.

"Take me out for some fun? Go to Baiyun Temple? No, it’s too late for that; how about a walk in the street?" She wanted to fully enjoy the happiness of newlywed life. Although the wedding hadn’t been ideal, the sense of freedom was nice—a few days to spend together with her husband, having a great time. Back home, she never lacked food, clothing, or pocket money; what she lacked was a companion, a confidant. Now she wanted to make up for that, to walk the streets and temple fairs in grand fashion with Xiangzi.

Xiangzi refused to go. Firstly, he thought it shameful to parade his wife around; secondly, a wife acquired by these means should only be kept hidden at home, nothing dignified, best not to show her off. What’s more, if they went out, how could they not run into acquaintances—every rickshaw driver in the west half of town knew Huniu and Xiangzi; he didn’t want to invite gossip and whispers behind his back.

"Talk it over, ok?" He was still squatting there.

"What’s there to talk over?" She came up, standing next to the stove. He put his hands down on his knees, staring dumbly at the flames. After quite a while, he spoke: "I can’t just sit around like this!"

"Such a glutton for suffering!" she laughed. "If you don’t pull a rickshaw for a single day, you get restless, is that it? Look at old Liu—for a whole lifetime he’s been in this business, and now owns a carriage yard in his old age. He doesn’t pull rickshaws or work himself ragged, just lives by his wits. You should learn from that; what’s so great about a lifetime of pulling? Let’s enjoy ourselves for a few days first—there’s no need to rush now, things aren’t going to depend on just a few days, why act like it’s life or death? I don’t plan to argue with you for a couple days, so don’t rile me on purpose!"

"Let’s talk it over first!" Xiangzi resolved not to give in. Since he couldn’t just walk out, he had to figure out how to get work, but had to start by taking a stand, not just drift about aimlessly.

"Alright, say your piece!" She dragged a stool over and sat by the stove. "How much money do you have?" he asked.

"See? I knew you’d ask that! It’s not like you’re marrying a wife, it’s like you’re marrying the money, isn’t it?"

Xiangzi felt almost winded, swallowing hard several times. Old Liu, people, and the carriage yard drivers all thought he was greedy, that’s why he got involved with Huniu; now,She said it herself! Her own car, her own money, lost for no reason at all, and now it's lying under a few bucks of his wife's money; even eating has to be swallowed down her spine! He wished he could grab her by the throat with both hands and strangle! Strangle! Strangle! Until her eyes rolled back! Strangle everything to death, then cut his own throat. They're not human, so they should die; he's not human either, so he should die; none of them should even think of living!

Xiangzi stood up, wanting to go out for another walk; he shouldn't have come back just now. Seeing that Xiangzi's expression wasn't right, she softened a little: "All right, I'll tell you. Altogether, I have about 500 bucks on hand. The sedan chair, renting the house—three months'[1] rent paid upfront, patching up the shack, making clothes, buying things, bringing things for you, all told we've already spent close to 100, and there's about 400 left. I'm telling you, you don't need to worry. Let's just enjoy it while we've got it. You, after pulling a rickshaw and sweating for years, should get to have some fun for a few days looking good; me, I've been an old maiden for so many years, I should have some joy for a bit too. When the money's almost gone, we can go plead with my old man again. Me, if I hadn't fallen out with him that day, I'd never have gotten away. Now my anger's all gone, and after all, a father is still a father. Him, he only has a daughter like me, and you're someone he likes—if we just bow our heads and apologize, I don't think there'll be anything we can't get past. Isn't it easy? He has money, and we can take it legitimately—there's nothing unreasonable about that; it's better than you working like a beast for strangers! In a few days, you go pay him a visit; maybe he won't see you the first time. If not, go a second time; give him all the respect, and he can't keep ignoring you. Then I'll go, and say some nice things to him too, and who knows, maybe we'll all move back together. When we move back, we can hold our heads high and no one will dare look down on us; but if we stay suffering here, we'll always be a pair of nobodies, don't you think?"

Xiangzi had never thought of this. Ever since Huniu came to the Cao house looking for him, he thought once he married her, he'd buy himself a rickshaw with her money and go out to pull it. Even though using his wife's money was a bit shameful, their relationship was already so complicated, there was nothing to do about it. He hadn't thought Huniu would have this trick up her sleeve. Pulling a long face and giving in, well, it really was an idea, but Xiangzi wasn't that kind of person. Thinking it over, front to back, he seemed to understand a little: when you have money, people can take it from you for nothing, and if you're wronged, there's nowhere to complain. And once someone gives you money, you have to accept it; after that, you can no longer see yourself as a person, you have a mind and strength, but you have to become someone else's slave: playing toy for your wife, servant for your father-in-law. A person seems to be worth nothing at all, just a bird—if you go out to find food on your own, you'll end up in a net. If you eat someone else's grain, you have to honestly stay in their cage, sing for them, and be ready to be sold any time!

He wouldn't go to see Liu Siye. With Huniu, the relationship was flesh and blood; with Liu Si, there was none. He'd already suffered at her hands, he couldn't go and beg her father again! "I don't want to sit around and do nothing!" was all he said, to spare any more talking and fighting.

"Born for hardship, are you!" she said, tapping and fiddling around. "If you don't want to be idle, then start a business."

"I can't! I can't make money! I can pull a rickshaw, I love pulling rickshaws!" The veins on Xiangzi's head were twitching.

"Let me tell you, you're not allowed to pull rickshaws! I won't let you come back to my bed dripping with sweaty stink! You've got your ideas, I've got mine, let's see who out-stubborns whom! You got yourself a wife, but I spent the money, you didn't shell out a cent. Think about it, between us two, who should listen to whom?"

Xiangzi was speechless again.

[1]

Three months' rent paid upfront for the first month of renting a house.

Sixteen  

When it came to the Lantern Festival, Xiangzi couldn’t stand it anymore.  

Huniu was very happy. She arranged for cooking Yuanxiao, making dumplings, strolling at the temple fair during the day, and lantern-viewing at night. She didn’t allow Xiangzi to have any opinions, but she never failed to please his palate, always finding ways to buy or make new things for him to eat. There were seven or eight families living in the big compound, most of whom only occupied one room; in one room there might be seven or eight people, both old and young. Some of them pulled rickshaws, some did small business, some were patrolmen, some were servants. Everyone had their own affairs, nobody had free time, even the children carried little baskets—going out in the morning for porridge, and in the afternoon to pick up coal cinders. Only the youngest kids, their buttocks bright red from the cold, played or fought in the yard. The ashes, dust, and dirty water were all poured out into the yard, nobody had time to clean up, so the middle of the yard was a sheet of frozen ice; when the older kids came back with coal cinders, they used it as a skating rink, laughing and making noise as they slid. The worst off were the old folks and women. The old people had neither food nor clothes, lying on the cold kang, waiting for the young to earn a bit of money to at least drink a bowl of porridge. The young ones who worked hard might bring money home, or maybe come home empty-handed; when they did return, they’d lose their temper and start arguing. The old people, their stomachs empty, had nothing to swallow but tears. As for the women, they had to take care of the old and the young, and also make do for the working men. Even when pregnant, they still had to work as normal, eating nothing more than cornbread and sweet potato porridge; no, not only did they work as usual, they also had to go out for porridge and pick up odd jobs—if they were lucky and everyone was fed and lying down, they’d sit holding a little kerosene lamp, washing, sewing, and mending for other people. The rooms were so small, the walls so broken, the cold wind drilling in from one side and straight out the other, carrying off every bit of warmth. All they had to cover themselves was tattered rags, with only a bowl or half a bowl of porridge in their stomachs, or maybe a fetus of six or seven months. They had to work and make sure the old and young were fed first. They were full of illness; not yet thirty, they had already lost their hair, but they couldn’t rest for even a moment, working themselves from sickness right to death; when they died, the coffins had to be raised by asking for charity from “kind people.” As for the young girls—at sixteen or seventeen, they had no pants, only able to wrap themselves in whatever tattered rags they found, working in the house—the natural prison—helping their mothers with chores. When they needed to go to the latrine, they had to make sure the yard was empty before sneaking out as if stealing; all winter, they never saw the sun or blue sky. The ugly ones would inherit everything from their mothers; the slightly pretty ones—even they knew they’d eventually be sold off by their parents to “go enjoy a good life.”  

It was in such a compound that Huniu felt very proud. She was the only one with enough to eat and wear, no worries, and the freedom to go out for walks. She raised her head high, coming and going, feeling her own superiority, and fearing others might get too close to her—she ignored that group of suffering people. Almost everyone who came here for small business sold the cheapest things, like boiled bones, frozen cabbage, raw mung bean soup, donkey or horse meat, all coming here to find buyers. Ever since Huniu moved in, the sellers of sheep’s head meat, smoked fish, hard wheat buns, and marinated fried tofu would shout their wares at her door. She’d carry a bowl, head raised, carrying all these snacks into the house, and the children, with fingers like iron bars in their mouths, would stare at her as if she were a princess. She came here to enjoy herself, and she couldn’t, wouldn’t, and didn’t want to look at other people’s hardships.  

First of all, Xiangzi didn’t approve of her behavior; he was born poor and knew what hardship meant. He didn’t want to eat those little snacks, pitying the wasted money. Secondly, even worse for him, was the meaning he understood: She forbade him from pulling rickshaws but fed him fine food every day—like fattening up a cow to milk it! He had completely become her plaything. He had seen: even an old, skinny mother dog, when running errands, would still choose a strong male dog. Thinking of this, he not only hated this life but also worried about himself. He knew a laboring man must take care of his body, as the body is everything. If he went on living like this, one day he’d become nothing but a bag of dry bones—all that size, but with nothing inside. He shuddered. If he wanted to survive, he had to get back to pulling rickshaws, go out and run, spend all day at it, and come back home to sleep dead to the world; not eating her fine food, not serving her fun. He decided to do this, not to give an inch; if she was willing to buy a rickshaw for him, fine; if not, he’d go rent one. Without saying a word, once the idea was set, he went to rent a rickshaw. On the seventeenth day, he started pulling rickshaws, renting by the “full day.” After two longer trips, he found something he’d never felt before: tight calves and sore hip joints. He knew what caused his problem, but to comfort himself, he figured it must be because he hadn’t pulled for over twenty days, his legs going stiff; after a few more trips, maybe he’d get used to it.  

He got another fare, this time a “team trip”—three rickshaws going together. Taking hold of his handles, everyone let a tall man in his forties lead. The tall guy smiled, accepting the respect—he knew the three other rickshaws were all “better” than his. Still, he worked hard—knowing full well he couldn’t outrun the three younger men, but not willing to coast on age. After over a mile, a young man behind him called out: “How’s it going, got the strength? Pretty impressive!” He panted and answered: “Running with you young guys, go slow—how would that work?!” He really didn’t run slowly—even Xiangzi had to put in seventy or eighty percent effort to keep up. But his running style wasn’t pretty: tall as he was, he couldn’t bend his waist, so his waist and back were like a single wooden plank; his whole body had to lean forward, with his arms seeming to trail behind; it didn’t look like running, more like forcing something forward. With his stiff waist, his hip bones had to move, and his feet more or less dragged on the ground, twisting forward as fast as he could. Twisting very quickly, but watching him you knew it was a struggle. On turns, he moved his whole body stiffly; everyone was nervous for him—he seemed to move forward regardless of whether or not the rickshaw could pass.  

When they arrived, sweat dripped from the tip of his nose and his ears and lips in big drops. He put down his rickshaw, hurriedly straightened up, grinned. When taking the money, his hand shook like he could barely hold on.  

Once you’ve finished a run together, you count as friends. The four men parked their rickshaws side by side. While they wiped their sweat and joked as usual, the tall man wandered off alone, coughing for a while, spitting out lots of white phlegm, and finally seemed to catch his breath and start talking: “That's it! But my heart’s still in it; waist, legs, just won’t cooperate! No matter how much I try, my legs won’t lift—just anxious!”  

“Those two moves just now, you didn’t slow down at all. You think that's easy?” The short young man, barely over twenty, chimed in: “Don’t be upset, we three are all tough guys—who didn’t sweat?” The tall man looked a little proud, but also embarrassed, and sighed.  

“Honestly, the way you run, you’re gonna break yourself in half at this rate, believe it?” another young guy said. “It's age, you can’t fake it.” The tall man smiled and shook his head: “But it’s not just about age, brothers! I'll give you some real talk now—don't start a family in this line of work, seriously!” Seeing everyone listen closely, he lowered his voice: “Get a family and you never get any rest, night or day, done for! Take a look at my waist, like a block—no flexibility! If I push too hard, I start coughing, my chest burns like fire! No need to say more—doing our job, we’re doomed to be lifelong bachelors! Even the goddamn sparrows get to pair up—us, no family allowed! And even if you do, get married, it’s one kid a year—I’ve got five already! All with their mouths open waiting to eat! Rickshaw rental fees high, food’s expensive, business is tough, what can you do? Better to be a bachelor for life—even if you rot in the workhouse and grow boils, that’s fate! Alone, when you die, you’re just dead! But with a family—old and young, several mouths—you die, but you can’t close your eyes! Don’t you think so?” he asked Xiangzi.  

Xiangzi nodded, but didn’t say anything.  

Just then, a customer came. The short guy was talking price but gave way and called out to the tall man: “Big Brother, you take this one! After all, you’ve got five kids at home!”  

The tall man smiled: “All right, I’ll run another trip! Strictly speaking, we shouldn’t work like this! Fine, I’ll make sure to bring back a couple of buns! See you later, brothers!” Watching the tall guy walk away, the short one muttered to himself: “Damn, work your whole life and can’t even touch a wife! Meanwhile, those damn rich folks hug four or five women each!”  

“Forget about those people,” another young man took over. “Look, doing this job, you really do have to be careful, the tall man’s not wrong. Tell me, what’s the point of starting a family? Just to show off at home? No way! And that’s if you’re lucky! Eating cornbread every day, the pressure from work and family—no matter how strong a guy is, he’s brought to his knees!” Hearing this, Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw forward and said, trying to change the subject: “Let’s go south, nothing here for business.”  

“See you!” the two young men said together.  

Xiangzi seemed not to hear them. As he walked, he kicked his legs—his hip joints were still a bit sore! He thought of quitting for the day, but didn’t even have the courage to go home. At home wasn’t a wife, but a bloodsucking demon!  

The days were getting longer—he went out for two or three more rounds, and it was just after five o’clock. After returning the rickshaw, he killed some time in a teahouse. After two pots of tea, he felt hungry and decided to eat his fill outside before going home. He ate twelve taels of meat pie and a bowl of red bean and millet porridge, belching as he slowly walked home. He knew for sure there’d be a thunderstorm waiting for him at home, but he felt calm; he was determined: no arguing, no fighting with her, just drop down and sleep. Tomorrow, he’d go out and pull rickshaws as usual—let her do whatever she wants!  

Entering the house, Huniu was sitting in the living room, gave him a look, her face so dark it seemed water would drip off. Xiangzi thought of making peace, forced a long face and tried to greet her. But he wasn’t used to this kind of thing, so he lowered his head and went into the inner room. She said nothing; the small house was as quiet as a cavern in the mountains. The neighbors’ coughs, conversations, and kids crying could be heard so clearly, yet also sounded so far away, like hearing voices on a distant mountainside.  

Neither was willing to speak first; with mouths shut, one after the other, they lay down, like a pair of silent old turtles. After a sleep, Huniu spoke, half angry, half amused: “Where have you been? Gone the whole day!”  

“Went out pulling rickshaws,” he muttered, half awake, something stuck in his throat. “Oh! Can’t stand not sweating, itching for it, you’re such a wretch! I made you food and you didn’t come home to eat, wandering off to enjoy yourself? Don’t push me, my old man was a bachelor, I can do anything! If you dare go out tomorrow, I’ll hang myself—I mean what I say!” “I can’t just sit idle!”  

“Why don’t you go to the old man?”  

“Won’t go!”  

“So stubborn!”  

Xiangzi really got angry; he couldn’t go on without saying what was on his mind, couldn’t hold back anymore: “I’m pulling rickshaws, saving up for my own rickshaw—whoever blocks me, I’ll leave and never come back!” “Hmm—” She dragged the sound out through her nose, long and winding.
