In that sound, she showed her pride and contempt for Xiangzi, but there was a turn in her mind as well. She knew Xiangzi was—though usually very honest—a tough guy. A tough guy’s words weren’t meant as jokes. Having finally caught him, she couldn’t just let go. He was the ideal man: honest, thrifty, strong. For someone like her at her age, it wasn't easy to find a treasure like this again. You have to be both tough and gentle—that’s the secret; she needed to tighten and loosen her grip so this headstrong man could never slip away. “I know you’re tough, but you should know I really care for you. If you won’t go ask the old man, here’s what we’ll do: I’ll go. After all, I’m his daughter anyway, so what if I lose a bit of face.”  

“If the old man takes us in, I’ll still pull rickshaws!” Xiangzi put his foot down.  

Huniu didn’t speak for a long time. She hadn’t expected Xiangzi to be this clever. Though his words were simple, he was clearly saying that he wouldn’t fall into her trap again—he wasn’t some dumb brute. That just made him more interesting; she’d have to rack her brains to keep this stubborn, sometimes willful man under control. She couldn’t hold him too tight—finding such a “big ox” wasn’t easy. She had to loosen and tighten her grip so he’d always be in her hand. “All right, if you want to pull rickshaws, I have no choice. But you have to promise, never pull for hire, always come back; you see, if I don't see you for just one day, I get anxious! Promise me, every night you’ll come home early!”  

Xiangzi remembered what the tall man said during the day! Eyes open, staring at the darkness, he saw a group of rickshaw pullers, small traders, laborers—their backs never able to bend, their legs always stiff. He’d become like that one day. But at the moment, it wasn’t worth tangling with her; as long as he could get back to pulling rickshaws, that counted as a win. “I'll always pull loose fares!”He agreed.

Although she said so, she really wasn't very eager to go look for Old Master Liu. She and her father used to bicker all the time, but things were different now—the tension wouldn't just blow over in a day or two, because she was no longer a part of the Liu family. A married woman is always somewhat distant from her parents' family. She didn't dare just walk right into their main hall and go home. What if her father really did turn his back on her? She might make a fuss, but if the old man stubbornly refused to part with the family fortune, there would be nothing she could do. Even if someone tried to mediate, when it came down to it, all they could do was persuade her to go back, since she had her own household now.

Xiangzi went on pulling his rickshaw as usual, while she paced back and forth alone in the room, repeatedly getting dressed to go see her father, but just thinking about it made her too lazy to actually do it. She was conflicted. For her own comfort and happiness, she knew she had to go back; but for her self-respect, staying away seemed the right thing to do. What if her father cooled off? If she could just bring Xiangzi over to the Renhe company, naturally he'd be given a job—no need to pull rickshaws anymore—and she could smoothly take over her father's business. The thought brightened her up. But what if the old man held fast? She'd lose face—not just lose face, but end up the acknowledged wife of a rickshaw driver; she would be, well!—no different from any of those women in the courtyard. Her heart suddenly turned pitch black. She nearly regretted marrying Xiangzi—no matter how ambitious he was, if her father didn't give his blessing, he'd be pulling a rickshaw for the rest of his life. At that thought, she even considered going back to her parents alone, cutting all ties with Xiangzi, not willing to lose everything for him. But then again, the joy of being with Xiangzi was indescribable. She sat by the edge of the kang, blank and lost, reminiscing about the happiness after the wedding; her whole body felt like a big red flower, fragrant and warm, blooming in the sunlight. No, she couldn't give up Xiangzi. Even if he pulled rickshaws, even if he begged, she'd have to stick with him forever. Look at those women in the yard—if they could stand it, she could stand it too. That's settled; she didn't want to go to the Liu family now.

Since leaving Renhe, Xiangzi had refused to go down Xianmen Street. These past few days while pulling his rickshaw, he always headed for the eastern city as soon as he left the house to avoid running into Renhe's rickshaws all over the west side—he would feel too embarrassed meeting them. But this day, after finishing work, he deliberately passed by the entrance of the company—not for any particular reason, just wanted to have a look. Huniu's words were still on his mind, as if he was testing himself to see if he had the courage to return to the company if Huniu could smooth things over with her father; before going back to the company, he wanted to see if he even dared walk down this street. Pulling his hat down, he sneaked past the company from afar, afraid someone might see him. From a distance, he saw the light by the main entrance and felt incredibly sad inside. He remembered what things had been like when he'd first come here, Huniu's seduction, and the birthday party that night—all vividly clear, like a series of pictures floating before his eyes. Mixed in with those pictures were others—Xishan, camels, Casa Mansion, detectives... all distinct, frightening, and joined together. The images were so clear, he actually felt a bit lost, almost as if he really was just looking at pictures and had forgotten he was a part of them too. When he thought about his relationship to these things, his mind grew restless and everything started spinning in confusion—he couldn't figure out why he had to suffer all this hardship and injustice. It felt like those events lasted a very long time, then suddenly very short—he couldn't tell how old he was supposed to be. He just felt he was much, much older than when he'd first come to Renhe. Back then, he was full of hope; now, he was stuffed with worries. He didn't know why, but those images would never lie to him.

Right in front of him was the Renhe Company. He stopped on the other side of the street, staring blankly at the incredibly bright lamp. Staring and staring, suddenly something clicked inside him. The four gold characters under the lamp—Ren He Che Chang—had changed! He was illiterate but remembered what the first character looked like: like two sticks joined together, not quite a fork, not quite a triangle, such a simple and strange character. Judging from how it sounded, it was probably "Ren." Now this "Ren" had changed, turning into "Ren" with the radical for heart—a character even stranger than the first. He couldn't figure out why. Looking at the two rooms in the middle—the ones he would never forget—they were both dark, with no lights on.

He stood there until he'd lost patience with himself, then lowered his head and walked home. As he walked, he wondered, could the Renhe Company have folded? He'd have to ask around, but it wasn't time yet to say anything to his wife. When he got home, Huniu was inside cracking melon seeds to kill time. "So late again!" There wasn't a hint of kindness on her face. "I'm telling you, I can't take this anymore! You go out every day and I'm scared to leave the house, full of penniless folks in this yard, I'm scared things will get stolen. I don't even have anyone to talk to all day—this won't do, I'm not made of wood. You better think of something, this can't go on!" Xiangzi didn't say a word.

"Say something! Are you deliberately trying to make me angry? You have a mouth or not? Mouth or not?" She got faster and sharper, her words crackling like a string of firecrackers. Xiangzi still said nothing.

"Fine," she was really frustrated now, but also a bit helpless, her face torn between crying and laughing, so agitated she couldn't fully blow up. "Let's buy two rickshaws and rent them out. You just live off the share at home, okay? Okay?" "Two rickshaws could bring in about 30 cents a day—not enough to live on! Rent out one, I pull the other one myself, just scrape by." Xiangzi spoke slowly but naturally; talk of buying a rickshaw made him forget everything else.

"Isn't that the same? You still wouldn't be home!"

"This works too," Xiangzi's ideas all seemed to be connected to rickshaws, "rent out one, get the full day's share. The other, I'll pull it half a day and rent it out the other half. If I do the daytime, I'll leave early and be back by three; if I do nights, I'll go out at three and come back at night. That's perfect!" She nodded. "Let me think on it, if there's no better idea, we'll do that."

Xiangzi was quite happy. If this plan could happen, he'd be pulling his own rickshaw again. Even if his wife had bought it, he'd save up little by little until he could buy one himself. Only then did he start to see that Huniu had her good side too. He even smiled at her—a pure, heartfelt smile, as if all the hardship in the past had been wiped away, and, smiling, he entered a brand new world, like changing clothes—just that simple, just that freeing!

[1]

Jue, means to lose. To be defeated.

Learning Resource Sharing 17

Xiangzi gradually found out what had happened to Renhe: Old Liu sold off part of the fleet, and the rest he handed over to a well-known cart owner in the west side. Xiangzi could guess why—the old man was getting on in years, and with no daughter to help him, he couldn't keep the business running, so he simply closed up shop and took the money to enjoy himself. Where did he go? Xiangzi couldn't find out.

As for the news, he wasn't sure if he should be glad or not. In terms of his own ambition and independence, since Old Liu had cut ties with his daughter, Huniu's whole plan had fallen through; Xiangzi could just keep pulling rickshaws for his living, relying on no one. But when he thought about Old Liu's money, it really was a shame; who knew what the old man did with the money—he and Huniu hadn't even gotten a single coin out of it.

But since things had come to this, he didn't dwell on it, wasn't even moved. He just thought, after all, his strength was his own; as long as he was willing to work hard for his money, he'd have no problem feeding himself. He told Huniu this flatly, with no feelings attached. She was the one who got upset. When she heard it, she saw her future at once—over! Everything was over! She'd be a rickshaw driver's wife for life! She'd never escape this slum courtyard! She thought maybe her father might remarry, but never thought he'd just shake off everything and leave. If he really did remarry, at least Huniu could fight for the inheritance—maybe even make peace with a new stepmother and get something for herself... there was always a plan, as long as her father kept the rickshaw business going. She never imagined he'd be so determined, so ruthless, turning everything into cash and secretly hiding it away! When she broke with him before, she always thought it was just a strategy and they'd eventually make up; she knew Renhe couldn't function without her. Who'd have thought the old man would just walk away from the rickshaw business?!

Spring was in the air, the buds on the branches turning rosy and fat. But in this big courtyard, spring never came to the treetops first—there wasn't a single flower or tree here. Instead, the spring wind first pitted the patch of melting ice in the courtyard, blew out some foul smells from the dirty earth, swept scraps and feathers into the corners, swirling them around in little eddies. Here, people struggled all year round. The old folks had only now dared come out to sit in the sun; the young girls were just now cleaning the coal smudges off their noses so a bit of pink skin showed; the women finally dared, with little shame, to send their kids outside to play; and the kids finally dared to run around with a torn scrap of paper for a kite, without freezing their little black hands until they cracked open. But the congee stalls stopped cooking, the relief aid stopped handing out rice, the charities stopped giving cash—it was like all the poor folks had been handed over to spring wind and spring sun! Just when young wheat was greening like grass and last year's grain had run out, rice prices went up as usual. The days were longer, so that even the old couldn't just go to bed early and fool their empty stomachs with dreams. Spring had come, but for this slum, it brought only more hardship. The lice had grown old over winter—especially tough now—sometimes crawling outside the old folks' or children's cotton padding to catch a bit of sunlight!

Huniu stared at the melting ice in the yard, at the tattered clothes, sniffed the stale, slightly warm air, and listened to the old people's sighs and babies crying—her heart turned cold. In winter, everyone hid indoors, all the filth frozen solid; now, people came out and the filth showed itself, even the bricked-up walls were starting to crumble, as if waiting for the rains to collapse completely. The whole courtyard was a riot of ragged color, blooming with the ugliest form of poverty—several times worse than winter. Hmph! At this moment, she felt she'd never leave this place; her little bit of money would run out someday, and Xiangzi would always be nothing but a rickshaw puller!

She told Xiangzi to watch the house and went to Nanyuan to find her aunt and get news of the old man. Her aunt confirmed that the old man had come by her place—probably on the twelfth day of the month—partly to thank her and partly to let her know he planned to visit Tianjin or maybe Shanghai, for a bit of sightseeing. He said: having spent all his life knowing nothing but Beijing, he couldn't call himself much of a man; while he still had breath, he wanted to travel around and see the world. Besides, he had no face to stay in the city anymore since his own daughter had disgraced him. That was all the aunt could report, and her opinion was even simpler: maybe he really did leave, or maybe he just said that and was hiding somewhere quiet—who knew!

Back home, she threw herself onto the kang and cried and cried, honest tears, no scheming or cunning—she bawled until her eyes were swollen.

When she was done, she wiped away her tears and told Xiangzi, "Fine, you're so tough! Guess I have to go along with you now! I backed the wrong horse. Married a chicken, gotta stick with the chicken; no point in saying anything else. Here, I'll give you a hundred bucks, go buy a rickshaw and pull it yourself!"

Here, she held something back: she'd planned to buy two rickshaws—one for Xiangzi to pull, one to rent out. Now she'd changed her mind: just buy one and let Xiangzi pull it; keep the rest of the money for herself. Money in hand meant power in hand—she wouldn't hand it all over. What if Xiangzi changed his heart after she spent all her money on rickshaws? She couldn't not think about that! And then, with Old Liu gone just like that, she felt nothing was reliable anymore—you never knew what tomorrow would bring. Better to enjoy life while you could, keep some spending money, eat whatever snacks she fancied—she'd always been used to snacking. Living off Xiangzi's earnings—he was a top-class rickshaw driver—plus her own little bit for pocket money, she'd take care of the present for now. The money might run out one day, but no one lived forever! Marrying a rickshaw driver—even if she had no choice—was already a sacrifice; there was no way she'd keep asking him for money every day while having none in her own pocket. This decision cheered her up a little; even though she knew the future looked grim, at least for now it wasn't total gloom. It was like reaching sunset—the horizon was already dim, but for now there was still some light, so she'd walk a few more steps while she could.

Xiangzi didn't argue; one rickshaw would do, as long as it was his, he'd manage to make six or seven dimes a day,Can chew grains. Not only did he not argue, he even felt somewhat pleased. All the hardships he had gone through in the past were simply for buying a rickshaw. Now that he could buy another one, what else was there to say? Naturally, one rickshaw to support two mouths wouldn't leave any money left; the rickshaw would get old one day, and there was no backup plan to buy a new one, a real risk! But since it was so hard to buy a rickshaw, now that he could get one, he should be satisfied—why worry about the distant future?

Er Qiangzi from the compound was about to sell his rickshaw. Last summer, Er Qiangzi sold his daughter, Xiaofuzi—nineteen years old—to a military man for two hundred yuan. After Xiaofuzi left, Er Qiangzi showed off for a while, redeemed all his pawned items, bought a few new clothes, and the whole family dressed unusually neatly. Er Qiangzi's wife was the shortest and ugliest woman in the whole compound: a protruding forehead, big jaws, barely any hair on her head, her teeth always exposed, a face covered in freckles that was repulsive to look at. She also had red eyelids, crying over her daughter while putting on a new blue long gown. Er Qiangzi had always had a violent temper, and after selling his daughter, he often drank several cups of liquor; after drinking, with tears brimming, he was especially prone to picking faults. Although Er Qiangzi’s wife got a new gown and could eat her fill, the happiness didn’t make up for the suffering, and she was beaten almost twice as often as before. Er Qiangzi was over forty and decided not to pull rickshaws anymore. So he bought a set of baskets, got a peddler’s shoulder pole, selling melons, peaches, peanuts, cigarettes, all kinds of goods. After two months of business, looking at the accounts, not only did he lose money, but lost a lot. He was used to pulling rickshaws, not doing business; pulling a rickshaw is a matter of brute force: you make it or you don’t; small business requires cunning, which he lacked. Rickshaw pullers know how to buy on credit, so he couldn't bear not to let acquaintances buy on credit; but once owed, it was hard to collect. As a result, he couldn't properly serve his regular passengers, and those who did business with him were all eager to take advantage and not pay. He couldn’t help but lose money. Losing money made him upset; being upset, he drank more. Drunk, he often quarreled with patrol officers outside, and turned violent with his wife and children at home. Angering police, beating his wife, all because of drink. When he sobered up, he was filled with regret and pain. Thinking again, that money came from selling his daughter, now lost for nothing, drinking and beating people—he felt he was not a man. At such times, he could sleep a whole day, handing over his misery to dreams.

He decided to give up business and return to pulling rickshaws, he couldn’t let that money all go to waste. He bought a new rickshaw. When drunk, he was completely unreasonable. When sober, he cared greatly about appearances. Because he valued appearances, he often put on airs, and always had a plan for everything. Having bought a new rickshaw and dressed neatly, he felt he was a higher-class rickshaw puller; he had to drink good tea and find good-looking passengers. He would park at the rickshaw stand, show off his rickshaw and his clean white shirt, chatting with everyone, never lowering himself to chase after business. Sometimes he’d snap his new blue dust cloth on the rickshaw, sometimes stamp his new white-soled shoes, sometimes stare at the tip of his nose, standing by the rickshaw and smiling, waiting for someone to praise his cart and start a long conversation. He could kill time like this for a day or two. Yet when he finally got a good passenger, his legs wouldn’t live up to his rickshaw and clothes, and he couldn’t run! This made him very upset. When he got upset, he thought of his daughter and could only go drink. In this way, all his money was drained away, leaving only that rickshaw.

Around the time of the start of winter, he got drunk again. The moment he walked in the door, his two sons—one thirteen, the other eleven—tried to hide. This infuriated him, and he kicked each of them. His wife said something, and he turned on her, kicking her in the lower belly. She lay on the ground, speechless for a long time. The kids panicked: one grabbed a coal shovel, the other a rolling pin, and fought their father with all they had. The three of them rolled in a heap, trampling their mother more than a few times in the melee. Neighbors came and finally pinned Er Qiangzi down on the bed, while the two kids hugged their mother and cried. She regained consciousness, but could never get out of bed again. On the third day of the twelfth lunar month, she stopped breathing, dressed in the blue robe made with the money from selling her daughter. Her family refused to accept this and insisted on taking the case to court. Friends pleaded earnestly, and finally her family relented, after Er Qiangzi promised to give her a proper burial and pay her family fifteen yuan. He pawned his rickshaw and got sixty yuan. At the turn of the year, he wanted to sell the rickshaw as he had no hope of redeeming it. When drunk, he even thought of selling a son, but nobody wanted one. He had approached Xiaofuzi’s husband too, but the man didn’t acknowledge him as a father-in-law at all, needless to say anything else.

Xiangzi knew this rickshaw's history and wasn’t keen to buy it. There were plenty of rickshaws—why this one? This inauspicious rickshaw, bought by selling a daughter and now for sale again after beating a wife to death! But Huniu didn't see it that way. She figured they could pick it up for just over eighty, a bargain! The rickshaw had only been used six months, the leather straps barely worn in, and it was a reputable brand from Xicheng’s Decheng factory. A secondhand rickshaw, about seventy percent new, would cost fifty or sixty. She couldn’t bear to miss this bargain. She also knew that after the New Year, money would be tight everywhere, so Er Qiangzi wouldn’t get a high price but desperately needed the money. She personally went to inspect the rickshaw, bargained with Er Qiangzi, and handed over the money; Xiangzi had no choice but to wait to pull the rickshaw, saying nothing since it wasn’t his money. Once the rickshaw was bought, he gave it a close look—it really was a solid frame. But he still felt uneasy about it. Most of all, he disliked the black lacquer body with bright brass trims: when Er Qiangzi ordered it, it had looked stunning with the black and white contrast, but Xiangzi thought it was ominous, like wearing mourning clothes. He longed to switch the covers for something earth yellow or light blue, at least to tone down that stark look a bit. But he didn't discuss it with Huniu, to avoid getting another tongue-lashing from her.

Pulling that rickshaw, everyone paid special attention, some even calling it the "Little Widow." Xiangzi couldn't stand it. He tried every trick not to think about it, but the rickshaw was with him from dawn till dusk, making him fretful, always fearing some mishap. Sometimes, memories of Er Qiangzi and his fate popped up—he felt as if he was pulling a coffin rather than a rickshaw. He kept seeing ghosts riding on it.

Yet ever since he got this rickshaw, nothing had actually gone wrong, though he still felt uneasily anxious. The days were getting warmer, people shed their cotton and almost felt fine in just single-layer shirts and trousers; Beiping hardly has any spring at all. The day was so long it became tedious; everyone felt drowsy. Xiangzi would head out early and return at four or five, already feeling he'd worked hard enough. But the sun was still high. He didn’t want to keep working, but didn’t want to pack up either, hanging indecisively while yawning long and lazily.

With such long days, when Xiangzi felt tired and bored, Huniu was even lonelier at home. In winter, she could warm herself by the stove and listen to the wind outside. Though life was hard, there was at least some comfort in "not having to go out." Now, the stove was moved outdoors, and there was nothing to do inside. The yard was filthy and stinking, not a single blade of grass. When she went to the street, she didn’t trust the neighbors, and even when she shopped she’d hurry straight back, never daring to linger. She was like a housebound bee, staring at the sunlight outside, unable to fly out. She couldn’t find common ground with the other women. They talked about household trivia—she was too rough and unruly by nature, disliked talking or listening to that stuff. Their grievances came from the bitterness of life—every little thing could bring tears; hers stemmed from dissatisfaction with life, with no tears to shed, only a desire to curse someone out for relief. She and they couldn’t understand each other, so it was best to mind their own business and skip the chit-chat[1]

.

Not until mid-April did she have a companion. Er Qiangzi's daughter, Xiaofuzi, had come back. Xiaofuzi's "man"[2]

 was an army officer. Wherever he was stationed he’d set up a simple home, spend a hundred or two on a young girl, buy a cheap bed and two chairs, and comfortably pass some time. When his unit was transferred, he’d just walk away, leaving girl and bed behind. Spending just a hundred or two for a year or so wasn't a loss—just for mending and laundry and cooking, hiring a servant would cost ten yuan or more per month, including food. Marrying a girl was like having both servant and bed partner, and guaranteed clean and healthy. If pleased, he’d buy her a floral dress—a few yuan at most. If not, he’d just make her sit around at home—she had little recourse. Once he left for good, he wasn't the least bit reluctant to part with the bed or chairs, since the couple months’ back rent was her problem—selling the furniture might not even cover that.

All Xiaofuzi could do was sell the furniture to pay the rent, coming home with just a floral cotton dress and a pair of silver earrings.

After selling his rickshaw, Er Qiangzi, aside from paying back the pawn with interest, had about twenty yuan left. Sometimes, he’d feel he was a widower in mid-life and terribly pitiful; since no one else felt sorry for him, he’d indulge himself with a couple of drinks and some good food, wallowing in self-pity. At such times, it was as if he held a grudge against money and would just spend freely. Sometimes he felt he should rest up and pull rickshaws again, work hard to raise his two sons to adulthood and pin some hope on them for the future. When thinking of his sons, he’d go out and buy a big pile of food for them, letting the two boys feast. Watching them wolf down the food, his eyes brimming with tears, he'd mutter: “Motherless kids! Hard-fated kids! Papa toils so you boys can have a future! I don't mind going hungry—what matters is you boys get enough! Eat! Don't forget your dad after you grow up!” At such times he spent plenty, too. Soon enough, the twenty yuan disappeared.

Out of money, whenever he drank and lost his temper, he’d ignore what the boys ate for a day or two. With no other choice, the kids would have to fend for themselves, scraping together a few coins to buy food. They’d act as attendants at weddings and funerals, follow carts to pick up scrap, sometimes managing to buy a few sesame cakes, sometimes only a jin of cheap sweet potatoes, skin, roots, and all. Other times, they'd have just one big coin between them, so they bought peanuts or roasted fava beans—inadequate to fill their bellies, but at least something to chew on.

When Xiaofuzi came home, the boys finally saw family: each hugged one of her legs, wordless, just smiling through tears. Their mother was gone—their sister was now their mom!

Er Qiangzi didn’t show much when his daughter returned. Her being home just meant one more mouth to feed. Yet seeing his sons so happy, he had to admit it was good to have a woman in the house, to cook and do laundry for everyone. He had nothing to say, just took things day by day.

Xiaofuzi wasn’t ugly. Though once very thin and small, living with the officer she’d put on some weight and grew taller. Round-faced, her eyebrows and eyes were well-shaped—nothing especially striking, but she looked sturdy and not unpleasant. Her upper lip was quite short; whatever her mood—angry or happy—she’d part her lips and show tidy white teeth. The officer had especially loved her teeth. When she bared them, she looked a bit dumb and indecisive, and also a little playful. This look made her—like any poor yet not ugly girl—like a wildflower: have a touch of charm or color, and someone will pluck and sell her at market.

Huniu, who normally ignored others in the compound, regarded Xiaofuzi as a friend. First, Xiaofuzi was fairly pretty; second, she had a pretty printed long dress; and third, Huniu figured that since Xiaofuzi had married an army officer, she must have seen something of the world, so was happy to spend time with her. Women aren't quick to make friends, but once they do, it happens fast; in just a few days, the two became close. Huniu liked snacks: whenever she had melon seeds or such, she'd call Xiaofuzi over to chat and munch together. In their chats, Xiaofuzi would naively show her teeth and describe stories Huniu had never heard. She hadn't had a good life with the officer, but in his good moods he’d take her to restaurants, the theater, so she had plenty to say, often making Huniu envious. There were many things she couldn’t speak of: to her, they were humiliations; to Huniu, they would be pleasures. When Huniu begged her to tell, she was embarrassed to say, but also embarrassed to refuse. She'd seen dirty picture books—Huniu never had. Such things, Huniu could hear again and again. She considered Xiaofuzi the most lovable, enviable, and even worthy of jealousy person. After hearing those stories, then looking at herself—her looks, her age, her husband—Huniu felt her whole life was just too cruel. She'd had no youth, nor any hope for the future, and as for the present,Once again, Xiangzi was just like a dead brick! The more dissatisfied she was with Xiangzi, the more she loved Xiaofuzi. Although Xiaofuzi was so poor, so pitiful, yet in her eyes, she was someone who had enjoyed good fortune and seen the world, so even if she died now, she wouldn’t be wronged. To her, Xiaofuzi represented all the enjoyment a woman ought to have.

Xiaofuzi’s hardships seemed invisible to Huniu. Xiaofuzi brought nothing back home, yet she had to—even if her father was useless—take care of her two younger brothers. How could she find money to prepare meals for them?

Erqiangzi got drunk and suddenly had an idea: “If you really care about your brothers, you’ll find a way to earn money to support them! Everyone’s counting on me, but I have to work like a beast for others all day long, so I have to eat first; can I run on an empty stomach? What if I fall on my head and die in the street—would you find that funny? You’re idle anyway, there’s an easy way right in front of you; if you’re not going to sell, what are you waiting for?”

Looking at her drunk cat-like father, looking at herself, looking at her two younger brothers who were as hungry as mice, all Xiaofuzi had left was to cry. Tears couldn’t move her father, tears couldn’t feed her brothers, she had to do something more practical. In order to feed her brothers, she had to sell her own flesh. As she held her little brother, her tears fell on his hair; he said, “Sister, I’m hungry!” Sister! Sister is a piece of meat for the brothers to eat!

Not only did Huniu not comfort Xiaofuzi, she actually wanted to help her: Huniu was willing to invest a little capital to help her dress up properly, then Xiaofuzi could pay her back with what she earned. Huniu was willing to lend her a place because her own room was too dirty, while Huniu had at least some kind of Xiangzi, plus there were two rooms, so everyone had space to move around. Xiangzi wouldn’t come back during the day, so Huniu was happy to help her friend, and she could watch more, learn more—things she lacked herself but wanted to learn. Each time Xiaofuzi used the room, Huniu had one condition: she had to pay her twenty cents. Friends are friends, business is business. For Xiaofuzi’s sake, she had to tidy the room well, which meant both labor and extra expense; of course she’d need to spend money on brooms and dustpans and the like. Twenty cents was absolutely not too much, and just because they were friends, she was giving her a good deal.

Xiaofuzi showed a bit of her teeth, her tears falling inside her stomach.

Xiangzi knew nothing, but he still couldn’t sleep well. Huniu had “helped” Xiaofuzi, and also wanted to find her lost youth in Xiangzi.

[1]

 Conversation, i.e., talking.

[2]

 Person; here, refers to a man. This term is only used in informal relationships between men and women.

Eighteen

By June, the big compound was almost silent during the day. The children got up early, carrying broken baskets to collect whatever they could find; by 9 a.m., the scorching sun would almost crack their thin, bare backs, so they had to bring home whatever they could find and eat the scraps the adults gave them. Then, the older ones, if they could find the tiniest bit of capital in the world, would buy and collect together some ice pops to sell. If they couldn’t find even that much capital, they would go out of the city and swim in the moat, maybe steal a few pieces of coal at the station, or catch some dragonflies and cicadas to sell to kids from rich families. The younger ones didn’t dare go far, so they’d go wherever there were trees outside the gate, picking locust larvae, digging up “King Kong bugs” [1]

 or other things to play with. Once the children were out, the men also went out, and the women all bared their backs in the house, no one coming out; it wasn’t for shame, but because the ground in the courtyard was already so hot it would burn their feet.

Not until the sun was about to set did the men and kids gradually come home, and then there would be the shadow of the wall and a bit of a cool breeze in the yard, while inside the rooms it was stifling with a day’s heat, like a bunch of fire cages; everyone sat in the courtyard, waiting for the women to cook. At this moment, the courtyard was very lively, like a market with no goods for sale. Everyone had suffered the heat all day, their eyes red, no one in a good mood; with empty stomachs, they were all starving and irritable. At the slightest disagreement, some would beat the kids, some would beat their wives; even if there was no actual fight, there would at least be a good bout of cursing. This noisy mess continued until everyone had eaten. Some kids fell asleep right in the courtyard, some ran out to the street to play wildly [2]

. After the adults were full, their tempers calmed considerably, the chatty ones would gather in groups to talk about the day’s hardships. Those who still hadn’t managed to eat and had nothing left to pawn or sell—even if they had something to pawn or sell—the day was too dark to do it. No matter how hot it was inside, the men would flop onto the kang without a word, or maybe they’d just shout and curse out loud. The women would go around gritting back tears, asking for favors everywhere, not knowing how many refusals before they managed to borrow a ragged 20-cent note. Clutching this cherished note, she’d go out to buy some mixed flour, cook up a pot of porridge for everyone.

Huniu and Xiaofuzi were not part of this daily rhythm. Huniu was pregnant again; this time it was real. Xiangzi went out early every morning, but she would only get up around eight or nine; the old belief was that it wasn’t good for pregnant women to exert themselves, and Huniu believed this by tradition, but also used it to display her status: everyone else had to get up early to work, but only she could lounge in bed as long as she liked. In the evening, she’d take a small stool and sit outside the front gate where there was wind, only coming back inside after almost everyone in the yard was asleep. She looked down on idle chatting with the others.

Xiaofuzi also got up late, but for another reason. She was afraid of the men in the yard looking at her sideways, so she wouldn’t leave her room until they’d all gone. During the day, she’d either look for Huniu or go for a walk, because she herself was her own advertisement. At night, to avoid the courtyard’s attention, she’d wander the streets and only sneak back in when she guessed everyone had lain down.

Among the men, Xiangzi and Erqiangzi were exceptions. Xiangzi was afraid to enter the big courtyard, even more afraid to go into his room. The talk of poverty among the others made him restless, so he preferred to sit somewhere by himself. Inside, he increasingly felt that Huniu was like a tigress. The little room was so hot and stifling, and with that tigress inside, every time he went in it was as if he couldn’t breathe. In recent days, he had no choice but to come home early, just to avoid Huniu’s nagging and trouble-making. Lately, with Xiaofuzi as a companion, she left him alone, so he came back later.

As for Erqiangzi, he’d hardly been coming home lately. He knew about his daughter’s business and had no face left to enter the courtyard. But he couldn’t stop her; he knew he wasn’t capable of supporting the kids. So he simply stayed away, out of sight, out of mind. Sometimes he hated his daughter; if Xiaofuzi had been a boy, she would never have brought such disgrace. Since she was born a girl, why did she have to land in his lap! Sometimes he pitied his daughter—his daughter was selling her body to support her two younger brothers! Whether he hated or pitied her, he was helpless. Whenever he drank and ran out of money, he couldn’t hate or pity her anymore—he’d come back and ask her for money. At times like that, he saw his daughter only as something that could bring in cash; after all, as her father, it was only right and proper for him to ask. At those times he also remembered his dignity: after all, didn’t everyone look down on Xiaofuzi? Even her father didn’t let her off—he forced her to hand over money, cursing all the while, as if cursing for everyone to hear—Erqiangzi was blameless, Xiaofuzi was shameless by nature.

When he raised a fuss, Xiaofuzi wouldn’t even breathe hard in response. Instead, Huniu would half curse, half scold, and manage to deal with him—of course, he always walked away with some money. This kind of money was only good for more drinking, because if he saw it while sober, he’d want to jump in the river or hang himself.

On the fifteenth of June, the heat went mad. The moment the sun came up, the ground was already on fire. Wisps of gray air, not quite cloud, not quite mist, floated low in the sky, making everyone feel suffocated. There wasn’t a trace of wind. Xiangzi looked at the gray-red sky in the courtyard, planning to go out and pull fares late—after 4 p.m.; if he couldn’t make enough, he could keep pulling all the way till dawn: nighttime was better than daytime no matter what.

Huniu urged him out, afraid he’d be in the way at home—what if Xiaofuzi brought a customer? “You think you’ll be comfortable at home? By noon even the walls in the room are scorching!”

He said nothing, drank a ladle of cold water, and went out.

The willow trees lining the streets looked sick, their leaves coated with gray dust, curled on the branches; the limbs were too lazy to move and drooped dispiritedly. Not a drop of water anywhere on the road, just desiccated white light. The dust on the sidewalks rose so high it mixed with the haze in the sky, forming a vicious cloud of gray sand, scorching the faces of passersby. Everywhere was dry, everywhere burned to the touch, everywhere stifling— the whole old city was like a kiln fired through, making it hard to breathe. Dogs sprawled on the ground, tongue lolling red; mules and horses flared their nostrils extra wide; street vendors didn’t dare shout; the asphalt roads melted; even the brass signs in front of the shops looked like they were about to melt. The streets were unusually quiet, only the dull clanging of metal from the menders’ shops sounding in the heat. The rickshaw pullers, knowing they wouldn’t eat if they didn’t work, were too lazy today to hustle for fares: some parked in a bit of shade, propped up the covers, and dozed in their cars; some ducked into tea houses; some hadn’t even brought out their carts, just came to the street to see if there was any business at all. Those who did manage to get fares, even the handsomest young guys, actually chose disgrace over risk—no one dared run, only dropped their heads and trudged along. Every well became their salvation: after a few steps, they rushed over to drink regardless of whether it was fresh-drawn; if they couldn’t beat the mules and horses to it, they joined them at the trough, chugging down huge gulps. Still others, overcome by heatstroke or exhaustion, simply collapsed in the street and never got up again.

Even Xiangzi became a little timid! After walking a few steps dragging his empty rickshaw, he felt the heat wrap his whole body from face to feet, even sweat oozing from the backs of his hands. But seeing a fare, he still wanted to run, thinking maybe there’d be some breeze if he got moving. After picking up a fare and starting off, he realized the harshness of the weather—nobody should have to work in this. Just by running, he could barely breathe; his lips felt parched, even if he knew he wasn’t thirsty, every sight of water made him want to drink. If he didn’t run, the poisonous sun would crack his hands and back open. By the time he finally reached his destination, his clothes were plastered to his skin. Waving his palm-leaf fan, the wind was hot. By then he had no idea how many mouthfuls of cool water he’d drunk, but he went to the tea house again. Two pots of hot tea went down, and he felt a bit calmer. The tea went down his throat, and immediately sweat poured from his body. It was as if his body were hollow and couldn’t store any more moisture. He dared not move again.

He sat for a long time, but became restless. Too scared to go out, with nothing to do, he felt the weather seemed intent on making trouble for him. No, he couldn’t just give in. He hadn’t been pulling rickshaws for just a day, this wasn’t his first summer—he couldn’t waste the whole day like this. He thought about going out, but his legs really didn’t want to move, his whole body felt weak, as if he’d never really cleaned off after a bath; he’d sweated plenty, but still didn’t feel relaxed. After sitting a while longer, he couldn’t sit still anymore—either way, sitting meant sweating, so why not just go out and give it a try.

The moment he went out, he realized his mistake. The gray haze up in the sky had dispersed—not so stifling, but the sun was even fiercer: no one dared look up to see where the sun was, everything everywhere was glaring, in the air, on the roofs, walls, ground, all brilliantly white with a touch of red; from top to bottom, it was like an immense burning mirror, each beam of sunlight like the focal point of the mirror, threatening to set everything on fire. In this white glare, every color was harsh, every sound grating, every smell full of the stench steaming up from the ground. It seemed as if the streets were empty, the road suddenly wider, vast with not the slightest hint of coolness, its bleached brightness frightening. Xiangzi didn’t know what to do, kept his head down, pulled his rickshaw, moving forward extremely slowly, with no plan, no destination, dazed and sluggish, his body covered in sticky sweat, giving off a sour stench. After a while, the soles of his feet and his socks stuck together, as if treading on wet mud, deeply uncomfortable. He didn’t want to drink more water, but when he saw a well he couldn’t help going over for another big gulp, not to quench thirst, just to enjoy the brief chill moving from mouth to stomach. As soon as it hit, his pores squeezed tight, his body shivered, and he felt a strange sort of comfort. After drinking, he began to hiccup, the water threatening to come back up!

Walking for a while, sitting for a while, he was always too lazy to hustle for fares. All the way till noon, he didn’t even feel hungry. He wanted to get something to eat as usual but felt nauseous at the sight of food. His stomach was full of all kinds of water, sometimes it gurgled inside like the sound of a mule’s belly after it’s had its fill.

Comparing winter and summer, Xiangzi always thought winter was worse. He never expected summer could feel this unbearable. He’d spent more than one summer in the city, but he didn’t remember it ever being so hot. Was it hotter this year, or was his body weaker? With that thought, he suddenly didn’t feel so dizzy, as if a wave of cold passed through him. His body—yes, his body was failing! He was scared, but there was nothing he could do. He couldn’t get rid of Huniu; he would soon become Erqiangzi, turn into that tall guy he’d met, turn into Xiao Ma’s grandfather. Xiangzi was finished!

It was just after one in the afternoon, the hottest moment of the day, when he picked up another fare. That was the hottest part of the day.It happened to be the hottest day of the summer, but he decided to go out for a run. He didn't care how hot it was under the sun: if he could make a run and it turned out not that bad, it would prove his body wasn't ruined; if he couldn't manage this job, then there was nothing else to say—better to take a tumble and drop dead on that burning ground!

He had only walked a few steps when he felt a bit of cool breeze, just like a little cool air sneaking in through the door crack into an extremely hot room. He couldn't believe it; he looked at the willow branches by the roadside and sure enough, they moved slightly twice. Suddenly, the street was crowded; people in the shops rushed outside, clutching palm-leaf fans over their heads, searching all around: "There's a cool breeze! There's a cool breeze! The cool breeze is here!" Everyone nearly jumped up and shouted. The willow trees by the road suddenly turned into angels, delivering a message from the heavens: "The willow twigs are moving! Dear Heaven, please grant us more cool wind!"

It was still hot, but people felt much calmer inside. Even a little bit of cool wind gave them a lot of hope. After a few breezes, the sunlight was not so intense—alternating between brightness and a bit dim, as if floating sand was swirling above. The wind suddenly picked up, and the willow twigs that had barely moved for half a day seemed to have suddenly received some great news, swaying and dancing as if they'd grown longer. When the gust passed, the sky darkened, and all the dust rose high into the air. When some of the dust settled, ink-black clouds appeared at the northern edge of the sky. The sweat was gone from Xiangzi's body; he glanced north, stopped his rickshaw, and put on the rain tarp—he knew summer rain comes suddenly, leaving no time to spare.

As soon as the tarp was on, there was another gust of wind, and the rolling black clouds soon covered half the sky. The heat rising off the ground mixed with the cool wind, along with the foul, musky dry earth, making it feel cool and hot at the same time. The southern half of the sky was still bright and sunny, but the northern half was black with clouds like ink—an ominous feeling, as if some disaster was imminent and everything was in chaos. Rickshaw drivers scrambled to put on the rain tarps, shopkeepers hurried to haul in the shop banners, hawkers packed up their stalls in a panic, and pedestrians quickened their pace. Another gust of wind. When it passed, the shop banners, stalls, and pedestrians on the street seemed to have been swept away by the wind—gone in an instant, leaving only the willow branches wildly dancing in the wind.

Before the clouds had even completely covered the sky, it was already so dark on the ground that it seemed the blazing, scorching noon had turned to night. The wind carried drops of rain, searching this way and that along the ground, colliding all over. Far to the north, a red flash burst out, as if exposing a blood-red patch beyond the black clouds. The wind slowed, but it was sharp and biting, making people tremble. When such a gust had passed, everything seemed at a loss, even the willows waiting in anxious suspense for something. Another flash, right overhead, and bright raindrops immediately fell, pounding hard and sending up dust mixed with a faint smell of rain. A few heavy drops hit Xiangzi's back, making him shiver. The rain paused, the black clouds evened out across the sky. Then another gust of wind, fiercer than before, sent the willow branches flying horizontally, dust whirling in all directions, and the curtains of rain fell; the wind, dust, and rain all mixed together, merging into one—everywhere was bleak, cold, and enveloped in gray, everything covered up, you couldn't tell where the trees were, where the ground was, or where the clouds were; everything in chaos, noise, and confusion. The wind passed, leaving only the straight lines of rain pouring from sky to earth, not visible as streaks but just as a sheet, arrows shooting up from the ground, thousands of waterfalls pouring down from the roofs. Within minutes, sky and earth were indistinguishable, rivers pouring down from above and flowing across the land, forming a dim and yellowish, sometimes brilliantly white, world of water.

Xiangzi's clothes were already soaked through, not a dry spot on his body; through his straw hat, his hair was also drenched. The water on the ground had risen above his feet, making it difficult to walk; rain from above pounded his head and back, swept across his face, and wrapped around his thighs. He couldn't look up, couldn't open his eyes, couldn't breathe, couldn't take a step. It was as if he were standing still in the water, unable to tell which way was the road, completely oblivious to front, back, left, or right, only knowing that bone-chilling water was being poured on him from all sides. He didn't know anything anymore, just a vague bit of warmth in his chest and the constant sound of rain in his ears. He wanted to put down the rickshaw but didn't know where to leave it. He wanted to run, but the water clung to his legs. So, half alive and half dead, head down, he dragged himself forward step by step. The passenger in the rickshaw seemed to have died, not making a sound and letting the rickshaw puller struggle for life in the water.

The rain eased, and Xiangzi straightened his back a little, exhaled: "Sir, let's take shelter and wait a bit!"

"Keep going! What do you mean by leaving me here?" the passenger stomped and shouted.

Xiangzi really wanted to just park the rickshaw and go find a place to shelter. But looking at himself, already drenched and dripping, he knew if he stopped he would shiver into a ball. So he grit his teeth and splashed through the water, running regardless of depth or obstacles. He had barely run far when the sky darkened again, then flashed bright, and the rain stung his eyes once more.

He made it to the destination, but the passenger didn't give a single extra coin. Xiangzi didn't say a word—he was struggling just to stay alive.

The rain stopped for a little while, then started again, much lighter than before. Xiangzi rushed straight home. Hugging the stove, he dried himself for a while, shivering like a leaf caught in wind and rain. Huniu made him a cup of ginger sugar water; he drank it down in one go, dazed. After drinking, he burrowed into the bedding and knew nothing else; half asleep, half awake, the sound of rain rushing in his ears.

After four o'clock, the black clouds began to show signs of fatigue, producing weak, not very red flashes of lightning. After a while, the clouds split open in the west, the black edges of the cloud peaks outlined in golden yellow, with wisps of white vapor running below them. The lightning had moved south, dragging a few not-so-loud rumbles of thunder. After another while, a crack in the western clouds revealed sunlight, making the rain-drenched leaves glow green and gold. On the eastern sky hung a double rainbow, both ends poking into the black clouds, their arch touching a patch of blue sky. The rainbow soon faded, and the black clouds were gone from the sky. The freshly washed blue sky and everything beneath it seemed to be a new, cool, beautiful world just born from the darkness. Even on the puddles in the courtyard, a few colorful dragonflies had arrived.

Yet, except for the children running barefoot after those dragonflies, the people in the compound had no mind to appreciate the clear sky after the rain. A section of the rear eaves wall of Xiaofuzi’s house had collapsed; the three sisters were busy lifting the bedding to block the hole. Several places in the courtyard wall had collapsed, but no one cared—they all just hurried to tidy up their own rooms: some had steps that were too low, water had already poured in, and everyone was frantically scooping water out with dustpans and broken bowls. Some had a collapsed gable and were trying to block it up. Some roofs leaked like a watering can, soaking everything, leaving them to hurry and move their belongings to the stove to bake dry or place them on the windowsill to dry in the sun. During the rain, everyone hid in houses that could collapse and bury them alive at any time, leaving their fate to the heavens; after the rain, they sized things up, and cleaned up their losses. Though the heavy rain had passed and maybe a half cent was lost on every kilo of grain, their losses could not be compensated in this way. They paid rent, but no one ever came to repair the houses; unless a place collapsed so badly no one could live in it, only then would a mason or two come and patch it up with a bit of plain mortar and broken bricks—a fix meant just to collapse again. If the rent couldn't be paid, the whole family would be thrown out, and their belongings seized. The houses were dilapidated, and they were dangerous enough to kill, but no one cared. Their meager earnings could only rent such dwellings; battered, unsafe, all considered their fate!

The worst loss came from illness brought on by the rain. With both adults and children out hustling for work all day on the streets, summer downpours could fall on their heads at any time. They all earned a living by toil, always warm and sweaty, but the northern downpours were so sudden and so cold, sometimes with hailstones as big as walnuts; ice-cold raindrops beating down on their open pores would at the very least force them to lie sick on the bed with a fever for a day or two. When the kids got sick, there was no money for medicine; a storm might make the corn and sorghum in the fields grow tall, but it also took the lives of many poor children in the city. When adults fell ill, things were far worse; after the rain, poets recited verses about dewdrops and twin rainbows, while in poor families, an adult getting sick meant everyone went hungry. A rainstorm might add a few more prostitutes or petty thieves, or send some to jail; when the adults were sick, children becoming criminals or prostitutes at least beat starving! The rain fell on the rich as well as on the poor; it fell on the righteous as well as the unrighteous. In truth, the rain is not fair, for it falls on a world without fairness.

Xiangzi fell ill. He was not the only patient in the compound.

[1]

 Jingang, that is, the pupa of the locust worm.

[2]

 Sahuan, originally referred to animals running about joyfully, but is also used to describe children behaving in this way.

Nineteen

Xiangzi slept fitfully for two days and nights, which scared Huniu. On reaching the temple, she sought a divine remedy: besides a bit of incense ash, there were two or three herbs. After she forced it down his throat, he did open his eyes and look around, but soon fell asleep again, murmuring unclear words. Only then did Huniu think to call for a doctor. Two injections and one dose of medicine later, he finally woke up and asked, with eyes open, “Is it still raining?”

When the second dose was ready, he refused to take it. He felt sorry for the money and also hated himself for being so weak—to be taken down by a mere rain? He wouldn't drink that bitter bowl. To prove he didn't need medicine, he thought of getting up and dressing right away. But as soon as he sat up, his head felt weighed down like with a huge boulder, his neck went limp, golden stars flashed before his eyes, and he toppled over again. Nothing more to say—he took the bowl and swallowed the medicine.

He lay bedridden for ten days. The longer he lay there, the more anxious he became; sometimes he'd crawl onto the pillow and cry soundless tears. He knew he could no longer earn money—so expenses would all have to be covered by Huniu. When her money ran out, all they had left was his rickshaw. With Huniu's love of spending and eating, he couldn't keep up, and to top it off, she was pregnant! The more he couldn't get up, the more his imagination ran wild; the more he thought, the heavier his anxiety, making it even harder to recover. As soon as he regained a bit of strength, he asked Huniu, “Where's the rickshaw?”

“Relax, I've rented it to Ding Si to drive!”

“Ah!” He couldn't relax about his rickshaw, worrying Ding Si or anyone else would wreck it. But since he couldn't work, of course it had to be rented out; how could it just sit idle? He calculated: if he pulled it himself, he could make 50 or 60 cents a day, or so. Rent, coal, rice, firewood, kerosene, tea—forget even new clothes, it was just enough for two people, but they'd still have to be stingy—unlike Huniu, who was so carefree. Now, with only a little over a dime coming in daily from the rental, he'd be losing 40 or 50 cents a day, not to mention medicine. If he never got better, what then? No wonder Er Qiangzi drank, no wonder his suffering friends acted up—pulling a rickshaw is a dead end! No matter how hard you work or how tough you are, you'd better not get married, fall ill, or have a single mishap. Hmph! He remembered his first rickshaw, all the money he’d saved—who had he wronged? He didn't get sick, or married, he just lost it so ruthlessly and unreasonably! Good or bad, it made no difference; this road only led to death, and you could never tell when it would arrive, you'd never see it coming. At this thought, his worries turned to resignation—whatever, if he couldn't get up, he'd just keep lying down, that was just how it was! He tried not to think about anything, just lay there in silence. But soon enough, he couldn't bear it; he wanted to get up right away and return to the daily toil—life may be hard, but hope lingers until the coffin closes. But he couldn't get up. Bored and pitiful, he sought Huniu’s attention with a few words:

“I said that rickshaw was unlucky, and it truly is unlucky!”

“Take care of your illness! Always talking about the rickshaw, you're obsessed!”

He said no more. True, he was obsessed! Since starting as a rickshaw puller, he’d believed the rickshaw was everything—but... As soon as he felt a little better, he got up. Looking in the mirror, he couldn't recognize himself: face unshaven, temples and cheeks sunken, his eyes deep pits, and that scar etched with wrinkles! The room was stifling, but he dared not go into the courtyard—his legs felt boneless, and he was afraid of being seen. Not only in this compound, but at every rickshaw stand in the east and west city, didn’t they all know Xiangzi as a standout young man? He couldn't let them see him like this, wracked by illness. He wouldn't go outside, yet he felt suffocated indoors. He wished he could just eat himself strong again and return to pulling. But illness destroys a person; it comes and goes as it pleases.

He rested for a month. Whether fully recovered or not, he took the rickshaw out again. He wore his hat low, hoping not to be recognized so he could take it easy. “Xiangzi” and “fast” were inseparable; he couldn’t bear to crawl along lazily and be looked down on.

His body wasn’t nearly recovered, but greedy to make up for lost days, he overexerted; after a few days, the illness returned. This time he picked up dysentery. In frustration he slapped his own face, but no use—his belly felt like it was glued to his back, and he was still running to the toilet. When the dysentery finally subsided, just squatting and getting up cost him effort, let alone taking the rickshaw for a spin.

He was laid up for yet another month! He knew Huniu's money was nearly gone! By the fifteenth of August, he decided to get back to work. If he fell ill again this time, he swore he’d throw himself in the river!

During his first illness, Xiaofuzi often came by to check on him. Since Xiangzi could never win an argument with Huniu, and he was so bottled up inside, he sometimes chatted with Xiaofuzi. This infuriated Huniu. When Xiangzi was out, Xiaofuzi was a good friend; when he was home, Xiaofuzi—according to Huniu—was “just flirting! Shameless!” She then demanded Xiaofuzi repay the money she owed, “and from now on, no more coming around!”

Xiaofuzi lost her place to receive guests, and her own place was so dilapidated—the kang mat was blocking a leaky wall. Left with no options, she went to register at the “Transport Company.” But the “Transport Company” didn't want someone like her. They introduced only “female students” and “ladies of good families”—it was an exclusive channel, requiring a lot of money, and ordinary folks like her weren't accepted. She ran out of options. She thought of joining a brothel, but had no capital to start her own place, so she’d have to sign herself over to a brothel group, losing all freedom—who would then look after her two brothers? Dying would be the easiest thing—living was already hell. She wasn't afraid of death, but didn't want to die, because she wanted to do something more courageous and noble than death. She was determined to see her two brothers earning money; only then could she die at peace. She was doomed to die anyway, but at least one should die to save the other two! After thinking it over, she saw only one path: selling herself cheap. Naturally, whoever would come to that tiny room of hers wouldn’t pay much. Fine—anyone who came would do, as long as they paid. This way, she saved on clothes and makeup; her clients didn’t hope to see her dolled up, they just wanted to get what they paid for; she was young enough—already a bargain.

Huniu's pregnancy made her clumsy, even a trip to the shops felt risky, and Xiangzi was gone all day, Xiaofuzi wouldn’t come by, and she was as lonely as a dog tied up in a room. The lonelier she got, the more resentful she became, thinking Xiaofuzi's price cut was meant to spite her. She wouldn’t stand for it: sitting in the outer room with the door wide open, she'd wait; whenever someone headed toward Xiaofuzi's room, she’d raise her voice and make snide comments, embarrassing both the client and Xiaofuzi. With fewer clients, Huniu was pleased. Xiaofuzi knew if this went on, everyone in the courtyard would take Huniu’s side and drive her out. She was only afraid, not angry; people in her situation put reality ahead of anger and tears. She brought her little brother over and knelt to Huniu. She said nothing, but her expression said it all: if even this didn’t work, she herself wasn’t afraid to die, but no one else would live either! The greatest sacrifice is to endure humiliation, and the greatest endurance is to be ready to fight back.

Huniu was stumped. Whichever way she thought about it, it didn’t sit right, but with that huge belly, she dared not fight. If she couldn’t win by force, she conceded herself an out: she’d just been messing with Xiaofuzi, and who would've thought it would get serious—Xiaofuzi was just too stubborn. With this explanation, they became friends again, and Huniu kept on supporting Xiaofuzi as before.

Since he started working after Mid-Autumn, Xiangzi had become much more cautious; two bouts of illness had taught him that he wasn’t made of iron. His ambition to make money wasn’t completely forgotten, but blow after blow made him realize how insignificant individual strength was; a hero must grit their teeth in adversity, but even then, blood may come with it! Though dysentery was cured, he still suffered occasional stomach pain. Sometimes, just as his legs started to stretch out in stride and he tried to speed up, a twisting cramp would hit his gut, slowing him down or even making him stop all at once, bow-headed, belly drawn in, forcing himself to bear it. Working alone on scattered rides wasn’t so bad, but if he was pulling a group car and suddenly had to stop, it left others confused and made him feel very awkward. Only in his twenties, yet he was already making a spectacle of himself—what would he do at thirty or forty? Just thinking about it made him break out in a cold sweat!

For his health, he was more than willing to return to being a porter. At least it was a job with breaks; work was tough, but the rest periods were longer, overall easier than pulling single fares. But he knew for sure Huniu would never allow it—after marrying, he’d lost his freedom, and Huniu was especially fierce. He accepted his bad luck. For half a year, from autumn to winter, he just muddled through, half coping, half struggling, never daring to slack off or relax, always suppressed and downcast, head bent to the grind. Head down, gone was his old brash, easy-going self who cared for nothing. When it came to making money, he still earned more than the average rickshaw puller. Unless he was doubled over in pain, he never let a fare go; he pulled whenever possible, never picking up any bad habits. He never hiked prices, never handed off customers midway, never waited selfishly for a better fare. Because of this, he worked harder but brought in money steadily every day. He never took shortcuts, so there was no risk involved.

But the money came in too slowly and there was nothing left over. In one hand, out the other, and at the end of the day—empty. He didn’t even dare to think about saving. He knew how to economize, but Huniu could spend! Huniu's due date was at the start of the second lunar month. All winter, her belly grew more obvious, and she’d ostentatiously stick it out, just to show how important she was. Staring at her own stomach, she could hardly even be bothered to get out of bed. She left all the cooking to Xiaofuzi, naturally meaning the leftovers and scraps went to Xiaofuzi’s little brothers. That added up. On top of meals, Huniu needed snacks—the more her belly showed, the more she felt she needed good food; she wouldn’t be deprived. She bought snacks at all hours, and told Xiangzi to bring her something home daily. Whatever he earned, she spent; her needs went up as his earnings did. Xiangzi couldn’t say anything. He’d spent her money while ill, so turnabout was fair play, of course she deserved to spend his now. If he tried to clamp down on spending, she’d instantly say she was sick—“being pregnant is like being sick for nine months, what do you know?” She was telling the truth. By New Year, her demands multiplied. Unable to go out, she’d send Xiaofuzi running to market again and again. Upset at not being able to leave, but too doting to go out herself, and stifled by staying in, she soothed herself by buying more things to look at. She always claimed she was buying for Xiangzi: “You've worked hard all year, don’t you deserve a treat? Ever since your illness, you’ve never fully recovered; if you don’t eat up at the end of the year, you’ll end up shriveled and useless!” Xiangzi couldn't and didn't argue; and when the food was ready, she’d eat two or three big bowls at a time. Afterward, with no exercise, she’d be stuffed, clutching her belly and insisting the baby was pressing on her!

After the New Year, she absolutely forbade Xiangzi to go out at night, as she never knew when she’d go into labor and was afraid. At this point, she finally admitted to her real age, though still wouldn’t say outright, and never repeated, “I’m only a little older than you.” With all this fuss, Xiangzi became dizzy with confusion. To continue life just meant having children, but even though he didn’t really want a child, the thought of soon being called “dad”—this simplest and most mysterious word—moved even the harshest person. Xiangzi, awkward and slow, saw nothing special about himself, nothing to take pride in; but the idea of that word suddenly made him feel dignified, as if being childless would have made life empty, but with a child, there was meaning. He wanted to give Huniu all he could, to serve and provide—she was no longer just one person; no matter how annoying she was, she truly deserved all the credit for this. But however much credit she deserved, her troublemaking was still impossible to endure. She’d change her mind every minute, fussing over gods and ghosts, while Xiangzi desperately needed rest to earn a living—no matter how she spent money, he needed a proper night’s sleep to work hard the next day. She wouldn’t let him out at night, nor let him sleep well; he was at wit’s end, drifting around all day in a daze, not knowing what to do. Sometimes happy, sometimes anxious, sometimes irritated, sometimes ashamed of his happiness, sometimes consoled amid anxiety, sometimes cheered in his gloom—his feelings spun in circles, confusing the simplest of people until he no longer knew north from south. Once, he even carried a fare past their destination, forgetting where they’d hired him to go!

Around the Lantern Festival, Huniu decided to have Xiangzi fetch the midwife.She couldn't hold on any longer. The midwife came and told her it wasn't time yet, and explained some signs that would appear when she was about to give birth. After enduring for two more days, she started to make a fuss again. The midwife was called back, but it still wasn’t time. She cried and shouted that she wanted to die, that she couldn't take this torment any more. Xiangzi had no solution; just to show he was doing his part, he had no choice but to grant her request and temporarily stopped pulling the rickshaw.

The fuss went on until the end of the month, even Xiangzi could see it was the real time—it had come. She no longer looked human. The midwife came once more and gave Xiangzi a hint that it might be a difficult birth. Huniu was older, it was her first child, she usually lacked exercise, and the fetus was large because she liked to eat greasy food during pregnancy; all these taken together meant a smooth delivery was out of the question. Besides, she had never had a doctor’s check-up, and the fetus's position hadn’t been corrected; the midwife didn’t have surgical skills, but all she could do was say: “I’m just worried it’s a transverse or breech birth!”

In this compound, people were used to talking about a child’s birth and a mother’s death together as a matter of course. But Huniu was at even greater risk than others—most women kept working and active right up to the day of labor, and didn't eat much, so the fetus wouldn't be too big, which made delivery easier. Their danger was in postpartum complications, but Huniu was the opposite. Her advantages turned into her misfortune.

Xiangzi, Xiaofuzi, and the midwife kept watch over her for three days and nights. She called upon every god and spirit, making countless vows, all in vain. In the end, her voice was hoarse and weak, just murmuring “Mama! Mama!” The midwife was helpless, everyone was helpless. It was Huniu herself who came up with the idea: she told Xiangzi to go outside Deshengmen and fetch Old Lady Chen—the medium who channeled the Toad Immortal. Old Lady Chen refused to come for less than five yuan; Huniu produced her last seven or eight yuan: “Good Xiangzi, hurry and go! Don’t worry about the money! When I’m better, I’ll be good to you! Go quickly!”

Old Lady Chen arrived, at dusk, with the “Boy”—a yellow-faced man in his forties. She herself was about fifty, wearing a blue silk jacket, a red pomegranate blossom on her head, and a full set of gilded jewelry. Her eyes were piercing; as soon as she came in she washed her hands, then lit the incense. She bowed her head before the altar, then sat behind it, staring blankly at the stream of incense smoke. Suddenly her whole body shook, as if she shuddered violently, hung her head, closed her eyes, and sat motionless for a long time. The room was so quiet you could hear a pin drop. Huniu bit her lip and dared not make a sound. Slowly, Old Lady Chen raised her head, glancing at everyone, nodding; the “Boy” tugged at Xiangzi, urging him to kneel and bow. Xiangzi didn’t know if he believed in gods or not, just felt that bowing couldn’t go wrong. In a daze, he had no idea how many times he knelt. When he stood up, he stared at those ghostly “spirit” eyes and at the burning red incense tips, smelling the smoke. In his heart, he vaguely hoped something good would come out of this ritual. He stood there dazed, cold sweat breaking out in his palms.

The Toad Immortal spoke in an old, somewhat stuttering voice: “Don, don, don’t worry! Draw a labor-accelerating charm!”

The “Boy” hurriedly handed over a sheet of yellow soft paper. The Immortal scratched at the burning incense a few times, then, wetting the brush with spit, drew on the paper.

After finishing the charm, she stammered a few more sentences: the general idea was that Huniu owed this child a debt in her previous life, so she had to suffer now. Xiangzi was muddleheaded and didn’t really understand, but he was a bit scared.

Old Lady Chen gave a long yawn, closed her eyes dazedly for a while as if waking from a deep sleep, then opened them. The “Boy” quickly relayed the Immortal’s words. She seemed quite pleased: “The Immortal is happy today, wants to talk!” She then instructed Xiangzi how to get Huniu to swallow the spirit charm, and also gave her a pill to take with it.

Old Lady Chen eagerly waited to see the charm’s effect, so Xiangzi had to prepare a meal for her. He asked Xiaofuzi to take care of it. Xiaofuzi brought back hot sesame flatbread and stewed pig elbow; Old Lady Chen still complained there was no wine.

After Huniu swallowed the charm, and after Old Lady Chen and the “Boy” ate, Huniu was still thrashing about. She kept struggling for over an hour, and her eyes gradually started to roll upward. Old Lady Chen still had ideas, calmly telling Xiangzi to kneel before a big incense stick. Xiangzi’s faith in Old Lady Chen was almost gone. But since he’d already spent five yuan, he figured he might as well try all her methods. Since he couldn’t bring himself to beat Huniu, he’d just follow this last-ditch plan—who knows, maybe something would work!

He knelt stiffly in front of the incense, not sure which god he was praying to, only that he wanted to be devout. As he watched the flames dance, he pretended to see shapes and shadows in them, quietly making his wish. The incense burned shorter and shorter, with black streaks showing in the flame's heart; he lowered his head, hands braced on the ground, already a bit drowsy. He hadn’t had a good sleep in two or three days. Suddenly his neck gave out, startling him awake. Looking up, there wasn’t much incense left. He didn’t check if it was time to rise, just propped himself up and stood, legs numb.

Old Lady Chen and the “Boy” had already slipped out quietly.

Xiangzi didn't have time to curse her; he rushed to check on Huniu, knowing things were now dire. Huniu was only gasping loudly, unable to make any sound. The midwife told him, you’d better get her to a hospital—she was out of options.

It was as if Xiangzi's heart suddenly cracked apart, and he began bawling with his mouth wide open. Xiaofuzi was crying, too, but since she was helping out, she kept her wits moderately intact. “Brother Xiang! Don’t cry yet! I’ll run to the hospital and ask!”

Whether Xiangzi heard her or not, she wiped her tears and ran outside.

She was gone for about an hour. By the time she rushed back, she was too winded to talk. Clutching the table, she coughed for a long time before speaking: it’s ten yuan just for a doctor to come by, and that’s only to take a look, not to deliver the baby. Delivery costs twenty. And if it’s a difficult birth and she needs to go to the hospital, that’d be dozens of yuan. “Brother Xiang! What can we do now?!” Xiangzi had no way out; he could only wait for fate to take whoever it wanted.

Stupidity and cruelty are just some of the realities here; but the reasons for such stupidity and cruelty go deeper.

At midnight, Huniu died, taking her stillborn child with her.

[1]

 Pulling shoulder to shoulder, that is, sharing evenly.

[2]

 Head to head, meaning first class.

Twenty

Xiangzi’s rickshaw was sold!

Money flowed out like water; he couldn’t hold it back. The dead must be carried out, even nailing up the obituary cost money.

Xiangzi seemed stupefied, watching everyone come and go in a frenzy, and just kept forking over cash. His eyes were alarmingly bloodshot, with a yellow-white crust at the corners; his ears were buzzing, and he wandered numbly after the crowd, having no idea what he was doing.

Only as he followed Huniu’s coffin out the city did he regain a bit of clarity, though still unable to think straight. There was no funeral procession. Aside from Xiangzi, there were only Xiaofuzi’s two younger brothers, each carrying a thin packet of paper money to toss along the road for the way-blocking ghosts.

Dazed and vacant, Xiangzi watched the porters bury the coffin—he didn’t cry. It felt like his brain was on fire, burning away all his tears; desperately wanting to cry, but nothing would come out. He stared blankly, barely aware of what was happening, until the “boss” came over and instructed him, and he remembered to go home.

Xiaofuzi had already tidied up the house. Back home, he collapsed on the kang, too exhausted to move. His dry eyes couldn’t close, so he stared dully at the ceiling, stained by old leaks. Unable to sleep, he sat up. After a glance around the room, he dared not look anymore. He didn’t know what to do with himself. He went out to buy a pack of “Yellow Lion” cigarettes, sat on the edge of the kang, lit one; he didn’t even like smoking, just stared blankly at the blue smoke from the tip. Suddenly tears streamed down—he thought not just of Huniu, but of everything. After years in the city, this was all he had achieved—this was it, this was it! Even crying made no sound. The rickshaw, the rickshaw—that was his livelihood. He bought one, lost it; bought another, now sold it; up and down three times, like chasing a ghost, never able to clutch it tight, and getting nothing but pain and humiliation in return. Now it was gone—everything was gone. Even his wife was gone! Huniu was tough, but without her, how could he ever build a home?
