All the things in the house were hers, but she herself was now buried outside the city! The more he thought, the angrier he got; his tears were cut off by rage. He fiercely sucked on the cigarette—didn’t like smoking, but kept puffing anyway. When he finished, he cradled his head in his hands, mouth and heart burning; he wanted to howl just to spill this heart’s blood and get it all out.

He didn’t know how long he sat like that. Xiaofuzi came in and stood dully by the kitchen counter in the front room, looking at him.

He jerked his head up and saw her—tears instantly welled up. At that moment, even the sight of a dog would have made him cry; full of grievance, he longed to pour his heart out to any living thing, to seek comfort. He wanted to talk, to get some sympathy; but with so many things to say, his mouth couldn’t get anything out.

“Brother Xiang!” She stepped closer. “I’ve tidied up all the things.”

He nodded and couldn’t even thank her; politeness in grief seemed hollow. “So what do you plan to do?”

“Huh?” He seemed not to understand, but immediately realized and shook his head—he couldn’t stop to think about plans.

She took two steps closer. Her face suddenly flushed, showing some white teeth, but no words came out. Her life had forced her to forget shame, but faced with something serious, she was still a sincere woman: half a woman’s heart runs on shame. “I was thinking…” That was all she managed. There was so much in her heart; but with her blush, it all scattered and she couldn’t recall a thing.

The true words of the human world are already scarce—a woman’s blush speaks louder than a thousand words; even Xiangzi understood her meaning. To him, she was the most beautiful woman, beauty in her very bones—even if she were covered in sores with all her flesh rotted away, he’d still find her beautiful. She was beautiful, young, strong-willed, and frugal. If Xiangzi should ever want to marry again, she would be ideal. He didn’t want to take another wife right away, had no mental space for such things. Yet since she was willing, and since hard life forced her to bring it up this soon, he couldn’t refuse her; she was so good to begin with, and had helped him so much. He could only nod. He truly wanted to walk over and embrace her, to have a good cry and shed all his grievances, then work together with her in hardship again. In her, he saw the solace a man can and should find in a woman. Though he wasn’t much of a talker, with her he wanted to speak freely; talking to her meant his words wouldn’t be wasted; a nod or a smile from her was the most perfect reply, making him feel like he really had a “home.”

Just then, Xiaofuzi’s second younger brother came in: “Sister! Dad’s here!”

She frowned. She’d just opened the door when Erqiangzi was already in the yard. “What are you doing in Xiangzi’s room?” Erqiangzi stared, eyes round, legs staggering like a garlic clove, swaying over: “Haven’t you sold enough yet, still want to let Xiangzi have a free go? You shameless thing!”

Hearing his own name, Xiangzi rushed out, standing behind Xiaofuzi. “I’m talking to you, Xiangzi,” Erqiangzi tried to square his chest, but could barely stand: “I’m talking to you, Xiangzi—be a man, would you? Taking advantage of someone, fine, but her? Of all people?!”

Xiangzi didn’t want to pick on a drunk, but his pent-up frustration wouldn’t allow him to restrain his rage. He strode forward. Four bloodshot eyes met, as if sparks would shoot out from the air between them. Xiangzi grabbed Erqiangzi’s shoulder as if he were picking up a child and threw him far away.

Alcohol magnified his guilt, flipping it into rage—Erqiangzi’s drunkenness had always been partly an act, but after being thrown like that, he sobered up halfway. He thought about counterattacking, but knew he was no match for Xiangzi. So he just went off quietly, but felt horribly ill at ease. He sat on the ground, unwilling to get up, but also embarrassed to keep sitting. His mind was a jumble; he just muttered: “I’m disciplining my children—what’s it got to do with you? Hitting me? You’re something! Not even fit!”

Xiangzi kept silent, just quietly waited for another attack.

Xiaofuzi, in tears, was at a loss. She couldn’t calm her father, nor did it feel right to see Xiangzi hit him. She searched her pockets and managed to collect a handful of coppers to give her brother. Normally, her brother would never dare approach their father, but seeing him thrown down today gave him some courage. “Here, go now!”

Erqiangzi squinted as he took the money, getting up while grumbling: “Look at you lot raised by girls! If you get on this grandpa’s nerves, I swear I’ll take a knife and butcher the lot of you!” By the time he neared the gate, he shouted: “Xiangzi! We’ll settle this outside later!”

[1]

After Erqiangzi left, Xiangzi and Xiaofuzi went back inside.

“I have no way out!” she muttered to herself, a sentence summing up all her troubles, and carrying boundless hope—if only Xiangzi would marry her, she would have a way out.

After all this, Xiangzi now saw many dark shadows in her.He still liked her, but he couldn’t shoulder the responsibility of supporting her two younger brothers and a drunken father! He didn’t dare to imagine that if Tiger Girl died, he would be free; Tiger Girl had her own merits, at least she’d helped him a lot financially. He didn’t dare to think what would happen if Little Fuzi were to mooch off him, but it was also true that her family couldn’t make a living. Whether you love or not, poor people have to decide based on money; the “seed of love” only grows in wealthy families.

He began packing up his things.

“You’re going to move out, aren’t you?” Little Fuzi’s lips turned completely pale.

“Move out!” He steeled himself; in a world without justice, the poor keep the tiny bit of freedom they have by being tough-hearted.

She glanced at him, then lowered her head and walked out. She didn’t hate, nor was she angry, just hopeless.

All of Tiger Girl’s jewelry and her good clothes were taken into the coffin with her. What was left were just some old, shabby clothes, a few wooden utensils, and some pots, bowls, and ladles. Xiangzi picked out a few of the better pieces of clothing and set them aside; everything else, including clothing and utensils, he sold off. He called over a “drummer” and sold the lot at a flat rate for a little over ten yuan. He was anxious to move out, anxious to get rid of these things, so he wasn’t in the mood to wait for more buyers or haggle about the price. The “drummer” packed everything up and left; the only things left in the house were his own bedding and the few pieces of clothing he had chosen, all placed on the kang without any bedding mats. The room was completely empty, and he felt a bit relieved, as if he had shaken off many entanglements, and that from now on he could fly far away. But it wasn’t long before he thought again of those things. The table had been taken away, but the legs left some traces behind—a few little piles of fine dust, sticking to the wall to form some small square blocks. Looking at these marks, he thought of the things, and of the people—like a dream, they were all gone. Good or bad, people or things, without them the heart has nowhere to rest. He sat on the edge of the kang and took out a “Yellow Lion” cigarette again.

With the cigarette, he also pulled out a worn-out small banknote. Absent-mindedly, he took out all the money he had; these past couple of days, he’d never gotten around to tallying up his finances. He took out a pile: foreign coins, small bills, copper notes, copper coins, all sorts. The pile wasn’t small, but after counting, it was still less than twenty yuan. Adding the money from selling the things, a little over ten yuan, his total assets were just a bit more than thirty yuan.

Putting the money on the kang tile, he stared at it, not knowing whether to cry or to laugh. There was no one in the room, no belongings, just himself and this pile of tattered, musty, grimy money. What was the point of it all?

Sighing deeply, he helplessly stashed the money in his chest, then picked up the bedding and those few pieces of clothing and went to look for Little Fuzi.

“Keep these clothes for yourself! Hang onto the bedding for now, I’ll go find a good rickshaw yard first, then come back for it.” Not daring to look at Little Fuzi, he said it all in a rush with his head down.

She didn’t say anything, just agreed softly twice.

Xiangzi found a good rickshaw yard, came back for his bedding, and saw her eyes were swollen from crying. He didn’t know what to say, but after much thinking managed to come up with: “Wait for me! When I’ve made it, I’ll come for you! I’ll definitely come!”

She nodded but said nothing.

Xiangzi only took one day off before going back to pulling the rickshaw as usual. He didn’t go at it with the same fierce determination as before, but neither did he slack off on purpose; he just scraped by, day after day, blandly but without hating it. In just over a month of this, he felt quite at peace. His face filled out a bit, but it wasn’t as rosy as before; it looked yellow, not particularly robust, but not overtly skinny either. His eyes were very bright, but showed little expression, always shining as if he was quite spirited, and yet seemed to see nothing at all. He gave off the vibe of a tree after a storm, standing quietly in the sun, not daring to move anymore. He hadn’t been talkative before, but now he was even less inclined to speak. The weather was warm now, and the willow twigs were covered in tender leaves. Sometimes, while waiting for fares in the sun, he’d lower his head, his lips moving in a mumble to himself; sometimes he’d tilt his face up to the sunlight for a nap; except when absolutely necessary, he barely talked to anyone.

He was addicted to cigarettes now. As soon as he sat in his rickshaw, his large hand would reach under the seat for his tobacco. Lighting up, he would smoke slowly, eyes following the rising rings of smoke, staring blankly, nodding slightly as though he saw something in them.

When he pulled the rickshaw, he was still more agile than most rickshaw men, but he didn’t run himself into the ground anymore. On turns and hills, he was especially careful—almost excessively so. If someone tried to race him, no matter how they tried to taunt or provoke him, he would keep his head down and say nothing, running steady and unhurried. He seemed to have seen through what being a rickshaw man really meant, and no longer hoped to gain honor or praise from the job.

At the rickshaw yard, though, he had made friends; though not chatty, even the silent wild geese prefer to fly in flocks. If he hadn’t made friends, his loneliness might have been more than he could bear. Whenever he took out his cigarette case, he would pass it around in a circle. If others saw there was only one left in his case and felt embarrassed to take it, he would simply say: “I’ll buy more!” If he ran into a card game, he wouldn’t hide away as before, but would come over and watch, sometimes even throwing in a bet, not caring whether he won or lost, as if just to show that he was sociable and understood why people deserved a bit of fun after working hard for days. When they drank, he joined them too; not drinking too much himself, but often buying some food and drink for everyone. Things he used to look down on now seemed somewhat interesting to him—since his own road was blocked, he couldn’t help but admit that others might be in the right. If any of his friends had a wedding or a funeral, he’d chip in forty copper coins for the gift or contribute to a pooled “public gift.” Not only did he pay, but he’d go personally to the condolences or celebration, because he realized that these things weren’t just about wasting money, but about fulfilling certain social duties. Here, people really cried or really laughed, not just making a fuss to stir things up.

He dared not spend those thirty-some yuan. He bought a piece of white cloth and, clumsily using a big needle, sewed the money into it himself, always wearing it close to his skin. He didn’t want to spend it, nor did he want to buy another rickshaw; he just kept it on him as a precaution—who knew what disaster might strike one day? Illness, accidents, could happen at any time, and one had to be prepared. People aren’t made of iron, and now he understood that.

As autumn approached, he got another monthly contract. This job was even easier than all the other household jobs he’d had before; if it weren’t, he wouldn’t have taken it. Now he understood how to pick and choose work, only taking monthly contracts if they suited him; if not, casual fares were fine, unlike before when he was desperate to get into a big house. He now realized that his health needed protecting; a rickshaw man who pushed himself like he used to would only end up dying, with nothing to show for it. Experience taught a person to be a little slick, because there was only one life to live!

This time, his job was near Yonghe Temple. His employer, Mr. Xia, was over fifty, well-educated and proper; at home, there was his wife and twelve kids. Recently, he’d married a concubine, but didn’t dare let the family know, so he’d specially picked a quiet spot to set up a small household with her near Yonghe Temple. In this household, there was only Mr. Xia and the new concubine, as well as a maidservant and a rickshaw man—that is, Xiangzi. Xiangzi liked this job a lot. For starters, the courtyard only had six rooms in total; Mr. Xia took three, the kitchen one, and the remaining two were for the servants. The yard was small, with a half-grown jujube tree by the south wall, with a dozen or so half-red berries hanging at its top. When Xiangzi swept the yard, it only took two or three passes from end to end, saving a lot of effort. There were no flowers or plants to water, though he did want to tend the jujube tree a bit; but he knew how unruly jujube trees were, always twisting and turning out of shape, so it seemed better not to bother.

There wasn’t much other work. Mr. Xia went off to his office in the morning and came back at five in the afternoon; Xiangzi just needed to drop him off and pick him up. Once home, Mr. Xia didn’t go out again, as if he were in hiding. His wife did go out fairly often, but always came back by around four so Xiangzi could fetch Mr. Xia—once Mr. Xia was brought home, Xiangzi’s day was done. Besides, the places Mrs. Xia went were just Dong’an Market and Zhongshan Park and such, and after dropping her off, there was plenty of time to rest. With so little to do, Xiangzi found it almost a game.

Mr. Xia was extremely tight with money, not willing to part with a single coin; wherever he went, he never looked at anything else on the street, as if there were neither people nor objects around. The wife, though, was loose with money, frequently going out shopping. If it was food and it wasn’t tasty, she’d give it to the servants; if it was a household item, she’d give it to the servants before buying new ones, to have an excuse to get money from Mr. Xia. Mr. Xia seemed to see his life’s purpose as exhausting himself to dedicate all his energy and money to the concubine; beyond that, he had no life or enjoyment. His money only left his pockets through the concubine—he wouldn’t spend any himself, let alone give any to others. It was said that his main wife and twelve children lived in Baoding, and sometimes went four or five months without receiving a single coin from him.

Xiangzi despised Mr. Xia: always bent over, hunched neck, sneaking in and out like a thief, eyes fixed on his toes, never uttering a word, never spending money, never smiling—even sitting in the rickshaw he looked like a skinny monkey; but when he did speak, once or twice, his words were always so cold as if everyone else was worthless and only he was a gentleman who knew how to behave. Xiangzi didn’t like this kind of person. But now he treated “work” just as “work”; as long as the monthly salary came in, why care about the rest?! Besides, the wife was quite open-minded, often giving him food and drink; forget it, just treat the monkey as an emotionless passenger. As for the wife, Xiangzi just took her as a woman who might sometimes give him small change, not that he liked her much. She was much prettier than Little Fuzi, and always smelled of powder and perfume and was wrapped in beautiful silks—not at all someone Little Fuzi could compare to. But despite her beauty and fashionable looks, for some reason Xiangzi always thought of Tiger Girl when he saw her; there was always something on her, not in her clothes or looks, but in her attitude or spirit, that Xiangzi couldn’t quite put into words. All he could think of was that she and Tiger Girl, in his words, were the same kind of woman. She was very young—at most twenty-two or three—but carried herself like someone much older, never at all like a newlywed, but more like Tiger Girl with no maidenly shyness or gentleness. She had a perm, wore high heels, and her clothes were tailored just so for her to walk with sharp angles. Even Xiangzi could tell that although she dressed so fashionably, she didn’t have the poise of other wives. But she didn’t seem like she was from a prostitute’s background. Xiangzi couldn’t figure her out. All he felt was that she was scary, scary like Tiger Girl. But Tiger Girl wasn’t this young, nor this beautiful; so Xiangzi was even more afraid of her, as if she carried all the fierceness and treacherousness he'd ever experienced from women. He simply didn’t dare look her in the eye.

After some time there, he became even more afraid of her. Pulling Mr. Xia around, Xiangzi had never seen him spend money; but Mr. Xia did sometimes go out to buy things—to the big pharmacy for medicine. Xiangzi didn’t know what kind of medicine he bought; but whenever he brought medicine home, the husband and wife seemed especially happy, even the usually silent Mr. Xia seemed to perk up. After two or three days of energy, Mr. Xia would become dull again, slouching even more, like a live fish just bought from the street—thrashing around in water for a moment, and then quickly quieting down again. Whenever Xiangzi saw Mr. Xia sitting in the rickshaw like a dead man, he knew it was time for another trip to the pharmacy. He didn’t like Mr. Xia, but every time he went to the pharmacy, he couldn’t help feeling sorry for this old, skinny monkey. When he saw Mr. Xia taking the medicine home, Xiangzi would think of Tiger Girl, feeling a kind of inexpressible sadness. He didn’t want to hate the dead, but looking at himself, looking at Mr. Xia, he couldn’t help but blame her; no matter how you looked at it, his body just wasn’t as strong as before, and Tiger Girl bore most of the blame.

He really wanted to quit this job. But to quit for something so unrelated seemed unreasonable; smoking his “Yellow Lion”, he muttered to himself, “Why stick your nose in other people’s business?!”

He shelved this matter, putting it off for the time being to deal with later.

When the chrysanthemums went out of season, Mrs. Xia had bought four pots of flowers, and when the maid Yang Ma broke one in an accident, the two started quarreling. Yang Ma was from the countryside and didn’t think flowers or plants were anything important; but since she broke someone else’s property, even if it wasn’t important, it was still her own carelessness, so she didn’t dare say a word. But when Mrs. Xia wouldn’t let it go and kept cursing in her rude, country way, Yang Ma’s temper finally flared up and she answered back. Country people, when angry, can’t keep their words measured; she blurted out the coarsest curses she knew. Mrs. Xia, jumping up and down, swore right back.Order Yang Ma to pack up and get out immediately.

Xiangzi never came to mediate; his mouth wasn't made for stopping fights, much less for settling one between two women. When he heard Yang Ma cursing Mrs. Xia, calling her a secret prostitute, a whore who'd slept with everyone, he knew Yang Ma was done for. At the same time, he realized that if Yang Ma was done for, he himself would be next; Mrs. Xia probably wouldn’t keep a servant who knew her past. After Yang Ma left, he waited to be dismissed; he figured he'd be packing up once a new maid arrived. But he wasn’t worried—experience had made him calm about starting and leaving jobs, there was no need for sentiment.

However, after Yang Ma left, Mrs. Xia became very polite to Xiangzi. With no maid, she had to cook herself. She gave Xiangzi money and told him to go buy groceries. When he brought them back, she instructed him on what needed to be peeled, what needed to be washed. He peeled and washed vegetables while she cut the meat and cooked, all the while chatting with him. She wore a pink housecoat, with green pants underneath, and white satin embroidered slippers on her feet. Xiangzi kept his head down and worked clumsily, not daring to look at her, but still wanting to; her perfume flowed strongly into his nose from time to time, as if telling him he had to look at her, like a fragrant flower attracting bees and butterflies.

Xiangzi understood the danger of women, as well as their charm; one Hu Niu was enough to make anyone fear women and yet not want to let them go. Besides, Mrs. Xia was on a whole other level compared to Hu Niu. Xiangzi couldn't help but shoot her a couple of glances; if she was fearsome like Hu Niu, she had far more to admire than Hu Niu did.

If this had been two years ago, Xiangzi definitely wouldn’t have dared to look at her like that. Now, he didn’t care so much: first, because he’d already been seduced by a woman and could no longer restrain himself. Second, because he was gradually slipping into the ways of a "rickshaw puller": what the average rickshaw puller thought was right, he now also considered right. Since his hard work and self-restraint had failed, everyone else’s behavior must be sensible—he had to become a "rickshaw puller," whether he liked it or not; there was no way to be different. So, picking up a bargain was considered right by most struggling folks, why shouldn’t Xiangzi take advantage too? He gave this woman a couple of glances, yes, she was just a woman! If she wanted it, Xiangzi had no way to refuse. He couldn’t believe she’d stoop that low, but what if? If she didn’t make a move, Xiangzi surely wouldn’t; if she made the first gesture, he was helpless. Had she shown her intent already? Otherwise, why fire Yang Ma and not hire someone right away, just have Xiangzi help with the cooking? And why wear so much perfume in the kitchen? Xiangzi couldn’t decide anything, or dare hope for anything, yet in his heart he kind of wanted to make a decision, kind of wanted to have some hope. It was like having a pleasant but unreal dream—knowing it’s a dream, but wanting to keep on dreaming. Some powerful force in life pushed him to accept his own weakness, while hidden in that failing was the greatest happiness—maybe the greatest pain, but who cared!

A little hope gave rise to some courage; a bit of courage fired up great passion; his heart blazed. There was nothing indecent about this, neither he nor she was indecent—desire was equal!

A hint of fear awakened his reason; a little reason cooled his passion; he almost wanted to flee immediately. There was only pain here—going down this road would surely end in ridicule!

Suddenly hopeful, suddenly afraid, his heart felt like he’d caught malaria. This was even worse than when he ran into Hu Niu; back then, he knew nothing, just a young bee landing for the first time in a spider web. Now, he knew exactly how careful to be, how daring he could be; compelled by confusion yet clearly afraid of falling!

He didn’t look down on this concubine, this secret prostitute, this beauty; she was everything, and also nothing. If he still had some personal excuse, he figured, really, it was that skinny old monkey, Mr. Xia, who was detestable and deserved payback. With such a husband, it was no wonder she did what she did. With such a boss, he—Xiangzi—had no reason to act differently. He grew bolder.

Yet, she didn’t acknowledge whether or not he looked at her. After cooking, she ate alone in the kitchen; after eating, she called out, “Xiangzi, time for your meal. Remember to wash the dishes afterwards. When you go pick up Sir this afternoon, buy dinner groceries too so you don’t have to go out again. Tomorrow’s Sunday, Sir will be home, I’ll go look for a maid. Do you know anyone who can recommend someone? It’s really hard to find a good maid! All right, go eat, don’t let it get cold!”

She spoke very openly, natural. Suddenly—in Xiangzi’s eyes—that pink housecoat seemed much more ordinary. He felt a bit disappointed, and from that disappointment felt ashamed, realizing that he was no longer a strong-willed person, not only not strong-willed, but a bad person! Clumsily he shoveled down two bowls of rice, feeling hugely bored. He washed the dishes and went to his room, smoking who knows how many “Yellow Lion” cigarettes in a row!

When he went to pick up Mr. Xia in the afternoon, he for some reason felt very hateful toward the old skinny monkey. He really wanted to run fast and, at a turn, throw the old guy half dead. Only then did he realize why, when he’d worked at another mansion before, the master’s third concubine and eldest son got into trouble, and after that got exposed by the master, how the eldest son almost poisoned the master; he’d thought the eldest son was just young and impulsive, but now he understood why perhaps the master deserved to die. Still, he had no intention of killing—he just found Mr. Xia annoying and hateful, but powerless to retaliate. He deliberately bobbed the handlebars up and down, jolting the old monkey a bit. The old monkey said nothing, but Xiangzi still felt awkward. He’d never done such a thing before, and even with a reason, when he did, he couldn’t forgive himself. Regret made him indifferent to everything; why bother making trouble? Anyway, he was just a rickshaw puller, best to do the job properly—what good is thinking of anything else?

He calmed himself down and forgot about the fruitless episode; occasionally, when it came to mind, he found it pretty funny.

The next day, Mrs. Xia went out to find a maid. She was gone a short while, then brought back a trial maid. Xiangzi gave up hope, but it just didn’t sit right in his heart.

After lunch on Monday, Mrs. Xia dismissed the trial maid, saying she was too dirty. Then she told Xiangzi to go buy a catty of chestnuts.

When he came back with the roasted chestnuts, Xiangzi called out at the door.

“Bring them in,” she said from inside.

Xiangzi went in—she was sitting at her mirror applying powder, still in the pink housecoat, but now with light green pants. Seeing Xiangzi come in through the mirror, she quickly turned and smiled at him. Suddenly, in that smile, Xiangzi saw Hu Niu, a young and beautiful Hu Niu. He froze there. His boldness, hope, fear, caution—all vanished, leaving only a breath of hot air, which could expand or contract, holding up his whole body. This breath made him advance if he did, retreat if he didn’t—he had no will of his own.

The next night, he carried his bedding and returned to the rickshaw depot.

What once scared or shamed him most, now he just laughed off and told everyone—he couldn’t pee!

Everybody hustled to tell him what medicine to buy or what doctor to visit. No one thought it was shameful; everyone sympathetically made suggestions and, blushing a bit, proudly shared their own experiences. Several young ones had caught this illness from prostitutes, several middle-aged ones had gotten it for free, several who’d taken monthly contracts had different but similar experiences, and even those who hadn’t had it themselves, had stories about their bosses worth sharing. Xiangzi’s illness brought out everyone’s secrets, and they spoke heart-to-heart. He forgot his shame, though he didn’t take pride in it either; he calmly accepted the illness, just as if he’d caught a cold or gotten heatstroke, nothing special. When it hurt badly, he felt a little regret; when it was comfortable for a while, he recalled a bit of the pleasure. Either way, he wasn’t anxious; life experience had taught him to take life lightly—what good would worrying do?

So much medicine and such folk remedies cost him over ten yuan; the illness was never truly cured. Carelessly, as soon as he felt better, he stopped taking medicine. Whenever the weather turned cloudy or seasons changed, his joints ached—he’d take some more pills or just tough it out, barely making it an issue. Since fate was bitter to the end, what was his body worth? Once he accepted that, even a fly could amuse itself on a dung heap, let alone a living person like him!

After the illness, it was as if he’d become another person. He was still just as tall, but that former upright spirit was gone; his shoulders slumped forward on purpose, lips drooped, a cigarette always hanging in his mouth. Sometimes he stuck a half-smoked one behind his ear—not for the convenience, but just to act cool. He still wasn’t much for talking, but whenever he did, he forced some witticism—even if it didn’t land right, it was at least the effort. With his spirit relaxed, his posture and demeanor got slouched and slack.

Still, compared to the average rickshaw puller, he wasn’t all that bad. When sitting alone, thinking of his old self, he still wanted to strive, unwilling to go completely downhill. Though striving didn’t accomplish much, it was no great feat to ruin yourself, either. At such times, he would think again of buying his own rickshaw. Of the more than thirty yuan he had, more than ten had been spent in vain on medicine! But with twenty or so yuan left, at least he had more hope than those who’d completely used up their savings. When he thought thus, he wanted to throw away the half pack of “Yellow Lion” cigarettes, quit smoking and drinking for good, and save money with grit. From saving money his thoughts would go to buying a rickshaw, and then to Little Fuzi. He felt a bit sorry toward her; since leaving the big courtyard, he hadn’t gone to see her, and not only hadn’t bettered himself, he’d ended up with a dirty disease!

But when seeing his friends, he went right back to smoking, drinking when there was a chance, and wiped Little Fuzi from his mind. Hanging out with his pals, he was never the ringleader, but if others wanted to do something, he couldn’t refuse joining in. Only by chatting and laughing with them could he forget for a bit the day's toil and his grievances. Present comfort chased away grand ambitions; he just wanted to enjoy himself a while, then fall into a deep sleep till dark—who wouldn’t when life was so dull, painful, and hopeless! The sore of life could only be numbed for a while with the poison of cigarettes, alcohol, and women—fight poison with poison, knowing the day would come when the poison would seep into his heart, but who had a better idea?

The less willing he was to work hard, the more he pitied himself. Before, he feared nothing; now he sought comfort and ease: if it was windy or rainy, he wouldn’t take the rickshaw out; if he felt sore, he’d rest for two or three days. Self-pity made him selfish; he wouldn’t even lend a yuan to anyone, always saving for days when it was rainy or windy. He could share cigarettes and alcohol, but never money—he was more precious and pitiable than anyone. The more leisure, the lazier he got, and when bored with nothing to do, he craved entertainment or a good bite to eat. When the waste of time and money came to mind, there was always a ready-made saying in his heart, forged by endless experience: “Back then I tried so hard, did a single good thing come of it?” Nobody could refute this, no one could explain it away—so who could stop Xiangzi from sinking lower!

Laziness makes people short-tempered. Xiangzi now knew how to glare at people. At passengers, at policemen, at anyone—he decided not to play nice anymore. When he worked hard, he never got a fair shake. Now he knew his sweat was precious; if he could shed one drop less, he would. If someone tried to take advantage, no way. He’d park the rickshaw wherever, couldn’t be bothered to move it, regardless of whether it was the right place or not. If the police came to interfere, he’d argue but not move, dragging it out as long as he could. Only if it was absolutely necessary to move the rickshaw, would he resort to cursing. If the police refused to take abuse, even fighting wasn’t out of the question. After all, Xiangzi knew he was strong; he’d beat up the policeman then go to jail, it wouldn’t be a loss. In a fight, he felt again his power and skill, smashing his strength into others’ flesh, seeing brightness—like the sun shining brighter than usual. Saving his energy in case of a brawl, he’d never even thought about that before, but now it had become reality, and even gave him a moment’s satisfaction. Looking back, how absurd!

It wasn’t just the empty-handed police—he wasn’t even afraid of the automobiles that sped through the streets. When a car came head-on, kicking up all the dust, Xiangzi wouldn’t dodge, no matter how the horn blared or how anxious the passengers got. With no other choice, the car had to slow down. When it slowed, Xiangzi would step aside and avoid much of the dust. If the car came from behind, he’d use the same trick. He’d figured it out: after all, cars wouldn’t dare hurt people, so why dodge early,How could it possibly kick up all the dust? The patrol officers are there specifically to clear the way for cars, afraid they won't go fast enough or kick up enough dust; Xiangzi is not a patrol officer, so he's not allowed to let his rickshaw go rampant. In the eyes of the patrol officers, Xiangzi is a first-class "troublemaker," but they don't dare mess with a "troublemaker" either. The laziness of the downtrodden is the natural result of hard work coming to nothing, and their streak of rebellion contains a bit of reason.

He was absolutely unrestrained with the passengers. Wherever they said to go, that's where he went, not a step more. If they said "to the alley entrance," and wanted him to pull inside the alley, "No way!" If the passenger glared, Xiangzi glared back even harder. He knew how much those men in Western suits feared getting their clothes dirty, and he also knew those men in Western suits—the majority—were both overbearing and stingy. Fine, he was ready for that; to put it bluntly, in the past, with just one grab, he could clinch the sleeve of a suit worth fifty or sixty dollars, stamping a big black handprint on it! Even after gifting them such a handprint, they'd still have to pay as usual—they knew how strong that big hand was, that one grasp could hurt their thin little arms.

He still ran pretty fast, but couldn’t pick up the pace for nothing. If the passenger urged him, his big feet would drag on the ground: "Faster? How much extra will you pay?" No courtesy; he was selling his blood and sweat. He no longer expected them to reward him out of the kindness of their hearts—a fair wage for fair work, it had to be settled in advance before he’d exert himself.

He no longer cherished the rickshaw. Now that his desire to own a rickshaw had faded, he didn't care one bit about someone else's. A rickshaw was just a rickshaw; when pulling it, earning enough for food and the rickshaw share was enough—job done. If he didn’t pull it, he didn’t owe the share, so as long as he had enough for a day's food in his pocket, there was no reason to take it out. That was all there was to the relationship between person and rickshaw. Naturally, he wasn’t about to deliberately damage someone else’s rickshaw, but he wasn’t going to go out of his way to protect it either. Sometimes, if it got damaged by another rickshaw driver unintentionally, he would never jump up angrily to argue—he’d calmly pull it back to the workshop, take out twenty cents if it cost fifty cents to repair, and be done with it. If the owner refused—that was easy enough; the final solution always ended up being a brawl. If the owner wanted to fight, Xiangzi would oblige!

Experience is the fertilizer of life; what kind of experience you have shapes what kind of person you become. You can't grow peonies in a desert. Xiangzi was fully set in his ways; he was not better than other rickshaw drivers, nor worse, just simply a rickshaw driver like any other. This way, he actually felt more at ease than before, and others found him easier to get along with. A crow is black all over—he no longer hoped to stand out as a lone white feather.

Winter had come again, the yellow wind from the desert capable of freezing many to death in a single night. Listening to the wind, Xiangzi buried his head under the covers, not daring to get up. Only when the wind died down, the howling and ghostly noises stopped, did he reluctantly get up, unable to decide whether to go out or take the day off. He was reluctant to grab the icy cold rickshaw handles, afraid of that suffocating, sickening wind. The fierce wind feared the sunset; it wasn't until after four o’clock that it completely calmed down, and a faint red glow broke through the dim sky. Forcing himself, Xiangzi took out his rickshaw. With his hands tucked in his sleeves and his chest pressed against the rickshaw handles, he shuffled along spiritlessly, a half-smoked cigarette dangling from his mouth. Soon it was dark; he wanted to quickly pick up a couple of fares so he could clock out early. Too lazy to light his lamps, it wasn't until the patrol officers urged him four or five times along the way that he finally did.

In front of the Drum Tower, he grabbed a fare under the lamp and headed toward Dongcheng. He didn’t even take off his big cotton robe, just trudged along inattentively. He knew this wasn’t the proper way, but so what if it wasn’t proper—who pays extra for doing it right anyway? This wasn’t pulling a rickshaw, it was muddling through; even with sweat on his brow, he wouldn’t take off his long robe. If it was good enough, it was good enough. In a narrow lane, a dog, perhaps annoyed at the sight of someone pulling a rickshaw in a long robe, started barking and chasing him. He stopped, grabbed his rag-whip, and chased the dog away with all his might. Not satisfied until the dog was out of sight, he even waited a while longer to see if it dared return. When the dog didn’t, Xiangzi was pleased: “You bastard! Think I’m afraid of you?” “What kind of rickshaw pulling do you call that? Answer me!” called the passenger unpleasantly.

Xiangzi’s heart skipped; that voice sounded familiar. It was very dark in the alley, and although the rickshaw lamp was on, the light was all down below, so he couldn’t see who was on the rickshaw. The passenger was wearing a big fur hat, mouth and nose wrapped in a huge scarf, only two eyes exposed. Xiangzi was still guessing. Then the passenger spoke again: “Aren’t you Xiangzi?”

Xiangzi understood. The passenger was Master Liu! He blushed hot all over, not knowing what to do.

“My daughter, where is she?”

“She’s dead!” Xiangzi stood there blankly, unsure if it was himself or someone else who said those two words.

“What? Dead?”

“Dead!”

“Fell into your damned hands and could have survived?!”

Suddenly, Xiangzi found himself: “Get out! Out! You’re too old, you wouldn’t survive a beating; get out!”

Master Liu’s hand trembled as he got down. “Where did you bury her? I’m asking you!” “None of your business!” Xiangzi pulled the rickshaw and left.

After walking a long way, he looked back, and the old man—a big black shadow—was still standing there.

[1]

 Pulling some stunts, showing off.

Twenty-Two  

Xiangzi forgot where he was heading. He held his head high, his hands tightly gripping the rickshaw handle, eyes shining, striding forward with big steps; all he cared about was moving forward, paying no attention to direction or destination. He was delighted in his heart and felt light as if he had dumped all the bad luck since marrying Huniu onto Old Man Liu. He forgot the cold, forgot to busy himself with work; all he wanted was to move ahead, as if somewhere up ahead he could find the old self, the unburdened, pure, determined, and ever-striving Xiangzi. Thinking of that black shadow standing in the alley, that old man, it seemed nothing more needed to be said: defeating Liu Siye meant defeating everything. Although he didn't punch that old guy, didn't kick him, the old man lost his only relative, while Xiangzi was free and at ease; who said this wasn't retribution! If the old man doesn't die of anger, he'll surely be close to death! Old Liu has everything, Xiangzi has nothing; but now, Xiangzi can still happily pull the rickshaw, while the old man can't even find his daughter's grave! Fine, let the old man have piles of foreign cash, all that temper of his; he can't subdue a penniless man who’s happy as long as he gets two meals a day!  

The more he thought about it, the happier he became. He truly wanted to sing a few lines out loud, let the world hear this song of triumph—Xiangzi is alive again, Xiangzi has won! The evening chill cut his face, but he didn't feel cold, only exhilarated. The streetlights gave off a cold light, Xiangzi’s heart felt warm and content, the world seemed full of light, illuminating his future. He hadn’t smoked for half a day and didn’t want to anymore. From now on, no more tobacco or alcohol; Xiangzi will start again, work hard as before, today he defeated Liu Siye, he will always defeat Liu Siye; Liu’s curse will make Xiangzi even more successful, give him more hope. With one breath, Xiangzi spit out all the bitterness and would forever breathe fresh air from now on. Looking at his hands and feet, wasn’t he still quite young? Xiangzi was going to be forever young, let Huniu die, let Liu Siye die, but Xiangzi—alive and strong—would live on. Villains will get their payback, all will die. That soldier who took his rickshaw, Mrs. Yang who starved her servants, Huniu who deceived and oppressed him, Liu Siye who looked down on him, Detective Sun who cheated him out of money, Second Mrs. Chen who made a fool of him, Mrs. Xia who tried to tempt him...all will die, only loyal Xiangzi will live, will live forever! “But Xiangzi, you’ve got to work hard from now on!” he told himself. “Why shouldn’t I work hard? I have ambition, I have strength, I'm young!” He defended himself: “With joy in my heart, who could stop Xiangzi from making a living and starting a family? Put those past days on anyone else, who could rejoice, who wouldn’t slide down? That’s all gone, tomorrow you’ll see a new Xiangzi, better than before, much better!”  

Muttering to himself, he put even more strength into his steps, as if to prove his own words—it’s not just talk, I do have physical strength. Although he’d been ill and suffered from periods of shame, what did it matter? With a change of mind, your body grows strong immediately, not a problem! After sweating all over, his mouth felt thirsty, he wanted a drink, and only then did he notice he had reached the back gate. Not bothering to go to a teahouse, he parked his rickshaw in the “parking spot” west of the city gate, called over a young tea seller carrying a big clay pot and a yellow earthenware bowl, and drank two bowls of tea like dishwater; it tasted awful, but he told himself he’d have to get used to this from now on, couldn’t keep spending all his money on good tea and food. With that settled, he might as well eat a little something more—something hard to swallow—as the start of a new life of hard work. He bought ten pan-fried stuffed buns, nothing inside but cabbage stalks, tough chewy wrappers and gritty in the teeth. No matter how hard it was to eat, he swallowed them all. After finishing, he wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. Where should he go now?  

Those he could turn to, those he could rely on, there were only two in his heart. Wanting to work hard and stand tall, he had to find these two—Little Fuzi and Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao was a “sage,” sure to forgive him, help him, and offer a good idea. Follow Mr. Cao’s advice, and then with Little Fuzi’s help; he’d work outside, she inside, surely they’d succeed, surely they would, there’s no doubt!  

Who knew if Mr. Cao had returned? No matter, tomorrow he’d go to Beichang Street to ask around; if he couldn’t find out there, he’d go up to the Zuo residence and ask. As long as he found Mr. Cao, all would be easily managed. Fine, tonight he’d take rides for the night, tomorrow look for Mr. Cao; once he found him, he’d go see Little Fuzi and tell her the good news: Xiangzi hadn’t made it yet, but was determined to turn things around, let’s work hard together and push forward!  

With this plan, his eyes lit up like a hawk’s, scanning all around. When he spotted a fare, he ran over at once, shedding his heavy padded coat before even settling the price. As he started to run, his legs didn’t feel as before, but a wave of heat supported his whole body and he pushed himself to the limit! Xiangzi was still Xiangzi; when Xiangzi worked with all his strength, there was no competition. When he saw a rickshaw, he pulled it, as if he’d gone mad. The sweat flowed freely and invigorated him. After one trip, he felt much lighter, his legs regained their bounce, he wanted to keep running, like a good horse wanting to run more, pawing at the ground after stopping. He kept running until one in the morning before heading back to the yard. After paying the rickshaw fee, he still had more than ninety cents left.  

He slept until dawn; rolled over and, opening his eyes again, the sun was already high in the sky. Rest after extreme fatigue was the sweetest enjoyment. Getting up, stretching, his joints crackled lightly, his stomach felt utterly empty, and he craved something to eat. After a bite, he smiled and told the shop owner, “Taking a day off, got things to do.” He calculated in his heart: take one day off, settle everything, start a new life tomorrow. He set right off for Beichang Street, just to try his luck—maybe Mr. Cao was back already? As he walked, he prayed in his heart: Please, let Mr. Cao be back, don’t let me come for nothing! If the first thing goes wrong, everything will go wrong! Xiangzi had changed, could Heaven really not bless him?  

Outside Cao’s house he reached out a shaking hand to ring the bell. Waiting for someone to come open, his heart was about to jump out of his chest. Standing in front of this familiar door, he didn’t think about the past, he just hoped to see a familiar face when the door opened. He waited, half-doubting there might be no one home—why else so quiet, so frighteningly quiet? Suddenly there was movement inside. It startled him. The door opened, and within the door’s creak came the most treasured, most affectionate “Yo!” Nanny Gao! “Xiangzi? Haven’t seen you in ages! How come you look thinner?” Nanny Gao was a bit fatter now. “Is the master home?” Xiangzi blurted out.  

“He’s home. And look at you, only thinking of the master, as if we all don’t know each other! Didn’t even ask how I’ve been! You really are the ‘shy carpenter—only one saw (sentence)!’ Come in! Doing well for yourself I bet?” She walked inside as she spoke.  

“Hmph! Not well!” Xiangzi gave a quick smile.  

“Well, master,” Nanny Gao called outside the study, “Xiangzi’s here!”  

Mr. Cao was in the room, busy moving his narcissus into the sunshine: “Come in!” “Go on in, we’ll chat later; I’ll tell the mistress; we talk about you all the time! A fool’s got fools who like him, don’t you forget that!” Nanny Gao rambled as she went inside.  

Xiangzi went into the study: “Sir, I’m here!” He wanted to greet him properly but the words wouldn’t come.  

“Ah, Xiangzi!” Mr. Cao stood in the study, wearing a short jacket, a gentle and pure smile on his face. “Sit down! So—” he thought for a moment, “we’ve been back for a while, I heard from old Cheng you were at—yes, Renhe yard. Nanny Gao went looking for you, didn’t find you. Sit down! How are you? Things going well?”  

Tears welled up in Xiangzi’s eyes. He didn’t know how to open up to others, because all his words were made of blood, buried deep in his heart. After calming himself for a good while, he tried to turn that blood into simple words that would spill out. Everything was still in his memory; as he thought, it all came back, he had to slowly sort and lay it out. He needed to tell a living history, though he didn’t know its significance, but that trail of grievances was real and clear.  

Mr. Cao saw he was thinking things over, sat quietly and waited for him to speak.  

Xiangzi lowered his head and stood dazed for a long time, then suddenly looked up at Mr. Cao, as if if he couldn’t find someone to hear him, he might not speak at all.  

“Go ahead!” Mr. Cao nodded.  

Xiangzi began to talk about the past, how he came from the countryside to the city. At first, he hadn’t wanted to talk about such useless things, but unless he said them, he couldn’t feel relieved, and his story would be incomplete. His memories were built with sweat and pain and couldn’t be told lightly or carelessly; as soon as he started, he couldn’t skip a thing. Every drop of sweat, every drop of blood, had spilled from his own life, so every incident deserved to be told.  

Coming into the city, how he worked as a laborer, then switched to pulling a rickshaw. How he saved money and bought his own rickshaw, and how he lost it...all the way up to his present situation. Even he found it strange how he could talk for so long, talk so freely. One thing after another, everything wanted to leap from his heart. The story seemed to find its own words, each sentence following the next, and every sentence was real, moving, or sorrowful. His heart couldn’t stop those things from coming out, his mouth couldn’t stop talking. There was no hesitation, no confusion; he seemed desperate to pour out his whole heart in one breath. The more he spoke, the better he felt, forgetting himself, as if he was wrapped inside his words; every sentence contained him—the determined, wronged, hardworking, and fallen Xiangzi. When he was done, he was sweating, his heart felt empty, comfortable in that emptiness, just like that cool sweat after fainting, so empty and comfortable.  

“Now you want me to give you some advice?” Mr. Cao asked.  

Xiangzi nodded; he was done speaking and seemed unwilling to open his mouth again. “You still want to pull a rickshaw?”  

Xiangzi nodded again; he had no other skill.  

“If you’re still going to pull a rickshaw,” Mr. Cao said slowly, “then there are only two paths. One is to save up and buy a rickshaw, the other, to rent and pull for now, right? Since you don’t have savings, borrowing to buy a rickshaw, with interest, would be much the same? You might as well rent and pull for now. Pulling by the month is better, shifts are steady, you get food and lodging. I think you should come back here again; my rickshaw was sold to Mr. Zuo, so if you want to come, you’ll have to rent one to use; how about that?”  

“That sounds great!” Xiangzi jumped up. “You don’t remember what happened before?” “What happened?”  

“That time you and Madame both ran to the Zuo residence!”  

“Oh!” Mr. Cao laughed. “Who’d remember that! I was too flustered that time. Madame and I went to stay in Shanghai for a few months, but actually it wasn’t needed at all. Mr. Zuo sorted it all out early on, that Ruan Ming’s an official now, he’s quite nice to me. Actually, you probably didn’t know about that; anyway, I totally forgot about it. Let’s talk about now: this Little Fuzi you mentioned, what should we do about her?”  

“I have no idea!”  

“I’ll think about it for you: if you marry her, and rent a room on your own, it’s not economical; rent, fuel, it all costs money, not enough. If she works with you, would it be such a coincidence—you pull rickshaws, she works as a maid—it’s not likely to find that easily! That’s quite a problem!” Mr. Cao shook his head. “Don’t take this the wrong way, but can she really be relied on?” Xiangzi blushed, choking up for a while before he finally spoke: “She only did that because she had no choice, I can swear on my life, she’s good! She...” His heart was in turmoil: so many feelings mixed together, suddenly about to burst out, all trying to get out; he was lost for words.  

“If that’s the case,” Mr. Cao said, rather hesitantly, “unless you two could both stay here. You take a room, you both take a room, and there's no problem with lodging. No idea if she can do cleaning, chores, but if she can do something, she could help Nanny Gao. Madame will have a baby soon, it’s too much for Nanny Gao alone. As for her, she eats my food for free, but I wouldn’t pay her a wage, how’s that?”  

“That sounds great!” Xiangzi grinned naively.  

“But I can’t decide that completely, have to discuss with Madame!”  

“Of course! If Madame isn’t sure, I’ll bring her over for Madame to see!” “That works too,” Mr. Cao laughed, surprised Xiangzi was so quick-witted. “Let’s do it this way: I’ll ask Madame first, and another day you bring her by; if she agrees, it’s settled!”  

“Then sir, I’ll go now?” Xiangzi was anxious to go find Little Fuzi and report this good news, something he hadn't even dared to hope for.  

Leaving the Cao house, it was probably around eleven o’clock, that most wonderful time of a winter day. The weather was especially fine, clear skies with not a cloud, sunlight streaming down from crisp, dry air, making one feel a pleasant touch of warmth. The crowing of roosters and barking of dogs, along with the vendors’ cries, could all be heard from far away, clear and piercing across the street, like the cry of a crane falling from the sky. Rickshaws had all rolled back their canopies, and the brass on them gleamed yellow. On the sidewalk, camels ambled along, slow and steady,Automobiles and trolleys race through the heart of the city, people and horses pass by on the ground, white doves fly in the sky, and the entire old city is filled with both motion and stillness—chaotically vibrant, tranquilly pleasant, a chorus of sounds and a myriad of lives all under the clear blue sky, with trees quietly standing everywhere.

Xiangzi's heart was about to leap out, to fly straight into the sky and circle with the white doves! He had everything: work, wages, Xiaofuzi—all settled perfectly in just a few words, far beyond what he ever imagined! Look at this sky—so clear and dry, just as candid and straightforward as Northerners are. When people encounter happy events, even the weather gets better; he felt as though he had never seen such a lovely winter day. To more tangibly express his joy, he bought a frozen persimmon, took a bite, and his entire mouth was filled with ice shards! The chilling coolness that pierced his gums slowly traveled from his mouth to his chest, making his whole body tremble. He finished it in a few bites, his tongue a bit numb, but feeling content inside. He widened his stride to go find Xiaofuzi. In his heart, he could see the compound, that little room, and his beloved; all he needed was a pair of wings to carry him straight there. As long as he saw her, everything in the past could be wiped clean, and from then on, they would open up a new world together. His eagerness at this moment even surpassed that of going to see Mr. Cao; the relationship with Mr. Cao was friendship, master and servant, an exchange of goodwill. She was not just a friend; she was about to entrust her entire life to him. Two people from hell, wiping away tears and moving forward hand in hand with smiles. Mr. Cao's words could move him, but Xiaofuzi could move him without even speaking. He had spoken honestly with Mr. Cao, but he was going to share even deeper feelings with Xiaofuzi—words he could say to no one else. She, right now, was his life; without her, nothing else would matter. He could not just strive for his own food and drink, he had to rescue her from that tiny room and then live together in a clean, warm home, as happily, decently, and intimately as a pair of birds! She could ignore Er Qiangzi, and ignore her two brothers too; she had to come help Xiangzi. Er Qiangzi could earn his own living anyway, and those two brothers could get by pulling a rickshaw together or doing some other work; Xiangzi simply could not make it without her. His body, his spirit, his affairs—all needed her. And she needed a man like him just as much.

The more he thought, the more anxious and excited he became; there were plenty of women in the world, but none as wonderful and suitable for him as Xiaofuzi! He had been married, had affairs; had met both the beautiful and the ugly, the old and the young; but none of them ever stayed in his heart—they were just women, not companions. True, she was not that pure, innocent girl he always imagined, but it was just because of this that she was more pitiable and even more able to help him. That silly country girl might be utterly innocent, but she would never have Xiaofuzi's abilities or understanding. Besides, what about him? He harbored many stains in his own heart! In that case, they really were a perfect match—neither higher nor lower, like two cracked pots that could both still hold water, just right together.

No matter how he looked at it, this was the most suitable thing. Having thought through all this, he started thinking about practical matters: first ask Mr. Cao in advance for a month’s wages, buy her a cotton robe, fix up her shoes, then take her to see Mrs. Cao. With a new, plain, clean long cotton robe, clean from head to toe, with her looks, age, and bearing, she would surely make a good impression and be liked by Mrs. Cao. No doubt about it!

Reaching the place, he was drenched in sweat. Seeing that broken gate was like seeing a hometown he hadn’t returned to in many years: the broken gate, broken wall, the few dry yellow grasses on the gateway, all so very lovable. He entered the gate and went directly to Xiaofuzi’s room. He couldn’t even be bothered to knock or call out—he just yanked the door open. As he pulled open the door, he instinctively stepped back. On the kang sat a middle-aged woman, wrapped in a very tattered quilt because there was no heat inside. Xiangzi froze at the doorway, and a voice came from inside: “What’s up! Bringing bad news? Why walk straight into someone’s room without saying a word?! Who are you looking for?” Xiangzi didn’t want to speak. All his sweat suddenly disappeared, and supporting himself on the broken door, he dared not give up all hope yet: “I’m looking for Xiaofuzi!” “Don’t know! Next time you’re looking for someone, ask before you open the door! Xiaofuzi or Dafuzi or whoever!”

Sitting at the entrance, he stared blankly for a long time, his heart empty, forgetting what he was even doing. Slowly, some thought came back, one just about the size of Xiaofuzi. Xiaofuzi came and went in his mind, like a paper figure on a revolving lantern, endlessly pacing back and forth to no effect, and he seemed to forget the relationship between them. Gradually, the figure of Xiaofuzi shrank, and his mind became more active. Only then did he realize he was miserable.

When it’s impossible to know the outcome of something, people always think on the bright side first. Xiangzi guessed that maybe Xiaofuzi had just moved and nothing more significant had happened. It was his fault for not visiting her more often. Guilt made him take action, to try to make up for his mistake. Best to go and ask around first. So he went back into the compound to ask an old neighbor, but got no definite news. Still unwilling to give up hope, he didn’t even think about eating; he wanted to look for Er Qiangzi, or finding her two brothers would do. These three men were always on the streets; they couldn’t be too hard to find.

He asked everyone he saw—at rickshaw stands, teahouses, everywhere he could, walking and asking all day long, but got no news.

That night, he returned to the rickshaw depot, completely exhausted, but still couldn’t forget the matter. After a full day of disappointment, he dared not expect anything anymore. It was easy for the miserable to die, and after the miserable die, it’s even easier for them to be forgotten. Could it be Xiaofuzi was already gone? Taking a step back, even if she wasn’t dead, if Er Qiangzi had sold her off somewhere far away, that was possible too—and worse than death!

Smoking and drinking became his friends again. How could he think things through without a smoke? How could he stop thinking without getting drunk?

[1]

 Skin, not burnt.

[2]

 Gritty, coarse flour full of impurities, the kind of feeling as if you're biting into sand.

Twenty-Three

Xiangzi wandered the streets like a lost soul and bumped into Little Ma’s grandfather. The old man no longer pulled a rickshaw, his clothes even thinner and more tattered than before, shouldering a willow stick, with a large clay pot hanging in front and a broken money basket behind, which held some sesame cakes, fritters, and a big brick. He still recognized Xiangzi.

While talking, Xiangzi learned that Little Ma had died more than half a year ago; the old man had sold the broken rickshaw and now made tea and sold cakes at the rickshaw stand every day. The old man was still as friendly and lovable as ever, but his back was far more hunched, his eyes constantly tearing in the wind, his eyelids swollen and red, as if he had just finished crying.

Xiangzi drank a bowl of his tea and spoke a little of his grievances. “You want to make it on your own?” the old man commented on Xiangzi’s words, “Who doesn’t want that? But who has actually managed it? Back then, I was strong and kind, been struggling to this day, and look at my state now! Strong? Even iron men can’t escape this web we live in. Kindness? What good is it! Good deeds and bad deeds bring their own reward? There’s nothing like that! When I was young, I was really warm-hearted, treating other people’s troubles as my own. Did it help? No! I even saved people’s lives—ones who jumped in rivers, ones who tried to hang themselves, did I get rewarded? No! Let me tell you, I could freeze to death any day now—I've seen enough, people who slave away trying to make it alone might as well try to jump over the sky. What can one person do?[1]

 Have you seen a grasshopper? By itself it leaps pretty far, but let a kid catch it and tie it with a string, and it can’t even fly. But when they swarm up, as a group, in a frenzy, they can eat a whole field of crops in a blink, and nobody can do a thing! Am I right? I meant well too, but I couldn’t even keep my own grandson alive. He got sick, I had no money for good medicine, and I watched him die in my arms! Enough, just drop it! —Tea! Who wants a hot bowl?”

Xiangzi finally understood: Liu Si, Mrs. Yang, Detective Sun—just because he cursed them, that didn’t mean they’d get their comeuppance; and he himself, couldn’t get anywhere just by trying to be strong. Relying solely on himself, just as the old man said, was like being a grasshopper tied up by a child—what good were wings then?

He didn’t want to go to the Cao household anymore. If he did, he’d force himself to be strong, but what’s the point? Better just to muddle along: if there’s no food, take the rickshaw out; if you have enough for the day, rest for a day, and figure out tomorrow, tomorrow. Not only was this a method—it was the only method. Saving money, buying a rickshaw, all just preparing for someone to steal it away, what’s the point? Why not just enjoy whatever happiness you can get?

Besides, even if he did find Xiaofuzi, he’d still have to work hard—not just for himself, but for her too, right? Since he couldn’t find her, it was like the old man losing his grandson—with no one to live for, what’s the point of trying? He told the old man about Xiaofuzi as well, treating the old man as a true friend.

“Who wants a hot bowl?” the old man shouted first, then thought for Xiangzi: “The way I see it, there are just two possibilities: either Er Qiangzi sold her to a household as a maid, or she’s in the White House. Hmph, more likely she landed in the White House! Why? Since Xiaofuzi, as you said, was once married, it wouldn’t be easy for anyone to want her; people looking for concubines want the whole package. So I’d say there’s an eighty percent chance she ended up in the White House. I’m almost sixty, seen all kinds of things: if a strong rickshaw boy disappears from the stand for a day or two, you go look for him—if he hasn’t left on a contract, he’s probably holed up at the White House; if one of our rickshaw girls or women is suddenly missing, seven or eight out of ten also end up there. We sell our sweat, our women sell their flesh—I get it, I know! Go look for her there, don’t really expect her to be there, but—Tea! Who wants a hot bowl?!” Xiangzi took off running outside Xizhimen.

Once outside the city limits, he immediately felt the emptiness—the trees stood thin along the road, not a bird on the branches. Gray trees, gray earth, gray houses, all stood quietly under the grayish yellow sky; looking out across all this gray, he saw the barren, cold Western Hills. North of the railroad was a patch of woods, and beyond it a few low houses—Xiangzi figured that must be the White House. He checked the woods—no sign of movement; further north he could see the watery land beyond the animal park, uneven, with just a few battered cattails left. No one outside the tiny houses, no motion at all. Everywhere was so quiet that he wondered if this really was the famous White House. Summoning his courage, he walked toward the houses; grass curtains hung over the doors, all freshly hung, yellow and a bit shiny. He’d overheard before that the women here all sat barebacked outside their houses in summer, calling to passersby. Their clients, even from far off, would start singing brothel tunes[2]

 to show they weren’t new to the place. Why was it so quiet now? Was business not happening in winter?

While he was puzzling, the curtain on the room by the side fluttered, and a woman’s head poked out. Xiangzi jumped—the face looked a lot like Huniu at first glance. He thought to himself, “If I’m looking for Xiaofuzi but run into Huniu, now THAT would be seeing ghosts!”

“Come in, silly boy!” said the woman, though her voice didn’t sound like Huniu’s; it was coarse and hoarse, like the voice of the old patent medicine peddler he often heard at Tianqiao, husky and full of urgency.

There was nothing in the room but the woman herself and a little kang; the kang had no mat, but a small fire burned inside, making everything stink terribly. On the kang lay an old quilt, its edge as greasy and shiny as the bricks of the kang. The woman, about forty, hair disheveled, still hadn’t washed her face. Below, she wore padded pants; on top, a blue cotton jacket, unbuttoned. Xiangzi had to duck his head to squeeze in, and as soon as he did, she wrapped her arms around him. The little jacket was unbuttoned, revealing a pair of extremely large, drooping breasts.

Xiangzi sat on the edge of the kang because he couldn’t straighten his neck if he stood. In his heart, he was quite pleased to meet her—he’d often heard people talk about a “Flour Sack” at the White House, and that must be her. The nickname came from her two big breasts. Xiangzi got right to the point and asked if she’d seen a Xiaofuzi. She didn’t know. After he described Xiaofuzi’s appearance, she remembered, “Yes, yes, there was such a person! Young, always showing a few white teeth. Right, we all called her ‘Little Tender Meat.’”

“Which room was she in?” Xiangzi’s eyes suddenly opened wide with murderous intent. “Her? She’s long gone!” “Flour Sack” pointed outside, “She hanged herself in the woods!”

“What happened?”

“Little Tender Meat was well liked after she got here. But she was a bit frail and couldn’t take it. I remember it exactly, there was one dusk, I was sitting out front with two or three other women. Sigh, it was just that time, a customer came, went straight to her room; she never liked sitting with us out front, and when she first arrived she was beaten for that...Later, when she gained a reputation, everyone allowed her to stay in the room by herself. Fortunately, those who came to visit her never went to see anyone else. She stayed about as long as it takes for a meal. After the guest left, he went straight for that grove of trees. None of us noticed anything strange, and nobody went inside to check on her. It wasn't until Old Forkstick went with her to collect payment that they saw a man lying in the room, stark naked and sleeping soundly. He had actually gotten drunk. Young Tender had stripped the guest's clothes off, put them on herself, and run away. She was truly resourceful. If it hadn't gotten dark, she would've never made her escape. Night fell, and dressed as a man, she fooled everyone. Immediately, Old Forkstick sent people out everywhere to look for her. Hmph, as soon as they entered the woods, there she was, hanging. When they took her down, she had already stopped breathing. But her tongue hadn't even stuck out much, and her face wasn't hard to look at; even in death, she still looked charming! Several months have gone by, and not a single thing has happened in the woods at night—she never came out to frighten anyone, how kind-hearted she was!..." Before she finished, Xiangzi staggered out. He walked to a patch of graves, neatly planted with pine trees, with a dozen or so mounds among them. The sunlight was already faint, and within the grove it was even dimmer. He sat down on the ground, which was covered in dry grass and pine pollen. There was no sound except for a few magpies in the trees wailing sorrowfully. This could never be Xiaofuzi's grave—he knew that. Still, his tears fell in streams. Nothing was left. Even Xiaofuzi had become dust! He had always been strong-willed, and so had Xiaofuzi. Now he only had these useless tears left; she had turned into a hanged ghost! A mat, buried in the dead heap—this is the fate of a lifetime’s hard work!

Back at the rickshaw yard, he slept in gloom for two days. He absolutely didn’t want to go to the Cao residence anymore; he didn’t even need to send a message—Mr. Cao couldn’t save Xiangzi’s life. After two days of sleep, he pulled out his rickshaw again, his mind entirely blank, not thinking about anything or hoping for anything anymore; he came out only for hunger, and once he was full, he'd go back to sleep. What was there left to think about, what was there to hope for? He watched a skinny dog, its bones showing, waiting by a sweet potato cart for scraps and peelings. Then he understood—he was just like that dog: each day's actions existed solely to scavenge some sweet potato skins and roots to eat. Just making do with life was everything—there was no need to think of anything else.

Humans have lifted themselves out of the beasts, but even now, humans still drive their peers back to being beasts. Xiangzi was still in that city of culture, but he had become a beast. It wasn’t his own fault at all. He stopped thinking, so even if he killed someone, he bore no responsibility. He no longer had hope, only numbly sinking down, deeper and deeper, into an endless abyss. He ate, he drank, he whored, he gambled, he was lazy, he was sly, for he no longer had a heart—it had been ripped out by others. All that was left of him was that tall hulk of flesh, waiting to rot, securing a place for himself on the dead heap.

Winter passed. The spring sun is nature’s clothing for all. He bundled up his cotton-padded clothes and sold them all. He wanted to eat something good, drink something nice—no need to save up for winter clothing, and absolutely no need to plan on seeing winter ever again; be happy today, let tomorrow be death! Who cares about winter? Unlucky enough to survive till winter, then deal with it then. In the past, every time he thought, it was of his whole life; now, he only thought of the present. Experience told him, tomorrow is merely today continued, tomorrow inherits the grievances of today. Selling his cotton-padded clothes made him feel extremely relieved; what’s better than having cash in hand now? Why save for that winter with its killer winds anyway?

Gradually, it wasn't just clothes—he wanted to sell anything and everything he didn't immediately need. He liked to see his possessions turn to cash, to be spent by himself; used by himself, they wouldn't fall into others' hands—that was safest. Sell things now, buy them again when needed; if there’s no money to buy, then just do without. Not washing his face or brushing his teeth—it didn’t really matter much, it saved money and trouble. Who was he trying to look decent for? Wearing rags while eating a pancake rolled with sauced meat—that was real! With good food in his belly, even if he died, there would be a bit of fat left, he wouldn't die like a starving rat. Xiangzi, once so dignified, became a skinny, dirty, low-class rickshaw puller. His face, his body, his clothes—none got washed. His hair sometimes went over a month without a shave.

He no longer cared about his rickshaw—new or old, as long as the daily fee was low, it was fine. If he got a fare and made a bit of profit, he’d dump them halfway. If the passenger wouldn't agree, he’d glare, maybe even get in a fight, spending a couple of days in the police precinct as if it were nothing! Pulling alone, he walked slowly, cherishing his own sweat. When running in a team, if he was in the mood, he'd race just for the pleasure of leaving others behind. At times like this, he was quite good at being a menace: cutting off other carts, deliberately making sharp turns, blocking those behind, or suddenly bumping the cart in front. He did it all. He used to think pulling a rickshaw meant carrying a human life, a moment’s carelessness could kill someone, but now, he acted badly on purpose; whoever dies, it didn’t matter—everyone has to die! He had returned to being silent and taciturn. Without a word, he ate, he drank, he caused trouble.

Speech is for people to share ideas and feelings, but he had no ideas, no hope—what was the point of talking? Except for haggling over price, he kept his mouth shut all day—his mouth was only useful for eating, drinking tea, and smoking. Even drunk, he wouldn’t make a sound, preferring to sit in a quiet place and cry. Nearly every time he got drunk, he’d go to the grove where Xiaofuzi hanged herself to weep; after crying, he’d spend the night at the white house. When he sobered up, his money was gone, his body was sick. He didn’t have any regrets; and if he ever did, it was regretting how strong-willed, careful, and honest he used to be. All there was to regret was now past, and there was nothing left to regret.

Now, wherever he could get any advantage, he’d do it. Bumming an extra cigarette, using a fake coin for a purchase, eating more pickled vegetables when ordering mung bean milk, pulling slower to earn a few extra coins—they all made him feel satisfied. He profited, someone else suffered—and yes, this was a form of revenge! Gradually, this expanded; he also learned to borrow money from friends with no intention of returning it; when pressed, he could play the deadbeat. At first, no one doubted him; everyone knew he was decent and reliable, so he could borrow money easily. He used the last shreds of his reputation to borrow everywhere, treating each loan as a windfall, spending it as soon as he got it. When people asked for repayment, he’d put on a pitiful act for sympathy; if that didn’t work, he’d borrow twenty cents to repay fifteen, spending the remaining five on alcohol first. After a while, no one would lend him even a cent, and he began to cheat money for expenses. Everywhere he’d hung around before, he went back to visit; whether it was the master or the servants, whenever he saw someone, he’d spin a tale and scam a few coins. If there was no money, he’d beg for old clothes, immediately selling them for cash, and spending it all at once on smokes and wine. He kept his head down, always scheming bad ideas, because a good idea could get him more than a whole day’s pulling; less effort, more money—it was a great deal for him. He even went to Cao’s house looking for Mrs. Gao. He’d wait far off for Mrs. Gao to go out shopping, and the moment he spotted her, he’d practically leap over, calling to her in the most touching way, “Sister Gao!” “Oh! You scared me to death! I thought, who could it be—Xiangzi! What happened to you?” Mrs. Gao’s eyes went wide, as if seeing a monster.

“Don’t mention it!” Xiangzi lowered his head.

“Hadn’t you already talked things over with the master? Why’d you disappear? I even inquired about you with old Cheng, and he said he hadn’t seen you. Where’ve you been? The master and madam were both worried!”

“I was seriously ill, nearly died! Please tell the master to help me get by a bit—when I recover, I’ll come back to work!” Xiangzi said the lines he’d practiced, simple, moving.

“Master’s not home. Why don’t you come in and see the madam?”

“No need! Look at the state I’m in! Please tell her for me.”

Mrs. Gao handed him two yuan. “Madam gave this to you; she told you to buy medicine quickly.”

“Alright! Thank you, madam!” Xiangzi accepted the money, already thinking how he’d spend it. As soon as Mrs. Gao turned around, he rushed to Tianqiao and played all day.

Once he’d worked all the old doors, he made the rounds a second time, but this time it wasn’t so easy. He realized this wasn’t a long-term route; he needed another way, a way easier than pulling rickshaws to make money. Before, his only hope was rickshaw pulling; now, he hated it. Naturally, he couldn’t break with rickshaws at once, but so long as there was some way to buy three meals, he absolutely refused to touch the handle. His body was lazy, but his ears were sharp—if he caught wind of anything, he’d be the first to rush ahead. Whatever citizen groups, petition groups—so long as someone was paying, he’d do it. Thirty cents, twenty cents—it didn’t matter, he was happy to wave a flag all day and shuffle along with the crowd. He felt that this was, no matter what, better than pulling a rickshaw—didn’t pay much, but took no effort. Holding a little flag, his head down, cigarette in mouth, with a half smile following along, saying nothing. When absolutely required to shout a few slogans, he’d open his mouth wide but say nothing—he wanted to save his voice. He didn’t bother to put effort into anything, because he’d toiled before and nothing good came of it. In these flag-waving, slogan-shouting times, if any danger arose, he would run first—and run fast. His life could be ruined by his own hands, but he’d never sacrifice anything anymore for anyone else. Anyone striving for themselves knows how to destroy themselves as well—these are two ends of individualism.

[1]

Bèng’er: means skill, talent, prospects.

[2]

Brothel tunes: popular melodies in brothels.
