Learning resource sharing 24

It’s the season to climb the temple hill and burn incense again, and the weather’s become sweltering hot.

The paper fan sellers seem to have all popped up from somewhere overnight, with cases over their shoulders, the strings of bells on the cases tinkling noisily to catch attention. By the roadside, green apricots are piled up for sale, cherries are an eye-catching red, trays of rose dates are swarmed by golden bees, glassy pink syrup shimmers with a milky luster in big porcelain bowls, the snack sellers’ barrows for rice cakes and jelly are tidily arranged, with sauces of every color laid out. People are changing into light and gaily patterned single-layer clothes, and the streets are suddenly alive with color—like so many rainbows scattered among the crowd. The street sweepers work harder, constantly sprinkling water onto the road; yet the fine dust still rises, making people irritable. In the drifting dust, however, there are long willow branches and nimble, lively swallows, making one feel brisk and refreshed. A kind of indescribably pleasant weather; everyone lazily yawns, tired and yet delighted.

Yangge dancers, lions, parade openers, five-tiger staff, and other performance troupes all head for the hills one after another. Drumming gongs and carrying trunks and boxes, waving apricot yellow flags one group after another, they stir the whole city to an unusual excitement, giving people a vague but intimate feeling, and filling the air with noise and dust. Participants and onlookers all felt a kind of enthusiasm, devotion, and excitement. The excitement of troubled times comes from superstition, and the comfort of fools lies only in self-deception. All these colors, all these sounds, the brilliant clouds above, the dusty streets below—all gave people spirit and things to do: climbing hills, visiting temples, admiring flowers... If nothing else, they could at least enjoy the fun by the roadside or chant a couple of Buddhist prayers.

With the heat, it seemed to rouse the old capital’s spring dreams, and everywhere became a place to stroll; everyone thought of something to do, as the temperature urged flowers, grass, fruit trees, and human enjoyment to rise together. In Beihai and Nanhai, the green willows and new reeds attracted youths playing harmonicas; men and women put boats on the water under the shade of willows, drifting among the young lotuses, singing love songs, and flirting with their eyes. In the parks, the peonies and peony-roses drew poets and scholars, who strolled slowly, fanning themselves with fine paper fans. When tired, they sat in front of red walls or under green pines, sipping enough clear tea to inspire idle melancholy, sneaking glances at the passing noble ladies and beauties from north and south. Even historically quiet places had, under the gentle wind and bright sun, been filled with visitors like waves of butterflies. The peonies at Chongxiao Temple, the green reeds at Taoran Pavilion, the mulberry groves and rice paddy of the Natural History Museum, all drew crowds and sunshades; even the Temple of Heaven, Confucius Temple, and Yonghe Palace gained a gentle bustle amidst solemnity. The adventurous and students went to the Western Hills, to the hot springs, to the Summer Palace, traveling, rambling, gathering, and carving graffiti on the rocks. The poor also had places to go: Huguo Temple, Longfu Temple, White Pagoda Temple, Earth God Temple, Flower Market—all livelier than before, with all kinds of roadside flowers displayed bright and fresh. For a few coins, “beauty” could be brought home. At the mung bean milk stands, the pickled vegetables were as vivid as big blossoms, topped with fiery red chili peppers.

Eggs are cheap right now, and the fried egg dumplings are golden and tender, making people swallow their saliva. Tianqiao is even more bustling; rows of brand new tea tents have sprung up, one right next to the other, with white tablecloths and flamboyant singing girls facing the old pines on the wall of the Temple of Heaven from afar. The sound of drums and gongs stretches for seven or eight hours, and the dry, crisp weather makes the music especially sharp, striking and unsettling. Prostitutes have it easy to doll up now; a single floral cotton shirt is enough to go out looking pretty, and also clearly reveals the curves of their bodies. Those who like peace and quiet also have places to go: by Jishuitan, outside the Wanshou Temple, the eastern kiln pits, and the western Baishi Bridge are all places to fish, tiny fish occasionally stir the tender reeds. After fishing, the head meat in a rustic teahouse, boiled tofu, bai gan liquor and salted beans, can all make you drunk and full; then, carrying your fishing rod and the small fish, walking along the willow-lined bank, under the setting sun, you leisurely enter that ancient city gate.

There is fun everywhere, excitement everywhere, everything lively and colorful. The first wave of summer heat is like a magical spell, making this old city brim with enchantment. It cares not about death, nor disaster, nor hardship; when the time comes, it displays its power, lulling millions of hearts as if into a dream, singing its own hymn of praise. It is filthy, it is beautiful, it is aging, it is full of life, it is chaotic, it is peaceful, it is lovable, it is the magnificent early summer Beiping.

It’s during this season that people long for some news exciting enough to break their boredom, news worth reading a few times without growing tired, news they can read about in the newspaper and then go see for themselves, such a long, clear day!

Such news has arrived! As soon as the streetcar rolls out of the depot, the newsboy is already shrieking at the top of his lungs, chasing people everywhere shouting, "News of Ruan Ming’s execution! News of the parade at nine o'clock!" One copper coin, another copper coin, yet another, all grabbed by the little black hands. On the streetcar, in the shops, in pedestrians' hands, every paper speaks of Ruan Ming: Ruan Ming’s photo, Ruan Ming’s story, interviews with Ruan Ming, big words and small, illustrations and commentary, whole pages about Ruan Ming. Ruan Ming on the tram, in people’s eyes, in the mouths of the talkers, it seems there’s no one else in the old city but Ruan Ming; Ruan Ming will be paraded today, executed today! Truly valuable news, ideal news—not only can you talk about Ruan Ming, in a while you can see him yourself. Women rush to dress up; old people head out early, afraid their legs are too slow and they’ll fall behind; even students want to skip classes for half a day to go have a look. By eight thirty, the streets are packed, excitement, hope, crowds and noise, everyone waiting to see this living news. Rickshaw pullers forget about drumming up business, shop routines are thrown into chaos, vendors are too lazy to shout, all eager for the prison van and Ruan Ming. In history there were people like Huang Chao, Zhang Xianzhong, and the Taiping rebels; they all took slaughter, but also loved to watch killings. Execution by gun seems too simple, they like hearing about slow slicing, beheading, skinning, live burials; it makes them shiver with delight as if from eating ice cream. But this time, besides being shot, there’s also a parade, and they almost want to thank whoever came up with this, letting them see a half-dead man bound on a cart, feasting their eyes; even if they’re not the official executioner, it almost feels like they are. There’s no good or evil in these people's hearts, they don’t understand right from wrong, can’t tell black from white, yet they cling to a few rites of propriety and wish to be called civilized; but they love to see their own kind hacked to pieces, as cruel and delighted as a child butchering a puppy. If ever granted power, anyone among them would sack cities, pile up hills of women's breasts and feet—and that would be their proud achievement. Failing that, they can at least feast their eyes on pigs or sheep being slaughtered, or on a human—a little indulgence. If they can’t even get that, they’ll curse a child with “may you be slashed a thousand times, ten thousand times”—to vent their malice.

The clear blue sky, a high red sun in the east, a few gusts of east wind, the willow twigs by the road gently swaying. On the eastern pathway, there’s a large patch of shadow, packed with people: old or young, male or female, ugly or beautiful, fat or thin, some dressed up fashionably, some just in little jackets, all chatting and laughing, waiting, and craning their necks now south, now north. When one person pokes their head out, everyone follows, their hearts beating a little faster together. This way, the crowd keeps pressing forward, inching closer to the curb, forming a wall of flesh, a jumble of heads in motion. Teams of patrolmen come out to maintain order, blocking, shouting, sometimes pulling out a muddy-looking kid for a couple punches, making everyone burst out laughing. Waiting, patiently waiting, their legs already sore, yet unwilling to go back empty-handed; those in front won’t move, those newly arrived at the back keep pushing forward. Arguments break out, hands and feet still, but mouths at war, cursing back and forth, with others cheering them on. Children get impatient, get a slap from an adult; pickpockets score, and the losers yell insults. Hubbub and shouting, all blending into a din, no one willing to budge, the crowd growing larger and more fixed, unanimous in wanting to see the half-dead prisoner.

Suddenly, everyone quiets—far off, an armed squad of police is approaching. "They're here!" someone shouts. Immediately the noise picks up again, the whole crowd surging forward like a machine, inch by inch—they’re here! They’re here! Eyes shining, everyone muttering, a chorus of voices, the street filled with the stench of sweat, the people of this land of propriety thrilled to watch a killing.

Ruan Ming is a short man, trussed up, sitting on the cart like a sick little monkey, head hanging, a white placard over two feet long sticking up behind him. The hubbub rolls like waves, everyone puckers their lips to criticize, somewhat disappointed: just such a little monkey? So ordinary and boring? Head down, face deathly pale, silent as a grave! Some people try to think of a way to prod him: “Hey fellas, give him a cheer!” Immediately, voices from all sides call out “Bravo!” as if shouting for a star on the opera stage, contemptuous, malicious, detestable, all shouting. Ruan Ming remains silent, not even lifting his head. Some people get genuinely anxious, really can’t stand such a soft prisoner, jostle up to the curb and spit at him a few times. Still, Ruan Ming doesn’t move—no reaction at all. The more the crowd watches, the more bored they feel, and yet they’re even more reluctant to leave—what if he suddenly cries, “Give me another twenty years and I’ll be a hero again!” What if he asks the tavern for two jugs of baijiu and a plate of spiced pork? No one dares leave, waiting for whatever might happen. When the cart passes, they follow after, for all his lack of display, who knows if, at Danpailou, he might not revive and belt out a few lines of “Silang Visits His Mother”? Keep following! Some even trail all the way to Tianqiao; even though Ruan Ming never did anything to win their admiration or satisfaction, at least they get to see him take the bullets, so in the end it wasn’t a wasted trip.

In this noisy season, Xiangzi walks alone, head down, slowly along the city wall at Deshengmen. When he reaches Jishuitan, he looks around. There’s no one; he quietly, stealthily, edges toward the lake. By the water’s edge, he finds an old tree, leans against the trunk and stands for a while. Hearing not a sound, he gently sits down. The reeds quiver slightly, or a small bird suddenly chirps, and startled, he jumps up, breaking into a sweat. He listens, he looks, nothing stirs, so he carefully sits again. After this several times, he grows accustomed to the flickers in the reeds, the chirps of birds, and decides not to panic. He watches blankly at the canal beyond the lake, a few small fish, their eyes bright like little pearls, gather and scatter, coming and going; sometimes pushing a bit of young duckweed, sometimes blowing bubbles from their mouths. By the canal, some tadpoles with legs stretched out, waggling their large dark heads. When the current speeds up, it sweeps away both fish and tadpoles, their tails crooked as they glide with the flow, but more come, struggling to stay put. A water scorpion darts across. As the current settles, the small fish regroup, opening their mouths to nibble on a floating green leaf or a bit of grass. Larger fish stay deep, seldom surfacing; if they do, it’s just a flash of back before they twist down again, leaving a swirl and a few ripples. A kingfisher streaks over the water like an arrow—all the fish, big and small, are gone, and the surface is left with only floating duckweed. Xiangzi stares at it all, as if seeing, as if not seeing, absentmindedly picking up a small stone and tossing it in, splashing the water and sending the duckweed flying; suddenly startled, half rising in fright.

After sitting for a long time, he surreptitiously feels at his waist with that big black hand. Nods, leaves the hand there; after a while, takes out a wad of banknotes, counts them, and then carefully slips them back.

His whole mind is occupied by that money: how to spend it, how not to let anyone find out, how to enjoy it and stay safe. He’s not thinking for himself anymore; he’s become an accessory to the money, everything guided by it.

The source of the money has already decided where it’s going. Money like this can’t be spent openly. This kind of money, and the people holding it, dare not face the sun. Everyone is on the street watching Ruan Ming, while Xiangzi is hiding at this quiet stretch of city wall, trying to find an even quieter, darker place. He no longer dares go out on the streets, because he betrayed Ruan Ming. Even facing tranquil water alone, with only the deserted city wall at his back, he doesn’t dare look up, as if haunted by a ghostly shadow that never leaves. Ruan Ming dying in a pool of blood at Tianqiao lives on inside Xiangzi, lives on in the wad of bills at his waist. He feels no remorse—only fear, fear of that ghost who pursues him everywhere and all the time.

After Ruan Ming became an official, he enjoyed many things he’d once thought deserved to be overthrown. Money draws people into foul society, brushing aside their noble ideals, willingly leading them into hell. He donned splendid suits, chased women, gambled, even tried a little opium. When his conscience arose, he believed it was society's wickedness that had entrapped him, not entirely his own fault; he admitted his actions were wrong, but blamed society’s lure for being too strong, impossible to resist. Soon enough, his money ran short, he recalled those passionate ideals, but he didn’t rouse himself to live them out; instead, he tried to turn his ideals into cash. To turn thought into money is like trying to pass school by cozying up to the teacher. A lazy man’s ideals can’t stand alongside his character; everything cashable in him will be sold off sooner or later. He took subsidies. The revolutionary organizations, in their haste, weren’t careful picking fighters; anyone willing to join was a comrade. But, those who take subsidies must produce results, by any means necessary; the organizations wanted reports. Ruan Ming couldn’t just take money without doing something. He got involved in organizing rickshaw drivers. As for Xiangzi, he was already a veteran at waving flags and shouting slogans; so, Ruan Ming got to know Xiangzi.

Ruan Ming, for money, sold his ideals; Xiangzi, for money, accepted them. Ruan Ming knew that, if necessary, he could sacrifice Xiangzi. Xiangzi never meant to do such a thing, but when the time came, he did—it was he who sold out Ruan Ming. Anyone who works for money fears encountering even more money; loyalty does not stand on financial ground. Ruan Ming excused all his misdeeds with his passionate beliefs. Xiangzi heard what Ruan Ming said, and felt it made perfect sense—but also envied Ruan Ming’s pleasures: “If I had more money, I’d be happy for a few days too! Just like Ruan Ming!” Money diminished Ruan Ming’s character, money dazzled Xiangzi’s eyes. He betrayed Ruan Ming for sixty yuan. Ruan Ming wanted the power of the masses; Xiangzi wanted greater—like Ruan Ming's—enjoyment. Ruan Ming’s blood stained the subsidy. Xiangzi stuffed the cash into his waistband.

He sat until the sun leaned west, and the cat-tails and willows by the lake glittered gold and red before Xiangzi finally got up and headed west along the base of the city wall. Cheating money—he was used to it by now; selling out a life—this was the first time. Especially when so much of what Ruan Ming had said made sense! The emptiness and height of the city wall made him more afraid with each step. Spotting a few crows on a rubbish heap, he’d circle wide to avoid alarming them and provoking their ominous caws. At the west end of the wall he quickened his pace, like a dog that’s stolen a bite to eat, and slipped out the Xizhimen gate. At night, the company of others could numb and reassure him, which was what he longed for; the White House was just such an ideal place.

When autumn came, Xiangzi’s illness no longer allowed him to pull a rickshaw, and his credit was so ruined he couldn’t even rent one. He became the caretaker of a small inn. At night, for just two copper coins, one could sleep there. By day, he only managed the sort of work that earned enough for a bowl of gruel. He couldn’t beg in the streets; he was too big, no one would pity him. He couldn’t paint up his body for temple fairs, having never learned the art of making sores look like dramatic tragedies. As for being a thief, he didn’t have the skills; thieves have their own organizations and connections. All he could do was earn his own keep—he had no backing or support. He struggled for himself and, for himself, accomplished his own death. He waited for that final breath, a ghost with a bit of life left; his soul was still individualism.This soul will rot and dissolve into the earth along with his body.

Ever since Beiping was designated the former capital, its grandeur, craftsmanship, cuisine, dialect, police... have slowly spread outwards, drifting to places with people and wealth as distinguished as the emperor, to lend them some prestige. Even that westernized city of Qingdao now has Beiping’s instant-boiled mutton; bustling Tianjin at midnight also echoes with the mournful “hard bread—buns” announcement; in Shanghai, in Hankou, in Nanjing, you can find police and attendants who speak the Beijing dialect, eating sesame seed cakes with sesame paste; jasmine tea has traveled from south to north, passed through Beiping for double-smoking, then journeyed south again; even the carrying pole porters sometimes manage to board a train to Tianjin or Nanjing to carry the coffins of high officials and noblemen.

But Beiping itself has gradually lost its former splendor. In cake shops, you might still find flower cakes after the Double Ninth Festival, but sellers of yuanxiao might have already closed up shop by autumn; even stores that have stood for two or three centuries suddenly feel the urge to celebrate anniversaries, seizing the chance to hand out flyers announcing huge sales... Economic pressures are forcing grandeur to find new directions, for looking respectable doesn’t put food on the table. However, the ceremonies for weddings and funerals still largely retain the traditional rituals and grandeur: marriage and burial continue to be taken seriously, and require a bit of display. The conductors, musical instruments, wedding sedans, and official canopies at such events are not easily matched by any other city. Elm-and-crane or pine-lion funeral banners, paper figures and sedan horses for funerals, full sets of ceremonial items and twenty-four kinds of musical instruments for weddings, still parade through the streets in official style, recalling the prosperity and elegance of peaceful times.

Xiangzi’s life mostly depended on these remnants of ceremony and tradition. For weddings, he carried flags and parasols for others; for funerals, he lifted floral wreaths and couplets; he neither rejoiced nor wept, he only walked the streets with others for those few coppers. Dressed in green or blue robes prepared by the sedan-bearers’ house or wedding-sedan shop, wearing that ill-fitting black hat, he could temporarily hide his tattered rags and look a little more respectable. When he worked for a wealthy family and everyone was ordered to cut their hair and wear boots, he got a chance to look clean from head to toe for once. When his illness made it hard for him to walk, it was perfect for just carrying a flag, or holding a couplet on the roadside and trudging slowly.

Yet even at this he couldn’t be called skilled. His golden age was already over, never having established a family by rickshaw, and every aspiration of his had turned into “just that.” Despite his size, he fought over carrying a flying tiger flag or a pair of short, narrow couplets; he refused to touch the heavier red parasols and ceremony boards. He would compete with old men, children, even women. He was unwilling to suffer the slightest loss.

Holding some little thing, he would keep his head bowed, back bent, dangling in his mouth a stub of cigarette picked up from the street, feebly dragging along. When the group stopped, he might still be walking; when all walked, he might lag behind a bit; he seemed not to hear the beat of the command gong. He never paid attention to keeping even spaces in the line or matching the formation on either side; he just marched on, head down, as if dreaming or pondering some profound truth. The man in red with the gong, and the official coordinator thrusting a silk flag, unleashed every village curse at him: “Bastard! I’m talking to you, Camel! Line up, for fuck's sake!” Still, he didn’t seem to hear. When the gongman gave him a rap with a drumstick, he rolled his eyes, hazily looked around, and cared little for what the gongman said. Instead, he watched the ground, searching carefully for cigarette stubs worth picking up. Respectable, proud, a dreamer, selfish, individualistic, strong, magnificent—how many funerals had Xiangzi joined others in sending off? Who could say where or when he would end up burying himself, this fallen, selfish, unfortunate offspring of a diseased society, the ghost of individualism’s dead end?
